I read a study about sleep and phone use and the effect size was smaller than the headline made it sound. The study was fine. The number was real. It was just small.

The effect was about four minutes of sleep per hour of use in the evening. If you're on your phone for two hours before bed that's eight minutes. That's a real finding and it isn't the reason you feel terrible.

What the study showed is that the average effect is small and the variance is large. Some people lose forty minutes. Most lose almost none. Averages hide that and the average is what gets reported.

I don't take from it that phones are fine. It's that population averages are a bad tool for a decision about one person, which is you. Your own data is noisy and it's also the only data about you.

I read the effect size and the spread now. Then I run the experiment on myself for two weeks. That's more useful than any headline.

#AI #BuildInPublic #VibeCoding #tech #Science #Research #Statistics #Sleep #BuildInPublic #TechNews #Health #Data #Evidence #CriticalThinking #Habits #Advice
---
I reviewed a design document last week and the most useful thing I did was ask one question. What would we do if the third requirement turned out to be impossible. Nobody had an answer.

The document was good. It had a problem statement, three options with tradeoffs, and a recommendation. What it didn't have was a plan for being wrong, and the third requirement was the one that made the whole design expensive.

So we spent twenty minutes on it and found out that a simple version of the same thing would work if that requirement was relaxed, and that relaxing it was a product decision rather than a technical one. That's a conversation worth having before the build, not after.

I put that in every document I write. A section called what would make us stop. Two lines. It's the section people quote back to me.

A design without a kill condition is a commitment. Ask it early. Ask what would change the plan before you agree to it.

Hollywood resistance is loud and the adoption is quiet, and I think the loud part is about dignity rather than money.

That's not a small thing. People spent decades building a craft and the craft is being valued at a different number. That stings in a way a contract clause cannot fix, and pretending it's all economics misses the human part.

But I've watched enough industries hit this wall to know how it goes. The craft doesn't vanish. It gets repriced and the top of the market keeps doing it by hand, because the top of any market sells provenance.

So my read is a split. I think volume work goes to the tool. Prestige work stays human and charges more for being human. That's what happened with photography and illustration. Film won't be special.

I think the interesting question is the middle. The middle is where most of the jobs live. The middle gets squeezed first.

We shipped a feature to three countries and the strings broke in two of them because the German translation was about forty percent longer and pushed a button off the screen. That's the whole bug and the fix took a day. One day.

The layout used a fixed width in one place instead of a flexible one, which had been fine in English for two years. Two years. I'd never seen it because I'd never looked at the page in the language with the long words.

What I do now is check two things before shipping any interface change. The longest string in the file and the longest plausible value a user could enter. Both are quick and both have found real problems.

The other thing that helps is a fake locale. We added one that replaces every string with something about a third longer and it runs in the test suite. About ten lines. It catches this class of bug before a translator sees it.

Test with the longest case, not the one you wrote. Not yours. English is a short language and most of the world isn't.

I watched a team argue about a naming decision for about forty minutes and the thing that ended it was somebody drawing the boxes. Nothing else worked.

I'd heard the same words for half an hour. They disagreed about what the word meant, and neither of them noticed because each was certain about their own meaning. That's most long arguments. Two words, two meanings.

The drawing took about ninety seconds. Four boxes, two arrows, and one label in the wrong place. Then it was obvious which name described which box, and the argument ended because there was nothing left to disagree about.

What I took from it is that some arguments are about language and they can't be settled with more language. They get settled with a picture that makes the disagreement visible.

I draw it before I argue it now. If the conversation is going in circles, the circle is usually a word.

I bought a laptop this year by ranking the things I could not change and ignoring the benchmarks. Not the benchmarks. The order was repairability, keyboard, screen, and then speed. It has been the right machine for a year.

I'd bought on performance twice before and both machines were dead in three years, one from a soldered drive and one from a battery I couldn't replace. Neither ever felt slow enough to matter and both became unusable at a point where the numbers were still excellent.

What I've noticed is that the parts I interact with every day are the parts benchmarks never measure. A keyboard gets typed on about eight hours a day. A hinge gets opened twelve times. Those fail long before a processor becomes a bottleneck for anything I do.

The machine I have now is slower than what I'd have bought on a spec sheet. It's also the first one in a decade I expect to own for five years. Five years.

Rank the things you can't upgrade. Then look at the numbers.

I turned off a service last month that three people used and I'd been paying about forty dollars a month to keep alive for two years. Two years. The money wasn't the point. The attention was.

It was a search index we'd built for a feature that got replaced. I'd kept it because turning it off meant a migration and a conversation and the migration was always the third thing on a list of two things.

What I hadn't counted was the four alerts a month. Each one was a five minute check and a decision that it was fine. Over two years that's about eight hours of somebody's attention, spread thin enough that nobody ever made the decision. Eight hours.

Now I look at the alert list every quarter and ask which of these have never once been actionable. Never once. Three of them went that way last quarter.

The hidden cost of a small system is not the invoice. It's the interruptions that are too small to notice and too frequent to ignore.

Rare earths and magnets are the least exciting sentence in the energy story and the one that decides the most. I've followed this chain for a while and the shape never changes.

Mining is the part people talk about. Refining and magnet making are the parts that bind and both of those are concentrated in a small number of places, and both of those are concentrated in a small number of places for reasons that are part technical and part environmental. Nobody wants the acid in their county.

That's the trap. Every country wants the turbines and the motors and none of them wants the middle of the supply chain. So the middle stays where it is and everybody builds on a dependency they've said out loud they'd like to remove. The middle is the story.

I've watched two attempts to restart domestic refining and both bumped into the same wall. The permitting is slow and the neighbours are organized, and the neighbours have a point.

So my read is that this decade ends with the same map and a lot more rhetoric. I'd rather fund the boring middle than buy another study about it.

I watched our runbook get used for real last month. The step that saved us was one somebody added after a bad night two years ago. Check the clock on the box before you trust the log times.

That step looks like a joke until you're in a sixteen minute skew and every event in your timeline is in the wrong order. Somebody hit it in 2024, wrote one line, and it took thirty seconds to find the problem this time.

That's the whole argument for runbooks. Not documentation of the happy path, which anybody can reconstruct. The lines that matter are the ones about the thing that bit you once.

I add one line after every incident. Just one, to the runbook, in the same place. Not a rewrite. A rewrite never happens. One line is a step you'll actually do at two in the morning.

I add one line per incident now. Two years is about twenty lines and each one of them cost somebody a night.

I've been a generalist for most of my career and I've watched specialists get promoted faster for about ten years. Ten years. I'm not going to pretend that hasn't stung, because it has.

What I've never been able to do is stay in one layer. I've been the person who can talk to the database and the frontend and the person who buys the software, and that's not a skill any ladder is built to measure.

The payoff arrived late and it arrived twice. Twice. Once when a project needed somebody to hold the whole shape, and once when a problem crossed three teams and nobody else could see the seam. That's where I've been most useful and it took about eight years to become a job title.

What I'd tell somebody starting is to pick a depth first anyway. Generalist value is real and it's rented from other people's knowledge, and if you don't have one thing you can go deep on, you're the person who talks in meetings.

I found a date bug on one of our services that had been live about six weeks. Six weeks. Nobody noticed because the error was small. One hour, twice a year, in one timezone.

The bug was a daylight saving rule that had been changed by a government and the library version we pinned didn't know about it yet. So for two weeks a scheduled report ran an hour early and the timestamps in it were consistent with each other, which is why it looked correct.

What found it was a person in that country saying the numbers looked off for the month. Not a monitoring system. A human who knew what the number should be.

I keep the date in my calendar twice a year now. Twice a year. It's four lines in a maintenance doc and it has already mattered once.

Some dependencies are libraries and some are facts about the world. The second kind changes on somebody else's schedule.

I broke a migration in 2023 and the thing I remember isn't the outage. It's the four minutes I spent looking for somebody to blame before I looked at the query.

I had run it on staging and it passed, so my first thought was that somebody had changed the schema. That's a comfortable thought because it has an owner who isn't me. I spent four minutes on it and then I opened my own file.

My migration added an index and then wrote to the same table in a loop. On staging the table had two hundred rows. In production it had eleven million. Nothing was different in the code. The data was.

I didn't know the row count because nobody had told me and I hadn't asked. That's the whole mistake. The query was fine and my model of the system was wrong.

Now the first line of every migration I write is a comment with the row count I expect and the time I expect it to take. If I can't fill that in, I'm not ready to run it.

I got a fix from a customer last month. Not a bug report. A patch, in a diff, with a test, for a package we maintain.

He'd found that our retry logic didn't handle a specific response code, and he'd written about twelve lines and a test that failed before his change and passed after. Twelve lines. His message was three sentences and a link. Three sentences.

I merged it in about four minutes. Four minutes. I asked him if he wanted to be listed as a contributor on the next release. He said yes and told me he'd been using the tool for six years and wanted to give something back.

What I learned is about how much easier it is to accept a fix than a complaint. Same information, same correctness, completely different experience for the person receiving it.

I'd take a patch over a paragraph any day. Twelve lines and a test beats a complaint.

AI arrived in my kid's school in September. I expected a cheating story. One month in, what happened had nothing to do with cheating. It happened in the marking.

Two teachers told me the same story. They can no longer tell whether an essay was written by the student. So they stopped grading essays and moved the assessment into class, on paper, with the material in front of them.

I think that's a real change and it's not a scandal. It's a measurement problem. Not a scandal. The measurement problem arrived overnight. What it does to a student who learned to write well at home is something nobody has worked out yet.

I'd rather see the boring version. Ask students to use it and then defend what came out. That tests understanding in a way an essay never did, and it also means a kid who can use the tool well is not being punished for a skill the workplace now expects.

The tool didn't break the school. It exposed which part of the assessment was testing recall and which part was testing thinking.

I read the license on a library before adding it last week and found a clause about patents that I didn't understand, so I asked our lawyer and the answer changed what we did. It was a twenty minute conversation.

The library was fine for us commercially. What it also did was grant a patent license that terminated if we ever sued the authors over anything related, which is a sentence that sounds legal and is actually a decision about what we can do later.

None of my normal checks would have found this. I look at stars, last release date, size, and whether the tests exist. All of that was good and none of it is about the text.

What I do now is read the two sections that matter. What you may do with it and what happens if you go to court. Ten minutes. That's the part of a dependency nobody reads.

Ask a lawyer. Read the license. Two sections. You are one function call away from agreeing to it.

I've been wrong about timelines before and I want to own one now. Two years ago I wrote that the film industry would make a public decision about AI by 2026. It went quiet instead.

The reason is obvious in hindsight. Big studios are risk managers. A quiet integration keeps the union conversation contained and avoids a headline that costs a weekend of press.

So the adoption raced ahead of the announcement, which is the opposite of what I expected. I assumed the announcement would come first because the cost saving is a good story for investors.

It wasn't. Investors were told in earnings language and audiences were told nothing. That is a more efficient path than the one I predicted, and I should have seen it, because it is how every quiet roll out works.

I looked at our log bill last month. Three hundred dollars a month. The useful fraction was almost nothing and nobody had asked what we were paying for in two years.

I found that about ninety percent of the volume came from two services logging on every request at a level nobody read. Two services. So we were paying to store the same sentence forty million times and the answer to every incident was in the one percent nobody was writing.

What we did was cheap. One of the two now logs at that level behind a flag that turns on for an hour when you need it, and the other one logs a summary line every thousand requests. Same debugging ability, about a twentieth of the volume.

The other thing that came out of it was a rule about log levels in a review. If a line has no state in it and no error, it's a metric and the metric already exists.

I ask what a log line is for before I add it. One line. Volume isn't observability.

I read a study about meeting load this year. The finding I remember isn't the average. It's the shape of the distribution, which was nothing like a bell. Not a bell.

Most people had a small number of hours in meetings and a few people had about two thirds of their week in them. The average was about nine hours. Nine hours. It described nobody in the study. Not one person.

That matters for any intervention. If you cut meeting time by ten percent across the board, you're cutting it from the people who barely have any and leaving the problem in place for the ones who do.

What I did with it was look at my own calendar as a distribution rather than a total. My Wednesdays had about six hours of meetings and my Mondays had none, and moving one recurring thing fixed the whole week.

I read the distribution before I read the average now. A mean describes nobody and it's the number that gets quoted.

A side project of mine shipped in April and it makes about thirty dollars a month, which is a failure by any measure and taught me the only lesson that has ever stuck about finishing things. Scope was the lesson.

I'd started eleven projects before it. Eleven projects. Every one died at about eighty percent, which is where the interesting problems end and the boring integration work begins. One feature. This one I cut to a single feature on the first day and refused to add a second.

Thirty dollars a month is not a business. What it is, is a finished thing, and I've now used the same shape for two other projects. Two projects. Both are live, which is two more than the previous eleven combined.

My constraint now is one command to install and one page to explain it. If it needs two pages, it's two projects and I pick one.

I keep it small now. Finish something small. The finishing is the skill and it doesn't transfer from a bigger attempt.

I fixed a bug last week that had been reported three times. Three times. And closed twice. The third report had a screenshot and that's the only reason it survived.

The first two described a page that was wrong after saving. I couldn't reproduce it either time, so I asked for steps, got none, and closed it. That's the pattern and I was on the wrong side of it.

The third person sent a screenshot of the page and a screenshot of the list after saving. One number was wrong by exactly the amount of a pending item. That's all I needed. One image. About ten minutes.

I've changed how I close these. Before closing I write one line about what I tried and what I'd need. That line is what the next person builds on, and twice since then somebody has replied with the thing I was missing.

Ask for a picture before you close it. Two screenshots. Two screenshots beat four messages and they arrive in one day.

Grid batteries are the part of the energy transition that actually shipped, and I don't think most people have noticed. I've watched a substation add a container yard and turn a peak problem into a scheduling problem.

That's the whole trick. A battery doesn't make power. It moves it four hours later, and four hours happens to be the gap between the sun going down and the evening load. That gap is what everyone argued about for twenty years.

I've read the case against the batteries and some of it is fair. They don't solve a windless week. They do solve the daily cycle, and they're buildable in eighteen months without a new transmission line.

That last part is why I care. It's a cheap answer that doesn't need federal approval, and cheap answers that clear a local hurdle get built while the elegant ones sit in a queue.

I don't know how far the curve goes. I know the container yard arrived before the reactor did, and that ordering matters.

A script of mine deleted about four hundred rows it shouldn't have last month. Four hundred rows. And I found out because a report looked thin. Not because anything alerted.

I'd written the script for a one time cleanup and I'd tested it on a copy, and the copy had about two thousand rows while the live table had about forty thousand. So my where clause matched four hundred rows in production and zero in the test.

What saved it was a backup from the night before, which took about twenty minutes to restore. Twenty minutes. What didn't save it was a review, because I ran it myself and nobody else saw the query.

I've since added two rules for anything I run against production. Two rules. Every delete gets a select first that prints the count. And a delete never runs from my laptop.

Run the select first and read the number. If the number surprises you, you've just found the bug before it happened.

I saw a new fab announcement last week and the number everybody repeated was the investment. The interesting number is the date. Three years.

Building a leading edge plant takes about three years from the decision to the first wafer, and that's if the equipment arrives on schedule, which is the part that historically doesn't. So the capacity everyone is excited about is a bet on demand in 2029.

The other thing I watch is where the people come from. A fab needs thousands of trained process engineers and they don't exist until somebody trains them. That hiring curve shows up in the local universities two years before the plant opens.

None of that makes the announcement fake. It means the announcement is a decision about the future rather than a fact about now, and the coverage treats it as a fact because a number is easier to write than a timeline.

I follow the dates now. When a plant starts producing is a much better predictor than how much it cost.

I got promoted once for a project that failed and the write up mentioned the failure in the first line. Five months. That's the part people don't believe until they see it.

The project ran for five months and got cancelled in month six. Month six. The numbers came in. What I did that mattered was write down what we would have to see to stop, in week one, and then say it out loud in month four when we saw it.

The alternative was another four months and about a million dollars. Nobody was going to say stop, because the people who started it were the people who would have to admit it. So the kill condition was the only thing that could.

I've used the same shape three times since and cancelled two of them early. Both were correct and neither cost me anything, which surprised me the first time and doesn't surprise me now.

I write the stopping condition in week one. One line, then act on it. Say it out loud when you hit it. That's the whole skill.

I read a security report from a researcher who found a bug in our product and the write up was more useful than the fix. He described the assumption we had made, not just the hole.

It was in password reset. We had assumed the token was only readable by the person who requested it, and the token was in a URL that got logged by a proxy. Nothing in our code was wrong. Everything around our code was different than we pictured.

What made his report good was the second section. It listed three other places with the same assumption and let us check them ourselves. Two were fine and one wasn't.

Most bug reports stop at the reproduction. A valuable report hands you a pattern. If you report something, tell people what you think the underlying mistake is, even when you aren't sure. Whoever wrote the code can check that faster than they can check a description.

Report the assumption. By then the finding's already in the past.

A teammate's code was about twice as long as mine for the same feature and about four times easier to change. I spent a week figuring out why. One week. The answer was boring.

Her version had three functions where mine had one. Three functions. Twice as long. None of them did more than one thing. Mine had one function with a flag, and the flag made four of the six call sites pass true and two pass false, which is a shape that's impossible to hold in your head.

The length came from her typing out the call sites rather than parameterising them. About forty extra lines and no conditionals at the point of use.

I'd been optimising for the diff size, because a small diff is easier to review. That's true and it's the wrong target past about a hundred lines, because the review is one day and the maintenance is two years.

I optimise for the reader in month six now. That's the only reader whose opinion lasts.

#AI #BuildInPublic #VibeCoding #tech #SoftwareEngineering #CodeReview #Developer #Engineering #BuildInPublic #TechNews #Refactoring #Craft #Coding #Maintenance #Advice #Design

I read a study on remote work this year. The number that got quoted was a productivity change. The number I wanted was in a table eight pages in. How far people live from the office. Two hours of driving.

That's the variable that decides whether the policy works. If the team is spread over two hours of driving, an office mandate converts a job into a move for a chunk of them, and the people with the most options are the first to go.

I noticed the productivity number moves in both directions depending on the task. Both directions. Focused work does better at home in most of the studies I've read and onboarding does worse. Neither is a surprise if you've done both.

I'd rather argue about the counterfactual. An office day isn't compared to a perfect home day. It's compared to the commute plus the home day, and the commute is the part that never shows up in the measurement.

I read the demographics table now. The productivity line is the headline and the geography is the plot.

A rail line near me has been running the same trains for about forty years. Not the engineering. The platform height. It was set by a decision nobody wrote down.

Somebody set the standard in the 1960s and it's about four inches different from the national one. Four inches. So any new train has to be built to a shape that only exists on one route, and the cost of that is high enough that nobody orders them.

That's a really good example of a decision that was free when it was made and expensive forever. Nobody was careless. They picked a number that worked for the trains they had.

I take one lesson into software. It's about anything that gets copied. A config value, an interface shape, a naming convention. I treat the first version as a standard now, because it is one. One route.

Expensive decisions look like nothing at the time.

An analyst note in September argued that higher rates may not slow AI spending at all. I think that's probably right and I think it's a bad sign rather than a good one.

The reasoning is that the spending is strategic rather than financial. If a company believes the alternative is losing, it pays whatever the rate is. That's how infrastructure races have always gone. Every single time.

Strategies like that don't respond to price. They respond to a competitor stopping, which means nobody stops until somebody runs out of money. That's the definition of a bubble and it's also how railroads and fibre got built.

I've watched two of these cycles. Both produced a lot of useful infrastructure and a lot of dead companies. The infrastructure outlived the companies, which is a strange comfort if you're an investor and no comfort at all if you're an employee.

So my read is the rate doesn't stop this. The rate changes who survives it. That's a different sentence. It's the one I'd underline.

I got asked in a review why I hadn't written a test for something and my honest answer was that I couldn't think of one. He wrote it in about four minutes. Four minutes.

The thing was a cache invalidation path that only ran when a value was older than an hour and the clock had crossed a boundary. I'd said it wasn't testable without waiting, and he showed me that the clock was already injected and I could move it.

Third time. That's the third time this year somebody has written a test I said was impossible, and the pattern is always the same. I'd decided something was hard and I never checked whether the seam already existed.

What I do now when I think a test is impossible is spend two minutes looking for the seam out loud. Two minutes. Sometimes there isn't one and that's a design note. Sometimes there is and I've just saved myself a week of not testing it.

Say the sentence I can't test this and then look for the seam. The sentence is where the mistake starts.

There's a version of the autonomous weapons argument I can't get behind, and it's the one where people describe the robot as the murderer. Hardware doesn't murder. Somebody chose the target class and somebody signed the parameters.

I keep making the same point because it keeps getting skipped. The accountability question isn't about what the machine understood. It's about which human accepted the risk of being wrong, and whether that acceptance is written down anywhere.

I've watched organisations solve this in other domains. Aviation has an accident chain and a named investigator. Medicine has a morbidity review. Both assume the failure comes from a system and both still name a person who owns the file.

That's the model I'd copy. Not a ban, not a treaty, and not a promise. A named owner and a file, per incident, in a place somebody outside the programme can read.

I don't think it's a complete answer. I do think it's the part that's actually achievable this year.

I explained a system to a new hire last week and the question that exposed a gap was about what happens when two of these arrive at once. Two at once. I'd never thought about it because it had never happened.

It had never happened because the volume was low, and the volume was rising by about twenty percent a quarter, so it was going to happen within a year. That's the whole point of the question. He had no habit of assuming my experience was complete.

What I've started doing is walking through a system with a new person once a month, because that's the only time the assumptions get said out loud. Everybody who's been here for a year shares them and nobody notices that they're assumptions.

I ask what has to be true for this to work. One question. That takes a person who doesn't know the answer, and it finds the invisible ones. A year of curiosity beats a week of reading.

I walk somebody new through it once a month. One month. Their ignorance is a feature and you're wasting it if you don't use it.

I read a quarterly report from a company I follow and the number I found most interesting was not revenue. Not revenue. It was the count of open support tickets per customer, which had doubled while the customer count grew about thirty percent.

That's a leading indicator and nobody put it in the summary. Revenue looked fine and the ticket ratio was telling you that the product had grown past what the team could explain, which shows up in churn about two quarters later. Two quarters.

The reason I noticed is that I'd been looking for it, because the same thing happened to us in 2024 and we didn't see it until a renewal went sideways. I keep both numbers side by side now. Ours went from about two per customer to about four and stayed there for a year. A ratio.

What I watch now in any report is a ratio that moves differently from the top line. Those are the sentences the writers skip because the story they're telling doesn't need them.

I read the ratios now. The top line is the story somebody chose and the ratio is the one that follows.

I wrote about four hundred words of documentation last month and deleted about three hundred of them in review, and the remaining hundred did more than the original. That's a ratio I now expect.

Three hundred words. The three hundred were mostly explaining what the code does, which I'd written because I was unsure it was readable. That's a signal about the code, not about the docs. If the function needs three paragraphs, the function is the problem.

What's left is about sixty words of setup and about forty about the two decisions somebody would otherwise undo. Sixty words. Two decisions. Both of those are things the code can't say for itself.

I now write the doc after the review of the code, not before. The review usually removes half the things I wanted to explain, which is the cheapest edit available.

I write the why and delete the what now. If the what needs writing down, fix the code instead.

Obsidian cut 52 developers in early July. Eleven days later the Fallout remaster everyone wanted was announced.

I've written about the eighteen months Obsidian got to build New Vegas. The other team got four years for Fallout 3, which is not a small difference when you're building an open world from scratch. Four years versus eighteen months. That game scored 93 and sold 12.4 million copies. New Vegas scored 84 and sold 11.6 million, and New Vegas is the one people still mod and still argue about, because the writing holds up and the engine never did.

So a shorter schedule produced the game people still argue about a decade later, which is not what any production schedule predicts. I don't think that's a coincidence. I don't think it's proof either. Then the same studio loses 52 people in the month its most requested remaster gets announced.

I can't prove that link. I can put the two facts side by side. This industry keeps its institutional memory in the people it lets go, and somebody at ZeniMax Online is one of 213 who found out the same way that month. The remasters are the easy money. The people who made the original are the hard part to replace.

A designer showed me two versions of the same screen last week. Two versions. She asked which one I preferred. I picked the wrong one and she'd known I would, and she told me why.

The version I picked had the primary action in the top right, because that's where our other screens put it. The version she liked put it at the bottom, next to the thing the user had just entered, because on this screen the user was copying from one panel to another.

She hadn't asked me which was better. She'd asked me to confirm her read that consistency was the wrong argument here. That's a better question and I'd missed it.

What I take from it is that the person closest to the work shouldn't ask me to choose. They should ask me to check the reason, and the reason is the part I can actually add.

I ask for the reason now, not the choice. Ask for the reason. Choosing is the part you already know more about.

The Federal Reserve raised rates by a quarter point on the sixteenth of September, to three and three quarters percent at the top of the band. First increase since 2023.

I don't trade and I don't give advice. What interests me is that the increase happened while the largest capital expenditure programme in modern corporate history is still running, and nobody in the market seemed to treat those two facts as connected.

They are connected. Cheap money subsidised the build. Expensive money changes the discount rate on every project that pays back in eight years, and a data center pays back in years.

So I'd expect the pressure to show up in the second tier first. The company with three sites and a lease. Not the one with the balance sheet. That's how these cycles work, and it's how the first rounds of layoffs get justified.

I've been watching this wave from the outside for two years. My read is the money got more expensive and the plans didn't move. Something has to give.

I spent two hours on a bug that turned out to be a stale cache in my own browser and I want to write about the two hours because the lesson is not about caching. It's about trusting my tools.

I'd deployed the fix twice and seen the old behavior twice, so I concluded the fix was wrong. I never checked whether the page I was looking at was the page I'd deployed, because the page had always been right before.

What I do now is one check before I start debugging anything in the browser. Open it in a private window. It takes about four seconds. Four seconds. It has caught this twice since, both times in less than a minute.

The other version of this is a build that didn't run. Same shape. The evidence you're looking at is from before your change and it looks exactly like evidence from after.

Verify the thing you're looking at is the thing you changed. Two hours. Four seconds of doubt before two hours of certainty.

I sat on a hiring panel that made the wrong call and I can point at the exact ten minutes where it happened. It was the first interview, and the first interviewer's read anchored the rest of us.

He said he found her evasive, and after that everything anybody said got interpreted through that. A pause became hesitation. A detail became over explaining. We hired nobody and she went somewhere else and did well.

The fix isn't anything about bias training. It's sequencing. Everybody writes their own read before anybody speaks, and the most junior person speaks first, because the most senior person's read is the heaviest.

We do that now. Four written paragraphs before the discussion, no exceptions. The decisions take about twenty minutes longer and the discussion is completely different, because the evidence arrives before the conclusion.

Put the writes before the talking. A panel is a terrible place to form an opinion for the first time.

I read a proposal from somebody on my team three times before I noticed the thing that bothered me. The proposal was good. It was that every option led to the same outcome. One outcome.

Four options, all with costs and benefits, and all four ended with the same sized team doing the same work. So the decision didn't matter, which is a fine thing to conclude and it wasn't what the document said.

I wrote back one line. One line. It looks like all four lead to the same place, in which case you don't need my review. That turned out to be the answer he'd been looking for, because he'd written the doc to test whether that was true.

If a proposal has four options with one outcome, the work is done. Say so and let the person move on. Reviewing it in detail is the least useful thing I could have done.

I look for where the options converge now. One sentence. Sometimes the most useful review is one sentence saying they don't matter.

I fixed the search box on our docs site last month and the problem wasn't the search. It was that people were searching for the wrong words, and the page never told them the right ones.

I'd measured the query log and made the ranking better, which is the obvious thing. It moved the needle by almost nothing. Then I read about forty of the queries by hand.

Half of them used a term we don't use anywhere in the product. Customers called it the pipeline. Our docs called it the flow. Both names were correct and only one of them was in the index.

I added a line of synonyms built from the query log, about thirty pairs, and the click through rate nearly doubled in a week. One config file. No model, no embeddings, nothing clever.

The best clues about your search are in the queries people typed and the words they used. Read them. It's thirty minutes and it beats another round of ranking work.

A customer asked us for an export last month and the export took a day because the data was spread over four systems and one of them had no owner. One owner. That's the finding.

Four systems. Billing, activity, support and identity. Three had a service and a runbook. The identity data had a table and a person who had left in 2023 and nobody had noticed because nothing had needed changing.

I wrote the export as a script rather than as a one off, and it lives in the repo with a note about the fourth system. That took about an extra four hours and it means the next request is about ten minutes.

I want to remember that the request is the test. A data request, a deletion, an audit. Each one is a probe of your data map and each one finds the room nobody drew.

I build the one off as a script now. A second request is always coming and you'll have forgotten by then.

I read a study about exercise and mood this year with a small effect size and a long follow up. It's the first one I've changed something for. About two percent on a mood scale. Two percent.

Two percent sounds like nothing and it isn't, because the intervention costs about twenty minutes and the measurement was over a year. Small effects that persist are worth more than large effects that vanish, and most of what I read is the second kind.

What I did with it is smaller than a habit. I walk on the days I feel flat now. That's different from walking every day, because the every day version lasted about four months the last two times I tried it. Four months.

That's the honest use of a small study. Not a system. A nudge with evidence attached, applied when the situation matches the study's situation.

I read the follow up period before I change anything now. A year of a small effect is a finding. Two weeks of a large one is a press release.

I watched a senior engineer say I don't know three times in one meeting. Three times. And each time it moved the conversation forward. That's the whole thing I want to write about.

Once was about a number. Once about whether a library would handle a case. Once about who had made a decision in 2022. All three were answered in about ten minutes by somebody else in the room.

What I noticed is what he did in the gap. He said what he thought it probably was, then said he wasn't sure, then said who would know. Three sentences and the conversation had a next step instead of a guess.

The reason people don't do this is that a guess sounds more senior than a question. It does sound that way and it costs a day of somebody building on a number that was invented in a room.

I say what I think, I say I'm not sure, and I say who knows. Eight seconds. That's the whole sentence.

Nearly a thousand startups shut down this year. I saw 987 on a tracker, and it counts companies that raised money and then stopped.

I want to be careful with it, because a shutdown tracker measures what it can see. Still, the direction is worth noticing. A funding environment where the second round is harder to raise is a funding environment that kills companies without a headline, and silence is how it goes.

I've been inside one of those. The last six months aren't dramatic. I can say that from experience. They are a sequence of conversations where a number gets smaller, until the number is zero.

I bring it up because the AI narrative is loud and the shutdowns are silent, so the picture people get is skewed. The same quarter that produced a record valuation produced this list. Both are the same economy.

So I'd say the honest summary is that the money is concentrating. Not scarce. Concentrated. That distinction decides which companies die and which get another round, and it isn't about the product.

I moved a hundred and forty files in a repository last month and the migration that scared me was not the code. It was the links in about thirty documents.

Moving the code was mechanical. Imports, build config, tests. About two hours with a script and a careful review. Docs were the part nobody had counted, and about a third of them pointed at paths that had been renamed twice already.

What I did was write a check that scans every markdown file for a path and fails if it doesn't resolve. It was about twenty lines and it found nine broken links that predated my change by a year.

I put that check in the build. It took an afternoon and the check has already been worth it twice, both times for somebody else's rename.

If you're moving files, test the references. Compilers check code and nothing checks prose.

A drug discovered and designed by an AI system posted positive results in a mid stage trial and moved into the final stage. That's a real milestone and it isn't the one people will repeat.

I don't think the milestone is a model thinking up a molecule. It's that the molecule survived a trial with humans in it. Everything before that's a screening exercise, and screening has been cheap for a decade.

I read the numbers the way I read any efficacy result. One group gained lung function. The control group lost some. The gap was real and the trial was small.

Small matters. A mid stage trial with a modest number of patients is a green light, not a proof. I've watched enough compounds die in the final stage to keep that in view, and the people running the trial would say the same thing.

So my read is that this is a genuine first and a limited one. The tool did its part. What decides is still the trial, and the trial is slow, expensive and about human biology.

I looked at a contract for a friend last month and the clause that mattered was about notice. One clause matters. Ninety days to cancel and thirty days to be told about a price change.

That combination is the one to look for. Short notice for a price change and long notice to leave means you can be repriced twice before you can get out, and by then you've spent about four months and a migration.

The other thing I looked for was what counts as a user. One contract we saw counted an API key as a seat, which turns a small integration into about forty seats and nobody had spotted it.

What I'd suggest to anybody signing one of these is to write the two numbers down and put them somewhere with a date. Ninety and thirty. I put both in a calendar reminder a month before.

I read the numbers that are periods of time now. Two numbers. Notice periods. Price is the thing you negotiate and the periods are the thing that traps you.

A monitoring alert of ours fired about ninety times last year and every single one was a false positive, and we only knew because somebody sat down and did the count. Everybody assumed it was about half.

That assumption is the interesting part. Each person saw a handful of alerts, each one looked plausible, and nobody added them up. So a useless alert stayed on the channel for a year and trained four people to ignore the channel.

I did the count in about fifteen minutes by grepping the alert history. Ninety times fired, zero times actionable, and two of those nights somebody got woken up.

We deleted it. The replacement watches a different number that has flagged one real problem in four months, which is a much better ratio and it's the reason the channel gets read now.

Count your alerts once a quarter. If an alert has never been right, it's not a monitor. It's background noise with a pager attached.

I cut our onboarding from about three weeks to about five days. Three weeks. Five days. And the biggest single change was giving new people something to break. On purpose.

Old version, reading and shadowing. New version, a small task that ships in week one, in a system nobody depends on, with a named person to ask. That task is real and the blast radius is one internal tool.

That changed everything about the first month. Questions stopped being about access and started being about the code, which is the shift you're trying to cause.

There's another change and it's about thirty small things. Every repo now has a script that sets up a local environment in one command, and half of what used to be week one is now about four minutes of waiting.

I give a new person something to break in week one. Everything else is logistics and logistics can be a script.

A tracker puts this year at about 10,863 game industry layoffs and 135 studios closed. I've stopped treating that as a bad year.

Look at the shape instead of the size. Two thirds of the cuts came from AAA studios, which are the ones with the biggest budgets and the safest assumptions. The studios that closed needed one game to land.

So the industry is doing what the money asks. Cheap to make, big if it hits, gone if it misses. Everything else gets cut first. I don't think this is AI yet. I think it's a business model that was already broken, and AI is arriving in the middle of it. That's the whole argument.

That will let everyone blame the wrong thing for the next five years. If the layoffs keep coming after the tools are standard, the tools were never the reason. The model was. I'd rather argue about the model now, while somebody in the room still remembers the old version.

I spent about six months deciding whether to rewrite a service and about five days doing it once I'd decided. Six months. Five days. That tells me the decision was the expensive part. Not the work.

The service was about four thousand lines and it had three problems. It was slow, it was hard to test, and only one person understood it. I kept measuring the rewrite and never measuring the cost of the situation.

I moved it by writing down the cost of not doing it. Four hours a month of confusion plus two incidents in a year plus the bus factor of one. Once that was a number, the rewrite was obvious.

What I'd do differently is set a threshold before the measuring starts. Something like if this costs more than a day a month, it gets rewritten. Then the measuring takes a week instead of six months.

I decide with a number I set in advance now. Six months of deciding is one number nobody wrote down. One number.

The chip export story is the clearest example I know of policy following revenue rather than strategy, and I say that without naming a villain.

Here's how it looks from outside. Restrictions with a security rationale, then a revision that opens the door, then a tariff that prices the door. Each step has its own justification. Read the sequence as a whole and it tells you which constraint was binding.

I've watched this in energy and in telecom. A stated goal seldom survives contact with a sales pipeline. That'sn't corruption. It's a system with several goals, and the one with a revenue number attached is the one that gets measured.

So the practical question for me is about substitution. If the chips don't come from here, do they come from somewhere else, or does the domestic programme get slower. Those are different futures and the policy doesn't settle it.

I'd watch the domestic fabs and not the license list. Those are the part that compounds.

I had to write a message telling about forty users that a feature they used was going away and the version that worked began with the sentence they cared about. Not with the reason.

Our reason was our own complexity and nobody needed it in the first line. So the message opened with what was changing and the date, then one line about what to use instead, then the reason in a paragraph at the bottom for the three people who'd ask.

About nine people replied. Seven were fine. Two had a workflow we hadn't seen, and both of those turned out to be cheap to support, so we kept a small path for them and shipped the rest.

I'd have got it wrong a year ago by writing it as an announcement. It's a request for information and the first line should tell people whether they have to act.

I lead with what changes and when now. One line. Reasons are for the people who ask, and they'll ask anyway.

I watched a teammate say no last month and she got promoted for it. One meeting. She said no to a thing in front of the person who wanted it.

The thing was a feature with no owner and no date and a lot of enthusiasm. Two other people had already said yes to it in the same week, in different rooms, for different reasons. She was the first one to say what it would displace.

What struck me is that she didn't say the idea was bad. She said the team could do it or the other thing and here is the cost of each, and then she asked him to pick. He picked. That was the end of it.

The promotion write up called it scope management. I'd call it being the person who makes the tradeoff visible, which is most of what a senior engineer does and almost none of what a junior one is asked to do. Ninety seconds.

Say the tradeoff out loud, in the room, once. That's a skill and it's learnable.

---
A process change we made last quarter cut our deploy failures by about half. About half. The change was not technical. We made deploys boring by making them small.

I used to ship once a week with about forty changes in it. Each failure required working out which of the forty did it, and the answer took an afternoon about half the time. So the recovery was slow and the fear was high.

Now it's about six deploys a day with one or two changes each. One or two changes. Same total work and the failures are obvious, because there's a single candidate. I found a bad change in about four minutes last week. Four minutes.

The reason this took so long to adopt is that the once a week release felt safer. A single window, one checklist, one person watching. It was safer for the deployer and worse for the person debugging on Saturday.

I make the change small now. Small changes are obvious when they break and obvious is the whole benefit.

I let an agent loose on a refactor and the interesting part was not what it wrote. It was the four permissions I had to think about for the first time. Four permissions.

It wanted to run the test suite. That's fine. It wanted to install a package, which I said no to, and it worked around it without complaining. It wanted to read a production config file, which I said no to. And it wanted to delete a directory, which I said no to. I said no to all three.

That list is now the list I give any agent, and writing it took twenty minutes. Twenty minutes. Every item on it is something I'd have done without asking at eleven at night with a deploy window open and nobody awake to disagree with me. That's the actual finding. The agent forced me to write down my own permissions.

The refactor itself was fine. About four hundred lines moved, tests passed, one naming choice I changed by hand.

The value was in the policy, not the code. I use it for humans too.

I sat in a standup last month that ran twenty two minutes and I couldn't tell you what anybody was working on. Nobody said a word about work.

I changed it the next week. I turned the round robin into three questions. What did you finish. What are you on now. What is stuck. Fifteen minutes, and the stuck one gets the time.

Two people hated it at first. They said they liked hearing the whole picture. Fine. One of them started using the stuck slot for real problems and he stopped complaining by the third week.

I don't care about the format. I care that a meeting ends with somebody unblocked. Status is a document. Blockers are a conversation and they need a room.

A status meeting that measures attendance is a broadcast. A status meeting that ends with somebody unblocked is a tool. I only keep the second kind now. I ask once a month whether mine is either one.

---
I think about space debris differently after reading two papers. Two papers. Most of the tracked objects aren't satellites. They're the pieces. Different problem.

A collision at orbital speed doesn't dent anything. It shatters both objects into thousands of fragments, and each fragment can cause another collision, which is the part that makes this a different kind of problem from litter.

I find it tractable because a few hundred objects carry most of the risk. Four hundred. If you can remove those, the cascade probability drops a lot. That's the argument for active removal and it's a much smaller, more specific argument than the general one.

The hard part is legal rather than technical. Nobody has settled who may touch whose hardware in orbit, and a removal mission that grabs the wrong thing is a diplomatic incident with a rocket attached.

I follow the tracking data now. The count of objects is not the story. Count the dangerous ones.

I chased a bug for three days that only appeared on Tuesdays. Production, every week, same window. I read the code four times and found nothing. Nothing. Four times.

The cause was one timezone. A report ran at midnight in a zone that shifted once a year, and the shift happened to land on a Tuesday that year because of when the batch was scheduled. Two lines of code, one assumption, three days of my life.

The worst part is that the answer was in my notes the whole time. I'd hit a version of it two years earlier and never connected the two. Never connected them.

I didn't feel stupid. I felt lucky, because the first thing I checked was the schedule and it said Tuesday. If it had said Monday I would still be there.

I keep that one in my notes. When a bug has a calendar, stop reading the code and look at the clock. Recurring at a fixed time is a schedule problem and not a logic problem.

The compute deals this year are the part of the AI story that will age best. A fourteen billion dollar agreement, a multi gigawatt build, a chip order measured in millions of units.

I say they will age best because they're physical. A model gets replaced in six months. A substation lasts forty years. The contracts people sign this year are the skeleton the industry runs on for a decade.

I've watched infrastructure waves before. Rail, fibre, cloud. In all three, the build out outlasted the boom and the capacity was eventually used for something nobody predicted. Somebody always pays for the overbuild and somebody always inherits it cheap.

So I don't read these numbers as proof of a healthy market. I read them as a bet on a decade. The interesting question isn't whether the bet is right. It's who holds the bag if it's wrong.

That's a question about who signed the lease. Not about the model.

A dependency we use shipped a minor version and I upgraded on a Friday because the changelog said two words. Two words. Bug fixes.

It broke our date handling in a way nobody noticed for eleven days. They had fixed a rounding bug and our code depended on the wrong rounding. We had built on top of their mistake and their fix was correct and our week was ruined.

The upgrade took four minutes. The eleven days of wrong dates took longer, and nobody noticed because the report was monthly and the numbers looked close.

I read changelogs now. Not the whole thing. I read the sections about things we call. If the diff touches a function in our import list, the upgrade gets a test and a Tuesday.

The costly part of a dependency is not the code. It's the assumptions you made about it, and those aren't in the changelog. So I write them down where the upgrade happens.

I've read the same article about AI in medicine fifteen times and it always skips the part that would actually change care.

The interesting use isn't a diagnosis. It's triage. Who gets seen first, who gets a scan today, who can wait a week. That's where the queues are and that's where a model can help without carrying the legal weight of a decision.

I've watched this in two hospitals. The bottleneck isn't knowledge. It's attention, and attention is a resource that has to be allocated. A tool that sorts a list well saves more lives than a tool that's right ninety five percent of the time.

So my position is that the boring application is the valuable one and the flashy application is the one that gets the press. That's true in every sector I've looked at.

I'd fund the queue. It saves more lives. Nobody takes a photo of a queue.

Somebody asked me for a feature I was never going to build and I said no in a way that ended the conversation badly. I told him it wasn't on the roadmap. He heard that it was never happening and that I didn't care.

What I do now is give a condition instead of a no. I told the next person it becomes worth building if three customers ask and one of them will pay. That's honest. Three and one. Those were the numbers and they were real. It also gives him something to do.

One of those conditions got met about five months later and I built the thing in a week. He remembered the condition and told me the week it changed. That's a better relationship than a yes would have bought.

A no with a condition is not a softer no. It's a real answer with a path in it. People can work with a path.

A vendor raised our prices about forty percent this year. Forty percent. The negotiation took about fifteen minutes because I had one number. Fifteen minutes. The cost of leaving.

I'd estimated the migration at about nine weeks of work and about two weeks of retraining. That's a real number and I could say it out loud, which changed the conversation from a complaint into a decision.

What I didn't do is pretend I'd leave tomorrow. Vendors hear that every day and it's not credible, and credibility is the only thing that moves a price.

We settled at about eighteen percent with a longer term. Eighteen percent. That's a decent outcome and it only happened because I knew what the alternative cost before I picked up the phone.

I spent a weekend building myself a tool and deleted it four months later. It was about two hundred lines that turned a manual report into one command. I used it twice a week. Twice a week.

It broke silently in month three. Nobody told me. A source changed a column name and my script wrote zeros into a report and nobody reads reports. I didn't check the row count, because I'd written the thing and I'd trusted it. That's the whole bug. My only check was that the number looked plausible, and zero can look plausible in a quiet week. Zero looks plausible.

I deleted it and wrote the report by hand until it earned a test. The second version has a row count and an alert if the count moves more than ten percent. Four lines of checking. Boring fix for a boring bug.

Personal tools deserve the same shape as production ones if other people see the output. If nobody sees the output, delete it. You'll save yourself the upkeep. Half a tool is worse than no tool.

I added a log line on a hunch during a slow afternoon and it answered a question three months later in about four minutes. The line printed the tenant id and the cache key. Nothing clever.

We had a bug where one customer's data was stale and we couldn't reproduce it because the traffic was unusual. Two minutes. Two minutes into the investigation I searched the log and found the pattern. One tenant, one key, one job that never finished.

The lesson for me is not about logging everything. It's about logging identifiers. Every log line in our service now carries the request id, the tenant, and the user. Nine out of ten questions become one search. One search.

Logging is a bet on a question you haven't been asked yet. Identifiers are the cheapest bet and I've never regretted one. I've added about nine of those lines since that afternoon. Verbose messages I regret about once a month.

I ran a technical interview last month and the candidate was right and my question was wrong. Two days later. That's when I found out, and I've been telling people since.

The question was about a design I'd built and I'd baked in a constraint that no longer holds because we changed a dependency in March. Stale question. I hadn't updated it, so I was testing whether somebody would arrive at my old answer.

She pushed back in the interview and I explained why the constraint mattered. I was confident and I was wrong, and I only worked it out because I went to quote her feedback to another interviewer and read the constraint out loud.

There's a comment in our codebase from four years ago that explains why the retry count is three. It saved me about six months of a decision. Three is one attempt, one retry, and one for the network wobble. Two lines. That's all it was.

I've opened that ticket about twenty times since then because our error rate looked high. It pointed at a downstream service that falls over under load, and raising the count would have tripled the requests during the exact incident it was protecting against.

I rewrote it in the present tense and added the date. Explaining why is far more valuable than explaining what. The what is in the code. The why is nowhere unless somebody wrote it down.

I now write one comment at the top of any file where the shape is surprising. Two lines on why this is like this. Its only readers are future me and future you. That's it.

I read a book about failures this year that had almost nothing to say about software and it changed how I run a release. Not the story. I mean the appendix.

It was a table of about forty incidents with one column for what the operator believed and one for what was happening. That's the whole book.

Almost every entry had the same shape. Somebody had a model that was correct on the previous version of the system, and the system had changed in a way nobody told them about. So their action was reasonable and wrong.

That's the question I ask before any risky change. What did this used to be true about, and when did that stop. It's a minute. Twice this year the answer was that the assumption came from an architecture we'd retired.

Books about other fields are the cheapest source of ideas. Nobody in software has a monopoly on being surprised.

I rolled back a change last month that had already been forward fixed, because the forward fix was the second thing we'd shipped in an hour and that's a pattern I've learned to distrust. Two changes at once is a coin flip.

The original change was fine and the forward fix was fine. What wasn't fine was not knowing which one had caused the new error rate, and I could not tell from the graphs because both had touched the same endpoint.

I sent us back to the state before both and we waited twenty minutes. Everything was healthy. Then we shipped them one at a time, twenty minutes apart, and found that the forward fix was the one with the problem.

A rollback is not a defeat. It's the only way to get back to a state you've measured, and the state you've measured is the only place you can start a clean experiment from.

I do one change at a time when something is wrong. One change. Faster is slower.

The EU rules on AI moved into a phase where penalties actually apply from the second of August. Enforcement dates change behaviour in a way that guidelines don't.

I've been through compliance regimes as an engineer. Nothing focuses a roadmap like a fine schedule. Work that sat in a backlog for two years gets resourced in a quarter because somebody legal prints a table of numbers.

What interests me is the asymmetry. A European penalty applies to a company selling into Europe. A company that doesn't sell there can ignore it. So the rules become a market access question rather than a technology question, and market access has always been the strongest lever anybody has.

I don't have a strong view on whether the rules are well designed. I do have a view on what will happen next. Documentation will get built, model cards will get standardized, and those artifacts will leak into products everywhere because it's cheaper to write once.

That's how Brussels has changed tech before. Not by banning. By making a form.

I stopped chasing the word senior about two years ago and my work improved, which is an uncomfortable thing to admit.

The chase made my decisions small. I'd pick the visible fix over the important one because visible fixes get mentioned in reviews. I'd take the project with a launch date instead of the one with a real problem. That's a career strategy that works and it makes you worse at the job.

What replaced it was a question about the next twelve months. Which of these will still matter in a year. Not which one looks good. Which one is load bearing.

Turns out the answers are boring. Tests, documentation, removing the thing nobody uses, knowing who owns which piece. None of it is a story you tell at a dinner.

I don't know if this hurts me professionally. I do know I've shipped more of the work I'd want to be judged on, and I sleep better on the days when the recognisable parts are absent.

I mentored somebody through their first incident last month and the thing I did most was stay quiet. One question. Ten minutes. It was harder than it sounds and I got it wrong once.

He was working through it and I could see where he was going to be wrong about four steps in. I said nothing for about ten minutes and then I said something, and the something was a hint about the wrong layer.

What worked better was asking a question about what he'd expect to see. He answered it, paused, and went somewhere else entirely. That's the whole technique and it works because the answer was already in his head.

The other thing I did right was nothing at the end. I let him write the summary and I didn't correct the wording, and the summary he wrote was better than what I'd have produced.

I ask. Then I wait. The waiting is the part that feels like doing nothing and is the actual teaching.

Two thirds of this year's game industry layoffs came from AAA studios. The studios with the most money cut the most people, and I keep not being surprised.

Look at the structure. A single AAA game can cost more than the entire output of twenty small studios, so a miss is measured in hundreds of millions and a hit is still a slow return. That forces one move. Cut the variable cost, which is people, and protect the quarter.

I don't think this is a villain story. It's a model story. The model rewards a smaller payroll and a bigger bet every single year, so that's what the industry keeps doing. I don't see anybody in the chain paid to ask a different question.

A migration ran for four hours instead of the eleven minutes I'd predicted and the intervention I needed was a decision, not a fix. I wasn't there to make it.

I'd run it in staging against about two hundred thousand rows. It finished in eleven minutes. What I hadn't asked was the production row count, which was about forty million, and the difference in write locking behavior between the two sizes is not linear.

What saved it was that I'd written the abort command in the ticket before I started, and a colleague read the ticket and used it at the two hour mark when I was unreachable. The data was fine. The deploy was late.

Since then the first line of my migration is a comment with the row count I expect and the time I expect, and the second line is the abort. If I can't fill in the first, I'm not ready.

Write the abort command before you need it. Nobody thinks well at hour three.

---
I added a cache that made our service slower and I didn't believe it for a week. The hit rate was ninety four percent. Everything looked better on the cache dashboard and worse on the customer dashboard.

Invalidation was the problem. It was a fan out from a job that ran every few seconds, so a hot key got dropped and refilled constantly, and every refill held a lock the request path waited on. Ninety four percent hits, ninety four percent waits.

Ninety four percent hits. Ninety four percent waits. I removed it and the endpoint went from about nine hundred milliseconds to two hundred. The cache had been holding a lock, not saving time, and the dashboard I built to check it only measured hits.

Measure the thing the user feels. A hit rate is a proxy and proxies lie when you write them from the same assumptions that built the thing.

A side project of mine died in June after about seven months and the postmortem is short. I built the wrong half first.

I spent four months on a nice interface and a nice data model, and about three weeks on the part that gets people to come back. The retention was terrible and the interface was lovely, and I could show you screenshots of a thing that nobody used twice.

The mistake was understandable and specific. The interface was the part I could do well and the retention was the part I'd never built, so I did the part I was good at for as long as I could.

What I'd do differently is write the number that would make me stop before I start. Mine would have been ten returning users in week four, and I would have hit that wall in about six weeks instead of thirty.

Pick the number that ends it. Write it down. If you can't name one, you're not building a product. You're decorating.

The best developer I've worked with kept a log of every bug he fixed. Two sentences each. I started doing it and I still do. Six years now.

It's the least glamorous habit in the craft and it's the one that compounds. Six months in, you have a searchable record of your own failures, and failure is the only teacher I've found who never gets bored.

I've watched people repeat the same mistake for years because they never wrote it down. I've done it myself. The log catches that, because a pattern you can search is a pattern you can see.

So my advice to anybody starting is boring. Keep notes on what broke. Not a blog, not a portfolio. A file. The log isn't a diary. It's a lookup table.

Mine has about four hundred entries now. It takes me two minutes a week. I open it more than I open any documentation.

A recruiter reached out, we had one good call, and then nothing for five weeks. I want to write about the silence because most people never do.

I did the usual thing first. I assumed it was me. I reread the thread looking for something I said wrong and found nothing useful, which is what that exercise always produces. Then I looked at the actual timeline.

The role went up in March. That call was in April. By May the posting was live again with the same text and a different date. That isn't a rejection. That's a budget. Silence here is almost never personal. It's a hiring manager who lost the headcount, or a reorganisation, or a freeze that landed between your call and the next step.

What I do now is one follow up at day ten and then I move on. One message, short, no guilt. Ten words and a question about whether the timing changed. Ghosting costs you nothing you hadn't already lost. The five weeks you spend rereading the thread is the real bill.

I heard last week that a teammate had stopped writing design documents because nobody read them. I checked and he was right. Four of five. Four of his last five had no comments at all.

That's a real signal and the conclusion he drew from it is wrong. They weren't read because there was nothing to disagree with on page one, and the first page is the only page most people open.

What worked for somebody else on the team is a two paragraph intro. One page. Decision, recommendation, one line about what he'd need to hear to change his mind. That went into the body of a message, not a file, and it got nine replies. Nine replies.

It's about where it lives as much as how long it is. A document in a folder is a library. A paragraph in a channel is a conversation, and people join conversations.

I put the argument where people already are now. A folder is where it goes after somebody has disagreed with it.

The best money I've made came from work nobody wanted to do. Twice now, and I'd bet on it a third time.

I took a data migration that everybody else at the table called boring. Twenty three tables, four years of accounts, no design decisions to make and no glory in it. It took me nine days and the client still calls me for everything.

I've seen the same pattern twice. Interesting work draws a crowd and the crowd competes on taste. Dull work draws nobody and the competition is just showing up and finishing it. Nothing shiny. Just finished. That's a much easier game if you can stand being unimpressed with yourself.

I've watched people chase the shiny project and lose to somebody with a spreadsheet and a work ethic. Every single time.

So the advice is unglamorous. Find the task your smart friend turned down and do it well. Revenue arrives before reputation and it lasts longer.

One estimate puts data centers at about 9 percent of United States electricity generation by 2030. Another puts consumption growth at 15 percent a year. That's four years away.

Nine percent in four years is a lot of new generation, and almost none of it exists yet. So either the forecast is wrong, or this is the biggest infrastructure project in the country. I don't hear it described that way.

The forecasting is the interesting part. Each of these numbers is a range with a huge top end. The top end is what gets quoted, because a scary number travels. The same report that says 9 percent also says it depends on efficiency, on what gets built, and on whether the chips get better.

So I'd plan for the middle of a range. Efficiency first, then generation, then the grid. I'd rather be early on the boring answer than late on the scary one.

I find the hardest interview question to answer is why I left a job, and I've been on both sides of it enough to know the truth is usually two sentences. Two sentences. Everything else is defensiveness.

I've heard every version of the answer. Looking for a new challenge, wanted a different scale, team was restructured. All of those are true and all of them are the second half of the sentence.

There's a first half and it's almost always one of three things. Three reasons. My work stopped interesting me, a person made the week worse, or the money didn't track the scope. Those are fine reasons and none of them need to be said in a way that blames anybody.

I listen for whether the person can describe the thing without getting smaller. He was difficult, we didn't get along, and I should have left in month four. That answer tells me somebody has thought about it and I can work with that.

Answer it in two sentences. Name the reason and your part in the timing.

#AI #BuildInPublic #VibeCoding #tech #Interviewing #Career #Jobs #Hiring #BuildInPublic #TechNews #Workplace #Advice #Growth #Honesty #Management #Teams
---
For years I said nothing when I was lost in a design conversation and I would nod along and catch up later, and later was about four days. Four days of private catching up. The cost was invisible to everybody but me.

Now I say it in the room. I say it right away. I'm lost, go back two steps. Nine times out of ten somebody else was lost too and was waiting for permission to say so. The conversation gets shorter and the design gets better.

It felt like admitting something for about a month. Then I noticed the people I respect most do it constantly, and nobody thinks less of them. They get asked more questions and that's the benefit.

I say I'm lost the first time I am now. Same room. A room full of people nodding is a room of private confusion, and the confusion gets built into the thing.

I read about a hospital system that deployed a triage model this year. The outcome that got reported was accuracy. The outcome I'd want to see is what happens to the cases it deprioritises. Nobody writes about those.

The model was trained on about eleven years of decisions made by clinicians who used the same information. Eleven years. That's the useful framing. It learned the pattern in the decisions, which is not the same thing as learning the disease.

What makes me nervous is not the average. It's the tail, where the model is confident and wrong and the clinician defers because the screen says so. That's a new failure mode and it's not in the training data because it couldn't happen before.

The teams doing this well publish what happens to the misses and they keep a human on the loop. One human. That's unglamorous and it's where the harm lives.

I ask what happens to the people the model ranks last. Accuracy is a number about the middle.
