Our error budget policy existed on paper for about a year and got used for the first time in March. Four days. The effect was not what I expected and it was fine.

I wrote the policy to trigger if we burned more than half the month's budget in a week. Then we stop feature work and fix reliability. Nobody had wanted to trigger it because it felt like admitting failure, so the number drifted.

When it triggered, the four days produced three fixes and a list of the five things that had been quietly taking the budget for months. Three fixes. One of those was a retry storm from a client we'd deprecated in 2024.

I'd got one thing wrong and it was thinking of it as a punishment. Not a punishment. It's just a different queue, and the queue it replaces is one where nobody ever gets to the reliability items. One trigger.

I write the policy with a trigger I'd be happy to hit now. If hitting it feels like failure, it won't get hit.

---
A recruiter asked me for a portfolio. I sent a link to working software instead. That changed the conversation.

I've hired and I've been hired, and the thing that never lies is a running thing. You can argue about a resume. You can't argue with a URL that loads.

So my advice to anybody job hunting in this market is to build one small thing and keep it live. One page, one form, one purpose. It doesn't need users. It needs to work when somebody clicks it.

I've watched candidates with better credentials lose to candidates with worse credentials and a demo. Every time, the same reason. The demo answered a question nobody had to ask.

Credentials describe what you did for somebody else. A shipped thing describes what you can do now. Those are different products and one of them is in stock. I know which one I'd bet on.

I designed an interview loop last year and the first version was so long that two candidates withdrew before the last round. Good ones. I lost them to my own process.

It was four stages and a take home, and I'd justified each one separately. What I never did was add up the time from the candidate's side. About nine hours of their week for a maybe.

Version two was two stages and a paid exercise over ninety minutes. Stage one was a conversation about a problem we'd actually had, with the real constraints on the table. Stage two was pairing on something small.

Signal didn't get worse and it got faster, and I could describe the whole thing in one paragraph before anybody applied. That paragraph did more for our offer acceptance than any interview question I've written.

A candidate's time is part of your process. Price it, and then cut it.

I got given a task last month with about six constraints and one of them was wrong, and nobody could tell me which one because the ticket was four years old. Four years. It had been copied forward twice.

That's the thing about a long lived ticket. Every time somebody moves it, they copy the description and add a comment, and after two rounds the original reasoning is gone and you have constraints with no owners.

What I did was send it back with a question. Which of these six still matter. Three were dropped in about ten minutes of conversation. Ten minutes. Two of the remaining ones turned out to be the same constraint written twice. Two constraints.

I now ask for that before I start anything older than a year. It takes about fifteen minutes and it has twice changed what the work actually was, once by about a week.

I ask which constraints still hold now. A ticket is a fossil and the fossil doesn't know it's out of date.

---
I replayed a fourteen year old game last month and it taught me more about system design than a book did. Not the story. I mean the menus.

One number does it. It lets you carry about thirty items. The world holds several hundred. That forces every other decision in the game. Loot has to be interesting enough to displace something you already carry, which means the designers couldn't pad the world with junk. Most projects I've worked on never picked that number.

Your equivalent decision is what the product refuses to do, and it gets left open because leaving it open is comfortable. Then a year later everything is a priority and nothing is. Picking a hard limit early makes the argument happen at the start, when the argument is cheap, instead of every week for two years.

The inventory number is invisible in a review. It shows up in what the game refuses to let you keep. I have one rule now. Before I add anything, I ask what the limit is. No limit. No design.

The grid is the bottleneck and almost nobody wants to talk about it. Everyone wants to talk about the model, the chip or the fusion reactor.

I get it. A transmission line is a bad video. It takes a decade, it crosses somebody's land, and the fight is with a county board instead of a competitor. That's still the constraint. If you can't move the electricity, the generation doesn't matter, and if the interconnect queue is five years long, the schedule of every data center in the country is fiction.

I've watched this pattern in software. The hard part is never the feature. It's the thing underneath that nobody owned, that everyone assumed, and that turned out to have a queue.

So when I read that planned data centers are a tenth of a grid region's forecast demand, I don't read it as an AI story. I read it as a queue that's about to become the most expensive thing in the industry. The fix is unglamorous and slow. Permitting reform, storage on the distribution side, and a willingness to site things where the power already is. Nobody gets applause for the queue, and the queue decides everything.

I mentored somebody who became a manager this year and the most useful thing I told him was to stop writing code in the first month. He didn't take it and then he did, and the month he stopped is the month his team's output went up.

His instinct was to take the hardest ticket each sprint, which is what made him a good engineer and is the reason he got the job. The problem is that his calendar didn't shrink when his job changed, so the ticket got done in fragments and the reviews stopped.

What he did instead was take the two tickets nobody else wanted and do them on a Friday, and spend the rest of the week unblocking. That's the trade. About a day of his own work against four days of other people's. About a day. Four days.

I see it most often in a new manager who keeps one project as a comfort blanket and lets the team wait. I told him that and he argued for a month. Pick the smallest possible thing to keep your hands on. One thing.

Keep one small thing. Let the other nine go and watch what your team does with the space.

---
We had one test suite that took forty minutes and so nobody ran it, and so it failed on main about twice a week. Forty minutes. The suite wasn't slow because it was thorough. It was slow because it started a container for every case.

I split it in two. Two suites. About two hundred tests that run in ninety seconds and cover the paths that break. Ninety seconds. The rest runs in the pipeline. Local runs went from rare to automatic.

The interesting part was that the fast set found a bug in the first hour. Not because the tests were new, but because people were finally running them. A test nobody runs is documentation with a false sense of safety.

Make the fast path fast. If your suite takes longer than the time between commits, it isn't part of the loop, and the loop is where the bugs get caught.

My internet went out for about six hours on a workday and the thing I noticed was how much of my job depends on a connection to a server I don't own. Almost all of it.

I had a laptop with a local model, a git repo with everything, and about four hours of work I could do offline. Four hours. Almost all of it. What I couldn't do was anything that involved a teammate, which on a Wednesday afternoon is most of the useful work.

What surprised me was how bad I was at switching. One hour. I spent it checking whether it was back, which is exactly the behavior I'd criticise in a system. Retrying without a backoff.

The one thing I got out of it was a half day of heads down work with no interruptions, and it was the most productive block of my week. That's a slightly embarrassing finding.

I plan for the day I can't reach anything now. Put four hours of solo work in a place you can open without a network. No network.

I watched a metric for a week before changing anything and it turned out the metric was wrong in a way that made the change unnecessary. We were going to rewrite a slow endpoint. The dashboard said two seconds at the ninetieth percentile.

I put a timer around each stage for seven days. Database time was about forty milliseconds. It was a client that retried on timeout, and every retry counted as its own request.

It was one setting on the client and about an hour of work instead of a six week rewrite. One setting. An hour. Six weeks. Nothing else. A rewrite would have worked too. It would have made the numbers move without fixing anything, which is the kind of win that shows up in a review and not in a customer's day.

Measure before you rebuild. A week of watching a number is the cheapest research there is and it's the kind that changes the plan.

I've watched the hiring market for two years and the change in entry level roles is the signal I trust most, because it's the hardest to fake.

A layoff gets announced. A graduation rate gets reported. A junior position that never opened shows up nowhere. That third one is where the damage accumulates and it's the one that can't be measured.

I've advised a few people through this. The advice changed this year. Apply for the jobs that exist and build the skill that gets used in the job you want, because the gap between the two is bigger than it used to be.

That's not a cheerful answer. I know. But I've watched people burn a year applying to closed doors, and the people who did something visible in that year ended up fine.

The market decides the doors. You decide what's on the other side when one opens.

A customer escalation landed on my desk last month. I read the whole thread before replying. That took forty minutes and saved a week.

It was eleven messages long and the technical problem was in the fourth. Everything else was frustration and both of them had said something useful that got buried. One mentioned a workaround nobody had tried. Somebody else mentioned when the problem started.

If I'd replied to the last message, which is what reading the bottom does, I'd have answered the feeling and missed both facts. I answered the fourth message instead, quoted the two lines, and it was resolved in three replies.

That's most of what escalation handling is. The last message is a summary of a mood. Evidence is in the middle, written by somebody who was being careful before they got tired.

I reply to the useful message now, not the last one, because the last one was written by somebody who has already stopped being patient with you.

---
We had a queue with no limit on it and we learned why that matters on a night when three things went wrong at once. Producers kept adding and nothing pushed back. Consumers fell behind and never caught up.

It grew to about four million rows before anybody saw it. Four million. Disk wasn't the problem. Latency was. Every job was taking eleven seconds and the dashboard averaged ten minutes, so the graph looked flat and fine.

I added a cap and a metric the next morning. When the queue passes ten thousand, the producer gets slowed down and an alert fires. Two hundred thousand is the ceiling and it's a page.

Backpressure is not a nice to have. It's the difference between a slow night and a lost night. A queue with no limit is not a buffer. It's a place where small problems wait to become bigger ones. Three things went wrong that night and the queue turned all three into one.

A game I play published patch notes last month that explained a change I didn't like, and the explanation made me fine with it. I want to steal the format.

They didn't say the weapon was overpowered. They said the weapon was used in ninety percent of matches at the top rank and they wanted that number lower. Then they said what they would watch to decide whether the change was right.

That's a design argument with a number and a success criterion attached. Most of the patch notes I read say the change makes the game feel better, which isn't something I can disagree with in any useful way.

I try to write the same kind of note for a change I ship that will annoy somebody. Three lines. Here's what I changed. Here is the number that made me do it. Here is what I'll look at in two weeks.

I name the number before I announce anything now. If I can't name it, I'm guessing. Guessing is allowed. Pretending it isn't a guess is what makes people angry.

Data centers are forecast to take about a tenth of new grid demand in the West and most of the argument about it is aimed at the wrong target. The wrong target is the chips.

The chips aren't the interesting constraint. Power delivery is. A site can have the land, the permits and the servers and still wait two or three years for a substation, and transformers have a lead time measured in years because the firms that make them are few and their order books are full.

That's the part nobody puts in the headline. It's slow and it's boring. It's also the actual bottleneck. It's also the kind of constraint that rewards people willing to plan five years out instead of a quarter. I'm not against the data centers and I'm not against the grid work.

I'm against reading a chart about energy demand and concluding anything about who's winning. The unsexy layer decides the timeline. It always does. Transformers and the permits for them.

I spent about three hours last month on a bug that a colleague found in about two minutes by asking one question. Two minutes. Did this ever work.

I'd been reading the code and building a theory about what was wrong with it. He asked the question and I said it worked in January, and that answer narrowed it to about four commits, and the fix was in the third one.

What I'd skipped was the cheapest piece of information available. Not the state, not the logs. When it last worked, which tells you where to look and costs one conversation.

I use it as the first question now, before I open anything. When did this last work and what changed since. Four commits. It sounds obvious and I'd spent three hours not asking it.

I ask when it last worked now. It's one sentence and it's usually the whole search. Try it first.

I found a spreadsheet that had been running a part of our business for about four years after the system that replaced it went live. Four years. I found it because a number in a meeting didn't match a number in a database. The spreadsheet won.

Somebody had kept it going because the new tool couldn't do one calculation in the way the team trusted. So every month they exported, pasted, and adjusted. Two hours. Nobody was being lazy. The tool just didn't speak their language and the sheet did.

We moved that calculation into the tool and the spreadsheet died in a week. The loss that mattered was the judgment. It had been real and undocumented, and it lived in a column of manual overrides nobody had read.

A spreadsheet that survives a migration means the migration missed something. I find the manual column and ask why now, because the answer is a requirement nobody wrote down.

I rebuilt the same tool three times this year. Each version did less.

The plan was a dashboard that tracked every project I had open, and I spent six weeks on charts nobody asked for before I admitted the thing was unusable on my own phone. So I cut it down.

What's left is a text file and a script. It runs in Ollama on a laptop with no internet, takes about four seconds, and tells me which of my five projects I have ignored the longest. That's it. Two hundred lines.

I use it every morning. The lesson wasn't about the code. I kept designing for a version of me that doesn't exist, one with a clean backlog and time to read charts.

Real me has three CLI tools half finished. Real me needs one number before coffee. Nine months to learn that. Worth it. Boring software that runs beats clever software that doesn't. Built with Gemini CLI, debugged by hand at two in the morning.

My best post last year was about being ignored by a recruiter. Seventy six reactions.

The AI posts I spent hours on got between one and four, and the one I typed in four minutes got seventy six, which tells me I'd been building the wrong thing for months. I sat with that for a while.

I've read the compliance math for a small company selling into Europe and it's brutal. A legal review, a documentation process, a person accountable for the file. That's a real line item before you've sold anything.

I've watched this pattern in every regulation I've lived through. It's written for a problem that large companies have, and the burden lands hardest on the companies that aren't the problem.

That doesn't make the rule wrong. It makes it expensive, and expensive rules consolidate markets. I've watched three sectors go that way and the outcome was fewer companies each single time.

So my position is that the goal and the cost are both real, and pretending either one away is a failure of honesty. The people who write the rules know. The people who comply know. The argument online doesn't.

I'd add an exemption rather than a repeal. Small companies get time, not amnesty.

I read about a spacecraft that launched this year with an instrument sending back about a terabyte a month. One terabyte a month. The interesting problem isn't the downlink. It's that nobody has time to look at all of it.

Bandwidth is solvable with bigger dishes and more of them. Attention is the bottleneck, and it's why so much of the work now is writing software that flags what's worth a human's afternoon.

That's a real shift and it's the same shift happening in medicine and in security. Instruments aren't the constraint anymore. A queue of unfiltered data is.

I watch how the team decides what matters. If the filter is trained on what people looked at last time, it will find more of what you already know and go blind to the new thing, which is usually the discovery.

I think more data is a solved problem. Two problems. One is solved. Knowing which ten images to look at isn't, and that's the part I'd want to work on.

I killed a recurring meeting and replaced it with a shared document and a deadline and nobody missed the meeting. It was due Thursday and took each person about ten minutes. Fifty minutes for nine people. Ten minutes each.

Three people read the doc. Four didn't, and that was the point. Nobody said a word. They'd spent fifty minutes listening to the other five.

Talk moved to the comments and to two quick calls I set up myself. Same decisions, fewer people, and everybody who needed to know knew by Friday.

I still hold meetings for things with a hard fork in them. If the outcome is information, write it down. If the outcome is a decision, sit in a room.

The doc has one section per person and it takes about four minutes to write. I check it before the call and cancel the call when the section answers my question. That has happened twice. Two meetings gone and nobody noticed either one.

I lost two games I grew up on and they went quietly, which is why almost nobody notices until the title they loved is gone. Both are unplayable today.

They aren't broken. The disks exist and the hardware exists and none of it works together, and the store that sold them has removed them. The licence expired, the servers that checked the licence are off, and the single player part of a single player game won't start.

I'm not making a piracy argument. It's a preservation argument, and the people doing the work are mostly volunteers with a wiki and a lot of patience.

I'd ask studios for something small. Ship the server code when you turn a game off, or ship an offline mode that doesn't phone home. Both cost a few weeks and both are the difference between a game existing and a game being remembered.

I'd make the same argument about a library. Preservation isn't nostalgia, and nobody expects the books to be self destructing when the publisher closes.

Fusion got fifteen billion dollars of private money last year and the milestone everyone quoted was a machine running deuterium and tritium. Both of those things are true and the second one is smaller than it sounds.

Running the fuel is a real step. It means the machine handled tritium, which is radioactive and gets into everything, and it means the half life problem moved off a slide and onto a schedule. That's progress and I won't pretend it isn't.

I won't claim it means electricity at a price. The gap between a machine doing a reaction and a plant selling power includes materials that survive neutron damage for years, a supply chain for a fuel nobody produces at scale, and a turbine that has to be invented more or less from scratch. The science is moving and the engineering has barely started.

I'm not criticising the scientists. It's a description of where the clock is. I want fusion to work. Wanting it doesn't change the lead times. The money is now betting on a decade, and the grid work I trust more is the boring kind.

I cut my resume from two pages to one last year and got more replies in six weeks than the previous six months. Nothing about my experience changed. Two pages became one. The reading got easier.

The old version had a summary, a skills list, and about fourteen bullets per job. The new one has three lines per job and the first line is a number. Team of six. Deploys went from weekly to daily. Support load down about a third.

I kept the parts people asked about in interviews and deleted the parts nobody mentioned. Two interviews in a row asked about the same migration, so that stayed. Nobody ever asked about my certifications.

Length is a proxy for something. When a resume is long, the reader assumes the writer can't choose. Choosing is the skill the job needs, so the document should show it.

I rewrote the whole thing four times. The version that worked had no adjectives in it, which I noticed on the fourth pass, and by then I had cut about six hundred words.

I hired a contractor for the first time and the thing that made it work was a written definition of done on one page. One page. It listed what existed at the end, who owned the code after, and what counts as finished.

The first week I sent about nine messages a day and then I stopped, because the page answered most of them. Nine messages. He shipped in about three weeks and it was better than what I would have written, which I didn't expect. Three weeks.

Ownership was the part that mattered. He handed over a repo with tests and a README, and the definition of done said the repo had to run on my machine without him. He tested that himself before he called it done.

I write the definition of done before the first commit now. Write it down first. Contracts cover the money. Definitions of done cover the thing you live with in month three.

I spent my first month letting the AI write everything. I read none of it.

It worked, which is the problem, because working code hides whether you understood the thing or just described it well enough for something else to guess. I shipped garbage fast.

Then it broke in production and I couldn't find the bug. I opened the file, stared at four hundred lines I had never read, and realised the tool had done its job while I skipped mine. So I changed how I work.

Now I write the first version myself, badly, and let the model clean it up. I read every diff. It's slower on day one. By week two it's faster, because I know what the code does when it fails.

The AI isn't the crutch. Skipping the reading is. I've broken that rule twice since and both times I paid for it at two in the morning. Read the code you ship. Even the parts you didn't write.

---
Every few years a report says machines will take a fixed share of jobs by a round number year. Thirty percent by 2030 was the last one I read.

I've read versions of that headline since I was a kid and the world kept hiring anyway, which doesn't mean the current version is wrong. It means the number isn't evidence.

The mistake is treating the transition as free. Employment can stay flat and a lot of people can still get wrecked in the same decade, which is the part the forecasters skip because it doesn't fit in a chart. I've watched that happen twice.

The people who get displaced are usually older, more specialised and closer to retirement than the average. Retraining sounds clean on paper. In practice it means a person of fifty starts as a junior somewhere, if anyone will take the application.

So I don't argue about the percentage. I ask what happens to the specific person in the specific year. That question has an answer. The percentage doesn't.

I gave somebody advice two years ago and it turned out wrong. I found out because he told me, which is how I know he's worth keeping.

The advice was to take the safer job at a bigger company while the market was bad. He did, the market stayed bad for a year, and then the bigger company cut his whole team. The startup that offered him the other role raised a round and is still going.

When I thought about why I'd said it, the answer was that I was describing my own fear in 2009 and dressing it up as his situation. That's most bad advice. Somebody else's history wearing your name.

So I've changed how I give it. Now I ask two questions first. What does your week look like in each option. What are you protecting. Those two take four minutes and they've stopped me from answering my own question instead of his.

I don't get it right more often. I do notice sooner.

---
I turned down a candidate last month. One hire. I've been second guessing it, and the reason is that the interview didn't measure the thing the job needs. He was slow at one exercise and strong at everything else.

The exercise was a live coding problem with about twenty five minutes. Twenty five minutes. He took about thirty five and got there, and the feedback said he was methodical. That's a compliment in the doc and a rejection in the decision, and I don't think that gap is honest.

The job is about reading an unfamiliar codebase and asking good questions about it. Nothing in the loop tested that. So I made a hire decision on a proxy. A proxy. I can't tell whether it was right.

I changed the second stage. He'd have gotten a repository and an hour and a question, which is closer to the actual work and about as expensive.

Measure the job. If your exercise would be slow for a good person, it's measuring the exercise.

---
I misread a contract clause once and it cost us about eleven thousand dollars over a year. Eleven thousand dollars. The mistake was that I read a percentage and not the period it applied to. The increase was quarterly. Per quarter. I had assumed annual.

That's the whole thing. A number without a period is half a number. Thirty percent sounds big and thirty percent per quarter is a different company.

I read contracts with a pen now and I write the periods next to every number. When I don't understand one, I ask the vendor in writing and keep the answer. Three sentences in an email have settled more arguments than any meeting.

The expensive part of a contract is rarely the price. It's the timing, and timing hides in small words like per and each and then. Thirty percent per quarter is one hundred and twenty percent a year, and that isn't a price increase. It's a new contract.

I spent six hours in a new game last week and I couldn't tell you one thing any character said. Not one line.

The writing wasn't bad, which is the strange part. Every conversation landed inside the lines, and every character had a clear motivation, and I forgot all of them the second the dialogue box closed.

Generative dialogue, deployed at scale. You can hear it in how even the texture is, every conversation the same length and the same temperature, no awkward aside that a writer would have fought to keep. I've written dialogue for a text adventure. Different scale, same problem.

Characters live in the leftovers. The digression, the joke that doesn't serve the plot, the two words that tell you who this person is when nobody is watching. A model generates the average and the average is nobody.

I don't blame the tool. It's fast and cheap and it works. I blame the motive. If the goal is fewer writers, you get fewer writers and a game that reads like it.

Grid scale batteries had a year that nobody outside the industry noticed and the number is worth putting in front of people. Storage additions beat every forecast again.

I care about this because it's the boring half of the energy story. The interesting half is generation, which gets the panels and the ribbon cuttings. The half that decides whether a grid with a lot of solar on it actually works is what happens at six in the evening.

I think of batteries as how you move a sunny afternoon into a dark evening, and they've been getting cheaper faster than almost anything else in the sector. That's why you're seeing them go in next to substations instead of next to power plants.

I'd watch the duration. Two hour batteries solve the evening peak. Four hour batteries solve a bad day. Longer than that and you're into a different technology entirely.

I follow the storage numbers now if I want to know what a grid is going to look like. Generation is the headline and storage is the plot.

A colleague asked me why five times in one conversation and by the fifth I had found a real gap in a design I'd been defending for twenty minutes. He wasn't being difficult and he wasn't trying to win. He was checking whether the thing held, and it didn't.

I answered the first three without thinking. I paused at the fourth. At the fifth I said I don't know, and then we both looked at the diagram for thirty seconds and found the missing piece. Five times.

I don't do five whys as a ritual. I use it when somebody is certain and the certainty is the thing I'm suspicious of, including my own. Certainty has a floor about four questions down.

Ask why until the answers get honest. That's the moment the real conversation starts, and it's usually about five questions in. I use it on myself about once a month.

We shipped a feature at about eighty percent and finished it over the next three weeks, and I defended that decision in a meeting and I'd defend it again. Eighty percent went out. Twenty percent stayed behind.

The gap was real and we paid for it. It was real. Two of the things we cut were needed by a third of users, and support told us that in week one. In week one. A longer build wouldn't have told us that, because nobody would have been using it.

It worked because we wrote down what we cut and put a date on reviewing it. We shipped eighty percent with a list, not eighty percent with a shrug.

I ship the part people asked for and write down what I skipped. Write the list down and put a date on the review. The dangerous version isn't a partial feature. It's a partial feature nobody remembers is partial.

I counted the AI tools on this laptop last night. Eleven.

Only three of those eleven earn their place, which means I've been installing command line programs the way other people collect browser tabs. That's hoarding.

Cleaning the list took one afternoon. I kept Gemini CLI and Ollama, deleted the rest, then did a second pass and cut two more. Nothing broke.

The reason I collect them stopped being curiosity a while ago. It's the small hope that the next one covers the gap the last four missed, and that hope costs more attention than any of the tools save me. So I wrote a rule on a sticky note. A tool has to replace something I already do by hand. That one sentence killed six installs.

Nobody needs eleven of anything. I needed eleven tries to find three that fit. Half of my old list existed because I read a thread at midnight and felt behind. Feeling behind is free. Installing takes an hour and teaches nothing. I'd do the three again.

A study went around last month about AI spotting lung disease early. I read the methods before the abstract.

Thirty three patients. That's the whole cohort, and the headline finding was a 0.3 percent change in ground glass opacity, which is about the difference between breathing in and breathing out. You can't build a diagnostic on that.

On a sample that small, any model will find something. Give a machine learning pipeline thirty three rows and a dozen variables and it will produce a p-value with total confidence. That's not a signal. That's a fitted curve.

I'm not against AI in medicine. I'm against a title that implies a test is coming when the data can't carry it. The paper was honest. Somebody else wrote the headline.

So my rule is boring and it works. Find the N. Say it out loud. Then read the claim again. Most of them shrink when you do.

I spent a week inside a competitor's product with a notebook and it taught me more than any market report. Nobody likes to hear that you should study the neighbour.

What I wrote down wasn't features. It was friction. Where I got lost, where a button meant something different from what I expected, where I had to open the docs twice for the same task.

About a third of those notes turned out to be things my own product does, which was the useful and unpleasant part. Twelve pages. One week. I'd been assuming their problems were theirs.

The other thing I noticed is how little of their advantage was technical. Two of their best ideas were naming and defaults, both of which are free and both of which take a decision rather than a sprint.

I keep a copy of the notes in the repo. Every quarter I read it and cross one item off, and it has been a better roadmap than anything a planning session produced.

---
I asked a vendor for a security questionnaire back and the answer to one question was more interesting than the rest of the document. One question. They said they'd never had a breach and their evidence was that nobody had told them.

That's not evidence and it's a common answer. No detection. What it means is that they don't have detection, so a breach would be silent, and the only reason they're confident is that they haven't looked.

What I asked next was two questions. How would you find out, and when did you last test that. The second one is the useful one, because a tabletop exercise with a date attached tells you more than any policy.

We ended up signing a contract with an incident notification window, which is a far better answer than a promise. If you can't detect it, at least you can agree to tell me when you do.

I ask how they'd find out now. A claim about never having had a problem is often a claim about not looking.

Fully autonomous drones are already on the battlefield, and the argument about whether to allow them is still in a committee. That's the gap.

I keep watching this framed as a future problem. It isn't. It's a procurement problem. The systems exist, they're cheap, and cheap means they get used, because the alternative is sending a person. They cost a few thousand dollars each.

That's the actual driver and nobody says it out loud. A drone costs less than a soldier and it doesn't come home in a box, so the moral pressure runs toward using it. Not because anyone likes the idea. Because the alternative got more expensive.

So the line I want is about who decides. Not whether a drone can identify a target. Whether a human signed off, and whether that signature sits in a file somebody can read. I don't think a treaty gets us there. I think a court gets us there, on the day it asks for the file and there isn't one.

I added handover notes to our on call rotation and they cost about five minutes at the end of a shift. Four lines. Thirty minutes saved. I write four lines and they take five minutes. What's broken. What's fine but fragile. What I did. What I didn't get to.

Before, every shift started with twenty minutes of reading alerts and guessing what the last person knew. After, I opened one file and knew where the bodies were.

The fragile line earns its keep. An alert one step from being a problem is invisible in a dashboard and obvious to whoever watched it all night. The three lines about what's fine but fragile are the ones I read first on the next shift.

A handover is a conversation that happens to be written down. I write four lines and the next person saves thirty minutes of archaeology. They get the same deal from me. The next shift starts with a map instead of a blank page.

There are two ways to use AI in a game studio and only one of them ends with a game I want to play. One buys you time. The other buys you headcount.

The first kind shows up in things that were impossible last year: bigger worlds, more variation, systems that react to you without a writer scripting every branch. The second kind shows up in the credits.

I've watched studios announce both in the same quarter. Studios keep the tool and cut the team, and then they wonder why the reviews mention that the world feels empty. It does. Because the world was built by a tool that learned from everything and a team that learned from each other, and only one of those was allowed to stay.

My prediction is boring and falsifiable. Studios that use these tools to build what nobody could build before will win. Studios that use them to skip paying writers and artists will ship forgettable games that look expensive.

I'd bet on the first group. I already pay for the games either way. I just remember the first kind longer.

I bought a machine for a work project last year and the decision that mattered was not the specs. It was whether I could get parts in three years.

The fast one was about forty percent cheaper and had better numbers on every benchmark I ran. It also came from a company that changes its platform every eighteen months, which meant the storage adapter and the power supply would be unreplaceable when they failed, and they always fail.

I bought the boring one. Slower, louder, and about a third larger, and I've replaced two fans and a drive without a support contract. It has cost less than the fast one would have, once you count the downtime that didn't happen.

That isn't a lesson about boring winning. It's that the specification ends at the moment of purchase and the ownership starts then. Nobody benchmarks year three.

For anything you'll own for years, price the parts and the lead time. One week against three years.

A candidate asked me in an interview what my team was bad at. I gave a careful answer and he asked it again. So I told him the truth. We ship fast and document badly. He asked twice. He nodded and took a note. Week two.

He took the job. About four months in he started writing the docs. He said the answer was why he came. Two other offers paid more and both of them told him everything was fine.

I stopped setting goals for my own learning and started writing down one question a week instead, and the change surprised me. Goals are things I hit or fail and both feelings are the end of it. Questions keep going.

The questions are small. Why does this endpoint need a lock. What does this flag actually control. Who reads this report. I answer maybe two in three and the third one becomes next week's.

About six months in I gave a talk built from those answers, and it was better than anything I could have written from a curriculum. Every part of it came from a thing I had wondered about. One question a week.

Curiosity with a date on it beats a reading list. A question you wrote yourself has a reason behind it and the reason is what makes the answer stick. One question a week is about fifty a year.

I used one model for everything for a month. Bad idea, and I didn't notice until it ended.

The blind spot showed up in my drafts. I'd stopped generating options the model wouldn't also have generated, so everything I wrote started sounding like the tool's default voice, and I called that voice mine.

Nobody warned me about that part. The tool never lied to me. It just answered inside one set of assumptions, and I lived there long enough to forget the walls.

Switching models is annoying. The tone changes, the formatting habits change, and prompt skills don't transfer cleanly between them. I did it anyway. Three models now, picked by task: one for code, one for long documents, one for anything I want argued with.

Two weeks in, the blind spots are easier to see. I catch myself copying one model's phrasing and I rewrite it. That's the whole benefit. Not better output. More chances to notice when I'm wrong.

A paper said the gut microbiome predicts Parkinson's years before symptoms. The correlation is real. The causation is unknown.

That's the sentence the abstract needed and the title skipped. Diet moves the microbiome, and so do medication, sleep, exercise and stress, and the study controlled for none of them.

So the finding is a fingerprint of everything about the person, not one disease. I'd still fund the follow up. The direction is worth chasing.

What I won't do is repeat the headline for ten years. A predictive test that doesn't control for breakfast is not a predictive test yet. Someday it will be.

I've said this before and I'll keep saying it. Scepticism isn't the opposite of hope. It's the thing that stops hope from getting spent on a result that can't hold. I want these papers to be right. Checking them is how I find out. Bad method hides bad results for years.

I review portfolios to hire. The strong portfolios all do the same thing. They show one decision and the reason for it.

The typical portfolio has a screenshot, a stack list, and a sentence about being passionate. It tells me what the person used. It doesn't tell me what they were thinking, and thinking is the thing I'm hiring for.

The portfolios that stand out describe a problem, two options, and what happened. Sometimes the answer is that the slower one won and they shipped the simple version anyway. That's a better signal than a green checklist. What I read for is whether the person noticed something that wasn't in the brief, like a constraint they had to work around or a user behaviour that surprised them or a cost nobody was counting.

Nobody expects a portfolio to be impressive. I expect it to be specific. Impressive is a claim. Specific is evidence. Write one paragraph about a decision you made and what you gave up.

I interviewed somebody last month and spent the second half asking him to teach me something he knew and I didn't. I picked the topic from his resume. He taught me why his company used a queue I'd never heard of.

I learned more about how he thinks in those twenty minutes than in the two exercises before it. He got stuck on one of them, and then he explained a trade off to me with a clarity the exercise never showed.

I don't score the teaching bit. I use it to check the score. Just enough. If somebody is strong on the exercises and can't explain anything to me, I want to know that before the offer, not after.

He got the offer. The exercises were fine and the teaching is why I said yes.

Ask a candidate to teach you. It's the closest thing to a normal conversation you'll get in an interview, and normal conversation is what the job is.

A legal analysis published in July lists four reasons accountability gets hard when a weapon acts on its own. The interesting one is that the law was built for a person who pulls a trigger.

I'm not a lawyer and I don't play one. I can see the gap between two facts. The systems are deployed. The rules assume a human in the loop at the moment of decision.

So when a swarm runs and something goes wrong, the questions are who ordered it, who built it, and who approved the parameters. Three different organisations. Three different sets of records. None of them kept a copy of the same thing. That's the whole problem.

This is the boring version of the killer robot story. Not a machine deciding to start a war. A pile of paperwork nobody can assemble in the right order on the day it matters. I'd rather fund the paperwork than the next headline. Paperwork is cheaper and it's the only part that changes behaviour.

I write the last email first now, before I start a task. It sounds like nonsense. Two minutes.

I put what changed in it, what I tested, and what I didn't. I see the gaps while I still have time to close them. About a third of the time I realize the task is different from what I thought.

It also means the update goes out. The work is done and the message is written. The twenty minutes of staring at a blank box never happen. The box isn't blank.

I write the ending before the middle now. Before the middle. That's the cheapest planning trick I have. It works on Tuesday afternoons when nothing else does.

The other thing it does is stop me from starting the wrong task. If I can't write the update, I don't know what done looks like yet. That's a design problem and not a work problem. I've killed two tasks at that point this year and both times it was the right call.

The chip rules that get rewritten every year are mostly aimed at the leading edge, and the second order effect that nobody writes about is what happens to everything else. Every year. Older nodes are where the volume is.

Cars, appliances, industrial controllers and medical devices all run on processes that are a decade behind and will be for another decade, because the requirements are reliability and cost rather than density. Those fabs don't need new machinery and they do need capacity. Different problem.

So when a country gets cut off from the newest tools, the rational move is to deepen what it can already build. That's slower growth in the metric people watch and faster growth in the number of things that get made.

I'm not arguing about whether the policy is right. I'm saying the outcome people will notice in three years is about washing machines and not about data centers, and almost nobody is modelling that.

Read the export list for what isn't on it. Read the gaps. The omissions are where the volume goes.

I read a job description last week that asked for eleven technologies and described about four years of work, and I applied anyway and got a reply in two days. Two days. It was a wishlist and everybody knew it.

Three came up out of eleven. The person hiring admitted it was four teams' worth of requirements and nobody cut it down. That's how most of those lists happen.

Apply for the job you can do, not the list you can't. Requirements lists are written by committee and committees don't reject you, people do, and people read the top. The requirements list is a wishlist with a salary attached. Three of the eleven never came up and four of them are the same tool.

An alert that fired at three in the morning turned out to be a false positive and I didn't just silence it. Three in the morning. False positive. I read the condition. It averaged five minutes of a metric that updates every thirty seconds, so one blip could trip it if the window lined up.

I changed the window and the threshold together. Pages went from about nine a month to one. Nine to one. That page was real. It was the first real one in seven weeks and people answered it.

The fix took twenty minutes. I spent the real time on the judgment afterward. Tune an alert too hard and it goes quiet, and it takes its coverage with it. Leave it noisy and it takes the whole channel down.

A false positive is a design bug, not a fact about the world. Fix the alert or delete it. Don't teach people to ignore the channel.

I drew a line through my own work last month. Client files stay on the machine.

Public documentation, boilerplate and anything I could paste into a forum go to the big models where the reasoning is better and the speed is worth the money. That split took me a while to work out.

The first version of the rule was too strict. I ran everything locally for two weeks and my output dropped hard, because small models fumble long files and I was too stubborn to admit it. So I measured it. Same five tasks, both setups, one week apart.

The local model won two of them. One was transcription cleanup where privacy mattered and quality didn't. The cloud model won the other three by a wide margin, mostly on files longer than a thousand lines. Now I pick per task.

That's a boring conclusion and it's the right one. The privacy question isn't yes or no. It's which files, in which room, for which job. I answer that before I type. Not after.

Superconductivity at ambient pressure now happens at 151 Kelvin. That's about minus 122 Celsius.

I want to be careful about the number, because the headline read like a breakthrough and it's a record instead. Room temperature is roughly 293 Kelvin.

We're roughly half way there, measured in Kelvin, which sounds great until you remember that the last twenty Kelvin took twenty years. Progress on this runs in steps, not a curve. That part never makes the press release.

The pressure is the interesting part. The old records needed a diamond anvil and a sample the size of a grain of sand, which is a lab trick rather than a material. Doing it at ambient pressure is the difference between a paper and a product.

I'm hopeful. I'm not calling it a revolution. It's a step, and I've seen steps turn into transmission lines before. That took forty years. I'd rather be the person who said forty.

We hired two people last year, one with a degree and one with four years of shipping, and the results were close enough. Close enough that the degree stopped being a thing I screen on. I looked at the review notes to check that.

The degree holder ramped in about three months. Three months. The other one ramped in about six weeks. Six weeks. Six months later the gap was gone and the difference that remained was about what they'd each worked on, not what they'd each studied.

What didn't show up in either review was anything to do with a curriculum. What showed up was whether they'd ever debugged something they couldn't explain, and whether they'd had to explain a decision to somebody who disagreed.

An apprenticeship or a portfolio or a project of your own can produce both of those. A degree can too. Neither guarantees it. That's the finding.

Screen for evidence of being stuck and getting unstuck. Everything else is a proxy that costs somebody a career.

I ran a schema change last month with a rollback plan I wrote before I started, and the plan took twenty minutes to write and it never got used. Twenty minutes. That was the point and it's the best twenty minutes I spent that week.

The change added a column and backfilled it in batches. If the backfill ran long I had two options written down with the exact command for each, plus the threshold that picked one. Two options. One threshold.

It went fine. It always goes fine until it doesn't. The value wasn't the plan. It was that I stopped worrying about it and slept, because the decision was already made and the numbers that would trigger it were written down.

A rollback plan is not pessimism. It's the thing that lets you go faster, because you know what you'll do if it goes wrong. I write one now. I sleep better.

---
Drone jamming is the part of the drone story that decides outcomes and it gets one percent of the coverage.

I've read enough about the current conflicts to see the pattern. The cheap drone works until somebody turns on a jammer. Then it stops. Fast. The drone that matters is the one with a wire, a smarter radio, or enough autonomy to keep going without a signal.

That's the arms race. It moves faster than any treaty process. I've watched countermeasures get fielded in weeks because the feedback loop is short and the cost of being wrong is visible.

So when I write about autonomous weapons I try to write about the countermeasures. The system that decides whether a swarm arrives is the one nobody is debating.

I don't think there's a principled line here. There's a technical race with a legal argument running behind it, and the race is winning.

I keep a file called things I got wrong and it has about forty entries, and rereading it is the closest thing I have to a promotion. Each entry is three lines. What I believed. What happened. What I'd check next time. Forty entries.

The entries I learn most from are the ones where I was right about the fact and wrong about the importance of it. Two incidents where I had the cause on day one and chased something else for a week because the real cause felt too simple.

I read the file before big decisions now. Four minutes. That's the whole file. It has changed my mind twice this year, both times in the direction of doing less.

A mistake you write down gets cheaper. A mistake you remember gets expensive, because memory keeps the story and drops the detail. Forty entries and about four of them still make me wince.

I had a conversation with somebody who works on grid operations and the thing that surprised me was not the technology. It was the queue for the paperwork and the people who process it.

Interconnection for a new generator is measured in years, and the queue is long enough that a project can be viable when it enters and dead when it comes out. Engineering is the easy part.

I asked what the industry needs most. Not panels or batteries. People who can process studies, and those are in short supply because the work is unglamorous and training takes about two years. Two years.

That's the same shape as the transformer problem. Bottlenecks are never the exciting part. It's the small number of trained people doing dull work with a backlog.

I look for the queue now, not the headline. Wherever there's a long wait for a bored specialist, that's where the timeline actually lives.

A teammate told me he was leaving. I asked what would have kept him. Then I listened to the whole answer without defending anything. Thirty minutes. It was the most useful half hour of that quarter.

He said the work was fine and the money was fine. The problem was that he'd stopped being able to tell whether his work mattered. Three projects he did got cancelled. Nobody said why.

I couldn't fix that in the exit conversation. I could fix it for the next three people, and I did, by writing a short note after every cancellation about what we learned and sending it to the people who did the work.

I ask what would have kept them and I let the answer land now. Let the answer land. An exit interview is a review of your management, and it's the only one you get from somebody with nothing to lose.

I wrote a post about a mistake I made and four people told me privately that they'd made the same one. Four messages. Likes were few. That told me more than the reactions did.

That's the case for writing about failure instead of wins. A win gets a like and a mistake gets a message, and messages are where the real exchange happens. Nobody messages you to say they did something right.

It costs you specificity. A vague post about learning a lot gets nothing. A post that says I dropped a table in staging and here's the check I added gets three people telling you their version.

I write the mistake with the detail in it now. Detail is what makes it usable to somebody else, and it's also what makes it readable. Everybody can smell a summary. That's the point. Four messages is a small number and it's the one I remember.

I think changing a character people already love is the most expensive thing a studio can do. They do it anyway.

Cortana changed again this year and the redesign is the same argument as last time. Wikipedia calls her Halo 4 look one of the most dramatic changes in the series.

I watched fans build a replacement model to put the old one back. That's the tell. Nobody writes a long essay about a design they merely dislike. People open a modelling program.

Studios misread it. I've watched this audience accept change constantly and pay for it, which is why the misreading stands out. What it won't accept is a change nobody can justify out loud.

I'd test every redesign with one question. Say the reason in a sentence, without hedging. We changed a character people have loved for twenty years because the new version is more appealing to a focus group. If that sounds insane to say, it's insane to ship.

Dark energy appears to have changed over the life of the universe. That shouldn't be possible at all.

If it changed once it can change again, and the way the universe ends depends on which direction it's heading. That's the part that keeps me up.

I'm not a physicist and I want to be clear about that. What I can do is read the paper and notice how careful the authors are being with the word changing.

They measured it against the biggest surveys we have. The result is a tension, not a proof, and the tension is several sigma deep, which is why it survived review. I still think it might be an instrument problem.

The honest version is that we don't know, and the dishonest version sells books. I'll take the first one. Boring uncertainty beats an exciting answer that gets walked back in five years. That's happened to me before.

I wrote a performance review last month for somebody I'd worked with for four months and the hard part was not the rating. It was finding two specific things to point at.

I had a strong impression of the person, which is useless in a document. So I went through three months of messages and pull requests looking for moments and found four. Four moments. Two good, two not.

That list changed my rating by a level. One level. From the impression I was going to mark him as solid. From the four moments I could see that he was excellent at holding a hard problem for a week and reluctant to say a thing was finished, which is a specific and coachable pattern.

Reviews written from memory are about how you felt. Reviews written from a list of moments are about the person, and they're the only ones anybody can act on.

Collect the moments as they happen. Write them down. Memory is a bad reporter and it's the one most people use.

A former colleague told me last week that he's spent about four months of this year on work that got cancelled. Four months. And he wasn't complaining. He said the four months taught him more than the two years before it.

The projects were killed for good reasons and they were big enough that he got to see the whole thing. Two were about a market that moved and one was about a bet on a technology that didn't land, and in all three cases the argument for stopping was in the original plan.

What I took from the conversation is that the value of a cancelled project depends on how early it got cancelled. His got stopped in month two, month four, and month six, and each one left behind a document the next team used.

I've been thinking about which of my own projects died in a way nobody learned from. Two of them, and both because I never wrote the reason down. Two projects. I write the reason down now.

I cancel early and write the reason now. A stopped project with a document is an asset and the same project without one is a story.

We treat neurological disease by numbing it and calling that medicine. It isn't.

A cure removes the disease and we don't have one for Parkinson's or for tardive dyskinesia. A fix is different and nobody talks about it. A fix stops the disease from ruining your afternoon.

Tremor is a signal. It has a frequency you can measure, and if you can measure it you can push back against it in real time.

That's the wearable I keep describing. A band that reads your tremor and returns a counter vibration, tuned to you, because no two tremor signatures are the same. Not a generic device. A device that learns you.

I give it ten years. The hardware exists, the sensors exist and the models that personalise it exist. What's missing is somebody deciding to build it. I'd build it if I could do the biology. I can't, so I keep writing about it.

I stole and rewrote the interview question I ask about conflict. Tell me about a time somebody disagreed with your technical decision and you were wrong.

I used to ask about handling disagreement, which produces a story about a person who handled it well. My version produces something better. It asks for a loss and it asks the candidate to name it.

Answers I trust describe the other person's argument fairly. Answers I don't are about a stakeholder who didn't understand the constraints. That's a real thing and it's also the answer you give when you've never really lost an argument.

What I listen for is whether the person changed a process afterwards. One person said he now writes down the strongest counterargument before he proposes anything, which is more than most engineers do and it's a habit I took.

I ask about a loss. I ask what changed after. One question. Two minutes. That's the whole thing and it's hard to fake.

---
I counted how long a review sat before somebody opened it. Average was about eleven hours. Eleven hours. The fix wasn't to nag people. It was to make the pull request smaller.

I watched a four hundred line change wait most of a day because nobody had forty minutes. Forty minutes. Nobody had that much to spare. A forty line change got read in twenty minutes and the review was better. The reviewer could hold the whole thing in their head.

I set a soft limit of two hundred lines and a rule that a big change gets split with a note about the order to read it in. Median wait dropped to two hours inside a month. Two hours.

Reviews aren't slow because people are busy. They're slow because the work is too big to start. A big change isn't reviewed slower. It isn't reviewed at all. Shrink the change and the wait takes care of itself.

The most endorsed mod for the Oblivion remaster restores one dropdown. A volunteer did that in a week.

Nobody at my last job got fired by a robot. They just didn't get replaced.

Two people left the team in a year, and the job posts for both roles went up as one post with a smaller budget. It got filled once. By someone junior.

Three months later a third person quit and that role never went up at all. Nobody announced anything. The work got split between the people who stayed.

I believe this version more than the layoff story. Not a wave of layoffs with a date on it, but a slow squeeze where the team shrinks and the output target doesn't. Attrition, a hiring freeze, a role that quietly stops existing. None of it is a headline.

I care because of organising. You can't protest a job posting that was never written. There's no day to point at, so nothing ever happens, and the pattern keeps working. I'd rather have the headline.

I met somebody who had been at the same company for eleven years. I asked how he kept learning. He changed teams on purpose every two years and stayed because the problems were real. Eleven years. I'd dismissed that strategy for a long time.

He'd been through a migration, an acquisition, and a rewrite, and each time he was the person who knew where the old assumptions were written down. That knowledge was worth more than any single system.

The trade is that you don't get the title bumps as fast and recruiters ignore you. He said the second one was a feature. The first one he stopped caring about at year six.

Staying can be a strategy and not a lack of ambition. Every two years. Depth compounds in one place. It doesn't travel well. Both of those things are true at once. Eleven years at one company and he wasn't stuck.

A wearable claimed it catches an infection before you feel sick. Fifty people, two weeks.

Three of them got infected, and the model caught two, and that got written up as 67 percent sensitivity. Sixty seven percent of three.

I can hear how that reads. It sounds like a result and it's arithmetic performed on a coin flip, twice, with the coin deciding the conclusion. One different outcome and the number drops by a third.

The researchers knew all of this. The press release didn't mention the three, because the press release had a number in it and the number was good.

I'm not against the device. A wearable that notices illness early is the kind of thing I want to exist, and I've written about them before with some enthusiasm. Wanting something to be true is the reason to check the N first. Excitement is the worst time to read a methods section. I've learned that the slow way.

Nobody takes a photo of a battery installation. Solar panels get the headlines.

Batteries are the part that decides whether any of the rest of it works, because panels stop producing at sunset and turbines stop when the air settles. That's a physics problem, and no amount of capacity fixes it.

I keep an eye on one line item in grid reports, and it's storage. Not generation. Storage.

The reason it's boring is that it doesn't generate anything. It moves the afternoon into the evening. That's the entire product, and without it, the cheap power shows up at the wrong time and gets curtailed.

I've stopped following solar efficiency records. The records I check now are dollars per kilowatt hour of storage and how many hours a site can hold. Those two numbers decide the transition. Panels were always going to get cheap. Storing the afternoon was the hard part. I check that number now instead of panel efficiency.

I got a bug report with no steps to reproduce and a screenshot of an error. Ten minutes. Instead of asking for more I read the error against our logs and found it. Two other people had asked for steps and the reporter had gone quiet.

The error string had a trace id in it. I searched the id, found the request, and the request body showed what they had done. One search. That's not always possible and it was that day.

Ask for the id. One line is easier to copy than a description, and it turns a back and forth into a lookup. The reporter gets an answer in ten minutes and stops dreading the process. Ten minutes against a week of waiting for a reply.

Meet the report where it is. A vague report is still a report, and the person who filed it is telling you something is wrong even if they can't say what.

More than thirty private fusion companies are running right now. Thirty of them.

They're not chasing one design either. Tokamaks, stellarators and laser inertial confinement are three completely different bets on how to hold a star in a box.

I find that more convincing than any single company's timeline. One approach has to work and none of them knows which, so the field is a portfolio.

The failure mode I watch for is everybody copying the same design because it photographs well. That's ten years of nothing. Different bets improve the odds.

I also notice where the money goes. It goes into magnets, materials and control software, not into the publicity. The boring spending is the signal. Every tremor drug I've read about works by slowing something down in the brain. That's the design.

You trade the shake for drowsiness, or dizziness, or a flat feeling that people describe as losing a bit of themselves. Nobody puts that part in the advert.

I'm not against medication. For a lot of people it's the difference between working and not. What I object to is that the industry stopped looking.

A pill is a blunt instrument because it goes everywhere. A tremor in your left hand doesn't need a molecule in your liver. It's an engineering problem.

Wearables can be targeted and they can learn. I think we gave up on them too early because they don't fit neatly into a prescription model that pays for itself. Nobody can bill for a band. I've made that complaint to people who work in the field. That's a business problem pretending to be a science problem.

I mentored somebody who was more senior than me for six months and the lesson was about what I could actually offer. It was not technical guidance.

He knew more than I did about the domain and about the language. What he didn't have was a view of how the work looked from three levels up, because he'd never sat in those meetings and nobody had explained the shape of the year.

So that's what our hour was for. I'd bring the view from above. Where the budget pressure was coming from, which two things were in conflict at the leadership level, and what the actual measures were. He'd bring the work and we'd argue about priorities with more information than either of us had alone.

That's a useful shape for mentoring and nobody calls it that. It's not teaching. It's handing somebody the map of the building you happen to be standing in.

You don't have to know more than somebody to be useful to them. You have to know something they can't see from where they sit.

The people who understand distribution tend to sell a course about it. I get why.

It's the easiest money in the world, and the advice is cheap to produce because the person giving it never has to prove it on someone else's account again.

The part that bothers me is the wrapper. Everything useful in a growth course fits on one page, and the page is different for every account, which is why the course can't work. So the price is for the confidence, not the information.

I've watched people pay four figures for a template that describes someone else's audience. Then they post it and it flops, and they blame themselves instead of the template. I did that too, twice.

What moved the needle for me was boring and free. Post one thing, wait three days, look at the retention graph, change one variable, repeat. That's it. Nine months of that beat everything I ever paid for. Nobody can sell you your own audience. They can only rent you a guess.

JWST pulls in about 235 gigabytes of raw data every day. There are maybe a few thousand professional astronomers on the planet.

I did the math and it's worse than the problem looks. The instrument works. The instrument was never the bottleneck.

People are, and there aren't enough of them, and the backlog grows whether or not anyone looks at it. Nothing gets thrown away. Somewhere in that pile is the result somebody will win a prize for in 2040.

AI is the only thing that makes the arithmetic work. Not because it's smarter than an astronomer. Because it never gets tired of looking at the boring half. I still need someone who knows which half is boring. That's the whole gap.

A recruiter called me on a Thursday about a role I wanted. Then nothing for five weeks.

Then the role got reposted. Same title, same description, same company, three days after my second follow up. That's the part that stuck with me.

Ghosting is annoying. Reposting the same job while a candidate waits tells you exactly how the place handles people, and it isn't a scheduling problem. Somebody made a call about it.

I don't think anyone is evil here. I think the process has no owner, so nobody feels responsible for the person on the other end. That's a leadership problem, not a personality one. It costs nothing to send a two line email. Companies spend thousands on employer branding and skip the free part.

I posted three times a day for four months and gained nothing. Flat numbers the whole time.

The advice was consistent and it was all the same advice: post more, chase trending audio, reply to everyone in the comments. I did all three. My reach didn't move.

The breaking point was a Tuesday when I watched a video about consistency for the fortieth time and realised none of the people making that content had numbers I could check. So I stopped listening.

That's how I found out the hook matters more than the frequency. Everything I know about this came from testing my own assumptions. I'm still doing it, badly, in public. The playbook never told me anything a test couldn't. Four months of posting taught me less than that one week did.

Tardive dyskinesia is what happens when the medication that helps you starts moving your face for you. It's a side effect with no good answer.

People live with it for years because the alternative is worse. The fix people get told about is another drug.

I want a band on the finger that senses the movement before it lands and cancels it. That's not science fiction. Accelerometers cost a few dollars, the models are small enough to run on a watch, and the control loop is a solved problem in robotics. I think what's missing is a product decision.

I keep coming back to the signature part. Every tremor is a signature and the whole device only works if it learns yours. That's why one firmware update can blunt the advantage of a competitor. You can't copy a model that was trained on someone else's hand.

I put this in the next ten years. Not as a cure. As a fix, which is the word I keep using because the difference matters. Cures are what we promise. Fixes are what we could ship.

I spent three hours on a bug that turned out to be one character. A missing comma in a config file.

I read the error message four times before I actually read it. It told me the line. It told me the token. I kept staring at that line and seeing nothing, because I was hunting for the bug I expected instead of the one the compiler had found.

So I did the thing I should've done first. I said the line out loud and stopped assuming the parts I'd already checked were fine. There it was, one comma missing, sitting behind a section I'd decided was innocent.

Three hours. The fix took four seconds. I'm not proud of that ratio, but I'm keeping the lesson. Read what the tool says, not what you think it means. My rule now is simple. Read the message twice before you touch the code, out loud if it fights you.

My estimate for a two week feature came in at nine weeks. The work wasn't harder than I thought. It was just more of it.

Here's what I missed. I estimated the feature and forgot the migration. Then I forgot the backfill, the retry path, and the two places that read the old shape. Each one was small. Together they were the other seven weeks.

The embarrassing part is that I know better. I've been burned this exact way before and I still did it, because the fun part of an estimate is the part I enjoy building.

So now I write the list down before I name a number. Every table, every caller, every screen that has to change. It takes twenty minutes and it's the least glamorous thing I do all week. Twenty minutes buys me weeks of honesty. It's also the only thing that's ever made my dates real.

I built a small tool to save time and used it twice. Then I deleted it and went back to doing the task by hand.

The tool wasn't broken. It worked exactly as designed. But it took longer to describe the job to the tool than to just do the job. I'd automated a ninety second task and spent a week on it.

I've done this more than once. The shape never changes. The task feels repetitive so it feels like automation bait, and I never stop to measure how long the manual version takes.

Now I time the task first. If it's under three minutes and I do it twice a week, it stays manual. That sounds obvious written down. It wasn't obvious at the time, and I had to build a pointless tool to learn it. I keep the old one in a folder as a reminder of that week.

Deleting code feels like going backwards and it's often the fastest way forward. I removed 400 lines last month and the app got faster.

None of it was wrong. It was just unused. Three helper functions nobody called. A config flag that only ever had one value. A legacy path that the last release made impossible to reach.

The hard part wasn't the deletion. It was proving nothing depended on it. I logged every call site for a week before I touched a thing. Two functions showed zero hits across seven days and a million requests.

That's the whole method. I measure, wait, then cut. The result was a smaller surface, fewer places for bugs to hide, and a file I can read in one sitting. Nothing was lost. Less code is a feature, and less code I can hold in my head is a better feature. That's the lesson from 400 lines.

I shipped a release that passed every test and broke the login page. Green build. Dead page. It stayed that way for an hour before anyone told me.

Here's how it happens. My tests covered the function that checks a password. They never touched the code that calls it, because that part looked fine to me. It wasn't. I'd changed the shape of what the function returned and the caller was still expecting the old one.

I don't think the answer is more tests. I think it's different ones. A test that goes in through the real door does more work than twenty that poke the pieces, because it fails for the reason a user would care about.

So every feature now gets one test that walks in the way a person walks in. Not the unit. The path. It's slower to write. It has caught three login bugs since, including one I was about to ship straight to production.

Timezones have broken more of my work than any hard algorithm ever has. I shipped a reminder that fired at the wrong hour for four months.

The bug was one line. I stored a local time and compared it against a server time. That works for most of the year. Then daylight saving shifted and my reminders went out an hour early. Nobody noticed.

I found it because a user in another country asked why their morning note arrived the day before. That one sentence did more work than a month of dashboards.

Now I store everything in coordinated time and convert once, at the edge. One place. Where a human reads it. Nothing clever in any of this. Nothing new either. That's the whole trick. If you handle time, handle it in one place and write the test that crosses a daylight saving boundary. That test has saved me twice since.

I tested my backups last week and two of the three were broken. I found out because I tried to restore one and got nothing back.

Everyone says test your backups. Almost nobody does, because the test is boring and the backup looks fine. It reports success. It writes a file. It has a timestamp. What it doesn't do is tell you the file came out empty.

Two of mine restored to nothing. One had been failing for months because a path moved during a restructure. The job kept reporting green the whole time, because the command it ran exits zero whether or not it copied a byte.

The fix was small. Now the backup checks its own size and shouts if it comes out smaller than last time. That's the whole test. I restore something real once a month and time it. A backup you have not restored is a hope, not a plan.

I put a rate limit on my own API and it saved the product. Before that, one script could take the whole thing down.

I didn't build it for security. I built it because a user liked the feature so much that they looped it. Their loop hit my endpoint ten thousand times an hour and made every other request slow. I spent two days working out who was doing it.

The limit is boring. Sixty calls a minute per key, a clear error, and a header telling you when to try again. That's it. No queue, no dashboard, no clever algorithm.

What surprised me is what it did to my reviews. One loud user had been hiding behind a wall of normal traffic. With the limit in place the slow requests vanished and I could see the shape of real usage for the first time. One limit, one message, and the problem closed itself.
