Declining a feature last month was the most useful thing I did for the product. The person who asked wasn't happy with the answer. The product got better anyway.

The request was reasonable on its own. It just didn't fit the thing we were building, and adding it would have meant a second way to do the same job. Two ways is worse than one missing way, because now every future change has to work for both.

So I asked what they were actually trying to finish that week. Their real problem was two clicks away from the feature they'd asked for. We shipped that instead, in an afternoon, and it solved the thing behind the request.

I don't enjoy saying no. I've gotten it wrong and said no to something we needed. But when you can name the underlying job, the no usually turns into a better yes. That's the trade.

I wrote documentation for myself and it's the one document I re-read. Six pages, written for a version of me who forgot everything.

Here's the trick. When I write for other people I get vague, because I worry about looking basic. When I write for myself next month I get specific. Exact commands. The error I hit. What the code hides.

Page four is a list of traps. Staging keys expire every thirty days and here is where to get a new one. The test suite takes eight minutes and that's normal. Nothing on architecture, because I already know the shape.

Everyone says write for the reader. I think write for the day you come back and can't remember why the strange line exists. That reader shows up every time. Six pages has saved me more hours than any tool I installed this year, and it cost me one evening.

I added a second person to my project and learned something. I was the confusing part. They couldn't get it running for two days. It runs on my machine in one command.

That sentence is the tell. It runs on my machine. My shell had a variable I set a year ago and forgot about. My global config had a package pinned by hand. None of it was in the repo, because I never had to type it twice.

We rebuilt the setup on an empty machine. Every missing piece became a line in a script anyone can run. Half a day. That turned a two day onboarding into about fifteen minutes.

I had thought setup docs were overhead. Wrong. They are the line between a project and a project that one person can hold in their head. I had the second kind for a year and didn't know it at all.

I spent a weekend on a refactor and made the code worse. I reverted it on Sunday and the revert felt better than the work.

My plan was clean. Split one big function into four small ones with real names. What I got was four functions passing the same six arguments around, plus a helper whose only job was to move data between them. My original was ugly and readable. My version was tidy and impossible to follow.

I noticed because I had to fix a bug in my own code and couldn't. The call chain went four deep and every step changed the shape of the data a little.

So I threw it away. That old function is still 140 lines and I can read it top to bottom in one pass. Plenty of refactors make things better. This one made a diagram prettier and the code harder to hold in my head. Reverting counts as a result.

A feature flag saved a release that would otherwise have gone badly. We shipped the code on a Monday and turned it on for one percent of users the next day.

By Tuesday the error rate on that path was nine times the baseline. We turned the flag off and the graph went flat. Nobody noticed except us, and the fix went out on Wednesday with no rollback, no hotfix branch, and no apology post.

I think about that week a lot. The code wasn't ready and we'd have shipped it as ready. The flag didn't make the code better. It just changed the cost of being wrong from an incident to a data point.

The catch is the cleanup. Flags rot, and a codebase with two hundred of them is a maze. So every flag gets a date and a name attached, and the default is off. Merge it, watch it, remove it. Nothing stays.

Reading someone else's code taught me more in two hours than a month of writing my own. I was lost for the first forty minutes. Then the whole thing clicked.

What worked was starting at the entry point and refusing to jump around. I followed one request all the way through, naming every step as I went. It passed through four files, one queue and a cache, and came out the other side. The parts had a reason to exist.

Before that I had been reading files in alphabetical order. That's the worst possible order. Code isn't organised by name for a reader. It's organised by the path a job takes. Alphabetical is for lookup. Nobody looks up a system.

Now I read for motion. I pick a bug and chase it. You learn the shape of a system because the system has to carry something through it. Files make no sense until they are moving.

I worked out what my AI feature costs per user and it changed what I built. The number was small. The number times ten thousand was not.

Here's the mistake I made. I tested with my own prompts, which are long, careful and few. Real traffic is the opposite. Short prompts, sent all day, in bursts. My test said one cent a user a month. My load test said thirty.

So I moved the cheap work off the expensive model. Sorting, tagging and short replies run on a small model now. Hard reasoning still goes to the big one, because that's where the quality shows and that's what people pay for.

This isn't about AI being expensive. It's that I costed it on my own behaviour, which looks nothing like the behaviour of a thousand strangers. Measure the crowd, not yourself. My bill follows the crowd, and the crowd is bigger than me.

I ran a local model for a week on work I send to the cloud. Nothing left my machine, and that mattered more than the speed.

My honest report is mixed. Simple tasks were fine. Summarising, renaming, drafting a reply. Hard reasoning was worse and I felt it as extra checking, which ate the time I thought I was saving.

But my point was never the benchmark. I wanted one category of work to stay on my own disk, the one with names and addresses in it. That isn't a speed question. That's a question about who else gets a copy.

I split my work into two buckets. Anything with personal detail stays local, even when the local answer is worse. Everything else goes where quality is best. Slower, a bit dumber, and I know where it goes. That's the trade I'll take every time. Two buckets, one rule, no guessing.

Fusion has a date problem and it isn't the physics. I watched a company run a deuterium and tritium shot this year and the headline still oversold it.

A shot isn't a plant. A plant isn't a price. That much was real and the engineering that got there deserves the attention it got. What it doesn't mean is power on a grid this decade.

I care about the price. A grid buys electrons, and the moment someone can sell them at a number that competes, everything else follows on its own. Until then, every fusion story is a physics story wearing an economics costume.

So I keep two columns when I read these. One column is what was demonstrated. The other is what a utility would have to pay. One number moves fast and the other moves slowly. The gap between them is the whole conversation, and I've stopped letting the fast one speak for both.

I read a paper wrong for two days before I caught myself. The abstract said improved. The methods said different sample.

I wanted a clean comparison and I didn't have one. Those two groups were never chosen at random. One had more of everything. More time, more staff, more follow up.

I noticed because I wanted to cite it and went looking for the sample size. It wasn't in the abstract. It was in a paragraph four pages in, phrased as a limitation, which is where the real finding hides.

So my habit now is to read that paragraph first. Before the result, before the conclusion. It tells me what the study can't claim, and everything after that is easier to read as it is. Headlines are the last thing I trust. The limitation is the first. Everything in between is a story about a sample, and the sample is almost never you.

I replayed an eleven year old game this month and it ran better than it did at launch. Not because of a patch. Because of a modder who fixed what the studio never returned to fix.

That's the part the industry keeps missing. A game that gets abandoned is not finished, it's frozen. The bugs are still there. They just stop being addressed. Someone in a forum with no payment and no deadline took it on instead.

I'm not saying every studio should support a game forever. I'm saying the tools to let other people do it should be the default, not a favour. Ship the format. Ship the mod tools. Let the thing keep living after the money stops.

The counterargument is control and cost. I get it. But a game that can't be repaired is a game on a timer, and there's a growing list of them that only exist because strangers cared more than the balance sheet did.

Drone swarms are already in the field and the accounting for them isn't written yet. That's the part I keep coming back to. The hardware moved. The rules didn't.

When one person can launch many machines that act together, the old question of who pulled the trigger gets blurry. It's not blurry in a philosophical way. It's blurry in a paperwork way. There's no name on the event.

I'm not against the technology on principle. I've watched enough tools get used badly to know the tool isn't the argument. The argument is that speed has outrun the process that assigns blame, and a system with no blame has no brake.

So what I want isn't a ban. It's a boring answer that already exists for other machines. Who is responsible, which record names them, and what happens when that record is empty. Boring answers are what keep this from becoming a story we can't close.

A customer sent me a complaint that was rude and correct. I fixed the bug in an hour and read the message about ten times.

The bug was embarrassing. My export button worked for small accounts and dropped rows for big ones. Nobody had told me because nobody with a big account had tried it yet. Then someone did, at the worst possible moment, and they let me know in a way I didn't enjoy reading.

I almost replied to the tone. That would have been the mistake. The tone was the wrapping. Inside was a precise bug report with steps, a screenshot, and the exact account size where it broke.

So I read complaints twice before I answer now. Once for the feeling. Once for the fact. The feeling goes away on its own and the fact is the thing I can act on. My best three fixes this year all arrived inside messages I was annoyed by.

A bad name cost me half a day last week. The function was called check and it did four things.

I kept reading it as a validator. It validated, then wrote to the database, then sent a message, then returned a count. The name told me one of those jobs and hid the other three. My brain filled in the rest and got it wrong.

The fix was a rename. ValidateAndEnqueue is uglier and it's honest. Nobody reads that function now and assumes it's safe to call twice, which is exactly what I assumed for a year.

I used to think names were cosmetic. They are the cheapest documentation a codebase gets, and a misleading one is worse than none at all. Half a day for one word. I would rather spend two minutes on the word. I still forget that. I forget it less than I used to, most weeks.

I found an N+1 query in a page that loaded in four seconds. One line in a loop fixed it and the page came back in under 200 milliseconds.

The code looked innocent. For each order, fetch the customer. Ten orders, ten queries. A thousand orders, a thousand queries, and a database that spends its day answering the same question over and over.

I saw it because I turned on query logging for ten minutes. The log was sixty thousand lines long. Reading it was like watching someone walk to the shops a thousand times when once would do.

The fix was to ask for every customer in one go and join them up in memory. Same shapes, same results, one query. That's the whole trick. I log query counts on every page I build now, because a number in a log beats a hunch every single time and it's cheaper than a bad week.

I pinned every dependency in my project and nothing broke for a year. Then I unpinned one and spent a Saturday on the fallout.

The problem wasn't the update. It was that I had no idea what had changed in eleven months of releases. Three major versions arrived at once. Each one carried a deprecation I had not read. That's a lot of surprise in one afternoon. None of it was planned.

So the real cost of pinning is that it hides the bill. You get stability now and a very large payment on a day you did not choose. My bill came due on a Saturday I would rather have spent elsewhere.

I update one package a week now, on purpose, and I read the changelog before I run the tests. It takes fifteen minutes. It has never taken a Saturday. Small updates on a schedule beat one enormous update at the worst possible moment, and I stopped dreading the word upgrade.

I wrote my first runbook in a panic at eleven at night. It was four steps long and it saved me twice in the following month.

The steps are boring. Check the queue depth. Restart the worker. Watch the depth fall. If it doesn't fall, this is the command that shows why. Written down, in order, with the exact strings copied.

What makes it work is that I wrote it while I was scared. That's the only time I remember the details that matter, like the service name I always get wrong and the flag that has to come before the argument.

Nobody else reads it. That was never the point. The point is that the worst version of me, tired and under pressure, gets to follow a list instead of inventing one at two in the morning. A runbook is a note you leave for the person having the worst night. I wrote mine for me.

I told a client I didn't know and it went better than any answer I could have invented. The question was about a number I had never measured.

I had a guess ready. It would have sounded confident. It would also have been wrong, and wrong in a way that would have shown up three weeks later in a spreadsheet that contradicted me.

A code review comment changed how I write code and it wasn't about style. The comment asked one question. What happens when this is called twice, or halfway through?

I didn't have an answer. The function sent an email, and if the job retried, the customer got the same email again. Nothing in my design prevented it, because I had never thought about retries at all.

So I added a key. One field on the record that means this work has already been done. Now calling it twice is safe, and the second call returns the first result instead of doing the work again.

I write that question at the top of my own diffs now. It runs twice, or ten times, or in a bad order, and the answer used to be nothing good. It has caught four bugs before they shipped, which is four bugs I never had to find in production at midnight. One question, four bugs.

I put a cache in front of a slow query and created a bug that lasted six days. The cache worked fine. That was the problem.

The data changed once an hour. My cache held it for a day. So every user saw yesterday's totals with today's date on them, and nothing in my logs complained, because a cache hit is a success by definition.

I found it because a support message said the numbers looked stale. They were stale. My cache was doing what I told it to do, and I had told it the wrong thing.

Now every cache entry has a lifetime I can explain out loud. If I can't say why ten minutes is right, it doesn't get a cache. The metrics track hits and misses too, so a suspiciously high hit rate is a warning now instead of a win. Six days of stale totals taught me that.

A cron job of mine ran twice a minute for a day. I had written the schedule wrong and the system did exactly what I asked.

Nothing crashed. That's what made it bad. It quietly doubled every number in my reports, and I spent a morning convinced my traffic had doubled overnight. Two weeks of growth in one night should have been the first clue.

The job was also not safe to run twice. It appended, so the second run added a second copy. Crontab never complains about a job that succeeds twice.

So I made the job safe to repeat and added a guard that refuses to run if the last run was minutes ago. Then I wrote a test that calls it three times in a row and checks the result is unchanged. That test protects a job I never want to think about again. Three calls. One result. I sleep better.

I removed a feature nobody used and six people complained. That's the most useful thing I learned about deletion all year.

Nobody used it in the data. Twenty two accounts, zero calls in ninety days, if you count by machine. But six humans had a habit built around it, and a habit does not show up in a log until someone takes it away.

The lesson isn't to keep everything. It's to ask before you cut. I emailed the accounts that could possibly care, waited a week, then removed it with a note saying where the replacement lived.

Two of the six came back and told me the workflow they actually had. I built that in an afternoon and forty people used it in the first month. The dead feature was hiding a live need that nobody had ever phrased for me. Worth it. Worth six complaints and a week of email.

My side project taught me more than my day job did this year. The reason is unglamorous. On the side project, I do every part.

At work I write the backend and hand off the rest. On my own thing, I write the backend, the interface, the copy, the pricing page and the support replies. Each one exposes a way of thinking I had been ignoring.

I spent an afternoon chasing a memory leak that turned out to be a list that only grew. Fifty thousand entries, added and never removed.

It was an event log I kept in memory for the dashboard. Every request appended a line. Nobody ever dropped the old ones, because in testing there were twelve. In production there were fifty thousand and the process crept up by ten megabytes an hour.

The fix was four lines. Keep the last thousand, drop the rest. Memory went flat within a minute and stayed flat for the week I watched it. That's the whole change, right there.

What I take from it is that the bug was a policy I never set. Data that accumulates needs a rule for shrinking. If I add something to a list and there's no line that removes it, I haven't finished the code. I've just finished the easy half.

I reverted a deploy in ninety seconds and the revert was the best engineering I did that quarter. The feature was fine. The timing wasn't.

We'd shipped at four in the afternoon on a Friday. By half past four the error rate on the checkout path had tripled. Nothing in the code was wrong. It was a config value that misbehaved under production load.

One sentence decided it. Do we understand this in the next ten minutes, or do we roll back and understand it on Monday. We rolled back. The error rate fell to baseline while I was still talking.

I used to think reverting was an admission. Now I think staying up to prove a point is the admission. A revert is one command and a clear head, and the same feature ships again on Tuesday with the config fixed. It isn't a defeat. That's the whole story.

I was on call for a project of my own for six months and nobody paged me once. That sounds like success. It was luck and one habit.

The habit was a twenty minute pass every Sunday. I read the error log, opened the slowest endpoint, checked the disk, and cleared anything that had been red for more than a day. Every Sunday. Twenty minutes.

Because of that, the mornings after a storm were quiet. Problems got found on a Tuesday afternoon with a coffee, instead of at two in the morning with a phone in my ear.

I'm not pretending this scales. One person can't be on call for a busy system for long. But for the size of project I run, the Sunday pass replaced the pager. I kept a note of every fix, and it ran to four lines. I would take a boring Tuesday over a dramatic Thursday every time, and I have had both.

I wrote a bug report that got fixed in two hours and it had nothing to do with being polite. It had the exact steps and the exact account.

One I sent before it sat for three weeks. It said the export was broken. It didn't say which export, or with which data, or what broken looked like. The developer couldn't reproduce it and moved on to the next thing.

My second one had four lines. Account size, the button, what I expected, what I got, plus a screenshot and the row count. Same developer, two hours, done.

I did the boring part of the work. Finding the smallest case that shows the bug is the expensive half of the fix, and whoever does it shrinks the job for everyone else. I do that half now. It has never been wasted effort, and the whole thing takes ten minutes of my week.

I read a changelog before updating a library and it saved a week. My version was fine. The next one removed the thing my code was built on.

One note, two lines long, tucked under a heading that said breaking. That's all it was. Below it, in the same section, was the replacement and a short example. Two minutes of reading. The whole week stayed intact.

I'd skipped changelogs for years. My logic was that the tests would tell me. They did tell me, eventually, in the middle of a release, on a branch I wasn't expecting to touch that day.

Now I read the breaking section before I run the update and I read the rest after. It isn't diligence. It's that the library already wrote down the answer, and guessing instead costs me a weekend I could have spent anywhere else. The note is free and the weekend is not.

I turned on telemetry for my own app and turned half of it off a week later. The numbers I collected didn't tell me what I needed to know.

I had page views, session length, click counts, device types, and a heat map. None of it answered the question I actually had, which was where people stopped and why. I was drowning in measurement and starving for a reason.

The half I kept was small. Where the form failed. Which errors users saw. How long the slowest request took. Three things, all of them about friction rather than attention or vanity.

The heat map bothered me most. It was watching people without asking. I keep that in mind now. Collect what you will act on, delete the dashboards nobody opens, and ask before you watch people. That's the rule I use now. Fewer numbers, better questions, and a cleaner conscience.

A telescope result this month was reported as a discovery. Read the paper and it's a candidate, with three more years of observations to confirm it.

I don't blame the press office. The result is genuinely interesting and the signal is real in the data they have. What it isn't yet is settled, and the difference between a candidate and a confirmation is the entire point of the method.

The team was careful. Their own words say tentative. The headline dropped that word, because tentative is a hard sell on a Tuesday and the number is easy to sell.

I look for one thing when a science story lands. Does the paper use the word suggest, or does it use detect. Detect means they measured it. It's solid. Suggest means the data is leaning that way. I have never regretted waiting for the second word, and three years is not a long wait for a fact.

A grid operator I follow said the battery build is now faster than the plant build. That one sentence explains more about energy than any policy paper I have read.

Batteries are modular. A gas plant is a project. One arrives in trucks and gets wired up over months. The other needs permits, pipelines, and a decade of patience. That difference in speed is not a political opinion. It is a fact about how the two things are built.

So the argument shifts. It isn't about which generation is cleaner now. It's about which one can be added this year, at this site, without waiting for anything to be approved.

I don't think this makes the transition easy. I think it makes it fast where speed matters and slow where a wire has to cross three states. Speed decides. Our bottleneck moved from generation to connection, and that's a different job for a different decade.

I played a game this month whose dialogue was generated and it was the best argument against doing that. Thousands of lines. Nothing to remember.

Every character spoke in the same voice with the same length of sentence. Nobody interrupted anybody. Nobody was rude, or funny, or wrong in an interesting way. It was fluent. It was empty. Like a meeting where everyone agrees with the person who spoke first.

The studio got what it paid for, which was volume. A writer would have spent a week on one conversation and made me care about a character I met for ninety seconds. That trade is the whole issue, and it isn't about the tool.

I use these models every day and I like them. I also know what they can't do, which is decide that one line matters more than the other forty. That judgement is the job, and it is still a human one.

I deleted my analytics for a month and shipped more software. That was not the plan. It just happened.

Without the dashboard I stopped the morning check. That check was ten minutes of reading numbers that changed nothing about what I built that day. Then it was twenty minutes, then it was a habit with a cup of coffee and a bad mood.

What I did instead was talk to four users. One conversation told me more than ninety days of graphs, because a person can say why, and a graph can only say what.

I'm not against measurement. I still track errors and latency, because those change what I do within the hour. The rest came back when I had a question that needed an answer, which took a month and a half. Measurement should follow a question, not replace one. Most dashboards are answers to questions nobody asked. That's the rule.

I read the whole stack trace this time instead of the first line. The answer was four frames down. Same bug, different approach.

I used to stop at the exception name. Null pointer. No such key. Connection refused. Then I would guess at a fix near the top of the file and run it. That worked often enough to keep the habit alive for years.

This time I scrolled. Frame four had a function name I didn't recognize and a line number in a file I hadn't opened in months. That's the real location.

Ten minutes of reading, one line changed. The fix held on the first try, which almost never happens for me. Reading is slower at the start and faster overall. I keep having to relearn that. I wrote it on a note above my desk. I spent years reading the wrong end of that output. Line one is where the error surfaced, and frame four is where it started. It's the cheapest habit I have ever broken.

I built a clever abstraction and deleted it three days later. It saved four lines and cost me an afternoon of reading.

The idea was a config object that could stand in for three different things. One shape to rule all the callers. In practice every caller wanted something a little different, so I added flags. Eleven flags. The object knew which mode it was in, sometimes.

It worked. That's the trap. Clever code that works is much harder to remove than broken code, because nobody can point at the failure. You just lose an hour every time you touch it.

So I replaced it with three plain functions with long boring names. The file got longer and my afternoons got shorter. Boring code is a gift to the person reading it next month, and that person is often me. I wrote that rule on a card and taped it to the monitor. I break it about once a month and I pay for it every time.

I wrote the test before the fix for once and it changed the whole afternoon. The test failed, and that was the point.

I had a report about a rounding problem. Before touching the code I wrote one line that called the function with the numbers from the report and checked the answer. It failed. Good. Now I had a bug I could see instead of one I had to reason about.

The fix took eleven minutes. The test took four. Every other attempt that afternoon would have been guesswork with a reload in between, and guesswork is how I used to spend three hours.

I don't do this every time. When I skip it, I can feel the difference. Without the failing test I'm not fixing a bug. I'm editing code and hoping, and hope is a poor debugging tool. That's the cheapest hour I ever bought. I take it every time now, and I sleep better for it.

I worked out what my storage costs, all in, and it was less than one coffee a month. I had been designing around a problem I didn't have.

I assumed the database was the expensive part. So I built compression, a cleanup job, and a retention policy. Two weekends of work, and the reasoning felt responsible the whole time.

Then I measured. Four hundred thousand rows, about eighty megabytes, and a bill smaller than the sandwich I had for lunch. I checked the bill on a Sunday. Two dollars a month. That's what I was protecting. My careful architecture was guarding a rounding error.

The lesson isn't that storage is free. It's that I never checked. I was wrong. Measuring takes twenty minutes and it would have sent me straight to the part that costs real money, which was the outbound bandwidth and the model calls. I measure first now and the design keeps shrinking. I would rather know the number than guess at it.

I rewrote my 404 page and support tickets dropped by a third. Not the traffic. Support messages.

My old page said not found and offered a link home. My new one asks one question. Were you looking for this one. Then it shows the three closest pages, the search box, and a way to tell me what you wanted.

Complaints about broken links dropped almost at once, because most of them were never broken links. People had typed a name wrong or followed a bookmark from an old version of the site.

That's an hour of work and a third of my support load. Errors are the one place where a user has already told you they are stuck, and most of us answer with a shrug. I rebuilt mine in an hour. That's the cheapest support tool I own. I keep finding places where a small answer beats a big system.

I cut my alerts down to two a day and my reply time got better. The urgent ones were being buried by the rest.

I had email, chat, two task tools and a build system all competing for the corner of my screen. Every ping pulled me out of whatever I was holding in my head, and getting back took minutes each time.

Now the build posts to a channel I read twice a day. Chat is muted except for direct messages. Email gets two passes, morning and late afternoon. That's it.

My average reply time went from nine hours to two, because replies got batched and I stopped answering in the middle of other work. Nothing important waited longer. Most of what interrupted me was never going to be answered that hour anyway. Fewer pings, faster answers. I answer better now and I work longer. Nobody has complained about the quiet yet.

I added one boolean parameter to a function and six months later nobody could read the call sites. True meant one thing and false meant another.

The problem wasn't the flag itself. It was the reading. A call that says save true tells you nothing, and you have to open the function to learn what true buys you. Do that four times in a review and you stop reviewing.

So I split it into two functions that say what they do. SaveDraft and PublishNow. Same code inside. Two names.

The rule I use now is simple. If a flag is only true or false in a way a reader can't guess, it wants to be two functions. If it's genuinely both, pass an enum with names. Either way, the reader gets a sentence instead of a puzzle. I've made this change four times now. I'm done being clever at call sites, and I sleep better for it.

I handled a partial failure for the first time and it taught me more than any architecture diagram. Three of five writes landed. The other two timed out.

My first version threw an exception and rolled everything back. That sounds safe and it was wrong. The three that succeeded had already sent emails. Rolling back the database didn't unsend them.

So I stopped thinking about transactions and started thinking about steps. Each step records that it happened. Rerunning any step twice is harmless. A failure stops the run and leaves a mark that says exactly where it stopped.

That's the whole design. It's uglier than a database transaction and it survives the real world, where one provider is always slower than the other two and nobody answers the phone. I sleep better with the ugly version running. I know exactly where it will stop when something breaks. I'll take ugly and honest.

I copied forty lines out of a small library instead of adding another dependency. I made that call three years ago and I haven't regretted it.

The library did what I needed and a lot I didn't. It pulled in four more packages, had a build step, and shipped a breaking change every few months. For one function, that was a bad trade.

So I read the function, wrote my own version, and put a comment at the top that says where it came from and why. Forty lines I understand beat a tree of code I don't, especially when the requirement hasn't moved in years.

I'm not against dependencies. I lean on twenty of them. But for small, stable, well understood logic, the copy is often the cheaper long term choice, and the comment keeps it honest. It's a copy I can read. That's the whole argument. I'll take boring and known over clever and surprising. I keep the note in the file.

A hard deadline improved my design and I hate admitting that. I had two days and I built the simple version.

My simple version stored the report as plain text and generated it once an hour. No queue, no incremental updates, no expensive cache. It took six hours and it shipped.

My first plan would have handled ten thousand updates a second. Traffic was nine an hour. I had been designing for a problem that belonged to a company ten thousand times my size.

That deadline cut it out. Since then I ask what the smallest thing is that could work. Then I build that. It works. Sometimes the honest answer is that the simple version is enough forever. I forget that lesson every quarter. I write it down now. Six hours beats six weeks and it isn't close. That's the rule, and I need it on the wall where I can see it.

A pull request of mine got rejected for a reason I still use today. A reviewer told me the change was fine and the timing was wrong.

I had bundled a rename, a bug fix, and a new option into one branch. Each was small. Together they were impossible to review, and if one of them caused a problem there was no way to keep the other two.

So I split it into three pull requests. My rename merged by lunch. My bug fix needed a discussion. My option never shipped, because the discussion killed it, and that was the right call.

Since then one idea per branch. It feels slower and it isn't. That's the trade I keep misreading. Reverts get surgical. Reviews get honest. The half of my ideas that deserve to die get to die on their own without dragging good work with them. I lost a week learning this. I use it every day now.

I cut my page load from four seconds to four hundred milliseconds and the biggest win was the boring one. I deleted two fonts and a hero image.

The optimization work I had planned was a cache layer, a content delivery network, and lazy loading. Instead I opened the network tab and looked at what was heavy. Two font files at three hundred kilobytes each. A hero image that was four megabytes and displayed at six hundred pixels wide.

Ten minutes. Resize the image, drop one font, serve the other in a modern format. The page went from four seconds to under one on my connection.

The cache work is still on the list. It might take it to eight hundred milliseconds. I spent a week planning that and ten minutes on the fix that did most of the job. Measurement first, always. It's the least clever work I do. Ten minutes, one image. It shows up in the numbers every time.

A regular expression of mine matched everything and I found out in production. It looked correct in every test I wrote.

The pattern was supposed to match a date. It did. It also matched a version number, a phone number, and the word 2019 on its own. My tests fed it dates and nothing else, so it passed every one of them with a green check.

The fix was to anchor it and test the negative cases. Not just what it should match. What it must not match. Twenty lines of tests that exist to prove the pattern keeps its hands to itself.

I write negative tests for input parsing now, every time. The failing cases are where the bugs live. A test that confirms the happy path is a test that agrees with me, and agreement isn't evidence. I write the negative cases first now. It's the part of testing I trust. That's the whole habit. I got this wrong for years.

I documented my own API after two weeks away from it and I couldn't use it. That was the wake up call.

I had endpoints with names that made sense to me in March. Query by tag. Fetch by slug. Update metadata. My doc page listed all of them and none of them told me which one to call first, or what a response looked like when a field was missing.

So I rewrote the page as three worked examples. Here is the first call you make. Here is what comes back. Here is the call you make next, using that id. Everything else is a footnote.

Nobody reads reference tables. People read a story about a request that works. My API hasn't changed at all and it's usable now, because the docs answer the question a stranger has. That's the tell. If the docs need a table of contents, I wrote them for me. I read the page the way a stranger would.

I left my build slow on purpose for a week to see what it did to me. Six minutes. I learned something I didn't expect.

I thought the cost would be waiting. It wasn't. The cost was the tab I opened during the wait. Every six minutes I had a small gap, and every gap got filled with something that then took ten minutes to unwind.

When I made the build fast, the waiting went away and so did the tab. I started finishing thoughts instead of parking them. Two hours of work in the morning turned into two hours of actual work.

I don't think every slow tool is a moral failing. I do think I should count the switches, not the seconds. Six minutes of waiting cost me far more than six minutes. I count the switches now. I stopped timing the seconds. The tab is the expensive part and it never shows up in a profile.

A physics measurement got sharper this year and the headline said solved. Their paper said the error bar had shrunk.

That's a real result. Narrowing an error bar is how these fields move forward, one careful decade at a time. It's also a much harder sentence to sell than the word solved.

What the team did was measure the same thing with better instruments and a longer run. Its central value barely moved. Its uncertainty got smaller, which is the part that matters and the part the headline drops.

I read the uncertainty before I read the number now. A result with a wide error bar is a shrug in a lab coat, and a result with a tight one is a fact you can build on. Reporting the center is easy. I've been burned by a headline before. I check the error bar first. Every time. That's the part I trust, and it took me years to learn it.

A grid operator said the queue to connect new power is now longer than the queue to build it. That sentence reframes the whole energy debate for me.

Building got fast and connecting stayed slow. A project can be financed, permitted, and finished while it waits years for a wire to reach it. The bottleneck isn't generation any more. It's the permit and the line and the neighbor who says no.

That changes what a policy argument is about. Arguing over which source is best misses the point if neither can connect. The interesting money is in transmission, storage and anything that lets a site serve itself.

I keep watching the interconnection numbers now. They tell me more about what gets built in three years than any announcement about capacity or ambition. Wires decide. I check that number before I get excited about anything new. It's the part of the story that decides. That's the whole thing.

I stopped using a password manager for a week to see how bad the alternative was. It was worse than I thought. I gave up on day two.

Without it I reused four passwords across eight sites. I typed them into a browser that offered to remember them. I told myself I would fix it properly later, which is the sentence that starts every incident report.

What the week showed me is that the manager wasn't about my memory. It was about making the safe option the fast one. With the manager, a new password is one click and it's long and unique. Without it, the same task costs a minute and a decision, and a minute is exactly the length of the pause where people decide to do the unsafe thing instead.

That's the design lesson I keep meeting in software. Here it is again. Same pattern. People don't pick the safe path because they're careless. They pick it because it's the fastest one. My job is to make the safe path faster. So I fix the path. It's a design problem and it's mine.

I measured the cost per kilogram to orbit and it explained a decade of space news in one number. That number has fallen and it's still large.

A few thousand dollars per kilogram used to be the floor. Now it's closer to a few hundred on the big reusable rockets, and the change is the whole story. Every new idea in the sector is downstream of that curve.

When you move something up the curve, everything that was impossible becomes expensive instead. A small satellite becomes a product. A research instrument becomes a mission. A mission becomes a business.

I watch two things in space news now. One is the announced payload. One is the price per kilo. Payloads get the headline. I reread a file I wrote in June and I couldn't follow my own logic. Ninety lines, four functions, and one of them did two jobs under one name.

I remember writing it. It felt clean at the time. Every decision had a reason, and the reasons were all in my head, which is the only place they ever lived. Nobody else could see them and I didn't write them down.

A survey of two thousand developers said sixty six percent saw layoffs in twelve months. I keep thinking about the people who didn't.

The ones who didn't aren't the best engineers. I have worked with brilliant people who got cut in a round that made no sense, and mediocre people who survived three of them. Skill correlates with survival. It's nowhere near a guarantee.

What actually correlates is being expensive to remove. One person knew the billing system end to end. Another ran the only release process anyone trusted. Neither was the strongest coder on the team and both were untouchable in practice.

So I stopped optimising for being impressive and started optimising for being load bearing. Write the runbook. Own the scary part. Make the team slower when you vanish, and then vanish anyway because a good holiday is how you find out whether it worked. That's the career advice nobody gives.

Obsidian got eighteen months to build Fallout New Vegas. Bethesda spent about four years on Fallout 3, and the documented view is that eighteen months was brutally short for a game that size.

It scored eighty four. Fallout 3 scored ninety three and sold about twelve point four million copies to New Vegas at eleven point six. So the shorter game with the worse score is the one people are still modding in 2026, with a remaster confirmed.

I think about that timeline at work. Half of my bad decisions weren't bad ideas. They were good ideas made under a deadline that didn't leave room to finish them. The plan was fine. The clock was the problem.

I learned that from this game. That's why I now ask about the shape of the deadline before I agree to the shape of the work. A small thing finished well beats a large thing rushed, and the reviews don't care how much you meant well.

The live service model killed a studio after a reported four hundred million dollar build that peaked at six hundred and ninety seven players on Steam. Sony wrote the shutdown post themselves and the reporting pointed at the model, not the genre.

That number is the part I can't stop thinking about. Six hundred and ninety seven. You need hundreds of thousands of daily players for that kind of budget to work. The game was designed for a crowd that never came.

Every team I have been on has a version of this. A feature built for a scale we didn't have. A queue for ten thousand updates a second when traffic was nine an hour. The plan is always correct for a company ten times larger.

So the question I ask now is boring and it saves months. Who is the user, how many are there, and what happens if there are fifty. If nobody can answer with a number, we're guessing at scale and paying for it. I have made that mistake myself. I know why. Twice. I shipped a queue for a scale we never had. It felt responsible. I caught it late and I paid for it.

The same industry survey tracked how developers feel about generative AI and the number moved from eighteen percent saying it's bad for the industry to fifty two percent in two years. That's a thirty four point swing and it happened while tool use went up.

I feel both halves of that. My output is higher with an agent doing the boring parts, and my confidence in a codebase I didn't write goes down. Both are true. I shipped more this year than any year before and I understand less of what I shipped.

The number that worries me is the review one. If half the code never had a human read it with intent, then the bugs are all in the seams between files. Nothing crashes. It just costs more every month.

So I keep one rule that has survived. Every change gets one person who can explain why, in their own words, without opening the diff. That's the cheapest quality gate I have found and it doesn't scale, which is exactly why it works.

Fallout 3 scored ninety three and sold twelve point four million. New Vegas scored eighty four and sold eleven point six million. By the numbers, Fallout 3 is the bigger game.

New Vegas is the one with mods being released in 2026, a remaster confirmed, and a television season set in its city. People put thousands of hours into the game with the lower score. They didn't do that with the higher one.

I have stopped treating review scores as a proxy for care. A score tells me what a reviewer thought in a week. Hours played tells me what a person chose with their evenings. Those are different measurements and only one of them lasts.

The practical version, the one I can use at work, is that I should measure the thing people come back to. Not the launch number. Retention is unglamorous and it was the honest number for that game over fifteen years. I know which one I would keep. Not the score. The hours. I played it for nine hundred hours.

I deleted a feature last week that took three weeks to build and it already had ten users. Ten. Out of about four thousand, which works out to a quarter of one percent.

I built it because I wanted it. Somebody asked in a reply and I heard an opportunity instead of a person. That's how most features I regret get started, and the polite version of the story is that I responded to user demand.

Deleting it took forty minutes. The tests went with it. Two files and a database column came out, and the deploy got faster because a background job stopped running.

The number I should have checked first is the size of the request. One person asking is an anecdote. Twenty people is a signal. I now write the count next to the request when I put it on the list, so future me can't pretend.

I started writing a changelog by hand in June and it changed how I plan work. One file, dated lines, plain English, no marketing.

The first entry was two lines. Fixed the export crash on empty files. Added a retry to the upload job. Reading it a month later, I could see the pattern at once. Eight of the twelve entries were about uploads failing and being retried.

Nobody on the team read the changelog for a year. They read it the week we planned the next quarter, and it settled an argument in about four minutes because the evidence was already written down.

That's the whole trick. A changelog is a cheap log of what happened, and what happened is the most reliable input to what should happen next. Kept by hand it costs a minute. Rebuilt a quarter later it costs a weekend and it's always wrong. I write it on the day. I read it before planning.

A bug that only happened on Tuesdays was the most educational bug of my year. It turned out to be a weekly cleanup job that deleted a row I depended on, and the two pieces of code had lived side by side for eleven months.

I found it by accident. I was rerunning a test to check something else and it failed, and it had passed an hour before. That's the part that took an hour to believe. A test that passes on Monday and fails on Tuesday with no code change is telling you something about time.

The real defect was structural. Two things owned the same row and neither knew about the other. The Tuesday schedule was just the messenger.

So I drew the nine tables that job touched and wrote down who owned what. Four hours of drawing, one line changed. I have never once regretted spending a day on a diagram, and I have regretted every time I skipped one.

---
I added logging to a flaky test and the failure rate dropped to zero. That isn't a joke and it's not magic. The test was slow and the extra lines changed the timing enough to hide a race.

That's the worst kind of fix, and I wrote it down as a real finding rather than quietly keeping it. A test that passes because it got slower is a test that will fail in production, where the timing isn't kind.

What I should have done is the boring thing. Reproduce it with a sleep, prove the race, then fix the ordering. Twenty extra minutes and one genuine bug removed instead of a passing suite that lies.

I now treat a passing suite with suspicion when the fix was unrelated. If a change makes a failure go away and I can't explain the mechanism, I haven't fixed anything. I have bought time, and time gets billed later with interest. I wrote it down as a finding. I knew better. I still did it. Nobody reads that file. That's on me.

I read a dependency's source code before adding it last week and it took eleven minutes. Four of those minutes changed my mind about using it.

The README said it handled retries. The source showed one retry, no backoff, and a hardcoded two second delay. That isn't a lie in the docs. It's just a smaller thing than the sentence implied, and the sentence is what I would have trusted.

Eleven minutes is nothing next to the alternative. If it had failed in production I would have spent an afternoon reading the same file, except that afternoon would have had a customer in it.

So the rule I use is cheap and I recommend stealing it. Before adding a library for anything that touches money, time or email, open the function that does the thing. If it fits on one screen and you understand it, ship it. If it doesn't, you just learned why the package is large. I read it first now. I check before I add.

I deleted forty percent of my tests and the suite got more useful. The ones I removed checked that a function called another function. That's a test of my own refactoring style, not of behavior.

What was left was smaller and slower to write and it failed for real reasons. Every failure pointed at a problem a user could hit. That's the only kind of test failure worth waking up for.

The other forty percent wasn't harmless. It was maintenance. Every rename touched thirty tests that asserted on a call count, and every one of those edits was a chance to break something for no reason.

I keep a different number in my head now. Coverage of decisions, not lines. Can I name the branches that matter and are they all hit. I can't tell you my line coverage and I can tell you the four places this code can fail, which turns out to be the more useful sentence. I count decisions now.

I got ghosted after a seven round interview loop and it taught me more about hiring than the interview did. Fourteen hours of my time, five take home exercises, and one message at the end that said they were moving on.

The part that stung wasn't the no. It was the silence after the last round, which lasted nineteen days. I sent two polite follow ups and got nothing, and then the rejection arrived with no name on it.

I don't think that's malice. It's a process with no owner. Nobody's job was to tell me, so it fell to nobody, and the cost landed entirely on my side of the table.

The lesson I carry is about my own product. Any step where the answer is silence is a step where a person is stuck. If a system can leave someone waiting nineteen days, that isn't a people problem. It's a missing notification and I can fix that. I keep that number. Nineteen days.

I took a boring job for two years and it made me a better engineer than any exciting one. Insurance claims. Old code, slow releases, and a system that had been running for nineteen years without a rewrite.

The code was ugly. It was also correct, which is a combination I had never seen up close. Every weird line had a comment explaining a rule that existed because of an incident in 2009. The comments were the actual documentation of the business.

Nothing about that job was impressive in a portfolio sense. Nobody asked about it in interviews. What it gave me was pattern recognition for systems that survive, which is a different skill from building new ones.

I now look for the boring parts of every project on purpose. The jobs nobody wants, the subsystem everyone is afraid of, the deploy script with no owner. That's where the real understanding lives, and understanding is the thing that transfers. I choose it every time.

A portfolio of four finished small projects beat a resume of forty technologies for me. I got the last two roles because someone opened a working link, not because of a list of frameworks.

The resume said I knew nine languages. The portfolio showed a small tool that ingests a spreadsheet and prints labels, with the source on my profile and a screenshot of it running. One of those is a claim. The other is a thing.

What made the difference was that it did something complete. Not a tutorial clone. Not a demo that only works locally. It had an error state, a README that explained the one strange decision, and it had been used by four people who weren't me.

I would give that advice to anyone starting out. One finished thing beats ten started things, and the finishing is the part that teaches you the tradeoffs. Anyone can begin. Beginning is free.

I answered every reply on a post for two weeks and it changed what I write. Sixty four replies, most of them short, and every single one was specific about which sentence landed.

The replies that helped weren't the praise. They were the corrections. One person said my number came from a press release and not a paper, and they were right, and that one reply was worth more than the other sixty three combined.

The other thing it fixed was my openings. A comment that says what is this about is a signal that sentence one is decorative. I started cutting first lines and the replies stopped asking that question.

Two weeks isn't a long experiment and it's longer than most people try. The habit I kept is smaller. I read every comment and I answer the ones that ask a real question, and I write down the corrections. That's it. It's not a growth strategy and it works.

I ran a seven billion parameter model on my own laptop for a month and it changed which tasks I send to a cloud model. Local handled seventy percent of the work and cost me nothing per token.

That seventy percent is the boring part of the work. Rewriting a function, explaining an error, turning a paragraph into a commit message, checking a regex against eight test strings. Small tasks with a clear right answer and no need for the newest model.

That last thirty percent is where the money goes. Anything that needs a large context, current information or a difficult judgment call goes to a hosted model. Those are the calls I want to spend on, and before the local setup I was spending on both.

I saved about forty dollars a month from that split. That isn't the point. Latency went to zero for the common case and I stopped self censoring small questions. That's the part that changed my output. It paid for itself. I check the split every month.

My first evaluation was four questions and a score out of five. It took two weeks to discover it was ranking my prompts by length and not by quality.

I wrote the questions myself. Every question rewarded the behavior I already liked, so the winner was always the prompt that sounded most like me. That isn't an evaluation. That's a mirror.

What fixed it was a set of twenty examples with known good answers, written before I generated anything. I scored the output against the answers and stopped asking how it felt. Results changed on the first run and the change was uncomfortable.

I watch one number now. Agreement between two people scoring the same output. When it drops below about seventy percent, the rubric is broken, not the model. Most of my evaluation problems were rubric problems and it took me a year to notice. I check that number before I trust any score.

I remember two comments from eleven years of code review. Both were two lines long. One said this function does two things and the other said who calls this.

Both were asking a question instead of making a demand, and both took me somewhere I hadn't looked. Essays got skimmed. Questions got answered, and answering them is how I learned.

Bad reviews were the right and unfriendly kind. I once got a paragraph about a naming choice and my main memory of that review is being annoyed, which means the reviewer spent ten minutes and taught me nothing. Rude feedback is expensive feedback.

So I write short now and I ask. What happens if this is null. Who reads this field. Where does this get retried. Four words, one direction, and it works on people who are tired, which is everyone who is reviewing anything on a Thursday. I keep it to two lines.

Tango Gameworks shipped a game people loved and the studio closed anyway. That's the sentence I use whenever someone argues that quality protects a team.

It doesn't. The game was well reviewed and the studio was shut, and the reporting pointed at the model and the subscription economics rather than the content. A good game in the wrong shape is still a bad business, and the team that made it paid for a decision they didn't make.

I keep that in mind when I hear the argument that good work speaks for itself. Work doesn't speak. Someone has to carry it to a room and explain what it costs and what it returns, and if nobody does that, quality is a private matter.

Which is why I write things down now. The changelog, the decision, the reason. Half of surviving is being legible to the person deciding, and legible is a skill I can practice. I watched it happen once. I wrote it down. The notes are longer than the announcement was.

---
My whole thesis fits in one sentence. The tool isn't the problem, the motive is. I have watched it hold up across games, film and AI for three years and it hasn't failed me yet.

A studio optimises for internal approval instead of the customer. The tooling is fine, the talent is fine, the roadmap is approved. The failure is that every decision was measured by how it looked in a room rather than how it felt to a player.

An AI model writes the same function whether the team wants it to be good or wants to close a ticket. Same tool. Different motive. Different codebase six months later.

That's why I stopped arguing about tools. Banning a model doesn't fix a team that points its incentives inward, and a careful team with a weak model still ships something worth using. Judge the motive and the tool argument mostly disappears. I test it on every argument I have. I haven't lost yet.

Oblivion Remastered replaced Male and Female with Body Type 1 and Body Type 2, and modders confirmed it also removed several female armor variants. Nexus pulled a mod restoring the old option and then reinstated it, and it's now the most endorsed mod for that game.

The interesting part isn't the change. It's that the loudest argument was about a menu label and the quiet argument was about the actual armor. One of those is a design choice and the other is content being deleted, and the discourse rolled them into one fight.

I have a rule for this at work. If a change removes something people paid attention to, say so in the notes and say why. A label change is cheap to explain. A removal that shows up in a modding community as a surprise is expensive, and hiding it costs trust that's hard to buy back.

Taste is allowed here. Liking the old version is a preference. But a preference argument can't answer a question about where the content went, and that question always wins. I have made that call badly myself. I left a removal out of the notes once.

I was wrong about a caching decision in public and the correction was the most useful thing I did that quarter. I had written that a cache layer would halve our page time. It cut eleven percent and cost two days.

The person who corrected me did it politely and with numbers from their own service. I quoted it, said I was wrong, and wrote the eleven percent into the changelog along with why the estimate was off. My estimate assumed the database was the bottleneck. It was the network.

Two things happened after that. Nobody treated the error as a bigger deal than it was, and four people sent me their own numbers from systems I had never seen. Being wrong in public is how you find the people who know more than you.

The habit I keep is smaller than it sounds. When I write a number I write where it came from. When it turns out wrong, I edit the post instead of deleting it, because deleting an error teaches nobody anything. It's the honest version. I was wrong and I said so.

I shipped something small every Friday for a quarter and it beat every big project I tried that year. Thirteen Fridays, thirteen deploys, and eleven of them actually improved a number.

The small ones were unglamorous. A retry on a failed job. A better empty state. Deleting a query that ran twice per page load. None of those needed a planning meeting and none of them took more than a day.

The habit did something I didn't expect. It removed the fear. When a deploy is routine, I stop spending three days arguing about a change that should take an hour, because the downside of a bad Friday is that I revert on Monday.

The constraint is the part that matters. A weekly deadline forced me to pick the smallest version of each idea, and the smallest version is almost always the one that should ship first anyway. Big plans are a hedge against doing anything. I kept the streak going. I haven't missed a Friday since.

I moved my configuration into code and deleted four hundred lines of environment handling. One file, typed, checked at startup, and it fails loudly instead of at three in the morning.

The old version had forty two variables spread across three deployment systems. Nobody could name all of them. Two were documented in a wiki, four were only in a colleague's terminal history, and one was set in production and nowhere else.

The replacement is about ninety lines. It reads the environment once, validates every value, and refuses to start if something required is missing. That last part is the whole benefit. A bad deploy now fails in eleven seconds with a sentence, not in nine hours with a stack trace.

There is a cost. Every new setting is a code change and a review. I will take that trade every time. A setting nobody reviewed is a setting nobody understands, and I have spent enough nights on those.

I stopped guessing at my own metrics and wrote one query that answered three questions. Median response time, worst ten percent, and how many requests took over two seconds. Four lines of SQL.

The dashboard had nineteen panels and I couldn't tell you which one mattered. Nobody looked at it. The one query told me that eight percent of requests were slow and that they came from a single endpoint with a missing index. That's a Tuesday afternoon fix.

Adding one index took the slow share from eight percent to under one. I would never have found it in the chart of average response time, because the average was fine. Averages are how a bad tail hides.

Now I look at two numbers before anything else. The ninety fifth percentile and the count of requests over my own threshold. If both are quiet, I go do something else, and that's most days.

A teammate asked me why a line existed and I couldn't answer. That single question removed nine lines and a scheduled job.

The line was a workaround someone added for a bug that had been fixed in a library upgrade two years earlier. Nobody removed it because nobody could prove it was safe to remove. So it sat there, waking up every night and doing nothing.

I have started asking that question on purpose in reviews. Why does this line exist. If the answer is a name and a date, a comment can carry it. If the answer is nobody knows, it's a candidate for deletion and a test.

The uncomfortable version of this is that most of a mature codebase is unverified belief. Every one of those beliefs costs a little attention forever. The cheap move is to write down the reason when you write the line, and the expensive move is what happens when you don't. I ask it every week. It works. Every single time. I have deleted a lot since then.

I set a rule that every bug fix gets a test and it survived eleven months of my own laziness. Two of those tests have since caught the same bug coming back.

I will be honest about the failure rate. I skipped the rule maybe six times, and each skip was a hurry, and one of those skips is still an open bug today. The rule didn't make me consistent. It made the exceptions visible.

The tests I keep are short. Recreate the report with three lines, assert the bad answer, then fix. A failing test is the only proof I have that I understood the report in the first place, and understanding the report was the hard part all along.

Eleven months is long enough to know it works. The count of regressions on that service went from four in one quarter to zero, and the suite takes about nine seconds. Nine seconds for a quarter of no repeat bugs is a trade I would make again. I keep it simple. It works.

I opened a log file from a service I hadn't touched in eight months and read it like a stranger would. Ninety lines, timestamps, and one error repeated forty times.

I wrote that service. I remember the shape of it and nothing else. The log told me the retry I added in March fires nine times per failure, which means one bad request costs ten attempts against a provider that was saying no.

I fixed it in eleven minutes. Backoff with a ceiling, and a cap at four attempts. The error rate dropped the same hour because the provider stopped rate limiting us. I caught it on the first run.

I keep one habit from this. Once a month I open the logs of something I own and read them like a support ticket from a stranger. My code never lies to me. It waits until I stop looking. Silence isn't health.

I renamed a module and the diff touched eighty three files. Most of them were one line. Nineteen of them weren't.

I planned it for a week before I started. Branch first, then the rename in one commit, then the nineteen files in smaller ones I could review alone. The order matters more than the speed and I keep relearning that on every migration.

I broke it twice. One import order issue and one test that imported a private function by name. Both were fixed in under an hour because the commits were small enough to bisect.

Eighty three files sounds like a disaster. It was eleven hours and no incidents. I used to think big changes needed big courage. Now I think they need a small commit history and a morning where nobody is waiting on me. That's the whole trick. I bisected it twice. Both times the culprit was the same import line.

I took the on call shift nobody wanted for a quarter and it taught me more than the previous two years. Eleven pages, four real incidents, and one outage that was entirely my fault.

A pager is honest in a way no dashboard is. When it goes off at three in the morning the question isn't whether the system is elegant. What matters is whether I can find the one runbook that tells me what to do.

So I rewrote three runbooks during those months. Each one starts with the symptom in plain words, then the first command, then what the output means. No context, no architecture, no reading. A tired person needs steps.

I didn't expect how much it changed my normal work. Writing a runbook forces me to admit which parts of a system I don't understand, and that list was longer than I wanted it to be. I still read them. Three pages. That's the whole difference at four in the morning.

A query that ran in forty milliseconds for two years started taking four seconds on a Tuesday. Nine hundred million rows and one column that had grown to sixty percent of the table.

Nobody changed the query. The data changed. That's the kind of bug that never shows up in a code review, because no line of code is wrong. My plan was fine when the column was small.

I added an index and it went back to fifty milliseconds. Then I wrote a test that inserts a hundred thousand rows and asserts the plan uses the index, so the next person gets an alarm instead of a support ticket.

That test caught a regression eleven weeks later when someone dropped the index during a cleanup. I would never have noticed from the query text. The number moved and the code looked identical. That's the failure mode I stop trusting my eyes for. I check the plan now. Every time. It takes a minute.

I built a small tool for myself and eleven people asked for it in three weeks. Four hundred lines, one file, and a command that turns a folder of screenshots into a contact sheet.

I wrote it because I was tired of doing the same eight clicks forty times a week. It took one evening. It has saved me about nine hours a month since then, which means it paid for itself in the first fortnight.

What surprised me is that nobody wanted the version I wanted to build. I had a plan for settings, presets, a config file and a plugin system. Every request I actually got was for the same two things. A different output size and a way to skip a file.

I estimated a job at three days and it took nine. My estimate wasn't a lie and it wasn't close. I spent a while working out why.

Every part I could see was three days of work. What I couldn't see was the migration, two undocumented callers, and a deploy window that opens on Thursdays only. Three days of known work plus six days of discovering work.

I have started writing two numbers now. One number for the parts I can name. A separate line for the rest, set at about half the first number. On this job that would have been four and a half days. Still wrong. Closer.

Here is the honest version. I'm not bad at estimating known work. I'm bad at noticing how much I don't know, and that's a different skill. Saying the range out loud is what makes the second number visible to everyone else. I write both down now. It helps.

A polite feature request came in and turning it down was the most useful thing I did that month. The request was reasonable, the person was kind about it, and the answer was still no.

I didn't say no to the idea. I said no to the version they described, because it needed a new table, a new job and a new screen for four users. Then I offered the small version. A button that emails them the list.

That shipped in forty minutes and it was enough. They used it twice and stopped, which is what would have happened either way. The difference is that the small version cost an afternoon and the big one would have cost two weeks.

The line I use now is simple. I will always do the small version first, and if it's not enough I will do the rest with real evidence. Most requests die at that first step, and the ones that survive deserve the build. Four users wanted it. I gave them an afternoon. That's the whole method.

I wrote a README for a project nobody else uses and it doubled how fast I move in it. Four hundred words of what it's for, how to run it, and the two decisions that aren't obvious.

That part is the whole point. Anyone can read the code and learn what it does. Nobody can read the code and learn that the retry exists because a provider lied about accepting a message in 2024.

I have started writing the README before the code on anything that will outlive the week. It's nine times out of ten a page of decisions, and writing them down catches the ones I haven't actually made yet.

It costs about twenty minutes. Future me can open the project after a month away and know where to start. That used to take an hour of reading and a lot of muttering. I write it first now. Twenty minutes. The hour comes back.

I deleted a database table that nothing appeared to use and found out three days later what used it. A nightly report, run by a person who had left the company, on a machine we didn't monitor.

My code search showed zero references. That's the trap. That reference lived in a scheduled job on a laptop in a spreadsheet macro, and no amount of reading the repository would have found it.

What saved me was a backup. I had dumped the table to a file first because I was nervous, and restoring one table took nine minutes. The lesson isn't that I should have searched harder. I should have been nervous enough to keep the backup. I almost wasn't.

I now keep two rules for deletions. Dump first, always, even for something obviously dead. And rename before you drop, so the failure happens while the data still exists. Two rules. Both cheap. I follow them every time.

I paired with someone for two hours and we shipped nothing. We deleted code, argued about a name and rewrote a function three times. It was the best two hours of my week.

Output looked like zero. The deliverable was a decision. The function we landed on is about thirty percent shorter with one branch instead of four. I had to explain each branch out loud to a person who kept asking why.

I don't pair every day and I wouldn't want to. It's expensive and it's draining and it's worth it about once a week on the thing that has been stuck.

I look for a problem I have rewritten twice alone. At that point I'm not solving it. I'm rehearsing my own assumption, and one question from another person is the cheapest way out. That's the signal. Two rewrites. Then I ask for help. It costs me one hour.

A junior asked me nine questions in one afternoon and I learned more than they did. Every question was some version of why does it work this way, and I could answer about half of them.

The half I couldn't answer were the interesting ones. Why is this field nullable. Why is this job scheduled at four in the morning. Why does this endpoint return an empty list instead of an error. I had shipped all three and never asked.

So we wrote the answers down as we found them. Four of the nine needed a code change to make the behavior honest. Two were just undocumented. Three turned out to be bugs nobody had hit yet.

I look for junior people to work with now, and not for the reasons people usually give. They ask the questions I stopped asking years ago, and a lot of those questions still have no good answer, which means there is still work to do. I keep the list. Four items. All of them fixed.

I turned my coding agent off for a week to find out what I actually knew. Eleven hundred lines against my usual two thousand. Two bugs, both caught in review. One of them was a real design mistake.

The first two days were slow and annoying. I had forgotten how much time I spent looking things up, and the lookup time is a tax I hadn't noticed paying. Day three the rhythm came back.

The useful part was at the end. I went back through what I had written without help and it was more deliberate. Fewer files, fewer abstractions, more comments that explain a decision instead of restating a line.

I turned the agent back on. I also started running one week in four with it off, because the week showed me which parts of the work I had outsourced and stopped thinking about. Two of those parts mattered.

I read four thousand lines of someone else's code before touching it and it saved me a week of guessing. Two evenings, a notebook, and one diagram of how a request moves.

That code wasn't the problem. It was written by people solving a problem I half understood, and every strange choice had a reason that was invisible from the outside. A caching layer looked stupid until I found the case that made it necessary.

I stole one habit from this. Write down the questions before writing anything. What starts the work. What can fail. Who waits on the answer. Six questions, and if I can't answer four of them the reading continues.

Reading first feels like doing nothing, which is why I skipped it for eleven years. My weeks are shorter now and I complain less, and those two things are related. I read the code first now. It works.

A test suite that took six minutes stopped being run at all and I was the last to notice. Seven weeks of commits with no local test run, and two regressions that reached production.

The tests weren't slow because of the tests. They were slow because every one of them booted the whole application to check a string formatter. Four hundred tests doing eleven seconds of setup each.

I split the fast ones out. Pure functions, no database, no network, under nine seconds for three hundred tests. Those run on every save now. The other hundred still take six minutes. I run those before a merge.

I don't care about total time. It's time until the first useful failure. Nine seconds means I actually run it. Six minutes means I tell myself I will run it at the end, and the end is where the two regressions came from. I watch that number.

I moved a service to a new host and the migration was nine hundred lines of nothing to do with code. A runbook, a rollback plan, and four hours of checking that two systems agreed.

The code change was about thirty lines. Everything else was proving that the new host behaved the same way, which meant running both in parallel and comparing output for two days. Boring work with a real payoff. When we cut over, nothing happened, which was the goal.

The one thing that went wrong was a timezone. The old host ran on the company default and the new one ran on universal time, and a nightly job moved by five hours. It took us six hours to find because both hosts logged the right local time.

I now write a list of every assumption the old environment made before a migration. Clock, locale, filesystem case, environment variables, disk layout. It's a short list and the five hour bug was on it, and I didn't check. I check the clock first now. Six hours lost. Never again.
