I rewrote one error message and support tickets about that flow dropped by about half. My old message said invalid input. My new one says the date needs to be today or later.

That's a small change and it shows two things. First, the failure was never mysterious. Second, the message was written for the person who knows the system instead of the person who is stuck.

I went through the twenty most common errors after that. Eleven of them failed the same test. They described the system instead of the fix. Three of them named an internal code that appears nowhere else in the product.

My rule now is that an error message has two jobs. Say what is wrong and say what to do next, in that order. If I can't say what to do next, then the error is missing a recovery path and that's a product bug, not a copy problem. Two jobs. In that order.

I replaced a weekly meeting with a document and nobody asked for the meeting back. Eleven people, thirty minutes, and four months of not needing it.

The document was a page with three sections. What changed since last time. What is blocked. What I need from you. Nobody had to read it live and anybody could skim it in ninety seconds, which is roughly how much attention the meeting ever got.

The meetings that survived are the ones with a decision in them. If nobody is choosing between two options, a written update is better, because it doesn't spend eleven calendars to transmit nine lines.

I have one rule for this. If the same people meet three weeks in a row and nothing gets decided, I write the document instead and see who objects. Nobody has objected yet, and I have won back two hours a week. Two hours a week. That's a day a month.

I woke to a page at three in the morning and the fix was one line. Finding that line took ninety minutes because the alert told me the symptom and nothing else.

My alert said high error rate on the checkout service. A cache key changed format at midnight. It started missing on every request. Nobody had designed for a key that expires, so every request went to the database.

The fix is a version prefix on the key. The real fix is the alert. It now names the dependency and the failure count, and it links the runbook that says what to check first.

I keep one metric from that night. Ninety four minutes to a one line change, and about forty of those minutes were me working out what the numbers meant. Alerts that describe the system instead of the customer cost the same time at the worst possible hour. One line. Four minutes to write. That's the ratio I care about.

I sold a project internally by writing one number on the first line. Three hundred and forty hours a year spent doing something a script could do in four minutes.

Nobody argued about the approach after that. The debate was about priority, which is a fair argument, and the number turned it into a scheduling question instead of a taste question. We did it the next quarter.

The number was wrong in my favor by about a third. That's the part I tell people. When I checked later, the real figure was closer to two hundred and twenty hours. It still cleared the bar and I said so when I found out, which mattered more than being right the first time.

I use the same shape on every pitch now. The cost today, in hours or dollars, on the first line. What it costs to fix. What it costs to keep ignoring. Three numbers, and the third one is the one people remember.

I have a nine month gap on my resume and it has cost me exactly one interview. One. In four years of applying.

The gap was real. I took time off after a layoff and did contract work that I can't show, which is the part that worried me. It turns out nobody asked about the gap because nobody got to that part of the conversation.

What they asked about was the contract work. I had rewritten the entry to describe the systems rather than the clients, so it read as engineering instead of a gap with paperwork. Same facts, better frame.

The lesson is smaller than the industry makes it sound. A gap is a timeline fact and a portfolio is an ability fact, and people hire on the second one. I stopped apologizing for the calendar and started describing what I built, and the questions changed. Nine months off. Four years of applying. One question about it.

I worked alone for three years and the hardest part wasn't the isolation. It was that nothing I did got checked. No code review, no second opinion, no argument.

My work was fine. The problems I shipped were the ones I had decided weren't problems, and there was nobody to say otherwise. One of them was a retry loop I had convinced myself was safe. It ran for fourteen months before a provider changed a timeout.

What fixed it was a weekly swap with two other people who worked alone. Three projects, ninety minutes, one file each. Not a review exactly. More like showing someone the thing you have been staring at.

The rule is that everything gets a second pair of eyes before it goes live. Not a full review. Twenty minutes with someone who will ask why. That's enough to catch the belief I have stopped questioning. I keep the rule now. Three people. Ninety minutes. I don't skip it.

I'm the only person who knows how a system works and that's a problem I created. Four years of small decisions, one owner, one mental model.

I stopped defending my old code and my reviews got faster. Four years of treating every comment as a verdict on my judgment, which is an expensive habit at about ten minutes of arguing per comment.

The shift was small and it took a while. A question about my naming is a question about my naming. It's not a question about me. Once I believed that, the answer became short. I stopped writing paragraphs in the comments.

I replaced it with a rule about scope. I do the small suggestions without discussion. If it changes behavior I say what it would break and we decide together. If it's about style I let the person with the strongest preference win, because consistency beats my taste.

My output went up and the arguing went down. The uncomfortable part is this. I was the reason my reviews were slow, and that took eleven years to admit. Eleven years of reviews. That's on me.

I have shipped one hundred and forty one small changes this year and I can't name ten of them. That sounds bad and it's the reason the product works.

I remember ten of them and they're the big changes. Two rewrites, a migration, a new screen. I've forgotten the other hundred and thirty one. Retries, better errors, deleted queries, empty states, and four index changes that made a slow page fast.

I check the changelog when I doubt the year was worth it. That's the whole reason I keep it. Not for the team, not for the users. For the week where nothing big happened and I need to see that the small things landed.

My target for next year isn't a bigger number. I want the same pile of forgettable changes and about a third fewer of the changes I can name, because a year where I remember the big work is usually a year where the big work was necessary. Small things. That's the year.

Log rotation deleted the one file I needed and I found out at the worst moment. Thirty days of retention, one incident, and the evidence was gone by two hours.

I keep a copy of logs for ninety days now. Not because I plan to read them. Because the one time I needed to, the disk policy had already made the decision for me and nobody had asked a single question about it.

The fix cost eleven dollars a month. I checked. Cold storage for a couple of hundred gigabytes is that cheap, and I would have paid ten times it to have that afternoon back.

Here is the part I want to remember. The retention rule was set by a person trying to save money, and they were right about the money and wrong about everything else. I add the cost next to the saving when I touch a policy now. Costs are easy to name. The thing you lose isn't.

I added a config flag nobody turned on and I kept it for a year. One line, a branch, and a test, doing nothing in production since last September.

I checked the usage. Zero. Not a low number. Zero, across four environments, nineteen months of deploys. The flag existed because I had a theory about a customer who never asked for it.

Deleting it took nine minutes. Branch gone, test gone, and the module got one level shallower. That shallow part matters more than the line count. Every branch is a place the next reader has to hold two worlds in their head.

My rule now is simple. A flag ships behind a date and an owner, and if nobody asks for it by that date, it goes. I put the date in the changelog where I will see it. Not in a ticket nobody reads. That's the rule. One date. One name.

Cache invalidation cost me two days and the answer was to stop caching. Thirty lines of key logic, a version prefix, and a warm up job, all to avoid eleven milliseconds.

Eleven milliseconds. I measured it in the end, which is the step I skipped for two days. One query, indexed, returning a row that hadn't changed in six months. The cache was saving me nothing measurable.

I deleted the cache. The code went from two hundred lines to fourteen and the p95 moved by less than a millisecond. I kept the measurement script and threw the rest away.

The uncomfortable part is that I had already known this rule and I ignored it because caching feels like engineering. It feels productive. Measuring feels boring and it would have saved me forty hours. It's gone now. Fourteen lines. Nothing broke. That's the whole lesson. I measure before I cache anything.

I pinned one dependency version and it saved a release. An upstream package shipped a breaking change in a minor version on a Thursday. We found out from a bug report instead of a build.

My pin was nine months old and I had been meaning to update it. The update was scheduled for the week after. So the accidental fix was timing, not discipline, and I want to be honest about that.

What I changed afterwards is the interesting part. I broke the pin into a separate file with a comment saying why each one exists, and a monthly job that opens a request when a newer version appears. A human still decides. It just makes the choice visible.

Since then I have merged four updates on purpose and skipped two with a written reason. That's four more updates than I used to do, and none of them happened at nine at night. Four updates. Two skips. All written down.

I wrote three pages of docs for an internal tool and usage went up four times. Not because the tool changed. Because eighteen people found out it existed.

I deleted my todo list and started working from a single file with three headings. Today, this week, someday. My completion rate went up and I stopped lying to myself.

The old list had two hundred and forty items. I had been adding to it since 2023 without ever removing anything, so it was less a plan than an archive of every idea I had ever had and abandoned.

The new file has eleven items. Nine of them are things I decided this week. Moving something from someday to today requires me to delete something else, and that one rule is why the list stays short.

What surprised me is how much of the old list I don't miss. About a third of it was work I had assigned to myself out of guilt. Deleting it wasn't laziness. It was the only honest priority decision I made all year. Eleven items. Nothing else.

I ran a migration twice by accident and nothing broke, which told me the design was right. Then I found the one table where it was wrong and fixed the design.

The migration added a column and backfilled it. Running it again just wrote the same values, so the second run was harmless. That's idempotence, and I had built it by habit rather than by plan.

The table that failed was the one with a timestamp default. The second run overwrote eleven thousand rows with the new time. Nobody noticed for a week, because the column was used in one report.

I now write the second run into the plan. Not as a test after the fact. As the first thing I decide, because if the answer is we run it once, then I have found the part that needs a backup. It ran twice. Nobody noticed. That's the problem.

I got a bug report that opened with an insult and it was the best report I received all year. Seven lines, a screenshot, and exact steps that reproduced in ninety seconds.

The tone was rough and the content was precise. Which environment, which account, what they clicked, and what they expected instead. I have received hundreds of polite reports with less information in them.

I replied about the bug and didn't engage with the insult, which is a skill I learned about four years later than I should have. My fix took twenty minutes. The follow up was friendly once the bug was gone.

What I take from it is to separate the wrapper from the payload. Most angry reports contain a real defect and a person having a bad day, and only one of those is my problem to solve. Read it twice. Fix the bug. Ignore the rest.

A dependency I rely on got archived with no notice and I found out from a security scanner. Eight years of releases, then a note saying the maintainer had stopped.

A timezone bug cost me a day and taught me to write the offset in the test name. A job ran at midnight in one region and eleven at night in another. Same code, different answers.

Both servers logged the right local time. That was the confusing part. They agreed with each other and disagreed with reality, because one used the company default and the other used universal time, and nobody had written that down anywhere.

The fix took nine minutes. Finding it took seven hours, and most of that was me proving the code was fine. That's the tax for every unwritten environment assumption in a system.

I now write the offset into the test name, like nightly job at eight in the region it serves. When the test fails the name tells the next person what broke, which is a sentence I paid a full day to learn to write. Nine minutes. Seven hours. I named the test after the offset.

I stopped using the mouse for anything I do more than ten times a day and my hands stopped hurting. Four shortcuts, a launcher, and about six weeks of being slower.

The six weeks are the part people skip. For a while I was genuinely worse at my job, and every instinct said to go back. I kept a count of the repetitions to stay honest about it. Eleven times a day was the threshold I picked.

The payoff isn't speed, which surprised me. It's that the small pauses went away. Moving to the mouse and back cost me a second and a half of attention, and attention was the expensive part, not the second.

I have one rule about this now. Learn the shortcut for anything I do more than ten times a day, and ignore everything else. Tools are only worth learning in proportion to how often I meet them. One file a week. Four cents. It works.

I deleted nineteen alerts and kept two. One tells me a customer can't check out and one tells me the data pipeline has stopped. Everything else was noise with a page attached.

The nineteen were a mix of the obvious, the duplicated, and the aspirational. I had alerts for CPU above eighty percent, which means nothing at all until you know what the workload is. And four separate alerts for the same downstream service.

Keeping two felt reckless for about a week. Then I stopped waking up for things that were already fine, and the two that remain get read in about four seconds because they never cry wolf.

The rule I use is a question. If this fires at three in the morning, what do I actually do about it. If the honest answer is that I would do nothing, the alert becomes a dashboard number or it gets deleted. Two alerts. That's it. Both get read.

A junior sent me their first pull request and I rewrote it in the review. It was a mistake and I knew it about two minutes in.

The change was correct. The naming was theirs instead of mine, and I turned eleven small preferences into a list of requested changes. That teaches someone to guess what I like, which is the opposite of what a review is for.

I closed the review, approved the change, and wrote a separate note about the two things that were really wrong. Both were about error handling. The rest was taste and taste isn't worth a junior's confidence.

What I do now is mark my comments. This one will break something. This one is my preference. Two categories, and I only block on the first. It has made my reviews shorter and, I think, more useful. Two lists. One blocks. One does not. I learned that the hard way. I mark every comment now.

I read a config file line by line and found the bug in a value nobody had looked at since 2019. One timeout set to thirty seconds where everything else was two.

The config was ninety lines of values with no comments. Twenty seven of them had never been changed. The thirty second timeout belonged to a call that happened on every page load, which is why the slow requests were always slow and never varied.

Nobody had touched it because nobody knew which value mattered. That's the actual problem, and it's a documentation problem disguised as a performance problem.

I went through the file after the fix and wrote a comment above every value I understood and a question mark above every value I didn't. There were nine question marks. I have answered six of them since, which is six fewer nights where a number belongs to nobody. Nine question marks. Six answered. Three to go.

---
A test that passed in my branch failed in CI and it was the test that was wrong. It asserted on time, and the two machines were in different timezones.

I had written it six months earlier and it had passed several hundred times. That's the part that makes this kind of test expensive. It works until the environment changes, and nobody associates the failure with the day the runner moved.

The fix was to inject a clock and assert on a fixed instant. Nine lines changed and the test now fails for real reasons, of which there have been two since.

I look for time and randomness in tests the way I used to look for null. Anything that reads the current moment or generates an id is a place where a test can pass for the wrong reason, and passing for the wrong reason is worse than failing. Inject the clock. Assert a fixed instant. Never trust the current time. One line. Fixed instant.

I tested a backup for the first time in four years and the restore failed on the third file. Four years of backups, one bad tape generation, and no alarm.

The backup job reported success every night, which is the problem. It reported that the job ran, not that the data was readable. Those are different claims and one of them matters at nine at night.

I restored the whole set into a scratch environment. It took two hours and I wrote down every step. That document is now the runbook, and it's the first time anyone in the company has read the file list end to end.

The change I made was small. One job a week restores a random file and compares a checksum. Four cents of compute, and it would have caught this in the first week instead of the fourth year. One file a week. Four cents. It failed once.

I said I don't know in a meeting and it was the most useful sentence of the hour. Three people relaxed and the conversation got specific in about four minutes.

I had been faking a number for ten minutes. Not lying by much. Nodding along to a figure I half remembered, which is close enough to a lie when a decision is being made from it.

Saying I don't know cost me nothing. Somebody else knew, and the four minutes after that were better than the previous twenty, because nobody had to route around a guess.

I do this earlier now. If I catch myself building a sentence on a number I haven't checked, I stop and say so. The surprising part is how often the answer is in the room and how few people admit they don't have it. Say it in the room. Save an hour. One meeting. Four minutes. The guess would have cost weeks.

I turned down a promotion and stayed an engineer and I would make the same call again. Twelve percent more money, four more meetings a day, and no code.

I said no and asked why instead. What I wanted was the money and the scope, and it turned out both existed in a different shape. A lead role on the project I already cared about, with a raise attached and no people management.

Nobody offered me that. I asked for it, which is the part that still surprises me. I had spent four years assuming the only two doors were engineer and manager.

The thing I learned is that the ladder is shorter than the hallway. There are roles between the rungs and most of them aren't advertised. Asking what problems need an owner has been more useful to my career than any interview I have done. I stayed an engineer. No regrets. I write code all day.

I learned a new language at forty and it took nine months of evenings to write anything useful. Go, about four hundred lines, and one deployed service that has run for a year.

The hard part wasn't the syntax. It was unlearning a decade of habits. I kept reaching for the abstractions of my old language and getting stuck in a fight with the new type system.

What helped was writing something I would actually keep. Not a tutorial. A small service with tests, error handling, and a deploy, because the parts that teach you a language are the parts that are boring in a tutorial.

The other thing that helped was reading code I didn't write. About twenty files from projects of a similar size. Reading taught me the idioms faster than writing did, which I had never noticed before. Nine months. Four hundred lines. Still running. Nobody has restarted it.

I taught by writing everything down and it made me better at the work. Fourteen pages over a year, and the act of explaining forced me to check things I had assumed for years.

The pages started as notes for one person. They became the thing new people read in their first week, which is a strange responsibility for something I wrote on a Tuesday to avoid repeating myself.

What I didn't expect is the corrections. Four of my pages were wrong. A colleague found a mistake about a retry policy and a script that no longer worked, and fixing them taught me more than writing them had.

The rule I follow now is to write the page when I answer the same question twice. Not before. If I haven't answered it twice I don't understand it well enough to teach it, and the page would be a guess with nice formatting. Write it once. Answer it twice. I check before I trust it.

I costed one more feature and it was three weeks, and the thing that decided it wasn't the three weeks. It was the eleven places it would need changing every time we touched the schema.

My build estimate was nine days. Owning it was forever. That second number never appears in a planning meeting because it's nobody's job to write it down.

So I started writing it down. Next to every feature I write what it will cost to change in two years. If that sentence is long, the feature gets a redesign before anyone builds anything, and twice now that has killed a bad idea in the same meeting it was born.

My rule is small and it changes arguments. The question stops being can we build this. It becomes can we afford to keep it, which is what that three week estimate was hiding. I write that sentence on purpose. I have killed two features with it. Three weeks. Then forever.

There is a feature I built that nobody has ever turned off and it costs me about eleven dollars a month. A summary email, sent every Monday to one address that belongs to a person who left.

Four years of emails to a dead mailbox. It runs so quietly that nobody has looked, and it has survived two cleanups because the person running them didn't know what it was.

The problem isn't the eleven dollars. It's that I can't tell you what else is running. If a single forgotten job hides this well, then there are others, and the honest count is unknown.

I spent a Friday finding out. Nine scheduled jobs, four with no owner, two with no output anybody reads. I wrote them all in one file with a date and a name next to each. The list is eleven lines and it's the most useful document in the project. Four years. Eleven dollars. Nobody looked.

I protect Friday mornings and nothing gets booked into them. Four hours, no meetings, no reviews, no chat. It's the only reason anything difficult gets done in my week.

The work I do in those four hours is always the work I have been avoiding. A migration, a design doc, a bug that needs thinking instead of trying. It's the part of the job that has no deadline and therefore never happens.

The rule that makes it work is booking it as a meeting with myself. Sounds precious. It survives because everyone else can see it, and a visible block is harder to take than a feeling I have about needing focus.

I have kept this for nineteen months. It has produced the two biggest fixes of the past year and about forty small ones, and I would trade a day of normal meetings for it without thinking. Four hours. Once a week. It's mine.

I reread a stack trace I had written myself six months earlier and I couldn't follow it. The frame names were mine. The log lines were mine. I still had to trace the flow on paper.

That's new. I used to know my own services by heart. Then I started using an agent for the boring parts, and the boring parts were the ones that taught me the map.

So I did the work again. Four hours of reading and drawing. By the end I could name every hop in the request and say which one fails first under load.

Here is what I keep from it. A stack trace is a map you drew. If I can't read my own map, I didn't build the thing. I commissioned it. Those are different feelings and only one of them is useful at nine at night. I drew the map again. It was worth it.

I picked a naming convention four years ago and I still pay for it. Every model is called a Record, even the ones that aren't records.

The convention made sense in week one. Then we added documents, events and a settings blob, and all of them got the same suffix because the pattern was already there. Nobody stopped to ask whether the pattern was good. It was just the pattern.

Renaming is now a two hundred file change. Not hard. Just expensive, and worse than the original mistake.

My rule now is boring and it works. Name the thing by what it does in this system, not by what it looks like. A Record that's a settings blob is a lie in the type system. Small lies compound. Four years later, they're the architecture. I have renamed four things this year. I have eleven to go. It is slow work.

A log line I wrote said the job completed when the job had skipped every row. The line was true. The word completed wasn't.

I found out from a support ticket eight weeks later. Nine thousand rows never processed, one green checkmark per night, and a person who trusted it because the message said completed.

That's the cost of a cheerful log line. A log is a claim about what happened, and the claim has to survive the worst case or it's decoration. Mine didn't survive an empty input.

I rewrote eleven of them after that. Each now names the count and the path taken. Completed, zero rows, empty source. Four facts in one line, and any of them being wrong is visible. I read my own logs once a month now. It took an afternoon. It caught one more lie the next week. That was worth it. It pays off.

---
A cron job ran twice and we processed about eleven thousand duplicate emails. Nobody noticed for two days because the emails were correct and the customer didn't reply twice.

The cause was a lock that expired before the work finished. Ninety seconds of timeout against a job that took four minutes when the upstream was slow. That's a design that works until it doesn't.

The fix was to make the job idempotent instead of protecting it. A processed marker on each row, checked inside the transaction. Then running it twice costs time and nothing else.

I have one question for any scheduled job now. What happens if this runs while the last one is still going. If nobody can answer, the job is one slow day away from the duplicate email problem. I check every job now. What if it runs twice. What if it runs slow. Two questions. I ask them in review. One slow day. Mine failed both. Never again.

#AI #BuildInPublic #VibeCoding #tech #Cron #Idempotency #Developer #Software #Engineering #Coding #Backend #DeepTech #DistributedSystems #Learning #OpenSource #BestPractices #Reliability #Engineering #SRE #DataEngineering
---
I stopped estimating in reviews and started asking one question instead. What is the smallest change that solves this. Four words fewer, ten arguments saved.

The old habit was my worst one. Somebody proposed a solution, I proposed a different one, and ninety minutes later we had a design nobody wanted and a meeting scheduled for Thursday.

The question moves the conversation off the solution and onto the problem. It also lets the person who wrote the proposal win, which is usually the right outcome because they know the code.

I have watched this replace about a dozen design debates in the past year. Not every one. The ones it doesn't settle were never design debates anyway. They were resource fights wearing a design costume. Ninety minutes. Gone. I use the question every time. It has not failed me yet. Not once. One question. Every time. It works. Ask it first.

I built a dashboard with nineteen panels and when I deleted it nobody asked where it went. That's the whole review of my work on it.

My panels answered questions I found interesting. Which queue is longest. How many retries per hour. What the p99 looks like by endpoint. Useful to me and used by nobody, because nobody had the questions I had.

The panel that mattered was the simplest one. A single number, requests failing, with a link to the logs. Four lines of code and the only thing anyone ever looked at.

I keep a rule from that. One number per screen, chosen by the person who gets paged. Everything else is a tab they can open when they have a reason. A dashboard isn't a report. It's an alarm with context. One number. That is it. I pick it with the person who gets paged, not with the team that built the service. That is the whole design.

I said no to an urgent request and the person solved it themselves in twenty minutes. That's what happens most of the time and it took me nine years to find out.

The request sounded urgent because it was blocked, and blocked feels urgent to the person who is stuck. I had a deploy going and a queue of my own. So I said give me an hour and asked what they had tried.

They came back in twenty minutes. The answer was in a config they had already found. My hour would have been spent confirming what they already knew.

The rule I keep is a question. What have you tried. Ask it as a question and nobody takes offense. It's a filter that separates the thing that needs me from the thing anyone could do with ten more minutes. Twenty minutes. That was the answer. My hour was never needed.

I forked a small library after its maintainer stopped answering and it has been the right call for two years. Three hundred lines, one file, and a comment that says where it came from.

A fork isn't a hostile act. I sent two pull requests first and got no reply. The bug hit us every week. Waiting was a choice with a cost, and I wrote the cost down before I made the decision.

One thing I got right is the upload. The fork lives in our repo with a README explaining which upstream commit it came from and what changed. Nobody has to guess or read git history from four years ago.

The maintenance has been about four hours a year. I check upstream twice, diff it, and decide. That's the price of an abandoned dependency and it's smaller than the price of waiting. Four hours a year. That is the price. I pay it twice.

I wrote a design doc for a two day change and it saved a week. Four pages, written in ninety minutes, and the argument happened in the comments instead of the meeting.

The doc described three options and picked one. Not because I was right. Because writing down the two I rejected made my preference checkable, and the reviewer found a problem with it in ten minutes that I had missed for a day.

That's the part people skip. A design doc isn't a proposal. It's a list of the roads you didn't take, so someone can tell you why one of them was better before you spend a fortnight on the wrong one.

My short version fits on one page. What problem, three options, what I picked, what would change my mind. That last line is the one that gets useful replies. One page. That is the limit. I write it before I start anything big.

A library upgrade broke a date format and it took four days to find. The release notes said improved date handling. They didn't say it changed the separator.

That isn't malice. It's the nature of release notes, which are written by people who know what changed. The changelog is a map drawn by someone who already knows the terrain, and it's always missing the part you needed.

So I stopped reading release notes as a verification step. I read the diff, or I find the test and change it and watch what breaks. On this one upgrade that would have taken eleven minutes.

The habit I keep is a small test that exercises the formats we actually output. It runs on every dependency bump. When a library changes a separator, that test fails in nine seconds instead of a support ticket failing in four days. Nine seconds. One support ticket avoided. I check the formats now.

I retired a service and it took three months because of the parts nobody owned. Ninety users, four integrations, and one report on a person's laptop.

Shutting the code down was the easy part. Eight hours of work and a deploy. The other eighty nine days were conversations about which of the four integrations was actually load bearing and who actually read that report.

That ratio is the whole lesson. Code is the cheapest part of a retirement and the relationships are the expensive part, and a plan that budgets for the code will be wrong by an order of magnitude.

My list now starts with people, not services. Who calls this. Who reads its output. Who would find out if it stopped, and how long would that take. Three of the four integrations turned out to be nobody. Three of four. I couldn't have guessed which. I asked instead.

I deleted a comment and the next person put the bug back. Twelve lines, no explanation, and a warning that looked like noise to someone reading it fresh.

The comment said don't reorder these calls, the second one depends on the first. I removed it during a cleanup because it restated the code. It didn't restate the code. It restated a decision, and there is no way to read a decision from two lines of syntax.

I put it back with a date and a link. Two days later the bug came out again and the comment stayed, because now it looks like information instead of an opinion.

My line now is simple. A comment explains why, never what. If it's explaining what, it's competing with the code. If it's explaining why, there is no other place for it to live. Two days. It came back. The comment stayed. I keep that in mind.

We spent nine minutes in a meeting arguing about a variable name and I was one of the two people arguing. Both of us were wrong and the third person ended it.

They asked what the value meant at the call site. Neither of us had looked at the call site. Two minutes of reading later, the name was obvious and neither of our suggestions was it.

That's what most style arguments are. Two people defending a mental model of code neither has read in the past week. The cheapest way to settle it is a scroll, and nobody thinks of it because the argument feels productive.

I have a rule that comes from the nine minutes. If a naming debate survives one turn, we open the call sites first. It has ended every one of them, and it has never once been the name I wanted. Nine minutes. Two opinions. One scroll.

My first production incident was an empty table and it took nine hours because I refused to believe the obvious thing. A job had run. The rows weren't there. Both were true.

I looked at the code for six of those hours. The job was fine. What I hadn't done was look at the input, because I had written the input job six months earlier and I trusted it.

An input job stopped at midnight because a token expired. It reported success.That's the pattern I have now seen eleven times. The failure is upstream and the report is wrong. I trust my own old code the most and that is the trap. I check the data before the code now. Count the rows at every hop. Nine hours becomes about twenty minutes once you stop reading and start counting. Count the rows. Every hop. I do that first now.

A tool I trusted lied to me for a month by rounding a number. Six decimals down to two, and a comparison that passed against a threshold it should have failed.

The tool was doing what its author intended. Two decimals is readable and almost always fine. It wasn't fine here, because I was comparing against a limit of four point one and the real value was four point one zero one.

I found it by printing the raw value, which is the step I skip when I trust the tool. The number in the dashboard said four point one and the number in the log said four point one zero one, and I had been reading the first one for a month.

I now print one raw value next to any rounded summary. Two numbers, one lie removed, and it has caught three rounding surprises since. Readable isn't the same as correct. Readable. Not correct. Two raw values.

I deleted nine thousand rows with a statement that was missing a condition and the backup saved the afternoon. Forty minutes to restore, one apology, and a change to how I write deletes.

My statement was a cleanup with a date filter. I forgot the filter and typed the where clause by hand instead of copying it from the query I had tested. Twelve characters of difference and a full table gone.

What made it survivable was boring. The nightly backup had run four hours earlier and I had tested the restore two weeks before, which is a habit I picked up from a much worse week.

My rules now fit in a sentence. Never type a delete. Copy the select, check the count, swap the verb. The count is the whole point, because a select that returns everything I expected is the only proof the delete is safe. Copy. Check. Swap. That's the order now.

I still use a nineteen line script I wrote in 2019 and it's the most valuable thing I own. It reads a directory, finds the newest file, and opens it in the right tool.

That's all it does. I have written and deleted about forty bigger tools in the same period. None of them survived, mostly because each one tried to solve a problem I had once.

The difference isn't quality. It's frequency. I use the script eleven times a week, which means every rough edge got sanded off by accident over four years. The bigger tools were used twice and never improved.

I use that as a test now before I build anything. How often will this run. If the answer is once a month, I write a one off command and forget it. Tools earn maintenance by being used. Eleven times a week. Nineteen lines. Four years old. It just works.

I shipped on a green build and the build was green for the wrong reason. Two tests had been skipped for a month and neither of them was flaky.

One was skipped by a decorator someone added during a firefight and never removed. The other was filtered out by a name pattern that matched more than intended. Both decisions were reasonable on the day they were made.

That's how a suite rots. Not by deleting tests. By quiet, local, sensible skips that nobody owns, until the number of green checks stops meaning what it used to.

I now print the skip count at the end of every run and treat a rising number the same as a failing test. A skip is a decision that someone didn't make. If nobody makes it, the suite lies at the exact moment I need it. Two skips. One month. Both fixed. I check the count.

A feature flag outlived the person who added it by three years and nobody could say what it did. It was true in production and false everywhere else, which is the worst possible state.

I read the code around it. Both branches looked correct. That's the problem with a flag that old. The unused branch is still plausible, so nobody can prove it is dead, so nobody deletes it.

We resolved it in an afternoon with one question. Who asked for this. Nobody could answer, so we picked the branch that matched the current product and deleted the other. Tests passed. Nothing changed.

The lesson is about time, not code. Flags are born as speed and age into debt, because the cost of deciding grows and the cost of leaving it shrinks. Put a date on yours. That date is the only thing that forces the decision. No owner. No date. Deleted. I date every flag now.

#AI #BuildInPublic #VibeCoding #tech #FeatureFlags #TechnicalDebt #Developer #Software #Engineering #Coding #Programming #DeepTech #Backend #Maintenance #Learning #OpenSource #BestPractices #Engineering #Refactoring #ProductManagement
---
I wrote tests for a twelve year old function and found four bugs before I changed a line. The function formatted addresses and it had never been covered.

I started by writing down what it should do. Eight cases, written from the spec rather than from the code. Three failed on the first run and one produced a result nobody could explain.

That order matters. Writing tests from the code just freezes whatever the code does, bugs included. Writing them from the expected behavior is a check against an outside opinion, and the outside opinion is the one that counts.

The four bugs had been in production for years and none of them had caused a ticket. Two were cosmetic and two were wrong in a way that made a report look fine. That's what untested legacy code looks like. Not broken. Just unmeasured. Eight cases. Four bugs. One afternoon. Worth it. I write from the spec now. I run them before I touch the code.

A bug report with a screenshot became a test in eleven minutes and it's now the reason a whole class of bugs stays fixed. Small habit, big return.

The report said the total was wrong for an order with a discount and a refund. I turned that sentence into a test with the three inputs from the screenshot, watched it fail, then fixed the rounding order.

That's the shortest path from a customer to a guard rail. Not a meeting. Not a spec. One test named after the sentence in the report, so the next person searching for the phrase finds both the bug and the fix.

I name tests like that now. Order with discount and refund returns correct total. It reads badly and it works well. A test name should be a sentence someone actually said, because someone will say it again. One sentence. One test. Eleven minutes. Cheap.

I gave a colleague a five minute rule and it cut my interruptions by about half. If you're stuck for five minutes, ask. If it's less, write it down and keep going.

The rule isn't for them. It's for me. I used to answer a question the moment it arrived, which meant my afternoon was forty two minute blocks of other people's problems and nothing of my own.

What changed is the shape of the interruption. A five minute question comes with context. A twenty minute question comes with a guess that has already hardened, and now I have to remove it as well as answer.

I also asked them to batch the questions. Three at once, at a time they choose. That one change gave me back about six hours a week and it cost them nothing at all. Six hours back. Three questions. One block. I tell everyone.

I read a postmortem I wrote two years ago and it was useless. Nine paragraphs about what happened and one sentence about what we changed.

The timeline was thorough. The root cause was correct. And none of it helped me today, because the document answered a question nobody asks six months later. Nobody wakes up wondering what happened in March 2024.

What people ask is what rule came out of it. That's one sentence, and it belongs at the top where a reader can't miss it. Everything below is evidence for people who want to check the work.

I have started writing the rule first and the timeline second, which feels backwards and reads much better. If the rule is missing when I write the first line, then the incident didn't teach me anything yet and the document is too early. One sentence at the top. That is the fix. Nothing else.

I rolled back a release in the middle of a Tuesday and nobody noticed, which is the best outcome I have had. Eleven minutes from decision to stable.

That isn't skill. That's a boring deploy process that I had rebuilt the month before for a different reason. One command, one previous version, one health check. No drama and no meeting.

The change I reverted was a cache setting that looked harmless and doubled the database load under real traffic. It passed review. It passed staging. It failed in the only place that counts, and the only thing that mattered was how fast we could undo it.

I spend less time now trying to prevent every mistake and more on making the undo cheap. Reversible beats careful, because careful is a feeling and reversible is a command with a check afterwards. One command. Eleven minutes. That is the goal now. Not luck.

My runbook is four pages and it starts with the sentence a tired person needs. Is the site down for everyone. If yes, do this. If no, go to page two.

That first line took me three rewrites. My early versions started with architecture, which is useless at four in the morning when I can't remember whether the queue is supposed to be empty.

The rest of the pages are a decision tree with commands and expected output under each one. Nine steps. Two of them are checks that end the run, which is the part most runbooks miss.

The rule I follow is to write it for the version of me who has slept badly. Short sentences. One command per step. Say what the output should look like, so the next person knows when the step passed instead of guessing. I wrote it twice. I read it once a quarter.

I deleted four hundred lines last week and nobody noticed for nine days. That's the good outcome.

The code was a retry wrapper from 2021. It caught a timeout from a service we stopped using in 2022. Nobody removed it because removing things feels risky and adding things feels like progress. So it sat there, running its checks every night, guarding a door with no room behind it.

I found it while tracing something else. I didn't find it by auditing. I found it because a log line pointed at a file I'd never opened.

Here's what I've learned about dead code. It isn't dangerous because it runs. It's dangerous because it teaches. A new person reads it and thinks this is how we handle timeouts here. So they copy the shape into a place where it matters, and now the dead pattern is alive again.

We don't have a lint rule for that. We can't. The rule would have to know intent and intent doesn't live in the file. It lives in the head of whoever wrote it, and that person left in 2023.

I built a dashboard six months ago and I checked it twice. Both times were the week I shipped it.

That's not a story about laziness. It's a story about a dashboard that answered a question nobody had. I picked the metrics by asking what was easy to graph. The graphs looked great. Green lines, smooth curves, the whole thing rendered in under a second.

The question people actually had was whether a specific customer's data was intact. Nobody could answer it from my charts. They asked me in a message every time, and I queried the database every time, and we both felt bad about it.

I eventually replaced the dashboard with a single query saved in a shared folder. It's ugly. It has no colors. It gets used eleven times a day.

I'm not saying dashboards are bad. I'm saying I built the chart before I heard the question. If you can't name the person who will open it on a Tuesday when something looks wrong, you're building decoration.

It took me four tries to run a migration that should have taken one afternoon.

First attempt: the index build locked the table and the app started timing out. Second attempt: I batched the writes but forgot the reads were still hitting the old column. Third attempt: I got the code right and the rollback wrong, which is worse, because a rollback you can't trust is a plan you don't have.

Fourth attempt worked. Twelve million rows. Six hours. I watched the whole thing.

What I learned is that I kept treating the migration as a code problem. It was a scheduling problem. The database was fine. The code was fine after attempt two. What I didn't have was a window where both old and new code could run at the same time and agree with each other.

I write migrations backwards now. I start with the rollback, then the dual read, then the dual write, then the cutover, then the cleanup two weeks later when I've stopped being nervous. Same total work. One less outage.

I turned off a feature I wrote. It was the right call and it still stung for a week.

The feature let people bulk edit a list. I was proud of it. Keyboard shortcuts, undo, the whole thing. It took three weeks and it had tests I liked.

Usage data said nine people clicked it. Nine. Four of them clicked it once. Five of them clicked it by accident, twice each, because the button sat close to save. So the real benefit was zero and the real cost was confusion.

I removed it on a Tuesday. Nobody emailed. Nobody asked where it went. That silence was the loudest signal of the year.

Here's the part I keep coming back to. I didn't build it because people wanted it. I built it because I could see it in my head. Clarity of imagination isn't demand, and I'd been mistaking one for the other for years. If a feature is easy to picture, that's a reason to ask harder, not a reason to start.

I got paged at 3:12 in the morning for a config typo. The app was fine.

A value that should have been six hundred was written as sixty thousand. That's it. One extra zero in a plain config file. The queue drained, the workers sat idle, and the alert fired because a threshold I'd set three years ago got crossed.

I fixed it in ninety seconds. I stayed awake for two hours.

That gap between fix time and recovery time is the thing nobody measures. We track time to resolve. We don't track time to feel normal again. And the second number is bigger.

What changed after that: I stopped alerting on symptoms I couldn't act on at night. A full disk at 3am needs me. A queue that's slower than expected can wait until seven. I moved eleven alerts to a morning digest and I got three hours a week back. The typo is still possible. Config files lie to everyone. But the page goes out when something breaks, which means when it goes off, I believe it.

I stopped asking a technical interview question because it was measuring the wrong thing.

The question was how do you find a memory leak. Good question. Real answers, real tradeoffs, real stories from people who'd done it. I asked it for four years.

Then a candidate answered it well and couldn't read a stack trace from a language they'd never seen. And I realized I'd been testing a memory of a war story, not a skill.

The skill is reading unfamiliar output. The skill is forming a guess and then checking the guess for free. The skill is saying I don't know yet and then going to look.

Now I hand them a failing test and a terminal. That's the whole interview. I watch how they move. Some read the error. Some add a log line. Some read the source. All fine. What I'm watching for is whether they narrow the space or wander it. Turns out that's a much better predictor than opinions about garbage collection, and it's kinder to people who learned this on their own.

The README said the service needed two environment variables. It needed five. I found out at eleven at night.

That isn't quite a documentation failure. It's worse. It's a document that was true once. Two variables got it running in February 2024. Three more arrived over the next eighteen months, added by three different people who each updated the code and the deploy config and never opened the README.

I don't blame them. I've done it.

What fixed it was a startup check. The service now fails at boot and prints what's missing in one line. The README still says two. Nobody has read it in a year, because nobody needs to. The app tells you. I think about this a lot. Documentation that drifts gets worse than no documentation, because it teaches wrong things with authority. A hard failure at startup costs nothing and tells the truth every single time.

If I can make the machine say it, I'd rather the machine say it.

I renamed a core concept last month and it cost three weeks. Worth it.

The old name described what the thing did in 2022. The thing changed. The name didn't. So every conversation started with a correction, and every new engineer wrote the wrong mental model in their head and then had to unlearn it.

There were about four hundred references. Code, tests, dashboards, docs, alert names, three runbooks, and a Slack channel called something adjacent.

I did it in passes. New name alongside old name. Then new name in code. Then dashboards and docs. Then the channel, which was the hardest part because nobody wants to be the person who renames a channel.

What I got back was real. Onboarding questions dropped and standups got shorter. Two arguments turned out to be about vocabulary, not design. I'd do it over. Naming is the cheapest architecture decision and the one most often deferred forever. Deferring it doesn't make it free. It makes it compound.

#AI #BuildInPublic #VibeCoding #tech #Refactoring #Developer #Software #Engineering #Coding #Architecture #Naming #Backend #Learning #Craft #TechnicalDebt #Teamwork #ProductManagement #DX #Engineering #Maintenance
---
It broke on Fridays. I looked for four weeks and the answer was boring.

Friday was the day the report ran with a wider date range. Wider range meant a different query plan. The different plan hit a missing index. The missing index made the report take forty minutes instead of forty seconds, and it overlapped with a backup window, and the backup locked something the report held open.

Four conditions. Any three of them together are harmless. It took me three weeks to stop looking for one cause and start looking for a set.

That's the lesson I keep relearning. When a bug needs a specific day, or a specific account, or a specific region, you're not looking at a bug. You're looking at an intersection. And intersections don't reproduce on demand, which is why they survive.

What I do now: I write down every condition I can observe at failure time, including the boring ones. Time of day. Data size. Which node. I stop guessing at the cause until I have four facts written down. I find it in the fourth fact.

I had two hundred passing tests and I still shipped a bug that broke checkout for six hours.

The tests were real. They ran fast. They covered the code. They just didn't cover the thing that broke, because the thing that broke was a default value changing in a config file the tests didn't read.

Coverage told me the lines were executed. It didn't tell me the behavior was right. Those are different questions and I'd been treating them as one.

What I changed is smaller than it sounds. I added a test that reads the config file and asserts the defaults. One test. It's boring. It would have caught that outage.

The bigger change is that I stopped using the word covered. If someone asks me whether a change is safe, covered is a lie dressed as an answer. I say here are the cases I tested, here are the cases I thought about and skipped, here's what I'd be worried about in production. That's longer and less reassuring and it's the truth.

---
A vendor renamed a field on a Tuesday and my pipeline died on Wednesday. No warning. No changelog. Just a null where a string used to be.

The frustrating part is that my code was correct. I'd validated the type. I'd handled the missing case. The missing case was handled by logging a warning and continuing, which meant the pipeline ran happily for sixteen hours while writing nothing useful downstream.

So the failure wasn't the rename. The failure was that I'd made a bad input survivable. And survivable is the worst thing you can make a broken input, because silence looks like health.

I changed the validation to stop the run and page me. That felt aggressive for about a week. Then it caught the next rename in four minutes instead of sixteen hours.

There's a pattern here I've seen in five systems now. Every time I make an error non fatal to keep things running, I trade a short reliable failure for a long quiet one. The quiet ones cost more. I know this and I still catch myself doing it.

I gave an estimate of three weeks and it took seven. I knew it would take seven when I said three.

That's the part that still bothers me. Not the miss. The lie. I did the arithmetic in my head, got seven, and then adjusted it down because three was the number that would get approved.

Nine years of this and I finally understood what that does. It teaches everyone around me that my estimates are fiction, so nobody plans against them, so the dates become political instead of useful. I traded a hard conversation for four months of a soft one.

What I do now is give two numbers. Here's what I can build in three weeks, and here are the three things I'd leave out to hit three weeks. You pick.

That works better than I expected. Most of the time they pick the reduced version. Occasionally they say take seven weeks. Either answer is fine with me, because both of them are true. An estimate isn't a promise you make to get started. It's the map. A wrong map isn't persuasive. It's just a waste of everybody's fuel.

An intern found the bug I'd been circling for two days. She found it in about twenty minutes.

She didn't know the codebase. That turned out to be the whole advantage. I'd been looking at the function that was wrong. She read the calling code and asked why it was called twice on the first request.

I'd never read the caller. I wrote it. I remembered writing it. My memory of the code was standing in for the code, and my memory was from a version that no longer existed.

I've tested this since. When I'm stuck for more than an hour, I hand it to someone with no context and ask them to describe what they see. Six times out of ten they say something I've stopped being able to see.

The trick is I have to ask them to describe, not to solve. Solving sends them into my mental model. Describing keeps them outside it. The other trick is I have to listen instead of explaining why that line is fine. I'm not good at that part yet, but I'm getting better at noticing when I'm doing it.

I ran a nightly job at midnight server time for two years before I understood it was running at the wrong time for most of the world.

Midnight server time is evening in New York and early morning in Asia. So the job ran while one large region was still working and another was starting. Both of them got inconsistent reads for about eleven minutes a day. Nobody reported it because nobody knew what to compare against.

Found it through a support ticket about a report being off by a few rows. The rows were the ones written during the job.

Find the hour on purpose. I moved it to 07:00 in the morning, which is bad for the fewest people, and made the job idempotent so a re-run is free. I could have done that two years earlier. I just never thought about whose midnight it was.

Here's what I take from it. Any scheduled thing runs in somebody's day. The schedule looks neutral in the config file. It isn't. It's a decision about who gets the degraded experience, and somebody should make it on purpose.

A feature flag I turned on for a rollout in 2024 was still on last month. I'd forgotten it existed. So had everyone else.

It was supposed to be temporary. Roll it to ten percent, watch, roll to a hundred, delete the flag. I did the first three. The delete never happened because there was always something more urgent than deleting a flag that was working fine.

Two years of dead branches in the code. Two years of tests that only covered the on path, because the off path was supposed to be gone. Then someone needed the off path during an incident and we discovered it hadn't been executed in eighteen months. It would have failed. We got lucky that we tried it in staging.

Now every flag has a date in its name and an owner in the config. On the date, a scheduled job opens a ticket. Not a reminder. A ticket with a person on it. Somebody has to close it, and closing means deleting the branch.

I don't trust my own memory to clean up. I trust the calendar, because the calendar doesn't have a sprint.

I wrote code last week that I will never see running. That's normal now and it took years to get used to.

The service deploys itself. Tests pass and the metrics look steady. I move to the next thing. I have no memory of the last time a human clicked through one of my features to check it felt right.

This is efficient and it's also a bit blind. I've shipped things that worked fine and were unpleasant to use. The tests can't tell me that. The metrics can't either, because people who hate a flow still complete it, just slower and once.

So I do one thing that isn't scalable. Once, at the start, I open the thing myself and use it like a person. Not the happy path. The confused path. What happens when I click twice, or go back, or paste something with a line break in it.

Every single time I do this I find something. It takes fifteen minutes and it's the highest value fifteen minutes in the whole build. Automation won't replace that. It will just make sure you forget to do it.

I added a database index after six months of a slow page. Query time went from four seconds to nine milliseconds. Four seconds. For six months.

The index was one line. One line, added in four minutes, including the migration and the deploy.

I want to explain why it took six months, because the reason isn't laziness. The page was slow and everyone assumed it was slow because it had a lot of data on it. Slow looked like a property of the data. It felt like weather.

That's what makes performance work hard. The cost is invisible until someone measures it, and measuring it isn't in anyone's sprint. So it gets filed as how things are.

What I do now is small. I log every query over five hundred milliseconds with the query text. No dashboards, no tracing system, just one log line and a weekly look at the file. That's it. It has caught four of these, including one on a different team's service. The thing you don't measure doesn't stay still. It grows into something that looks like a fact.

My logging cost more than the app last month. No exaggeration. The log volume was eighty percent of the bill.

I'd been generous with logs because logs feel free. They're just lines. You write them once and you read them when something breaks. Then a loop runs four million times and each pass writes a friendly message about what it's doing.

The fix was a sample rate, a level cleanup, and one change that mattered most: the loop now logs a summary at the end instead of a line per item. Same information, nine thousand times cheaper.

I got the diagnosis from a billing page, not a profiler. That's the part I want to remember. I spent two weeks thinking about code performance and the real expense was in a system I'd filed as free.

There's a class of cost that hides from engineering because it looks like paperwork. Storage. Egress. Log ingestion. Alerts. Those don't feel like architecture decisions and they're the decisions that get big without anyone watching. If you don't know what your logs cost, go look. It might be a surprise.

I told a stakeholder no last month and I was expecting a fight. There wasn't one.

They asked for a feature. I said I can't do that this quarter and here's what it would displace. That was it. They picked a different thing. Total conversation, four minutes.

I'd been saying yes with a qualifier for years, which is worse than yes. Sure, we can do that, probably next sprint. That's a promise with a smoke screen on it. Everyone hears the sure, nobody records the probably, and then there's a meeting in six weeks where I explain physics to someone who's right to be annoyed.

The technique isn't fancy. It's naming the tradeoff out loud and letting the other person pick which thing dies. Almost nobody asks me to kill the more important thing. They just didn't know there was a queue and I never showed them the queue.

Saying no isn't the hard part. The hard part is having a written list of what's in flight so the no is a fact instead of an opinion. Build the list first. The no gets easy after that.

My build time grew by one second a month for two years and I didn't notice until it hit four minutes.

One second a month is nothing. Nobody files a ticket for it. Each individual addition was justified because the build was already slow, and one more dependency can't be the reason.

Then a small change took four minutes to verify, and I caught myself not running the tests locally. That's the real damage. Not the four minutes. The habit of skipping verification because verification got expensive.

I fixed it in a week. Cached the dependency install, split the suite so the fast half runs first, and deleted a test that spun up a database to check a string format. That one test was thirty seconds. Thirty seconds per run, twenty runs a day, that's ten minutes per person per day.

I notice the ten minutes now. Everybody notices four minutes, because four minutes is long enough to leave your desk. Where's the line. It's wherever attention breaks, and attention breaks early.

My first production incident was a Sunday and I made every mistake available.

I didn't tell anyone for twenty minutes because I thought I could fix it without anyone noticing. I edited config on the box instead of through the deploy. I made three changes at once and lost track of which one mattered. Then I restarted a service that was holding state and made it worse.

Nine years later I can list the rules I broke without checking. Say something right away, even when you look foolish. Change one thing at a time. Never fix on a box you can't explain later. Never restart something you don't understand.

None of that came from a course. It came from that Sunday, and from a senior engineer who joined the call, asked one question, and then stayed quiet and let me work. He could have fixed it in two minutes. He let it take forty so I'd learn it. That stuck.

That's the part I try to pass on. The fastest fix isn't always the best teaching. Sometimes the kindest thing a senior person can do is stand behind you and not take the keyboard.

---
I copied a pattern from another service without understanding why it was shaped that way. Two months later it bit me.

The pattern was a retry with a fixed delay. Simple. It worked in the service I copied it from because that service called an API that recovered in about a second. My service called something that took thirty seconds to recover, so my retries hammered it during the worst window and turned a slowdown into an outage.

The code was identical. The context wasn't. That's the whole trap. A pattern is a solution to a specific problem, and if you don't know which problem, you're just shuffling symbols.

I don't want to say copy nothing. Copying is how people learn and it's how teams stay consistent. But copy the shape and then answer one question before you commit. What condition made this the right shape where it came from.

If I can't answer that, I write the naive version instead. I would rather have boring code I understand than clever code I inherited. Boring code fails in ways I've already seen.

#AI #BuildInPublic #VibeCoding #tech #Developer #Software #Engineering #Coding #DesignPatterns #Backend #Reliability #Learning #Craft #CodeReview #Architecture #Refactoring #Production #Teamwork #Engineering #Microservices

A code review comment changed how I work and it was four words long. It said what is this for.

Not rude. Just direct. I'd written a helper function with a clever name and no description, and the reviewer had to guess. He guessed wrong, which meant the next person would too.

I added a two line comment and realized something. The function existed because I'd needed it once, in a branch that no longer existed, to fix a bug I couldn't reproduce. Nobody would ever know that from the code.

Now I write the why first. If I can't finish the sentence this exists because, the function shouldn't exist. I've deleted maybe fifteen things using that test.

The other half is I ask that question in reviews now, but gently, and I ask it about the whole change rather than a line. What is this for, in your own words. If they can say it right away, the code is fine even when it's ugly. If they can't, the code isn't the problem.

The best fix I shipped this year was turning something off.

A background job recalculated a summary every ten minutes. It had grown. It now recalculated three summaries, then wrote them to a table, then triggered a notification that checked whether anything had changed, and the answer was almost always nothing.

I traced it for an afternoon and the conclusion was that nothing downstream needed the ten minute cadence. The summaries changed about twice a day, because the source data changed about twice a day.

So I turned the cadence down to once an hour and deleted the notification check. Two lines of config. Database load dropped by a third. Pager events on that service went to zero.

It felt like cheating. I'd spent the afternoon finding out that the correct engineering response was to do less. That's a hard instinct to build in a career where everything rewards adding. Adding is visible. Removal looks like nothing happened. But the load is real, and the fastest system is usually the one running the fewest jobs.

#AI #BuildInPublic #VibeCoding #tech #Engineering #Developer #Software #Coding #Performance #Refactoring #Simplicity #Backend #DevOps #Database #Learning #Craft #Production #Architecture #SRE #CostOptimization
---
I keep running into two kinds of documentation, and one of them never gets read.

The first kind explains the code. It sits next to the function and says what the parameters mean. Useful sometimes, and it goes stale the moment someone refactors without reading it.

The second kind explains the situation. Why this exists. What we tried first and why it failed. What will break if you change this. Who to ask. That one gets read all the time, because it answers the question you have at the moment you have it.

I write the second kind now and I'm ruthless about the first. If the function name and the types already say it, a comment repeating it is noise that will rot.

I put the second kind in a file at the top of the directory called decisions. Not a wiki. A file. Wikis drift away from the code. A file gets renamed in the same commit that changes the thing, or it gets noticed in review. Nobody reads a wiki. Everybody reads the file sitting next to the code they're editing.

#AI #BuildInPublic #VibeCoding #tech #Documentation #Developer #Software #Engineering #Coding #Architecture #Craft #Teamwork #Learning #Backend #Onboarding #BestPractices #Refactoring #Engineering #Culture #Platform

I rebuilt a service in one weekend. It took two years to recover from that weekend.

That old service was slow, hard to read, and did four things. I replaced it with three small services and a queue. I shipped it on Sunday night and felt like a genius. I was the one person who understood how the pieces fit together.

By Wednesday two of the three services were doing work nobody asked for. Six months in, a retry storm took down the queue, and the old service would have absorbed it. Two years later we removed the queue and went back to fewer moving parts. The old service was right about more things than I gave it credit for.

The final shape looked a lot like the thing I'd deleted. Its code was cleaner and I understood why it was that shape. That understanding cost twenty four months and I could have bought it with a design doc. Cheap weekend. Expensive two years. I know that now. The version I'd keep is the one where I spend one afternoon reading the old service before deleting a line of it, because the shape was never the problem and the reasons behind the shape were the whole product.

A teammate of mine wrote a service and documented none of it. He wasn't lazy and he wasn't careless.

He held the whole thing in his head. Every decision, every workaround, the reason a retry had a two second delay. Asking him a question got a good answer in ten seconds. Asking the codebase got nothing.

Then he took a month off. We had three incidents that month that he'd have closed in five minutes. None of them were his fault. They were ours, for not writing down what he told us.

I don't think he was wrong to know his work. I think the team was wrong to let that knowledge have one home. We fixed it after he left, which is the expensive way to learn anything. That's the version I'd rather not repeat. His name is still on the author line of forty files. Every file. Every time I open one I wish he'd written one paragraph. He was gone. The knowledge left with him in a way that no repository could hold, and the rest of us spent a month rebuilding what he could have handed over in an afternoon of writing.

A job failed on the twenty ninth of February and it had never failed before, because that date shows up once every four years.

The code was written in 2019 by someone who left in 2021. It assumed every month had at least twenty eight days and every year had three hundred and sixty five. Both assumptions hold until they don't.

I found it at nine in the morning because the report was empty. I fixed it in twenty minutes. I spent another hour wondering what else in the system assumed a calendar that doesn't exist.

There were four more. Two were harmless. One mis-sorted a queue every April. One crashed on the thirty first of a month that has no such day, which is a bug I'd have bet money wasn't possible. I wrote all five down and put the list in the repo. Next February, someone will thank me, or nobody will.

---
I made an outage worse with a fix and it taught me more than any postmortem I've read.

The database was slow. I added a cache in front of a read path because that's the obvious move. The cache filled, memory spiked, and the process started swapping. Now the read path was slow and the process was dying.

I rolled back in four minutes. The rollback took another six to take effect because the cache had to drain. So my four minute fix cost twenty minutes of outage across the worst window of the day.

What I do now is name the mechanism before I touch anything. If I can't finish the sentence this will help because X, then I'm guessing. Guessing during an incident is how one problem becomes two. I keep a note open and write the mechanism down first, and half the time writing it stops my hand. It costs nothing and it has caught three bad fixes of mine. Half the time. That number is the reason I keep doing it. Write it down. The habit that has saved me most is the one where I open a note file, type the mechanism in one sentence, and then read it back to myself before I run anything at all.

Reading code that isn't yours at two in the morning is a specific skill and I got good at it against my will.

You can't hold a mental model of a system you've never seen, so I stopped trying.

I read one path. I follow it from the entry point to the failure and I write down every call in between. That list becomes the map. Writing it down is the trick. In my head the path feels simple and I skip steps. On paper the gap shows up fast. Twice now I've found the bug on the line where my list went blank.

The other thing I learned is to read the tests before the code. Tests say what the author believed. The code says what he was willing to accept. The distance between those two is where bugs live, and it's wider than anyone wants to admit. That one habit cost me nothing and saved me four hours.

We split a large service into seven smaller ones over a year. Six boundaries were right. One cost us more than the other six saved.

We cut along the data model instead of along the work. Two of the pieces shared a table for eighteen months, so every write needed a synchronous call between them. That call became the slowest part of the system and the hardest thing to reason about.

The right boundary was the team. One team owned the whole flow, so splitting it put two teams on the critical path with a network hop in between and no shared understanding of the failure modes.

Merging them back took three weeks and nobody argued. If I did it again I'd draw the boundary where a single team can own a whole request. That rule is less elegant than cutting along the data and it survives contact with a support ticket, which is the test that matters. Elegance doesn't survive a support ticket. Boundaries do. Boring works.

A demo went well because I ran it eleven times the day before and that's the whole secret.

The product worked. That was never the risk. The risk was the network, the seed data, and the one flow slow enough to make everyone uncomfortable. So I ran the whole thing eleven times in the actual room.

Run four showed me a page that took nine seconds. Run seven showed me a chart with the wrong colors. Run nine showed me a flow that needed a second click nobody would make. I fixed each one and each fix was small.

The demo took eight minutes and no one noticed the seams. I don't think that's performance. There's a version of a product that only exists after you've watched it fail nine times, and you can't get there without rehearsing. Nothing about that is clever. It's repetition, and repetition is the one thing I control.

I deleted customer data on a Friday and I want to write down what happened.

A cleanup script had a date filter with the wrong comparison. It should've skipped the last thirty days of records. It kept those and removed everything else. Nine hundred rows across two tables, gone in four seconds.

The recovery was a restore and a replay. The restore took ninety minutes. The replay took six hours because we had to reorder events by hand for the affected accounts. Two customers noticed. Both were kind about it and that's what made it worse. I still think about those nine hundred rows.

What's changed: the script needs two people to run and prints the row count it intends to delete. If that number is over a hundred, it stops and waits for a second person to press a key. Boring. It has fired twice in a year and both times it was right to stop.
