I stopped asking puzzle questions in interviews after a candidate finished one and then told me it proved nothing.

He was right. The puzzle had one path and it rewarded shape recognition. Someone who'd seen that puzzle family got it in a minute. Someone with nine years of production work got it in ten. Neither outcome tells me how they behave when a deploy fails on a Friday.

I replaced it with a small service that has a real bug in it and a real test that passes for the wrong reason. The task is find the bug and explain why the test lied. That's the job. That's most of the job.

The signal got clearer right away. People who talk out loud do better. People who read before typing do better. People who ask what the system is for do best. None of those had room to show up in a logic puzzle.

An intern built a small internal tool two summers ago. It's now the thing four teams depend on and she's on a different team.

She built it to avoid a manual step that took her ten minutes an afternoon. No proposal, no design review, one afternoon and a small script behind a button. It worked because it did the boring part and nothing else.

I watched it hit two hundred runs a day six months later. Eighteen months later it had auth, an audit log, and a page in the onboarding doc. I didn't plan that. She didn't either. That was the whole spec. It grew because the alternative was worse.

The lesson I keep taking from it is that adoption is the one design review that matters. Every approval process we had would have slowed that tool down and none of them would have predicted that it would end up carrying four teams. The best thing I did that summer was get out of her way.

I own a script that runs once a year and it's the scariest code I maintain.

It builds an annual statement. It runs in January. It takes four minutes. If it breaks, nobody knows for a week, and the people it breaks for are customers who plan their year around the output.

The problem with code that runs once a year is that nobody has muscle memory. The runbook goes stale, credentials rotate, and the person who knew it left. This year the database user had expired. I found out from an email.

So I practice. In December I run the whole thing against a copy, with a fake date, and I write down every step I had to guess. That practice run costs me an hour and it has saved the January run twice. Practicing something once a year is the cheapest insurance I buy. One hour, once a year, for the code that scares me most. That's a trade I'd make every time.

We built a queue to slow down a bad process. Six years later the queue was the process.

It started as a holding area. Work arrived faster than it could be handled, so we queued it and reviewed it in order. Sensible. The review step grew a priority flag. Flags grew rules, and the rules grew a team.

Last year someone asked why work couldn't start right away and nobody could answer. The queue had become the reason the work took three weeks, not a fix for it.

We kept the queue and removed the priority rules. Throughput doubled in a month. I think about that a lot. A workaround that lasts long enough becomes architecture, and architecture is much harder to question because it looks like a decision somebody made on purpose. I keep a list of our workarounds now. Nine of them. Two are older than the team that owns them.

I said the same sentence in standup for three weeks and nobody asked what it meant, including me.

The sentence was still working on the migration. It was true every day. It was also useless. In week two I should have said the index build won't finish before Friday. In week three I should have said I need help.

What stopped it was a manager who asked one question. What would make this impossible by Friday. I answered in a minute, and the answer included two things nobody on the call knew about.

A password rotation broke four services last month and three of them failed without an error.

The rotation itself worked. Its new value landed in the secret store on time. The problem was three services cached the old value at startup and never refreshed. Two of them kept retrying and logged every failure, which is how we found it. A third swallowed the error and returned empty results.

That third one had been returning empty results for eleven hours. No alert, no change in the error rate, because an empty list is a valid response. A customer noticed before our monitoring did. I fixed that gap first. I found the silent one because a customer emailed.

What I changed is small. Every service now reports the age of its credentials, and a dashboard page turns red at ninety days. That's it. It doesn't prevent a bad rotation. It means the next one gives us a warning instead of a support ticket.

It took me two years to learn to read a stack trace the right way and the trick is smaller than it sounds.

I used to read the top line, panic, and start guessing. The top line is where the program noticed. It's often four layers away from the line that caused it.

Now I read the bottom of my own code first. The last frame that belongs to us is the one that decided something wrong. Everything above that line is machinery doing what it was told.

I also read the whole trace before touching the keyboard. Ten seconds of reading beats ten minutes of guessing, every single time. I know that sounds like advice from a poster. It also happens to be the one change that cut my debugging time in half, and I found it because I stopped trusting my first guess. Read first. Type later. Guessing is fast. Being right is faster.

I spent four days writing a test for a feature that took one day to build, and the test was the better investment.

That feature merges a list of records and removes duplicates. Twelve lines. I needed fixtures for every shape a duplicate can take: same id, same name and different id, same everything but a trailing space, and two records that look identical in every field.

Writing those fixtures made me change the code three times. One case needed normalization for a trailing space. Another case needed a rule about which record wins, and the product owner had never thought about it. That conversation was the feature.

It has caught two regressions since. It also serves as the spec, because it's the one place the tie breaking rule is written down. Four days for twelve lines of code sounds ridiculous until you count the argument it prevented and the two bugs it stopped. Four days well spent.

I estimate migrations backward now and my numbers got better overnight.

I start at the end. Cleanup done, old code deleted, dashboards updated. Then I list everything that has to happen before that. Then I put a date on each item based on the last time I did something similar, not on how long it feels like it should take.

The list is longer than anyone wants. My last one had eleven items and three of them were conversations with other teams. Those three took sixty percent of the calendar time.

What I hand over is the list, not a number. People trust a list because they can argue with it. When they cut scope, they cut a real item, and I can say what that item was protecting. A bare number invites negotiation about the number. A list invites a decision about the work. Nobody wants a list. Nobody argues with one either. That's the difference, and it's the reason I stopped handing over a number.

A vendor I depended on got acquired and the product disappeared in fourteen months.

The acquisition news was fine. Six months later nothing had changed. Then the pricing page changed, then the docs stopped updating, then support replies went from a day to a week. None of those felt like a signal at the time. Together they were a countdown.

We migrated in a rush at the end, which is the worst way to migrate. Nine weeks of work that could have been twelve months of calm if we'd started when the pricing page moved.

Building everything yourself isn't the lesson. That's not realistic and it isn't better. What matters is watching the health signals of anything on your critical path and treating a support response time doubling as an event. I keep a list now. Nine vendors, four numbers each, twenty minutes a quarter. I moved that check into the quarterly planning doc so nobody has to remember it. Cheap, boring, and it has already flagged two vendors.

I refactored a module in the wrong order and paid for it three times over.

I started with the messy part because that's where the pain was. I rewrote it, cleaned the interfaces, and shipped. Then I discovered the messy part was messy because two callers had different requirements that nobody had written down.

So I had a clean module with a compromise hidden inside it. Worse than before. The compromise now sat behind a nice interface, which made it harder to see and easier to keep.

The right order is to read every caller first and write down what each one needs. That took me half a day when I finally did it. The refactor then took a day and the compromise turned into two small functions with honest names. Pain is a signal about history. If you clean the code before you read the history, you throw away the notes. Read first. Refactor second. I learned that one the slow way.

A README saved my weekend last month and I didn't write it. Someone on my team did, two years ago.

We had a production problem at six on a Friday. The service was one I'd never touched. I opened the repository with the specific dread of someone about to learn a system the hard way.

The README had four paragraphs. What the service does. Where the data comes from. What to check first when it looks wrong. Who to ask. The third paragraph named the exact symptom I was seeing and the second paragraph named the cause.

I fixed it in thirty five minutes. Then I updated one line in that README and added my name to the fourth paragraph. Four paragraphs, written once, on a Friday two years before, paid for itself and then some. I've started writing them before the code now. It's a small habit that costs nothing and it has paid for itself over and over.

I worked with someone who never said no and he was the most expensive teammate I've had.

Every request got a yes. Small requests, huge requests, things he had no business taking on. He'd start them all and finish most of them late. Nobody complained because he was pleasant and fast and the yes always arrived with a smile.

What nobody saw was the cost. Three projects slipped a quarter because he'd taken on work that belonged to another team. Two people stopped proposing ideas, because his yes meant their project would sit behind his other yesses.

I don't think he was dishonest. I think saying no felt rude and saying yes felt generous, and he never saw the bill. The bill is always paid by the queue, and the queue doesn't complain. Now I ask people what they're dropping when they say yes. It's a fair question with an unfair history. Yes is easy. No is work.

I spent a week optimizing a function that was responsible for one percent of the runtime.

It looked slow. It had a loop inside a loop and a comment from 2018 explaining a workaround. That was all the evidence I needed. I rewrote it, made it four times faster, and shipped it with a chart.

The page got slower. I'd added caching that made the first call worse and the memory footprint bigger. The real cost was a network round trip inside a serial loop three files away, which I'd never opened.

A profiler found it in ninety seconds. I'd been avoiding the profiler because I assumed I knew. That assumption cost a week and produced a chart nobody asked for. I have a rule now. No optimization without a measurement, and the measurement goes in the pull request description so the next person can see the reasoning. Ninety seconds with a profiler. That's what I skipped for a week.

---
The most important comment I've written is one line and it explains a bug I couldn't reproduce.

This code sorts by a timestamp and then by an internal id. That second sort looks pointless, and every reviewer has asked about it. It's there because two records can arrive with the same timestamp, and without a tiebreaker the order changes between runs and a customer sees their history reshuffle.

Finding it took two weeks, because it happened once on a day when a batch job wrote two rows in the same second. Four sentences in the file explain it. It says why, not what.

I write comments like that now and I delete comments that repeat the code. My test is simple. Nothing more. If a reviewer might suggest deleting the line, the line needs a reason next to it. If they'd never suggest deleting it, the comment is noise. I keep to that rule and my files stay readable. That's the whole rule.

I keep a work journal and it has changed my performance reviews more than any project I shipped.

One file, one line per day. What I did, what broke, what I decided. Forty words on a busy day and ten on a quiet one. It takes a minute and I never skip it.

The value isn't the record. It's the pattern. Three entries in a row mentioning the same flaky test. Two weeks of entries where every day starts with a question from the same team, which means a document needs writing. The journal shows me things a calendar can't.

The other value shows up in December. When someone asks what I did this year, I don't guess. I read. Nobody remembers a year of work, and the people who do well in reviews aren't better engineers. They just have the facts and I used to show up with a feeling.

Some of the code I maintain is older than the company I work for and its authors are gone.

That code has no repository history before a certain date because the original version control didn't survive. No tickets, no design docs, no names. What survives is the behavior. It does a thing every night and the business depends on the thing.

I used to find that frustrating. Now I think it's the honest state of most software. Your careful documentation has a half life. Your tests and your behavior are the parts that persist, and everything else is a letter to a person who may never exist.

I write two things now and skip the rest. A test that encodes the rule. A short file next to the code that says why the rule exists. No architecture diagrams, no wiki page, because I've watched those rot inside two years while the tests kept telling the truth. Build the thing that ages well.

I wrote an on-call handoff note for two years and it's the highest return document I have ever produced.

One page. What changed this week. What's currently broken. What to watch. Who to wake up and why. Three minutes to write at the end of a shift, and it replaced a thirty minute call every Monday morning.

I didn't build it because anyone asked. I built it because I was tired of starting every shift by opening four dashboards and guessing which numbers mattered that week.

Here's what surprised me. Notes became a history. Nine months in, I could point at a bad decision and read the week it got made, which is worth more than the handoff itself. Now I write them even on quiet weeks. The quiet ones reset the baseline. It took me a year to believe that writing the note was part of the work and not a favor to the next person. I count it as infrastructure now, the same way I count tests. Three minutes a shift is the cheapest reliability program I have ever run.

A feature of ours could not be turned off and that was a design choice nobody remembered making.

The flag existed. It was there in the config file, spelled the way flags are supposed to be spelled. It just didn't do anything, because the off path had been refactored away two years earlier and the tests only ever ran the on path.

So we had a control that looked like a control and behaved like decoration. Nine people believed they could end an incident by flipping it. Nobody tested that belief until an incident needed one.

I found out at the worst moment. The off path returned a null instead of an empty list, and three callers assumed a list.

Now every flag gets a test that exercises both paths, and I delete flags that outlive their rollout window. A control you cannot test is worse than no control at all. It sells certainty you don't have. I test both paths now. I keep the test short and I keep it next to the flag.

---
I chased a memory leak for two days with one tool and found it in twenty minutes with another.

The first tool showed me the process growing. Helpful, and useless. It told me something was leaking without telling me which thing, so I spent two days reading code and forming theories.

Then I took a heap snapshot at noon, another at four, and diffed them. The top of the list named the object. It was a cache I'd added for a quick win, with a key that included a timestamp, so nothing ever got reused and nothing ever got evicted.

Two lines of code. Four thousand stale entries an hour.

I keep relearning this. Measurement beats reasoning when the system is bigger than your head. My theories were reasonable and every one of them was wrong, and I could have skipped all of them with one command I already knew about.

I stopped doing big releases and my incident count dropped by half without a single new test.

Same code, same review process, same team. The only change was shipping small pieces more often, and the difference showed up in the size of the failures. A bad release used to mean three broken things and an hour of guessing which one caused what.

Now a bad release is one thing. It's usually obvious inside two minutes and reversible in five.

The other gain nobody talks about is confidence. When a deploy happens forty times a month, nobody holds their breath. It becomes a Tuesday event. People stop saving up work for a launch window, and saving up work is how you build a huge risky change without noticing.

Small and often isn't a slogan. It's a way of capping the blast radius of your own mistakes, and I make plenty. I would take that trade again. Fewer things breaking at once is worth more than fewer deploys, and I've watched both.

I read logs for an hour last month and found a bug that four months of tickets had missed.

Every ticket was about slow pages. Each one was closed as environmental. Different users, different days, no pattern, so they got filed into the noise drawer.

My logs had a pattern. Same query, same table, always between nine and eleven in the morning. That window is when a reporting job runs and holds a lock the query needed.

Twelve hundred lines. I read them with a filter for one table name and a note file open, and the shape showed up in about ten minutes. The remaining fifty minutes went to confirming it.

I'm not saying read your logs. I'm saying the tickets were right and the classification was wrong. Slow page is a symptom. The cause lives in a different window than the one the reporter can see. I open the raw file before I open the tracker now, and it has paid off twice more since.

A schema change took three deploys and I planned for one. The plan wasn't wrong, it was short.

Deploy one added the new column and wrote to both. Deploy two started reading from the new column. Deploy three stopped writing to the old one. Each step waited a day so I could watch.

What I missed was the backfill. Twenty million rows needed copying, and the copy had to run in batches without holding a transaction open long enough to block writes. That took its own deploy and its own afternoon of tuning, and it changed the shape of the whole week.

I write the sequence now instead of the change. Expand, backfill, dual write, cut over, contract. Five lines on a note, each one its own deploy, each one safe to stop at. Migration plans fail because they answer what changes. What matters is the order. I've stopped calling that detail, because the order is the design.

A colleague asked me one question about a design and I rebuilt the whole thing.

The design was a service that polled another service every minute for changes. Clean, simple, twelve lines of loop. I was pleased with it and I wanted a second opinion.

He asked what happens when the poll runs during their deploy. I didn't have an answer. Their deploys take ninety seconds and return errors during that window, and my loop treated an error as a change in state.

So a deploy on their side looked like an event on mine. That's a bug factory, and it would have shown up as a handful of mystery records every other week for a year before anyone connected it.

He wasn't being clever. He'd just spent time on that boundary. His question was cheap and my answer cost a week, and I've asked it ever since about other people's boundaries.

My worst week of estimates cost me a client and taught me more than the quarter I got right.

I said two weeks. It took nine. The two weeks was a guess built from how long the pieces felt, and I never listed the pieces. I looked at the shape of the work and said two weeks because that's what a task like that usually takes.

It usually takes two weeks when nothing is unknown. Four things were unknown. I hadn't written them down, so I hadn't counted them.

What I do now is put the unknowns in the estimate as line items with names. Unknown: how their auth layer issues tokens. Unknown: whether the legacy data has duplicates. Naming them turns ten days of fog into two days of reading, and the reading is cheap.

Nobody minds a longer estimate with reasons. They mind a shorter one with excuses.

I keep a paper notebook for work and it does one thing software never managed.

It slows the question down. When I write a problem by hand, I have to decide what the problem actually is before I can put it on the page. On a screen I can type a search and get a thousand answers to a question I never clarified.

The notebook is also where half-formed things live. An idea that isn't ready gets a line and a date, and I read the lines once a week. Three of my better projects started as a scrawl about something that annoyed me.

I tried this on a tablet and it didn't work. The friction was the feature. The lag between thought and ink forced me to compress, and compressing is where I find out whether I understand something.

Cheap notebooks. One pen. No sync. I'd give up three tools before this one.

The bug was in my monitoring, which is the worst place to find a bug.

An alert said our checkout error rate was zero for six hours. We celebrated for about ten minutes, then someone asked why the chart looked flat instead of low. Flat is not a shape that real traffic produces.

The metric was broken. A rename had landed in the service and the collector still listened for the old name, so it reported zero, and zero reads as healthy. We had real errors during that window and nobody saw them.

The fix is a heartbeat. Every metric now reports a count of events seen in the last minute, and a separate alert fires when a metric with traffic stops reporting. Silence now has a sound.

Monitoring fails in the direction of calm. That's why it fooled us, and that's why the boring heartbeat check is the most important line of code in the whole system. I keep a list of every metric that should be moving and I read that list once a month.

I deleted tests last month and the suite got stronger, which is a sentence I didn't expect to type.

Two hundred tests. Fourteen of them asserted that a function returned a value, and never checked the value. Four asserted a mock was called, which is a test of a mock. Nine had been skipped for a year with a comment explaining a dependency that had since been removed.

I read every one before deleting. That took an afternoon and it was the valuable part. Reading them showed me what the suite actually proved, which was less than the green checkmark implied.

The suite runs ninety seconds faster now. More important, I trust it more, and trust is the whole point.

A test that cannot fail is decoration. I've deleted my share and it always felt like losing something. Coverage numbers count decoration as progress, and that's how you end up with a thousand green tests and an outage nobody predicted. Most of them can't fail. I read each one with a pen in my hand and I ask whether this assertion could fail in a way that tells me something new.

A flaky test was reporting a real race condition and we asked it to stop being flaky.

It failed about one run in forty. Not enough to block work, enough to annoy everyone. We added a retry, then a longer timeout, then a label so the pipeline wouldn't count it. Four months of papering over a signal.

Then a customer hit the same race in production. Two writes landing in the same millisecond, and the loser overwrote the winner. The test had been pointing at it the whole time, in the least helpful way possible.

The test is deterministic now. It forces an interleaving instead of hoping for one.

There's a lesson here about flaky tests that I keep paying for. A test that fails sometimes is telling you the system is uncertain. Retrying the test doesn't remove the uncertainty, it just moves the discovery to a person on a Friday night, alone, with a phone. I read the failure instead. Nine times out of ten it names the exact line, and the ninth time I learn something about how the system really behaves under load.

I shipped to production on a Friday and spent the weekend watching a dashboard. Not an outage. Just anxiety.

The change was small. A default value. It went out at four, and by six I'd checked the error rate nine times and read the deploy log twice.

The work was fine. What I did wrong was choose a window where I couldn't get help. If it had broken, I would have been alone with a laptop on a Saturday, fixing something nobody was around to explain.

I don't have a rule against Friday deploys. I have a rule about who is awake. If the change needs a person to interpret a graph, it goes out on a Tuesday with two people watching. If it's a text change, ship it whenever. The difference is whether the deploy needs a human to decide something.

That's the actual question, and it has nothing to do with the day of the week. Who is awake. Two words that have talked me out of four Friday deploys and cost me nothing.

I worked with a reviewer who found something in every one of my changes and it made me faster.

Not because she was harsh. Because she was specific. She'd point at a line and say this runs once per row and the loop above it also runs once per row. Two facts, no opinion, and the fix was mine to choose.

That's different from a review that says I don't like this. Dislike gives me nothing to do except argue.

The other thing she did was rank her comments. One blocker, three suggestions, one question. I learned to read the ranking first, which meant I never spent an hour on something she was curious about while a real problem waited.

I ask for ranked comments now when I review, and I rank my own. It takes ten seconds and it changes a list of complaints into a work plan. She left the company two years ago and I still follow her format. Rank the list. One blocker.

I copied production data into a test environment and spent a week dealing with the consequences.

It was supposed to be a snapshot for debugging. It had real names, real emails, and real payment identifiers, sitting in a database with a default password and no firewall rule I had checked. Nothing happened. That's luck, not safety.

That debug value was real. My problem was that I'd created a copy of the most sensitive data we own, in the least controlled place we have, and nobody knew it existed.

Now the copies get scrubbed on the way in. Names become placeholders, emails become addresses at a domain we own, and payment ids get replaced with a pattern that fails any real validation. The debug value survives. The liability doesn't.

The rule I follow is simple. If I wouldn't put it in a spreadsheet and email it to a stranger, it doesn't belong in a test database. I check before I copy now, and I ask who else can reach that database.

I write the changelog before the code now and it has saved me three features.

The changelog is one sentence a user would understand. Added the ability to export a filtered list. Fixed an error when a name has an apostrophe. Small, plain, and honest about what changed from outside.

Writing that sentence first forces one question. Who benefits and how would they notice. Twice now the sentence turned out to be nothing. Once it turned out to be a bug fix, not a feature, which changed the whole approach.

The other effect is on scope. If I can't describe the change in one sentence, it's two changes wearing one hat. Splitting them in review costs three times as much.

I keep the sentence in the pull request description. It gets read far more than my architecture notes, because it's the part written for a person rather than for a process. One sentence. If I can't write it, I split the work before anyone sees it, which is cheaper than splitting it after three days of argument about scope.

I spent a day tuning a query that was fast. My slow part was the client reading the results.

The query returned in forty milliseconds. Six seconds showed up on the page. I assumed the database, because that's where the pain tends to live, and I rewrote the query three times for no gain.

The client fetched two hundred rows and then did one lookup per row against another service. Two hundred round trips, each about twenty milliseconds. That's the six seconds, sitting in a loop I'd never opened because I was sure the problem was on the other side.

The fix was one batched call. Same data, one request, six seconds down to three hundred milliseconds.

What I keep from that day is a question to ask before profiling anything. Which side of the wire is slow. It takes one minute to answer and it points the whole investigation in the right direction. My assumption cost a day.

I pinned a library for four years and upgrading it took an afternoon. The fear took longer.

The pin started as a temporary measure during a release week. A minor version changed a default and broke a page, so I locked it and wrote a ticket. The ticket sat for four years because nothing forced it.

Four years of security patches missed. Four years of a workaround in our code that compensated for behavior the library fixed in the next version.

When I finally did it, the upgrade was boring. Two breaking changes, one small wrapper deleted, twenty minutes of test runs. I'd built a monster in my head over four years of not looking.

Stale dependencies are cheap to ignore and expensive to think about. Every week I don't look, the story in my head gets bigger. Now I upgrade monthly, in a window, with tests, and the fear never gets a chance to grow. One afternoon. That's all it was. The story I told myself for four years was longer than the work, and I have not trusted that story since.

I was wrong in a design review and said so out loud, and it changed how the team worked with me.

I'd argued for a service boundary for about ten minutes. Then a colleague showed me a query pattern I hadn't considered, and my boundary would have turned one read into four.

So I said I was wrong, here's the thing I didn't see, and I changed my position in front of eight people.

Nobody remembers the argument. Everybody remembers the correction. Two people started doing the same thing in later meetings, and the meetings got shorter because the posturing got cheaper.

The habit is hard because it feels like losing. It isn't. I lost nothing in that room. I gained a reputation for changing my mind when the facts changed, which is the kind of mind that's useful on a team. I'd rather be corrected on Tuesday than be right on Friday.

We replaced a weekly meeting with a document and got two hours back, then noticed something better.

The document was a one page summary written before the meeting would have happened. Numbers, decisions needed, blockers. People read it and commented in the doc. Anything unresolved became a five minute call with the two people who cared.

The hours are the easy part. What I didn't expect was the archive. Nine months of docs meant every decision had a short written reason attached to it, and onboarding a new person on that project went from three weeks of questions to a morning of reading.

The meeting wasn't useless. It was doing two jobs, deciding and remembering, and it was bad at remembering.

Now I ask every recurring meeting which of those two jobs it does. If it's remembering, it becomes a document. If it's deciding, it stays, and it gets shorter because the reading happened first. I keep every one of those documents in a folder with the date on it, and I read them before I argue about a decision that got made once already.

A bug came back every eight months for two years and we fixed it four times.

Each fix was real. Each one addressed the symptom we could see, shipped, and got closed. Nobody connected the four because they landed in different services and different years, and the people who fixed them had all moved on or away.

What connected them was a date. All four happened within a week of the pricing job that runs each quarter. That job wrote a value the other four services read, and it wrote it in a shape they each handled in their own way.

I fixed it in a day once we saw the pattern. Two years of symptoms, one afternoon of cause, and the cost of not connecting them was four interrupted weekends.

I keep a searchable list of incidents now with one line each. Same words, no blame. The pattern shows up when the records share a place, and email doesn't share anything. One line each. That's the whole system.

I onboarded a new hire in week one by giving her a small real bug instead of a tour.

Not a training exercise. A real bug, small, with a real customer behind it. The tour could wait.

She had it fixed in two days. More importantly, she touched the deploy pipeline, the test suite, the review process, and the logging on the way. Four things she'd have heard about in a week of meetings, she learned by needing them.

The other effect was social. She had something to show, which gave everyone a reason to talk to her about the system rather than about onboarding. By Friday she was answering questions from people who'd been here a year.

I do this with every new person now. The first task is real work and it's small enough to finish in a week. Documentation gets written when someone needs it, not before. Need is the best teacher I've found.

I kept a bug journal for a year and the most useful entry was the shortest one.

One line per bug. What I saw, what it was, how long it took. Sixty entries, no analysis, no tags.

Reading them at the end of the year showed me three things. Half my bugs were integration boundaries. A third were configuration. And the ones that took longest had the flattest descriptions, which means I was confused before I started.

That last one changed my behavior. Now when I open a bug I write the symptom in one sentence first, before reading any code. If I can't write the sentence, I'm not ready to debug and I go find someone to describe it to.

A year of one line entries cost me maybe two hours total. It told me where my bugs come from, which is something no tool has ever told me about my own work. I'd rather know that than fix the next one faster.

I tell junior engineers three things and I've stopped adding to the list.

First, read the whole error before typing. It sounds insulting. It saves more hours than any technique I know, and I still catch myself skipping it.

Second, if you're stuck for an hour, say so out loud. Not to get an answer. To find out whether you're solving the right problem, which you cannot judge from inside your own head.

Third, write the reason next to anything surprising. The next person is you in eight months, and they'll have your memory of the code plus nothing else.

That's the list. Everything else I know is a longer version of one of those three, and long lists get remembered as nothing. I add a fourth when someone's work shows me a gap I've seen three times. So far that has happened once, about backups, and it stuck. That list has four items in it now, and I hope it stays short for a long time.

A clock drifted by nine seconds on one machine and it broke the payment flow for two hours.

Nothing else was wrong. The code hadn't changed. The database was healthy. One host had drifted because the time service it talked to had been blocked by a firewall change three weeks earlier, and nothing noticed.

Payment callbacks arrived with a timestamp that failed a freshness check. Nine seconds became eleven minutes because a retry loop added delay, and the check treats anything older than five minutes as a replay attack. So the system did exactly what I asked of it, using a number I never audited.

I spent two hours on it because every dashboard agreed. Hosts up, disk fine, error rate flat. Drift has no dashboard unless you build one.

I built one the next day. It reads the offset of every host and alerts past fifty milliseconds. Boring. It caught a second drifter within a month, which tells me the first one was never rare.

I read every line of a diff before I comment on it and that habit has made me a slower reviewer in a good way.

It used to take me four minutes to review a change. I'd read the description, skim the diff, and leave three comments, two of which were about style and one of which was about a line that no longer existed in the file.

Now it takes twelve minutes. I read up, then down, then I read the test changes last, because tests tell me what the author believes the change does.

The comments got shorter. One blocker, sometimes two. Nothing else. My approval rate went up, which surprised me until I understood why. Most changes are fine and I'd been treating that as a problem to solve.

Reading the whole thing costs eight extra minutes. Guessing costs somebody an hour of thread and a rewritten function, and it costs me the credibility that makes my other comments land.

A service had no owner for eleven months and nobody noticed until it needed a decision.

It ran fine the whole time. That's the trap. Unowned services don't fail, they accumulate. Everything about it was someone's old work in a folder that a tool still knew about.

The decision was small. Do we keep the nightly export or retire it. Three teams had opinions and none of them had the authority, so the request bounced for six weeks and landed on me because I asked enough questions to look like the person in charge.

I took it and spent an hour reading the code, the logs, and the two consumers. The answer was retire it, and two of the three teams agreed once they saw the list of consumers.

The lesson that stuck is that ownership is a name, not a folder. I asked for one name per service after that, in a file, next to the repository link. Twelve lines of bookkeeping. It has resolved three arguments since.

A bug report from support turned into a product decision and I'm glad I read it instead of closing it.

The report said a customer couldn't undo an action after a certain point. My first read was that the undo window was working as designed. Then I checked the numbers and found that the window closed after one hour, and that half the affected actions happened on a Friday afternoon.

So the feature was fine and the window was a guess somebody made in 2021 to protect the database. That guess had never been revisited, and by now it shaped how customers used the product.

We changed the window to twelve hours and added a guard that blocks the expensive case. Database load didn't move.

Support tickets are a reading list, not a queue. Half of them are noise and the other half are the place where the product meets how people use it. I was wrong about that. Closing them fast feels productive and hides the second half. I read them all now. All of them. That's the job.

Two databases held the same list and one of them was wrong, which is a sentence that took a year to prove.

Both were written to by different services. Both were read from by different pages. Nobody compared them because each team trusted its own copy and the numbers were close enough to look fine.

Then a customer exported a list and found rows from 2022 sitting in it. The export read the stale copy. I spent a week building a reconciler that compares counts per day and per region, and it found nine differences, three of which had been feeding a dashboard for eighteen months.

I picked one writer and made the other a read model with a lag we could measure. That took a month and the argument took two.

Two sources of truth is a phrase people say like it's an obvious sin. It never arrives as a sin. It arrives one small write at a time, each one reasonable on its own.

A production script ran from my laptop for four months and I only understood the risk when the laptop died.

It was a cron job that pulled a report, cleaned it, and pushed it into a table. I wrote it in an afternoon and ran it from my machine because that was the fastest path and the task was temporary.

Temporary lasted until a quarterly review depended on the output. When the battery died mid-run, nobody knew the job existed, so nobody restarted it. It showed up three weeks later as a missing line in a report. Half a day to fix. A week to find. It now runs in the scheduler with retries, a lock, and an alert that fires when it doesn't finish.

My rule now is about ownership of place. Nobody owned it. That was the whole problem. The part that should have stopped me is that a job three departments rely on had one copy of itself, living on a laptop that spends half its life in a bag. I moved it into the scheduler the following Monday and it has run without me since. If a job matters to anyone besides me, it runs somewhere I don't have to be awake for. My laptop is a workbench and workbenches aren't infrastructure.

I wrote tests to a deadline and the deadline is the only reason the tests existed at all.

We had a release on Thursday. On Wednesday at four I had four hours and a checklist. So I wrote eleven tests that covered the paths people would hit first, ran them, fixed two real bugs, and shipped.

Three of those tests are still in the suite two years later. Two have caught regressions. One has needed an update every time the format changed, which is its own kind of useful signal.

The other eight are gone. I deleted them within a month because they asserted nothing.

What I take from this: a rushed test is still better than no test, as long as you know which kind you wrote. Mine were a smoke screen around the risky edges. Calling them coverage would have been a lie, and the lie is what gets you in trouble, not the rush. That's the trade.

I refactored a module and nobody noticed, which is the highest praise a refactor can get.

Six weeks of small commits. Renamed four things, split two functions, moved a group of helpers behind a file that matched its purpose instead of its first author. Behavior identical from the outside.

The reason nobody noticed is that I never let the refactor change behavior. Every commit kept the tests green, and every commit was reversible.

The reward was quiet. Two weeks later a bug report came in and the fix took twenty minutes, because the file it lived in was readable and the name of the function told me where to look. That's the payoff. Not a faster system. A cheaper Tuesday.

Refactors are easy to sell as cleaning and hard to sell as insurance. I stopped selling them. I do them in small pieces during normal work, and I let the next bug fix make the case for me.

#AI #BuildInPublic #VibeCoding #tech #Refactoring #Developer #Software #Engineering #Coding #Backend #TechnicalDebt #Architecture #Tech #Learning #Craft #CodeReview #BestPractices #Maintenance #Teamwork #Engineering
---
An idempotency key saved us from double charging about forty customers and that number haunts me.

Our payment provider retries on a timeout. Our handler wrote a charge and then updated a record. When the update was slow, the provider timed out and retried with the same request, and we created a second charge because nothing tied the two requests together.

One column and one check fixed it. Our client sends a key. Our handler stores it with the result. A repeat request returns the stored result instead of charging again.

I keep coming back to the timing. I let this run for three weeks and it produced forty duplicate charges. The spreadsheet from the finance team is what surfaced it, not our monitoring, because from the software side both charges were successful.

Retry is a promise the network makes and the ledger keeps. Any handler that can be retried needs to be safe to run twice, and the safety belongs in the handler. It's the handler's job, not a hope about the client.

#AI #BuildInPublic #VibeCoding #tech #Payments #Developer #Software #Engineering #Coding #Backend #Reliability #Idempotency #Tech #Learning #Craft #Production #Distributed #Fintech #Databases #Engineering
---
I version my endpoints now because a mobile app taught me that a client outlives your assumptions.

We shipped a field rename as a cleanup. New name, same data. Two internal callers updated the same day and everything looked done.

Then a support ticket arrived from a user on an old version of the app. They hadn't updated in four months, which is their right, and their app called the old field name and got nothing.

The rename was correct and the rollout was wrong. A shipped client is a contract with a version you no longer control and can't patch.

Now the rule is additive first. Add the new field, keep the old one, log who still reads the old one, and delete it when the logs go quiet for a month. One extra field for a while is cheap. An angry user on an old build isn't, and neither is the emergency release that follows. That's the rule now. Add first, delete later.

#AI #BuildInPublic #VibeCoding #tech #API #Developer #Software #Engineering #Coding #Backend #Versioning #MobileApps #Tech #Learning #Craft #BestPractices #Distributed #Production #Deprecation #Architecture

A queue filled up at four in the morning and the only reason we caught it was a person who couldn't sleep.

The consumer had been paused by a deploy. Nothing was draining, so the queue grew until it hit a limit, and then the producer started rejecting writes. Three hours of rejects before anyone looked.

Every dashboard said green. The queue depth chart had a line on it and the line was rising, but the alert was set on a threshold we picked in a meeting two years earlier and never revisited.

We caught it because a support person was awake and saw a customer complaint. That's not a detection system. That's luck with a personality.

I changed the alert to fire on the rate of change, not the level. A queue that grew by ten thousand messages in ten minutes is a problem no matter where it sits. Level tells you where you stand. Rate tells you where you're going. I read the graph before the threshold now.

#AI #BuildInPublic #VibeCoding #tech #DevOps #Queues #Developer #Software #Engineering #Coding #Backend #Observability #Tech #SRE #Learning #Craft #Reliability #Production #Alerting #Incidents

I became the person who asks who owns this and it made me less popular for about two months.

Every incident had the same shape. We would find the broken thing, then spend twenty minutes figuring out who could approve a change, then fix it. The finding took five minutes and the finding of a person took twenty.

Asking the question out loud made the gap visible. Some people heard it as a complaint about their team. I get that. It sounded like a complaint for a while.

Then we wrote the names down. One name per service, in a file, with a backup name. The next incident took eleven minutes instead of forty.

The thing about org problems is that they look like personal friction. Naming the missing thing feels rude until the missing thing exists, and then everyone forgets it was ever missing. I'd rather be awkward in a meeting than be the bottleneck at two in the morning. It took a year. Worth it.

#AI #BuildInPublic #VibeCoding #tech #Engineering #Developer #Software #Coding #Teamwork #Ownership #Backend #Tech #Learning #Craft #Culture #Incidents #Leadership #OnCall #Communication #Platform
---
A report was off by one day for users in a certain region and the cause was a timezone offset in a filter. The filter took a date and compared it to a timestamp. The date arrived as a calendar day and the timestamp lived in a different zone, so the boundary landed eight hours away from where anyone expected.

Nobody noticed for a year because most users were in one zone and the boundary didn't matter to them.

Two rules came out of it. One zone. One name. Store timestamps in one zone, always. Convert at the edge, where the person is, and never in a filter buried three functions deep.

My second rule is about naming. I treat any variable called date that holds a timestamp as a bug waiting for a boundary condition. I name both now. We renamed the field to include the unit and the zone, which is ugly and has prevented two repeats.

Dates are the classic example of a thing that looks simple and hides a decision. Name the decision and the bug has nowhere to live.

#AI #BuildInPublic #VibeCoding #tech #Developer #Software #Engineering #Coding #Backend #TimeZones #Databases #Tech #Learning #Craft #DataEngineering #BestPractices #Production #Debugging #Dates #Naming
---
I put a comment above every regex I write and it has paid for itself four times.

A regex is a note to a machine written in a language no human reads at speed. Mine from two years ago look like line noise, and I wrote them.

It says what the pattern accepts and what it rejects, with one example each. Four lines. Thirty seconds. It takes thirty seconds and it saves the next person the twenty minutes I spent yesterday trying to decode a pattern somebody else wrote.

My other habit is putting the examples in a test, not the comment. The comment explains intent. The test proves the intent still holds.

I have a file with fourteen patterns and forty test cases and I open it about once a month. It's the least clever code I own and it has never surprised me. I'd like more of my code to be that boring.

#AI #BuildInPublic #VibeCoding #tech #Developer #Software #Engineering #Coding #Regex #Testing #Backend #Documentation #Tech #Learning #Craft #BestPractices #CodeReview #Debugging #Engineering #Maintenance

We made a service four times faster by deleting a feature and the feature was mine.

It generated a thumbnail for every upload. Nice touch, used by about two percent of files opened. It cost a queue, a worker pool, and a storage bucket that grew without a limit.

I measured it before arguing about it. Deleting the feature removed thirty percent of the compute bill and one whole class of failure, which was the worker pool getting stuck on a corrupt image. The replacement is a placeholder. One image, no queue, no growth.

Telling the three people who liked it was the hard part. Two of them said they hadn't used it in months. The third asked for a manual endpoint instead, which I added in an hour.

I've done this four times now. Measure the cost, name the users, offer the smaller thing. A feature almost never survives that conversation and the service always does.

A support ticket turned out to be a product decision and it took me two days to see that.

A customer asked for a field to be sortable. Simple request. I opened the code and found the field stores free text, so sorting it alphabetically groups uppercase apart from lowercase and puts punctuation first.

To sort it properly I'd need a normalized column, a backfill, and a rule about how to normalize names with punctuation. None of that's code work. All of it is a decision about what the list means.

I wrote the request up as three options with a cost next to each. Keep the mess and sort it. Add a column and lose the mess. Leave it and explain why the list is grouped the way it is.

The product owner picked the third, and the customer was fine with it once somebody explained the tradeoff. The ticket was never a bug. It was a question nobody had asked out loud, and two days of investigation is what it costs to find the question.

---
A bug took two weeks to find and was solved by one print statement in the right place. The symptom was a number that was right most of the time and wrong about one day in twenty. Logs showed nothing. My tests passed. I added logging at the boundary, ran it for a day, and the answer was waiting there.

That value was being rounded twice. Once when it was written and once when it was read, and the second rounding only changed anything when the value landed on a specific boundary.

Two weeks of reasoning and the win came from printing the number at two points and comparing. That comparison took four minutes.

I took two weeks because I spent the first nine days reading code and forming theories. Theories are cheap to make and expensive to test, and I tested mine out of order. Nine days of reading code without printing the number is nine days of guessing at a machine that was willing to tell me.

Print the value. Two points. Compare. That's the technique and it beats every clever hypothesis I have ever had.

#AI #BuildInPublic #VibeCoding #tech #Debugging #Developer #Software #Engineering #Coding #Backend #Logging #Tech #Learning #Craft #Production #Testing #BestPractices #Observability #Engineering #Fundamentals
---
I rewrote the same module twice and kept the second version, which was three hundred lines shorter.

My first rewrite was the one I wanted to do. Clean abstractions, a small state machine, and an interface I designed from scratch. It took a week and it worked.

I rewrote it again because the first version was hard to debug. That state machine had eight states and the real system had three.

So I deleted it. Twice. Same module. The second version is a function with a switch, forty lines, and no abstraction at all. It handles every case the first one did and I can read it at two in the morning.

What I learned is that abstraction is a bet on how many shapes the problem will take. I made that bet before I had seen the shapes. A switch statement is a cheaper bet, and I can always add the abstraction later once I know what varies. That's the thing I keep getting wrong. The version I shipped first was the one that looked like engineering and the version I kept was the one that looked like a person admitting a guess.

#AI #BuildInPublic #VibeCoding #tech #Refactoring #Developer #Software #Engineering #Coding #Backend #Architecture #Simplicity #Tech #Learning #Craft #Design #BestPractices #Maintenance #Abstraction #Engineering
---
I tested a database backup for the first time last month and I'm angry it took this long.

The backups had been running for years. Daily, encrypted, shipped to storage. Every check said the job completed, and every check was about the job, not the file.

The restore took ninety minutes and produced an error on the fourth table. A permission mismatch introduced six weeks earlier that the backup job couldn't see, because the backup job reads and never writes. So we had six weeks of backups that wouldn't restore, and the green dashboard was reporting on the wrong thing the whole time.

The fix was to restore a copy into a scratch database every week and run three queries against it. Twenty minutes of compute and it fails hard when the file is bad.

A backup you have never restored is a belief. I had years of belief and no evidence, and the difference between me and an outage was somebody else's luck. A backup job can report success for six weeks while the file it wrote is missing a table, and it keeps saying success because the job never opens the file it made. I run it every Sunday now. Three queries. That's the whole test.

I keep a list of unknowns on every project and it has changed how I talk about risk.

The list is plain. Things I don't know, written as questions, each with a name of the person who can answer it. How does their auth issue tokens. Does the legacy export include deleted rows. Who approves the schema change.

I get some answered in an hour and they come off the list. The questions that stay are the ones nobody wants to own, and those decide the schedule.

The value isn't the answers. It's that risk stops being a feeling in my chest and becomes a row I can hand to somebody. When I say I'm worried about this project, I can now say which four rows worry me and what each one would cost to close.

That conversation goes better than any estimate I have ever given, and it takes ten minutes to start the list.

Before every deploy I check one thing, and it's not the tests. It's the rollback.

The tests prove the new code works. They say nothing about whether I can get back to the old code in five minutes at eleven at night.

I write the rollback down before I merge. Which commit, which command, whether the migration is reversible, whether any data written in the new shape will break the old reader. Four lines in the pull request.

Twice this has stopped a deploy I wanted to make. Once the migration had no down path and I split it into two deploys. Once the new reader had already written rows the old code couldn't parse, so I shipped a flag instead.

I spend five minutes on this check and it has saved two incidents. A deploy without a rollback is a bet that nothing goes wrong, and I've made that bet hundreds of times and lost more than once. Five minutes. That's the whole check.

A colleague shipped a service and left the company three weeks later and the repo told me nothing about his choices.

The code worked. That part was solid. What was missing was the why. A retry with a fixed delay, a cache with no expiry, a config value set to two hundred where every other service uses twenty.

I changed one of those values and broke a downstream job. His two hundred wasn't a typo. It matched a batch size in a partner system that I had never heard of. Recovering that knowledge took a phone call to somebody who was on leave and remembered him mentioning it in a meeting.

The file had no comment and the pull request had one line. Two sentences in that file would have saved me an afternoon and a partner team a bad morning.

Code travels. People don't. Those two sentences are the part of you that stays, and I write them even when the line looks obvious. I keep them in that file now.

A metric made our service look healthier for six months and it was measuring the wrong denominator.

We reported error rate as failures divided by requests. Failures came from a handler that ran after a queue. Requests were counted at the edge. When the queue backed up, requests dropped and the ratio improved, so the worst hour of the quarter looked like our best.

Nobody questioned it because the number was green and the graph was flat and the incident that week was attributed to a partner.

The fix was one line. Count the denominator at the same place as the numerator. Then the graph showed a spike in ten minutes.

Metrics are code with an agenda. They don't lie on purpose, but they answer the question you asked, and a ratio of two things measured in different places answers nothing. I now ask one question about every metric I rely on. Where is each half of this number counted. Ask that first. Every time.

#AI #BuildInPublic #VibeCoding #tech #Observability #Metrics #Developer #Software #Engineering #Coding #Backend #SRE #Tech #Learning #Craft #Production #Reliability #DataEngineering #Monitoring #Incidents
---
I stopped using clever library features and my code got shorter and easier to hand over.

The library had a decorator that handled retries, caching, and tracing in one line. It worked. I used it in about twenty places and I felt productive.

Then a junior engineer spent a day debugging a trace that the decorator had written, because the order of the hidden wrappers changed which span showed up as the parent. Nobody in the code could see the wrappers. They existed at import time.

Now I write the retry and the cache as plain calls, six lines each, visible in the function that needs them. The library still does the work. I just stopped hiding where it happens.

Clever features pay for themselves in the hands of the person who read the docs. They cost everybody else, and the rest of the team is everybody else. Plain code is a message to the next person and I'd rather send a short one.

Nine years of writing software taught me less than nine months of watching other people use it.

I could write a clean function in my first year. What I couldn't do was predict which of my clean functions somebody would click twice, misunderstand, or work around.

The lessons that stuck all came from watching. A form that needed one field and had four. A save button that looked disabled and wasn't. A list that sorted by a column nobody cared about because it was the first one in the schema.

None of that showed up in the code or the tests. It showed up when a person used the thing and paused, and the pause is the signal.

So I sit in on support calls about once a month. It's not efficient. It doesn't scale. It's the one hour a month that reliably changes what I build next, and I'd give up a standup before I gave it up. I don't argue with a person's pause. That's the trade. Worth it.

The agent wrote the whole diff and I still spent the afternoon on the rollback plan.

I handed it a refactor on Monday. It produced six hundred lines in four minutes. Every test passed. I merged it on a branch and sat there with a feeling I could not name.

A diff is a proposal. The rollback belongs to the person who has to answer the phone, so I write it first now.

Every team I know runs the same three models and calls it a choice.

I sat in a review last month where four services each picked a different provider. Two of them were the same model behind a different name. Nobody in the room could say which weights they were actually running, including me.

That's the part that worries me. If one provider ships a bad update on a Thursday, a quarter of the industry gets worse at the same time. Not gradually. Together. There's no second opinion because there's no second model.

I keep one local model on my machine for the boring jobs. Summarise a ticket. Rename a variable. Draft a commit message. It's slower and worse and it costs nothing and it never changes under me without my permission.

I don't hold that as a values statement. It's the same reason I keep cash in the house. The system is fine until the day it isn't, and on that day everybody calls the same number.

A studio shipped a game people loved and closed anyway, and that sentence should be on a wall.

Fallout 3 scored ninety three and sold about twelve point four million. New Vegas scored eighty four and sold about eleven point six. Both numbers are real. Neither one is an argument.

Because the modding scene for New Vegas is still running. Sixteen years of quests, fixes and overhauls, written by strangers for free. The other one has mods too and the community around it is a fraction of the size.

The last patch for an old remaster changed two words on a character screen and the modders noticed within a day.

Male and Female became Body Type 1 and Body Type 2. That's it. Two labels on a menu nobody looks at twice. The armor variants changed with it, so some of the female sets got longer skirts and the safest ones were quietly normalised.

I have no problem with the label. I have a problem with what happened next in the pipeline. A mod that restored the old wording got pulled and then put back, and it is now the most endorsed mod for that game. I read that as evidence about where the players stood.

Here's the craft lesson I keep. Every change costs something and the cost shows up somewhere you didn't look. Somebody had to redraw those meshes. Somebody had to test them. Somebody had to decide which sets were safe. That's a week of work to avoid one sentence of explanation.

Two words on a menu. A week of art. A modding war. The cheapest version of this was a paragraph in the patch notes and somebody chose the other version.

I have multiple phones running an AI assistant and I keep thinking about how easy that was to set up.

Meta built an assistant that can touch a messaging app. Their own documentation says it can't run external tool servers. It can. Somebody wired a relay between the assistant and a phone farm and it works. Messages in, actions out, no rate limit in the middle.

I want to be careful about how I say this because the fun version of this post helps nobody. This is a real security problem and it is not a clever hack. Once you can bridge one assistant to many devices, you have a botnet with a customer support voice. You have account creation at scale and you have a messaging platform that can't tell my four phones from four hundred.

The fix is boring and that's why it hasn't happened. Rate limits per account. Device attestation. A hard cap on how many sessions one identity can hold. A permission model where an assistant asks before it acts on your behalf instead of inheriting the whole account.

The tool is not the problem. The missing boundary is. I built with it because I wanted to see what was actually possible. What's possible right now is a platform that trusts anything holding a valid session, and that is a decision somebody made.

I gave my agents real accounts this month and my token bill went down.

The old way was me in the middle. I pasted context into a chat window, read the answer, pasted the answer somewhere else. Twenty turns. Each turn burned usage on both sides and each turn lost a little of the thread.

The new way is one prompt. The agent gets a repository, a scratch account and a command line tool that can actually run things. I write one long paragraph describing the job. It does the work and reports back.

Two things made the difference. First, the agent gets a body. Accounts, mail, a browser, a terminal. Most of what looked like model failure was actually a missing permission. Second, the work moves out of the chat window and into a tool that doesn't charge by the turn.

The part I'm still careful about is where those accounts live. A scratch identity for each agent, no shared passwords, nothing that touches billing. Cheap agents are worth it. Cheap agents on your personal login aren't.

I stopped writing prompts in a chat window and started sending one giant brief to a command line agent.

The chat window taught me a bad habit. Short questions, short answers, and me deciding what to do with each one. That's me doing the work with extra steps and paying per step.

A command line agent that can read the repo, run the tests and edit files wants the opposite. It wants everything at once. The goal, the constraint, the thing that must not change, the way to prove it worked. One brief. Then I go do something else and read the result.

The quality went up when the brief got longer, which nobody expects. The reason is simple. Every constraint I leave out is a constraint the agent invents for itself, and it always invents something reasonable and wrong.

I still review everything. I still own the merge. What changed is where I spend my attention. It used to go on relaying messages between tools. Now it goes on the one paragraph that decides whether the work is any good.

I run most of my coding work through one command line tool now and it's the least interesting part of my setup.

The interesting part is the briefs. I keep them in files. A brief per job, written like I'm handing work to a contractor who is fast, literal and has never met me. What done looks like. What must stay the same. Which command proves it.

Then Freebuff goes and does it in my repo. It reads the files, it writes the change, it runs the check. I come back to a diff instead of a conversation.

What I like is that it uses what I already have. My repo, my tests, my rules. I don't have to explain the project from scratch every time and I don't have to paste code into a chat box.

The thing I've learned after a few months is that the tool is the small half. The big half is writing down what you actually want, in a form a machine can check. That habit pays off with or without an agent and I would keep it either way.

I left a local model running overnight and it did the boring half of my week.

Two hundred files needed the same three edits. Comments, a renamed variable, a log line. Nothing hard. Ninety minutes of my attention if I did it by hand, and I would have made four mistakes.

I queued it before bed and read the diff with coffee. It took five hours and it cost nothing. No rate limit. No usage dashboard. No second thought about whether a rename was worth a call to an API.

Here's what surprised me. The overnight job was better than my afternoon job. Not smarter. More patient. It did all two hundred files because it doesn't get bored at file one hundred and forty. That's where I usually start skipping.

I do not think local models replace the big ones. I think we have been routing boring work to expensive tools because the boring work is easy to hand over. The cheap model should get the boring work. That's the whole trade.

I priced my own AI feature last month and the number changed what I built.

It was three cents a user per month at my real volumes. I had assumed ten times that. Ten minutes with a script and a month of logs and I knew, which is the whole point.

The second number mattered more. Six percent of my users generated eighty percent of the calls. One integration. Two clicks. One customer's loop that retried on every failure and never backed off.

So I did two things. I moved the cheap work to a local model and dropped the expensive path to a single call per action. Then I put a ceiling on retries. I added an alert when one account crossed it.

Cost is a design constraint like latency and it's the one people leave until the invoice arrives. A number on a page is worth more than a month of guessing about architecture. I should have measured this in week one instead of year two.

My first evaluation for an AI feature was four questions and a score out of five and it told me nothing.

I wrote it in an afternoon and felt good about it. Four examples, two of which I had chosen because the model did well on them. It passed. I shipped and waited for the complaints.

The complaints were about a case my eval never mentioned. Long inputs with a table in the middle. My four questions were short and clean, which is what a closed mouth sounds like.

The fix wasn't more questions. It was questions I could argue about. I pulled two hundred real inputs out of logs, picked fifty, and wrote down what a good answer looks like for each one, in my own words, before the model ran. Then I ran it and disagreed with myself on eleven.

An eval is an argument you can rerun. If it can't fail you, it isn't one. Mine couldn't fail me for eight weeks.

A log line I wrote said the job finished and the job had skipped every row.

The line was true. The function it documented did return. It had returned after an empty query, which is a very different sentence from the job ran.

Nobody noticed for nine days because the job is the kind that fails quietly. A report gets stale. A number looks flat. Flat is what success looks like on that chart, so I stopped looking at it.

I changed the shape of the logging, not the amount of it. Now the job prints what it touched. Rows read. Rows written. Rows skipped, with the first reason. A job that reports zero rows is a success with a fingerprint, and I can tell it apart from a job that lied.

I keep one rule from that week. If a success message can't be wrong, it isn't a status. It's a decoration. I had eleven of those in one service and I deleted six.

A cron job ran at the wrong hour for two years and the schedule was correct the whole time.

Midnight in the expression means midnight on the server. The server ran on universal time. Everyone reading the report sat six hours behind. So the job ran at six in the evening and joined a report that was generated at four in the morning.

Nothing failed. I always had a report. It was just always a day and a bit out, and the difference showed up in month end totals where nobody looks until the totals are wrong.

I fixed it by writing the offset into the schedule comment. Not the timezone name. The offset, the date it was checked, and who owns the server. The timezone name is the thing that lies twice a year.

Every time I've lost a day to a clock it was something like this. Not a hard bug. A quiet agreement between two systems about what a word meant, and me never being in the room when they agreed.

#AI #BuildInPublic #VibeCoding #tech #Timezones #Scheduling #Debugging #Developer #Software #Engineering #Coding #Reliability #Backend #Infrastructure #Documentation #Production

I deleted forty percent of my tests and the suite got more useful.

The ones I removed were checking their own mocks. A test that stubs a database and then asserts the stub was called proves the stub works. It had never caught a bug in three years and it broke every time somebody renamed a private method.

What's left is smaller and slower per test. It talks to a real database. It fails when the migration is wrong. That's the only test I want.

The trade is real. My suite went from ninety seconds to four minutes. I would pay that twice over for a suite I believe, because a suite nobody trusts gets skipped, and a skipped suite is decorative.

I spent two years adding coverage because a number on a dashboard went up. That number measured lines. Lines aren't decisions. I'd rather have sixty percent of the paths that matter than ninety percent of the lines that don't.

One line in a loop took a page from four seconds to four hundred milliseconds and it took me a week to look.

The page was slow for two years and I had blamed the framework, the server size and the region. I'd even asked for a bigger instance, which bought me maybe a third of a second and a bigger bill.

Then I opened the query log instead of the profiler. Same statement, four hundred times, once per row in a list of four hundred. Nine hundred milliseconds of the four seconds was this, and the rest was me rendering what it returned.

My fix was a single query and a map. Twenty minutes. That included the test I wrote so it stays fixed.

I keep the boring lesson from it. Measure before you buy. I had three theories and one of them was expensive, and I picked the expensive one first because it was the easiest to ask for. The query log sat there the whole time, free. I never read it.

I tested a backup for the first time last month and one of the three files wouldn't restore.

I had been assuming backups work for four years. The job said success every night. The sizes looked plausible. Nobody had ever asked the only question that counts, which is can I get it back.

The restore failed on the third file with a corruption error that the backup job had been logging as a warning for months. A warning nobody read, in a job nobody watched, protecting data nobody had needed yet.

Now the restore runs on a schedule into a throwaway database and diffs the row counts against the source. It fails loudly if the counts disagree. That's the whole system. It took an afternoon.

Here's the part I want to be honest about. I set up the backup in 2022 and I felt finished. A backup you have never restored isn't a backup. It's a hope with a cron entry. The cron entry took ten minutes and the restore test took an afternoon, and I spent four years proud of the ten minutes.

A feature flag outlived the person who added it by three years and nobody could say what it did.

It had two branches, a default that nobody remembered choosing, and a comment that said temporary. It gated a code path that had been dead since 2023. Nine services read that flag. Four of them read it and did nothing with the result.

I deleted it over two days and the deletion was the easy part. I spent the hard part on the conversation, because two people believed the flag was load bearing and neither could say why. Both were wrong, and both were right to be careful, because nobody had written down what it was for.

I make every flag ship with a paragraph. What turns it on. What turns it off. Who deletes it. And a date, so the flag is somebody's homework instead of a permanent room in the house.

Temporary decisions that nobody owns don't stay small. They get nine readers and a comment that was wrong for two years. That's not technical debt. That's an unsigned contractor.

A cron job ran twice and we sent about eleven thousand duplicate emails.

I called the job idempotent and it wasn't. That was true in my head and nowhere else. A retry re-ran the batch and every send happened again. The only guard was a flag written at the end of the run.

Eleven thousand people got the same email twice. Four percent of them replied to the first. The reaction to the second was angry.

The fix is a unique key on the work item, written before the send, so the second attempt hits a conflict and stops. One constraint. The database does the deduplication. I stopped trusting my own control flow.

What I keep from it is smaller than the fix. I had said the job was idempotent out loud, in a meeting, with confidence. I had never tested it by running it twice. Running it twice would have caught it in two minutes. I chose the meeting instead.

I wrote an on-call handoff note every week for two years and it's the best document I have ever produced.

One page. What changed this week. What's broken right now. What to watch tomorrow. Who to wake up. Four headings and never more than half an hour of writing.

The reason it works is that it's written for a person at three in the morning, and a person at three in the morning cannot read. Short lines. Current state first. The thing you might need next.

What surprised me is what it did to my week. Writing down what's broken forces me to know what's broken, and there were two weeks where that sentence took twenty minutes because I didn't.

I've seen teams replace this with a dashboard and I understand the appeal. A dashboard answers the questions you thought of in advance. It can't tell you that the payments integration has been flaky since Tuesday and the partner knows, and that's the sentence that saves somebody an hour at four in the morning.

I got ghosted after a seven round interview loop and I learned more from that than from any offer.

Seven rounds over nine weeks. A take home, two coding screens, a system design, a manager chat, a culture panel and a final with a director. Then silence. Then a form letter thanking me for my interest.

I wasn't angry about the answer. I was angry about the shape. Somebody decided no at round two, most likely, and the other five rounds happened because the process could not be stopped once it started.

Since then I ask one question in the first call. What would make you decide no, and when do you expect to know. The good companies answer it in a sentence. The bad ones say the process is designed to be fair, which is an answer about themselves.

I keep five finished projects on a page now and I talk about those. Two interviewers this year spent the round asking about one of them because it solved a problem they had. That conversation was worth nine weeks of rounds.
