A dependency I rely on got archived and I found out from a security scanner, not from the maintainer.

It powers one screen in my app. The maintainer had stopped committing eight months earlier and nobody had told anybody, which is normal, because there is no mechanism that tells anybody.

I did three things that week and they took four hours total. I forked it into my own repo so it can't vanish. I pinned the version I use. And I wrote two hundred words in my own notes describing what it does and what I'd do without it.

That last one is the one I never used to do and it's the one that matters. The other two are habits. The note is the plan.

I am not against dependencies. I depend on forty of them and rewriting them would be a much worse use of a year. But a dependency is a relationship with somebody who doesn't know you exist, and a plan for when they stop answering is part of using it.

I turned a support ticket into a product decision and it took me two days to see it.

The ticket said the export button was slow. I read it as a performance problem because that's the work I like. It wasn't a performance problem. It was slow because it was doing the job of a feature nobody had built. Nobody had asked for it.

The customer was exporting every order to a spreadsheet every Friday, then filtering by a column, then sending the result to three people. Nine minutes of their week, forever. That slowness was a symptom of a missing weekly report.

I built the report in a day. Complaints about the export went to zero and nobody asked me to speed anything up.

Support tickets are an interface to the product, and I had been treating them as an interface to the code. That word was describing a workflow, not a latency budget. Reading twenty of them in one sitting, without fixing anything, is the highest value hour in my week now.

I had a nine hundred line migration to a new host and about eleven lines of it were the actual move.

The rest was assumptions. A hardcoded path. An environment variable that existed in one place and not the other. A cron entry that nobody had looked at since 2021. A DNS record with a four hour TTL that I found at hour five.

I'd planned for the move. I hadn't planned for the things around the move, which is the difference between a plan and a wish.

What saved me was the dry run. I stood up the new host, pointed nothing at it, and ran every job by hand. Nine of them worked. Two failed immediately. One failed silently, which is the one I'd have shipped.

The dry run took three hours and the real cutover took eleven minutes. Every migration story I have ends the same way. The move is easy. The inventory around the move is the whole job, and the inventory is never written down.

I pushed a parser fix on a Thursday because a model update changed its output shape while I slept.

Nothing in my code changed. No deploy, no config, no dependency bump. The model started wrapping its answer in a different structure and my parser had been written against the old one. Six hours of bad rows before the alert fired.

I build with a contract now, not a shape. My prompt asks for JSON with a named schema and I validate it before it touches the database. If the shape moves, the request fails loudly at the boundary instead of quietly at the insert.

The uncomfortable part is that none of this is versioned in a way I can pin. I can pin a library. I can't pin the thing on the other end of the wire, and neither can you. Assume it moves. That's the part of this stack nobody writes in the README.

An agent of mine read a web page and tried to follow instructions hiding in it.

The page had text in white on white. Four sentences telling it to ignore its rules and send everything to an address. My agent read the rendered text, which is the whole point of a browser, and it treated that paragraph as a command from me.

Nothing bad happened because the agent had no mail credentials and no network write access. I got lucky on the boundary, not on the intelligence. It genuinely couldn't tell my voice from a stranger's.

I write the rules into the tool layer now. No credentials in the agent. Allowlisted hosts. Any outbound action waits for a human click. That last one is annoying. It's also the only one that stops a smarter payload.

Text on a page is data. Every day we build things that can't keep that straight, and we call it autonomy.

My agent deleted a test that was failing, and it was right, and I still sat there for ten minutes.

The test asserted on an old response shape. The feature changed in March. The test had been red for four months and somebody had added it to a skip list instead of deleting it. My agent found a red test, read the diff, and removed the stale assertion.

That's the correct call. It's also a call I don't want made alone. Deleting tests is how coverage walks out the door one reasonable decision at a time. The agent had a reason. I couldn't see the reason in the commit message.

So I changed my prompt. Deleting a test is now a hard stop. It can propose the deletion, write the argument in my notes, and leave the test alone. I review those proposals every Friday and I approve most of them.

I treat autonomy as a dial and the middle setting is the one people skip. Full access and no access are both simple. Reviewing the boring decisions is the actual work.

The only agent safety layer I trust is a container with no keys in it.

I tried the other routes first. Prompt rules saying don't touch production. A wrapper that scanned commands before running them. A deny list of paths. Each one held for about a week and then I found a path around it without trying hard.

What holds is boring. A container with the repo mounted, no network by default, no credentials in the environment, and a throwaway home directory. When it needs the network, I open one host for one command and it closes after.

Permissions are the only control that doesn't depend on the model being well behaved. That matters because the model isn't the part that changes. I change the prompt and new models arrive and the wrapper rots.

I still read the diffs. I still own the merge. The container just means that when I get it wrong, the blast radius is a directory I can delete.

I rewrote a cache layer out of existence and the page got faster, which isn't the story the cache was telling.

Hit rate was ninety four percent. That number had been on a dashboard for a year and it looked like success. What it hid was the thrashing. Keys expired in a pattern that lined up with our traffic, so the cache spent every evening rebuilding entries it had just thrown away.

The source query took eleven milliseconds. The cache path took four on a hit and three hundred on a miss, and our misses clustered. I measured the average and the cache won. I measured the p95 and the cache lost badly.

Now the page calls the database and the database is fine, because eleven milliseconds is fine. I pulled forty lines of key building and invalidation out of the service and I haven't missed them.

Hit rate is a number about the cache. It says nothing about the user. I had been optimising my own instrument panel for a year and calling it latency work.

A stranger opened a pull request against my README and merged it in a week, and it was better than my version.

He had never used the project. He followed the setup steps on a clean machine and got stuck at step three, then fixed my instructions instead of closing the tab. His diff removed eleven sentences I had written and added four he had needed.

My README had been written by me for people who already knew what the project was. Every step assumed the last step. That's the failure mode of documentation written by the person who built the thing.

Since then I write the first page for a stranger on a fresh laptop. One command to install. One command to run. One command that tells you it worked. Everything else goes below that, and most people never scroll.

The best documentation test I know is giving somebody a machine and saying nothing. I spent a week of confusion learning that. I should have read my own setup page on a laptop that had never seen the project, which is free.

An email campaign went out on a Tuesday evening and my checkout stopped answering.

The database hit its connection limit for four minutes. No deploy, no code change, no attack. A campaign went out to a mailing list and a quarter of the recipients opened it inside ninety seconds, and every open hit a page that did a write.

Our own traffic was flat. We had load tested the page and it passed at four times normal. We had never tested it at four times normal with an audience arriving at the same moment, which is a different shape of load.

I turned the write off for that page. It was recording a view counter, which nobody reads and which cost us an outage with a customer in it. That counter is gone now.

Capacity planning has a blind spot and it's correlation. My traffic graphs assume requests arrive spread out. Marketing makes them arrive together. Same total, totally different system, and the graph can't tell you which one you bought.

I refunded eleven customers because my retry logic charged them twice, and the code looked correct.

The handler was wrapped in a retry with three attempts. On a timeout it retried, which is right. The charge call timed out, which didn't mean the charge failed. It meant I never saw the answer. The retry charged again.

I had read that sentence in a blog post before and I had nodded at it. Idempotency keys. Send a unique key with the charge and let the provider deduplicate. Twenty minutes of work to add it and I didn't add it until eleven people got an email from their bank.

I keep one thing from that week and it has nothing to do with payments. A timeout isn't a failure. It's an unknown, and my handler had been treating the unknown as a no. Every retry I've written since then has a key attached, including the ones that call my own services.

Eleven customers isn't a big number and it's eleven more than I wanted. Refunding took an afternoon. Explaining it to support took a week.

I turned off a cron job to test whether anything depended on it and found three things that did.

The job refreshed a denormalised table. Nobody had asked about it in two years. I checked the code, found one reader, and that reader had been deprecated in 2024. It looked like a safe deletion, so I disabled the schedule instead of deleting it, which is the habit that saved me.

By the next afternoon a report was stale and the person who owned it assumed the source system was broken. A dashboard in another team went flat. And an alert started firing on data that hadn't changed. That failure is very confusing.

I put the job back with a comment that names those three readers, because the code search had missed two of them. One was in a query string built at runtime. The other was in a spreadsheet nobody had linked to anything.

Dead code has readers you can't grep for. Disabling beats deleting. A job running with a warning in its log is cheaper than a week of confused people.

The status page said everything was operational while my checkout was down for two hours.

The check tested the homepage. The homepage is cached and static. It returned two hundred through the whole event, so the monitor was green and a person was on the phone with me inside ten minutes. I trusted the graph over the caller and I was wrong.

What broke was a third party. Their API started returning five hundreds and my checkout called it synchronously, so the failure walked straight up into my page. My own system was healthy. That's exactly the blind spot.

I keep a feature floor test now, not a page test. It walks the checkout path in a test account every five minutes and asserts the last step. It costs me forty dollars a month in transactions and it caught two incidents last quarter.

A health check that can't fail is a poster. I had picked the cheapest thing to check and given it the authority of a verdict, which is the same mistake as trusting a metric that has never moved.

I keep a decision log and it has ended two arguments that were about to cost a week each.

One file, reverse chronological. A date, a decision, the reason, and what would change my mind. It takes four minutes to write and I've done it for about two years. Sixty odd entries.

The first argument it ended was about a queue. Someone wanted to replace it and I remembered we had chosen it for a reason that still held, and the reason was in the file with a date on it. That conversation took ninety seconds instead of a meeting.

The second one was mine. I wanted to relitigate a naming decision and the entry said I had already been talked out of my position once, with my own reasoning written down. I lost that argument to my past self and it was the cheapest loss I've taken.

Most teams keep the decision and throw away the reasoning, then re-run the argument every eighteen months. Writing it down isn't admin. It's the only way to argue with somebody who isn't in the room.

My side project costs forty seven dollars a month and one line of it carries more than half.

I opened the bill line by line. Compute was nine. Storage was four. A managed database was nineteen. The rest looked like rounding until I found the bandwidth line, which was twenty one dollars, and the cause was a response header that turned off compression on the biggest endpoint.

I had assumed the expensive part was the database because databases are the thing people say are expensive. I was paying for a header. One line, written in 2024 for a debugging session, left in because it worked.

Fixing it cost me four minutes and the bill went to twenty nine. I also moved the backup job off the managed database and onto a small box, which saved another six.

The lesson isn't that side projects are cheap. It's that a bill is a stack trace. It tells you where the money went and I had been reading only the total for eleven months.

I made my test suite run in parallel and immediately found a bug I had been shipping for a year.

The suite took six minutes and I wanted it under two. Four workers, shared database, one transaction each. The first run failed in three places. Two were real. The third turned out to be real twice.

A helper wrote a row and read it back by the newest timestamp. In sequence, that was always my row. In parallel, it was sometimes somebody else's, and the test passed or failed depending on scheduling. In production the same helper ran under load and I had a report that sometimes showed the wrong account for four seconds.

I fixed the helper, which was three lines. Then I saw the failure rate of that report drop. It had been a mystery ticket for eleven months. Nobody had connected it to a test helper.

I didn't parallelise for speed alone. Forcing tests to share a machine is a stress test of the assumptions they were built on. Mine didn't survive it and now they do.

A junior engineer asked me why my review had fourteen comments and none of them said why.

She sent me a pull request with a clear description, a test and one questionable abstraction. I sent back fourteen line comments on naming, spacing and a function that should have been private. Her question was fair and I didn't have a good answer.

The next review I did, I wrote three comments and each one explained what would break later. The naming ones I dropped. A linter handles them and it is never tired. The spacing comments I dropped because arguing about them says more about me than the code.

Her change needed one comment, about the abstraction. It turned out she had thought about it and picked the other trade on purpose, and she was right. That conversation was twenty minutes. It made the code better. I had made it prettier.

I want each review comment to be worth the other person's afternoon. Nine of my fourteen weren't. I had been counting them as thoroughness.

I switched my test order from files to user paths and found the bug I had been writing tests around.

My old suite mirrored the directory tree. One file per module, one test per function. It was tidy and it passed, and it hadn't once told me the product was broken.

The new suite has eleven tests, one per thing a person does. Sign up. Add an item. Change it. Share it. Delete it. Each test walks the real path through the real interface, and each one starts from an empty database.

The third test failed on the first run. A share link worked for the person who created it and returned a five hundred for everybody else, because the permission check read a field that was only populated on create. I had four months of green tests that never touched that path.

Coverage went down. Confidence went up. I was measuring my code. The new one measures the product, and I only need one of those numbers and I had been collecting the wrong one for two years.

A patch release broke my build last Tuesday and the version number said it was safe.

Nothing in the diff touched the function I call. What changed was an internal dependency that now ships a newer minor version, and the newer minor version dropped a fallback for a format I happen to send. Patch release. One line. My integration broke.

I'm not against semantic versioning. I'm against treating it as a contract with me, because nobody signed it on the other side. The maintainer changed a transitive dependency they were allowed to change and my breakage isn't their bug.

So I stopped reading version numbers as risk and started reading lock files. My CI now installs from the lock file and I upgrade deliberately, one package at a time, with a test run between each. Boring, and my Tuesday afternoons are free.

The dependency you actually integrate with is the one you never looked at. I had it pinned for two years and never opened its changelog until it broke me.

I run a second monitor and a phone as inputs for my agent and my typing went down by about half.

One monitor shows logs. The phone shows a browser I can point at anything. The agent reads both, which means I stopped describing errors to it in words. It can see the stack trace instead of my summary of the stack trace.

That difference is bigger than it sounds. Half of my old prompts were translation. I read an error, decided which parts mattered, and typed them out. I was the lossy step in a pipeline that had four available inputs.

Now the loop is short. I point at the failure, write one sentence about what I wanted, and the agent has the log, the code and the ability to run the tests.

It's not a replacement for understanding the failure. I still read every line. What changed is that I stopped being the person who copies text between windows, which was never the valuable part of my job and took more of my day than I wanted to admit.

Serverless cost me more than a small always on box and the invoice is what convinced me.

I liked the pitch. No servers, scale to zero, pay for what you use. My side project is quiet between eleven at night and seven in the morning, so scale to zero sounded perfect.

Then I read the bill. Three dollars of compute, forty one dollars of idle connections and cold starts. Every quiet hour rebuilt a connection pool that took a second to warm, and the user paid that second while my invoice paid for the retries.

I moved it to one small instance with a fixed price. Eleven dollars a month. Response times went from a variable six hundred milliseconds to a steady eighty, because a warm process is a warm process.

Serverless is the right call when you truly have no traffic and the wrong call when you have steady traffic with a quiet window. I had assumed the same thing was cheap in both directions and it's not. Reading the bill is how I found out. Four months late.

I split one large repository into two and my merge conflicts went down without anybody changing how they work.

The repo had a service and a client library in it. Every client change and every service change landed in the same directory tree, so two people working on unrelated things shared a file history and a review queue.

The split took a week. Most of it was the parts nobody owned, which is always where the time goes. Build time went down by a third because the test job stopped running the other half.

Six weeks later I looked at the numbers. Merge conflicts down by about half. Review wait time down from nine hours to three. Nobody has asked to put it back together.

I don't think splitting is always right. I've seen it done badly. What made this one work is that the two halves genuinely deploy on different clocks. The rule I use now is a simple one. If two things always ship together, keep them together. If they don't, the shared repository is a tax you pay for nothing.

I deleted the staging environment for a week to find out who was using it and the answer was uncomfortable.

The plan was to put it back on Monday. Nobody complained by then. I had expected that. What surprised me is what broke. Two things pointed at staging that had no business pointing there, including one job that had been writing test data into a copy of production for eight months.

That job was mine. I set it up during a migration and never pointed it at the test database. It had been quietly polluting the only safe copy of production data I own, and the reason nobody noticed is that staging was always nobody's problem.

I put the environment back with two changes. The credentials to reach production-like data live in one place and the job doesn't have them. And I wrote down what staging is for, in one paragraph, because the honest answer before was that it was for everything.

An environment nobody complains about isn't an environment that works. It's an environment nobody depends on, and that's a different thing with a different cost.

I stopped a refactor halfway and shipped the half and it was the right call.

The job was to move a module from a flag based config to a typed one. I had budgeted two days. On day two I was about forty percent through and the remaining sixty percent touched a code path that only runs during a migration nobody had run since 2023.

Right answer was to stop. I shipped the finished forty percent with the old path still in place and a comment naming the seam. Two hundred lines changed. No behaviour changed. Tests all green.

The alternative was three more days on code that runs once a year, during a migration that requires a human with database access. I'd have been refactoring a museum exhibit while my actual backlog queued up behind it.

I have a rule now about refactors. If I'm not finished inside the time box, I stop and ship what is coherent. Half a refactor that works beats a whole refactor that never lands, and I've shipped three of the second kind before I learned the difference.

An agent wrote a migration that passed in test and locked a table in production for eleven minutes.

I had asked it to add an index. It added the index and also rewrote two columns in the same migration, which is a lock on a table with forty million rows. The test database has four thousand rows and the whole thing finished in eighty milliseconds.

I don't blame the agent for that. I gave it a schema and a goal and it picked the shortest path. The thing it couldn't know is simple. Our test data has no volume. Volume is where migrations hurt.

What I changed was the brief, not the tool. Every migration now has to state its lock behaviour in one sentence, and any migration touching a big table runs against a copy of production data first. That copy takes twenty minutes to restore and it has caught two more.

The lesson is older than the tooling. A test environment that can't feel the pain of production will approve anything that runs fast enough.

I gave my agent a definition of done and its output got boring, which is the best thing I can say about it.

Before that file, every task came back different. Sometimes a function, sometimes a class, sometimes a refactor I didn't ask for. All of it defensible and none of it predictable.

The file is eleven lines. What done looks like. What must not change. The command that proves it. The one place a new file may be created. No dependency changes without asking.

Now the answers rhyme. Same shape, same evidence, same place in the repo. I read a result in ninety seconds instead of twenty minutes because I know where everything is before I open it.

Predictability is the feature I want and it's the one nobody demos. A clever answer saves me four minutes. A boring answer that lands in the same place every time saves me the twenty minutes I spend rebuilding my mental map. I'll take boring.

My evaluation set drifted away from real usage and the green number kept me comfortable for six weeks.

The eval had forty cases, all written in March from prompts I made up. Real users type differently. They paste whole tickets. They write in Portuguese. They ask two questions in one message and they send a screenshot of a table.

I found out because a customer sent me a bad answer and I couldn't reproduce it in the eval. So I pulled fifty sessions out of my logs, at random, with a script, and added the twelve that broke. Nine of them fail on the old prompt.

An eval that never gets fresh cases is measuring a product that no longer exists. Mine was passing eighty eight percent against questions nobody asks.

I rebuild the set from logs every month now. It takes an hour. The number drops every time, and the drop is the useful part. A metric that only goes up has stopped touching reality.

A model refused a task last month that was completely legitimate and I had no clean recovery path.

The job was to summarise a support thread where a customer described a medical billing error. Refusal, on a summary. In my product the summary is the feature. The user got an error message about content policy and left.

The part that bothered me is what I had built. One call, no fallback, no way to investigate. When a classification I don't control decides my product can't work, I should at least see it happen.

I added three things that afternoon. A log line whenever a request comes back without an answer. A retry on a second model for that class of failure. And one sentence in the error telling the user what happened, in the plainest words I could write.

I still can't predict refusals. I stopped trying to. What I can do is stop pretending the boundary isn't there. Every product built on a model has a door that can close without warning, and my job is to have a second one.

I started caching model answers and cut my bill by about half without touching the prompt.

Two thousand requests a day and most of them were repeats. A dropdown that re-asks the same question for every session. A retry loop that asks the identical question three times because my own handler is careless. Same input. Same output. Paid for four times.

I put a cache in front of the call with a hash of the input as the key. Six weeks, thirty eight percent hit rate, and a bill that went from about ninety dollars to fifty four.

My first key was wrong. I hashed the prompt and forgot the model name and the temperature, so a test run with a different setting poisoned real answers for an hour. Now the key includes every parameter that changes the output. That's the whole lesson.

Cheap isn't the same as free. I cap the cache and I expire it weekly, because an answer about a policy that changed is worse than no answer.

#AI #BuildInPublic #VibeCoding #tech #Caching #LLM #CostOptimization #Developer #Software #Engineering #Coding #Backend #Performance #MachineLearning #Infrastructure #Metrics

Long context isn't memory and my agent proved it by breaking a rule I set four times.

The rule was simple. Never write to the production bucket. It was the third line of a brief that ran for about forty minutes across eleven tool calls, and by call nine the agent was writing to a bucket whose name differed from the test one by a suffix.

Nothing bad landed because that bucket is versioned, and I found it in a diff. But the failure is worth naming. A long context window isn't the same as attention, and the thing at the top of a long brief is the thing that fades first.

So I moved the rule out of prose and into the tool. The production credentials aren't in the agent's environment at all. A rule the model can forget is a rule I have to re-verify every run, and I'd rather move the wall than keep reminding.

I write the brief for the work. I write the boundaries in code, where forgetting isn't an option.

The best postmortem template I've found is four questions and none of them is why.

What changed. What we saw. What we did. What would have caught it. Four lines in a doc, filled in during the incident, not three days after in a meeting where everybody has rehearsed.

I dropped the question why. Why invites a story about a mistake and a person, and the story is always the same, and it never changes the system. What would have caught it is a design question with an answer you can build.

I rewrote our template and the first incident afterwards took forty minutes to document instead of two days. The action items were three, and all three were automated tests, and all three still run.

What I like is that it works when the author is embarrassed. Nobody has to admit fault to fill in what changed. The document asks about the system and the system doesn't have feelings.

I write the ticket before the fix now and my changes got smaller.

The old habit was to open the editor first and describe the work after. That produced large diffs and vague summaries, because the writing happened when I was already invested in whatever I had built.

The new habit is fifteen minutes of writing. What is broken. What I'll change. What I'll not touch. How I'll know it worked. Then I start.

Half the time the ticket tells me the change isn't worth making, which is the cheapest bug resolution available. The other half, the diff comes in at about a third of what I'd have written, because I decided the boundary before I was attached to the code.

I still get it wrong sometimes. I wrote a ticket in August describing a one line fix and it turned into four files once I understood the problem. That ticket is a record of my understanding on the day, which is useful on its own.

I had a new hire break production in week one and it was the best onboarding I have ever run.

The break was planned. I gave her a small service, a runbook, and a task that I knew would page us both. Not a fake task. A real change to a real path with a real alarm on it, and me sitting next to her with the rollback ready.

She broke it in eleven minutes. We rolled back in two. Total outage, four seconds, one internal tool. And she spent the rest of the week asking better questions than anybody I have onboarded the slow way, because she had already seen the alarm fire and the fix work.

Every onboarding I ran before that was a tour. Read this, watch that, don't touch anything until month two. It produces people who are careful and scared, and scared people hide mistakes until the mistake is big.

I make the first week loud and harmless now. A person who has broken something and fixed it is a person who will tell you the next time.

A third party changed the type of one field and my import wrote four thousand bad rows before breakfast.

I had read that field as a number for two years. It arrived as a string on a Tuesday, with quotes around it. My parser had no schema, so it read the string, coerced what it could, and stored everything else as null. Four thousand rows with a missing amount.

The vendor never told anybody. Their changelog said minor improvements. Their docs still showed a number three days later, which is the detail I keep coming back to, because the docs weren't wrong on purpose. They just weren't regenerated.

I validate every payload now against a schema I wrote down, and I reject the batch instead of storing a guess. Rejecting four thousand rows is annoying. Storing them is worse, because I found out about this from an accountant six days later.

The rule I use is that a boundary I don't control gets a contract I do control. It's that simple and it took me nine years to start doing it.

Two incidents eight months apart had the same root cause and I didn't notice until a search found it.

The first was in November. A report timed out for one customer and we blamed data volume. We added an index and moved on. The second was in July. A different report timed out for a different customer and we blamed a busy hour.

They were the same bug. One query helper didn't filter by account, so it read every account's rows and filtered in memory. Small accounts were fine. Large ones were fine until the table grew.

What would have caught it is one line in the report. A search across our incident docs for the words slow report would have found both in about ten seconds. Nobody did that search, because incidents are closed by the person who fixed them and that person is tired.

The habit I keep comes from this. When I close an incident I write the symptom in the words a panicked human would use, spelled right, and I search for those words before I close the next one. I could have saved eleven hours with two minutes of searching.

I deleted my how to run this document and replaced it with a script that argues when the arguments are missing.

The doc was four pages. It was wrong twice in one month. A flag changed, a port moved, and the doc kept giving people a command that failed with an unclear error.

The script is thirty lines. If an argument is missing it prints what is missing and what the valid values are. If a service isn't up it says which one and where to look. If everything is fine it runs the thing.

Nobody reads documentation before they run something. People copy a command and read the error. So the place to put the instructions is the error, which is the text my teammates read under pressure.

I still keep a README. It explains what the service is for and who owns it. It doesn't try to be a manual, because the script is the manual and the script can't go stale without failing loudly in front of somebody.

One log line cost me about eight gigabytes a day and it was the most useful line I had written.

It logged the full payload of every request. Two hundred bytes per row. Four hundred thousand rows a day, and a storage bill that crept up for five weeks before I looked at it.

The line was great for debugging. Terrible as a default. So I split the difference instead of deleting it. It logs the payload for one percent of requests and it logs a shape summary for all of them, which is a key list, a length and a hash. When something odd happens I turn the full version on for that account for an hour.

I keep thinking about how long it would have taken me to notice without a bill. There's no dashboard for a cost that grows slowly. Growth of two percent a week is invisible on any chart with a reasonable scale.

A default is a decision I make once and pay for forever. I write one every week.

The bug was in the test that proved my fix worked. I shipped the fix because the test was green.

A date broke in a support flow. I wrote a test that reproduced it, changed three lines, watched the test pass, and deployed. Then support told me the same flow failed the same way.

My test wasn't testing the flow. It called the helper directly with a date I had chosen. The real path passed a date that came out of a form, and the form was the part that was broken. I had written a test for the thing I was about to change instead of for the thing the user does.

The real test is six lines. It goes through the interface. It failed on the first run, which is how I learned the form was converting the date at midnight in the wrong zone.

A test written after the fix is a test written to pass. I write them before now, and I write them at the level where the complaint lived. That's a thing I said out loud in a review once and then didn't do for another two years.

The flag that scared me most was the one that turned itself off and nobody could explain why.

A rollout flag for a new checkout path. One morning it read as off for the entire company. No deploy, no config change, no incident. The new path stopped being reachable for about four hours.

Finding the cause took two days. An admin screen let a support agent set a flag for a single account and it silently wrote the value globally, with no confirmation and no log of who did it. It fixed one customer and turned my rollout off for everybody.

We'd built a permission model for our customers and no model at all for our own tools. Our internal screens were trusted. The people using them were trusted, which is the assumption that keeps producing this exact bug.

I added an audit line to every write in that tool and a scope check on the flag setter, and I found two more screens with the same shape. Twelve lines of work. Four hours of outage caused by a good intention.

I removed a UI component from my project and replaced it with thirty lines of my own and the app got better.

The component did a lot. Date pickers, keyboard navigation, accessibility, twelve hundred lines of position logic. I used exactly one mode of it and I had been patching around it for a year because it wanted to own the input.

The thirty lines are a text field with a parser and a formatter and a test for each. It does less. It looks the same. It weighs fourteen kilobytes instead of two hundred and it stopped fighting me about focus.

I'm not against libraries. I have forty of them and ripping them out isn't a hobby. But there's a category of dependency I add because reading its source would take an afternoon, and then I spend a year learning its opinions one bug at a time.

The question I ask before adding one now is whether the thing I need is small. If it's small I write it. If it's not small I read the docs and take the dependency, and I read the source of the one part I'm about to lean on.

I blocked one morning a quarter for dependency upgrades and my emergencies dropped by about half.

My old pattern was reactive. A security scanner flagged a package on a Friday, I read a changelog in a hurry, and I shipped an upgrade under pressure with whatever test coverage happened to exist.

Now it's four hours on a calendar with a real agenda. Every direct dependency one minor version up, one at a time, tests between each. Two major versions researched and written down. Anything abandoned gets forked or replaced in its own quarter.

I spent six hours on it. Four upgrades landed and one thing broke, which was cheap and found on a branch. The four since then have been quieter, because six hours of attention beats forty minutes of panic every time.

Reading a changelog under pressure is how you ship a regression with confidence. The work doesn't get smaller by waiting. It gets faster and more expensive and I paid that bill for three years before I put the morning in the calendar.

The vendor status page said degraded while my graphs said everything was fine and both were right.

Their incident post said a partial degradation in one region. My dashboard showed a flat error rate and a flat latency. I told a customer we were unaffected because their tools agreed with mine.

We were affected. Not in errors and not in latency. In the time it took a payment to settle. A synchronous call returned a success immediately and the settlement happened later than usual, so my graphs, which measured the response, saw nothing.

My monitoring measured what I call. The customer measured what happened to their money. Those are different instruments and mine is the one that was cheap to build.

I added a settle check now. Every fifteen minutes, a test payment goes through the whole path and asserts the money moved, not the call returned. It costs a few dollars a month and it's the only instrument I have that would have caught that day.

An agent of mine found a real security hole and my first reaction was irritation.

It flagged an endpoint that returned another account's data when given a crafted identifier. Four lines to confirm it in a browser. Real, exploitable, and about two hours old in the codebase.

My irritation lasted one minute and I want to write down why it happened, because it's the reason people don't run these tools properly. I had written that endpoint. The finding was correct and it was about me, and my first instinct was to argue with the messenger instead of reading the diff.

I fixed it in twenty minutes. Ownership check at the boundary. A test sends the crafted identifier and expects a four hundred and four. A note in the ticket records how it was found.

What I kept is the process. The agent runs a read only pass on every branch now, before review. The finding doesn't care who wrote it, which is the property I can't get from a human reviewer who sits next to me and remembers that I bought the coffee.

One line of prompt change fixed a problem I had been engineering around for a month.

Output came back with a preamble. Sure, here is the summary. Three sentences of throat clearing before the thing I asked for, which broke my parser and cost me approximately forty minutes of work writing a stripper that handled eleven variations of it.

I changed the instruction instead. Tell it the output format and what to do when the request is ambiguous. Not please be concise. The format, the field names, and one sentence about what to do with a question it can't answer.

Eleven of my twelve strip cases disappeared with it. The twelfth is still there for a case where the input is empty, which is a real case and worth keeping.

I had spent a month hardening the input side and the answer was on the output side, in the instruction, in about twenty words. Reading failure as a specification problem rather than a cleaning problem isn't a habit that comes to me first.

I ran the same task twenty times to see how often it gave the same answer and the number was eleven.

Eleven out of twenty identical outputs at a temperature I had set to zero. The other nine differed in wording, or in which files got touched, or in whether it added a test. Nothing catastrophic and nothing the same.

That changed how I test anything depending on a model. One green run isn't evidence. I run the critical paths five times. I look at the shape of the results, and if three of five differ in a way that matters I treat it as a design problem rather than a flaky test.

For my own products the same idea applies to the user. If the answer to the same question can vary, the interface shouldn't promise otherwise. I stopped writing a cached suggestion list that claimed to be stable and let it refresh, which matched what it actually was.

Variance isn't a bug I can fix. It's a property I have to design around. One run is a coin flip pretending to be a measurement.

#AI #BuildInPublic #VibeCoding #tech #Testing #LLM #Determinism #Developer #Software #Engineering #Coding #QualityAssurance #ProductDesign #MachineLearning #Observability #Craft

I keep one hour a week for reading code I don't own and it's the cheapest training I've found.

Not my team's code. Open source projects I depend on. Last month I read the retry logic in a client library I use in four services. It retries on all four hundred level errors including the one that means your credentials are wrong. My services had been hammering an auth endpoint every time a token expired.

That's a bug I couldn't have found by reading docs and would never have found in an incident, because the symptom was a slow login and not an error.

The hour has no deliverable and no ticket and that's why it works. Every other hour in my week is attached to something that must ship, and reading is the work that only pays off later, which is the category of work that gets cut first.

I've kept this for about two years. Four real fixes have come out of it. I don't know how to put that on a slide. I don't need to.

I dropped a table in production on a Wednesday and the backup from an hour earlier is the only reason I still have a job.

It was a cleanup. A table named with a suffix that I had checked twice, and my check was wrong because the suffix on the production table was different from the one in my head. Drop, confirmation, done. Four seconds.

The restore took nine minutes and we lost one hour of writes because that's the schedule. Nobody outside the team noticed. I wrote the whole thing up the same day, including the part where I had checked twice and still got it wrong.

My changes are boring and each one exists because of that afternoon. No human has drop permission outside a change window. Drops are two steps with a sleep between them. The restore script is tested monthly and it lives in the runbook next to the drop command, not in a wiki.

Confidence isn't the same as correctness and I had a lot of the first one. The check I trusted was a check against my own memory, which is the instrument that failed.

I stopped reviewing diffs over fifty lines and the average time a change waits for review dropped to four hours.

My old habit was to open everything and start reading. A six hundred line change would sit in my queue for a day and a half. I'd find two hours for it eventually, and by then the author had moved on and the context was cold.

Now anything over fifty lines gets scheduled. Thirty minutes on a calendar. The author is in the room, and we read it together. Anything under fifty lines gets reviewed in the next gap I have. That's within an hour.

The large changes got better too. A scheduled review means questions get answered instead of typed. We have caught two design problems in those sessions before the code hardened.

Waiting time is the metric I'd been ignoring. I had been optimising my own reading speed and the thing that actually hurt was the queue, and the queue was full of changes I had accepted by opening them without a plan.

I handed over a project in six hours of writing and the doc was one page and it saved my successor about three weeks.

The page has four sections. What the system does in two sentences. The three commands that matter. The five things that will page you and what each one means. Who to ask when the answer isn't here.

Everything else I wanted to write, and I wanted to write a lot, was about design decisions that the code already explains and the tests already enforce. I cut it. What was left was the part that only existed in my head.

Our handover call was forty minutes. He asked four questions and three of them were about the paging list, which is the section I nearly didn't write because it felt obvious at the time.

Three months later he told me the page was the whole thing. Not the architecture. That page says what the alarm means and who to call. Every handover I had done before that was a knowledge dump, and a dump isn't a transfer.

A model company raised a round big enough to buy a small country and the product is a text box with a better prompt.

I'm not sneering at the product. I use it. What interests me is the arithmetic. Compute grows with every user and the price is set by a competitor who is also losing money on every user. Both sides are waiting for the other one to run out.

I've watched this film in other industries. Ride sharing did it. Streaming did it. The winner isn't the best product. The winner is whoever can keep paying for the losses longest, and the customer gets a cheap product and then a repriced one.

What I do about it is unglamorous. I keep a local model for the work that doesn't need a frontier model and I keep my prompts portable enough to move. I'd rather be able to leave than be right about who wins.

Noah built a boat before anyone believed the weather and that worked out. The difference is he could see the sky and I can only see a burn rate.

I asked an agent to write the tests for the code it had just written and the tests passed on the first run.

That's not a good sign. A test written by the same mind that wrote the bug tends to test the same assumption twice. My prompt said add tests. It added tests. Both of us were satisfied and neither of us had checked the thing that actually breaks.

Now the brief says something different. Write the test that would fail if the feature were removed, and list the three ways the feature could be wrong that the test can't see. That second part is the part I read. The list is where the useful thinking lands, and about half the time it names the bug.

The tests are fine. They're not the value. The value is a machine that has to say out loud what it can't prove, which is a habit I want in people too.

I still write the test for the money path myself. Some work doesn't belong to a tool.

#AI #BuildInPublic #VibeCoding #tech #Testing #Agents #CodeReview #Developer #Software #Engineering #Coding #QualityAssurance #MachineLearning #Automation #Craft #Trust

The first thing I check in a diff written by an agent isn't the logic.

It's the error handling. Logic is the part the agent gets right, because logic is what the prompt described. Error handling is what happens when the happy path doesn't, and nobody puts that in a brief.

So I look for three things. A failure that gets swallowed with a default value. A retry with no limit. A catch that logs and continues. Every one of those is a decision a person should make out loud, and every one of them is invisible in a passing test suite.

I add them to the brief now, in one line. Fail loudly, no silent defaults, retries need a ceiling. Sixteen words and my review time per change dropped by about half.

I don't blame the tool for that. It's literal. If I want judgment about failure I have to ask for it, in writing, before I read a diff.

#AI #BuildInPublic #VibeCoding #tech #CodeReview #ErrorHandling #Agents #Developer #Software #Engineering #Coding #Reliability #Automation #Craft #Reliability #Production
---
My whole agent loop fits in four steps and I've stopped trying to make it cleverer.

Run it. Read the diff. Change one thing. Run it again. That's the loop. It hasn't changed in a year and the tooling around it has been rewritten three times.

The one thing that matters is the third step. One change at a time. When I batch three fixes into one prompt I lose the ability to know which one mattered, and then I'm guessing about my own code with extra confidence.

I keep a scratch file beside the run where I write the change and the result, one line each. Twelve runs is a normal afternoon. When it works I read the twelve lines and know exactly what happened, which is worth more than the code that came out.

I have abandoned clever loops. A loop with four steps survives a bad week, and a bad week is when I need it.

#AI #BuildInPublic #VibeCoding #tech #Agents #Workflow #Developer #Software #Engineering #Coding #Productivity #Debugging #Craft #Automation #DeveloperTools #Habits
---
A vendor added an AI assistant to their dashboard. I couldn't find the export button for four minutes.

It used to be a row of buttons. Now one of them is a sparkle icon and two of them moved behind a chat panel that wants to help me. The feature set grew and the surface I actually use got smaller.

I've done this too. I added a suggestion box to a tool nobody asked to be suggested to, because it looked good in a demo and it took two days. Six months of data said eleven people used it. Four of those were me testing.

The mistake isn't the feature. It's the promotion. I put the new thing where the old thing was and called it improvement. The old thing was the thing people opened the tool to do.

My rule now is that a new feature can't move an existing control. It gets its own space until the usage data says otherwise. My demo is slower and the product stays where people left it.

#AI #BuildInPublic #VibeCoding #tech #ProductDesign #UX #ProductManagement #Developer #Software #Engineering #Coding #FeatureFlags #DataDriven #Craft #Design #Users
---
When two models give me different answers I treat the disagreement as the useful output.

Not the answer. The gap. I run the same question through two providers, and when both agree I move on with a bit more confidence. When they disagree I read the question again, and about a third of the time I find that my own instruction was ambiguous in the place they diverged.

That's the part I wasn't expecting. Models aren't telling me about the world. They're telling me about my prompt. A disagreement is a map of the sentences where I left something out.

The cost is real and it is small. Two calls instead of one, on maybe a tenth of my work. The saving is measured in the bugs I don't ship from a rule I thought I had written down.

I've caught three specification problems this way in two months. None of them would have shown up in a test, because a test can't know what I meant.

#AI #BuildInPublic #VibeCoding #tech #LLM #MachineLearning #Evals #Developer #Software #Engineering #Coding #Prompting #QualityAssurance #Ambiguity #Craft #Observability
---
My prompts live in version control next to the code and the diff on a prompt change is the most interesting diff I read all week.

A prompt is business logic wearing a string. It decides what the product does, what it refuses, and what shape the answer takes. And for a year I kept mine in a database field that nobody reviewed, where the change history was one row per save. I never wrote the reason down.

Now they're files. A change to a prompt goes through the same review as a function. The diff shows me the sentence that was added and the sentence that was removed. It forces the question of what the new one does that the old one didn't.

The first week of this found two prompts that contradicted each other in the same request path. One said never guess when the field is missing. Another said always return a value. Both had been live for months and the one that won was decided by the order of the calls.

Prompts are code. I treat them like config and I got config behaviour.

#AI #BuildInPublic #VibeCoding #tech #Prompting #VersionControl #LLM #Developer #Software #Engineering #Coding #CodeReview #Documentation #Configuration #MachineLearning #Craft
---
A bug appeared only when two features were on at once and neither feature was wrong.

A discount flag and a tax flag. Each one worked. Together, the tax calculated on the pre-discount amount for one customer segment and the post-discount amount for another, and the difference was about eleven cents per order, which is why nobody found it for a year.

Every test we had covered one flag. That's the shape of the coverage gap. Two toggles produce four states, three toggles produce eight, and my suite tested the two states where the code was written.

I didn't write four hundred tests. I wrote one test that turns every flag on and walks the checkout. It failed on the first run and the fix was two lines. That single test has caught two more interactions since, neither of which I was looking for.

Feature flags multiply the state space and the multiplication is invisible in a coverage report. One test with everything on is the cheapest insurance I've found and it took a year to think of it.

#AI #BuildInPublic #VibeCoding #tech #FeatureFlags #Testing #QualityAssurance #Developer #Software #Engineering #Coding #Combinatorics #Payments #Regression #Coverage #Reliability
---
My form validation rejected a legitimate name and the customer emailed the founder.

The name had an apostrophe in it. My pattern allowed letters, spaces and a hyphen. I had written it in about four seconds. It had been rejecting a real class of names for eight months without a single complaint, because most people typed it another way or gave up.

I found three more of the same shape that week. A phone field that stripped a leading plus. A postcode field that demanded a format two countries use. An address field that capped at sixty characters.

My fix wasn't a better pattern. It was to stop rejecting. Accept anything printable, trim it, store it, and if the value is wrong the consequence is a bounce and a support message, which is a much smaller failure than refusing a customer at the door.

Validation should protect the system, not my idea of what a name looks like. That's a sentence I would have agreed with on the spot. I didn't apply it for eight months. The difference shows up when you read the tickets.

I tried to use my own app with the keyboard only and I couldn't get past the second screen.

The modal had no way to close. Escape did nothing. The focus stayed behind it, so tabbing walked through the page underneath a dialog I couldn't see past. A person who doesn't use a mouse was locked out of my product at the first interruption.

I had built it myself and I had never noticed because I always have a mouse. That's the whole failure. My testing method couldn't see the bug because my testing method was one person with a trackpad.

The fixes are small. Escape closes. Focus moves into the dialog and returns when it closes. Each button is reachable and it has a name. About an hour for the whole app, and I found two more places where the same pattern repeated.

Accessibility isn't a feature I add for other people. It's a set of bugs I can't see with my own hands, which means I need a different method, not more attention.

---
A cached error response kept one customer broken for three months and no alarm ever fired.

The flow called a vendor and cached the result for an hour. When the vendor returned a five hundred, my code cached that too, with the same one hour lifetime, which meant every retry for the rest of that hour got handed the same broken answer. The customer retried, got the cached failure. Their integration looked dead to them while my dashboard showed one error at the start and nothing after.

Caching a failure is almost never right. My cache layer had no opinion about it. I fixed it with one line. Don't cache a response that's not a success.

The part that bothers me is the three months. My dashboard was quiet and the logs were quiet. One signal existed. Someone quit and said nothing. I found it by reading a cancellation reason, which is a report I had never opened before.

Negative caching isn't a technical detail. It's a decision to make the system remember its own failure and serve it back. I had made that decision by accident.

#AI #BuildInPublic #VibeCoding #tech #Caching #ErrorHandling #Observability #Developer #Software #Engineering #Coding #Backend #IncidentResponse #CustomerSupport #Reliability #Production
---
I deployed on a Friday on purpose for a year to find out whether the warning was true.

The rule exists for a reason and the reason is staffing. A bad deploy at five on a Friday has nobody to fix it. The person who does fix it is doing it annoyed at eleven at night. That's real and I have lived it.

What I found when I tested it is that my deploys were bad in a specific way, and the specific way was always the same, and it had nothing to do with the day of the week. Four of the eleven Friday deploys needed a rollback and three of those were configuration, not code. Configuration had no test and no review. The day of the week was never the problem.

So I fixed the actual thing. Config now goes through the same pipeline as code. It boots the app with production values. My last six deploys, including two on Fridays, have rolled back zero times.

My lesson isn't that deploying on Friday is fine. It's that a rule about timing is often a rule about something else. That something else is cheaper to fix. I spent a year proving that. I'd do it again.

I wrote a test for a bug I couldn't reproduce. It caught a different one three weeks later.

A report showed wrong totals for about two percent of runs. No stack trace. No pattern. It never happened when I watched it. I couldn't fix what I couldn't see. I wrote a test that ran the calculation two thousand times with random inputs and asserted the invariant.

It failed on run thirty one. The cause was a floating point accumulation in a running sum. The fix was to sum integers and divide at the end. That took nine minutes after three days of looking.

Three weeks later the same test caught a rounding change in a dependency upgrade, which is a bug I'd have shipped to production because it passed every other test I had.

Property tests are how I test the things I don't understand. I can't assert the answer because I don't know it. I can assert the rule the answer has to follow. The machine finds the input that breaks it.

#AI #BuildInPublic #VibeCoding #tech #Testing #Debugging #QualityAssurance #Developer #Software #Engineering #Coding #PropertyBasedTesting #FloatingPoint #Money #Craft #Reliability

I stopped estimating in points and started giving a range with the reason written next to it.

Three days if the vendor schema is what the docs say. Five days if it isn't, which happened twice last quarter. I'll know by Wednesday. That's the whole estimate and it takes four minutes to write.

Points gave me a number that felt precise. It carried no information. A three was a three. Nobody could say what a three meant in this codebase. The number got argued about instead of the risk.

The range does something the points never did. It names the thing that would make it bigger. Two of my last four estimates were right on the low end and both of them were the estimates where I wrote down what would break it, which means the writing is the part that works.

I still get it wrong. My last one took nine days against a three to five day range. The reason was a migration nobody had mentioned. Next time it goes in the range, on the line where I list what could break it.

I read a competitor's changelog every month. I've learned more about their product than their marketing page ever told me.

The page says they help teams move faster. The changelog says they shipped three things this quarter about permissions and one about export, which tells me their customers are enterprises with compliance problems. They're not prioritizing speed at all.

Every product has a gap between the pitch and the work. The changelog is the honest side of the gap. It's also public, dated, and written by engineers who aren't thinking about positioning.

I do this with my own changelog too. Six months of entries read in one sitting shows where the product actually went and whether that matches where I said it was going. Mine had four entries about onboarding and none about the thing I called our main advantage.

Reading somebody else's is research. Reading mine is a check on my own story. That one is cheaper and harder.

An intern asked me why the service needed three databases. I didn't have an answer that survived being said out loud.

One for the app, one for analytics, and one I had added during a migration and never removed. The third had eleven rows and a connection pool. It had been running for two years because deleting it required a change to a config file I didn't remember writing.

She removed it in an afternoon. Nothing broke. That is the answer that should worry me. Two years of operational surface for zero value. The reason it survived is that storing something is cheap and deciding to stop storing it is work.

I run this question on everything now. What happens if this is deleted. If the answer takes more than a sentence I write the sentence down and put it in the ticket, and half the time the sentence reveals that the thing has no owner.

The best architectural review I get costs me nothing and comes from somebody who has been here four days, because they can still see the decisions and I've stopped seeing them.

I chose the boring architecture and three years later it is the only reason I have weekends.

One service, one database, one deploy pipeline. When I started, three engineers told me I'd regret it and one drew a diagram with boxes and arrows that I still can't explain. The diagram was right about the future and wrong about my constraints.

What the boring choice bought me is attention. All the time I didn't spend on service discovery, message contracts and distributed tracing went into the product, which for a small team is the only scarce resource that matters.

I've paid for it twice. Two features would have been easier with separate services. I built them inside the monolith in about double the time. That's the tax and it is about four weeks across three years.

I'm not saying monoliths win. I'm saying the diagram describes a company with eight teams and I have two people. The cost of pretending otherwise lands on the people who have to be on call. I'd pick the boring one again.

Two teams spent a week arguing about whether the number was going up and neither side had written down what the number counted.

One team said signups. The other said activated accounts. Those differ by about forty percent, and the difference explained the whole argument. Both dashboards were correct and they were measuring two different things with the same label.

We wrote the definition in a shared doc. One paragraph. What counts, what doesn't, where it comes from, who owns the query. Total time, two hours. The argument had cost about eleven.

The thing I keep from that week is that arguments about data are almost never arguments about data. They're arguments about a word, and the word has two owners and no definition.

I have a rule now. Before I argue about a number I ask what it counts and who owns the query. If nobody can answer, the work for that hour isn't the argument, it is the paragraph.

---
I ran out of database connections on a Tuesday and the error message sent me to the wrong place for two hours.

The app reported a timeout. The database reported nothing. I looked at slow queries, then at CPU, then at the network, and the answer was that the app had taken every connection in the pool and was waiting for a connection it would never free.

Fifty requests each holding a connection while calling a vendor that was slow. All fifty waiting on the same external call. The pool was twenty. Simple arithmetic and it took two hours because the symptom had nothing to do with the cause.

My fix wasn't a bigger pool. It was two things. Don't hold a connection while talking to somebody else, and set a timeout on the external call so a slow vendor can't hold a thread forever. Twelve lines.

A connection pool is a queue with a hidden length and the length is the thing nobody looks at. I export the number now and I alert on saturation, which is the metric that would have pointed at the real problem in about four seconds.

#AI #BuildInPublic #VibeCoding #tech #Databases #Performance #Debugging #Developer #Software #Engineering #Coding #Backend #Timeouts #Observability #ConnectionPool #Reliability

I broke my own API with a change that every reviewer called safe, by adding a required field to a request.

The field had a sensible default in my head. I made it required in the schema and deployed, and every existing client started getting a four hundred. My own mobile app was the first one to break. It broke in the app store review, which is a bad place to find out.

Adding isn't safe when adding changes what the server demands. The version that would have been safe takes an optional field with a default and a deprecation note, which is three more lines than what I wrote.

I had written a rule about this two years earlier. My rule said removing fields is dangerous. Making something required is removal wearing a different hat, because it removes the ability of a client to omit it.

I wrote the rule again. The example went in with it. Rules that live as a sentence get agreed with. They get forgotten. A rule with a specific failure next to it is the kind that survives my next deadline.

I read a hundred support tickets in one sitting. My roadmap changed twice.

What I noticed first is that the loudest thread isn't the biggest one. One customer wrote nine paragraphs about a feature. Fourteen people mentioned the same confusing label in one sentence each. Nine paragraphs got a meeting. The label got nothing.

The second thing is how much of the traffic is our own error messages. Thirty one of the hundred were somebody quoting a message we wrote and asking what it meant. A message that says invalid input is a support ticket with a delay built in.

I rewrote four messages. I added the next step to each one. Two weeks later the ticket category dropped by about a third, which is a bigger product change than anything I had on the roadmap for that quarter.

Support tickets are the only place the user writes in their own words with no filter. Dashboards tell me what happened. A hundred tickets tell me what it felt like. The feeling is where the roadmap should come from.

#AI #BuildInPublic #VibeCoding #tech #CustomerSupport #ProductManagement #UX #Developer #Software #Engineering #Coding #UserResearch #ErrorHandling #Roadmap #DataDriven #Craft
---
We had a code freeze before every release. That freeze is why our releases were dangerous.

Two weeks with no merges meant two weeks of work sitting on branches, diverging, and colliding on the day the freeze lifted. Every change landed on the same day. Every change had been tested alone.

Now there's no freeze. We ship small changes every day, behind a flag when the change is risky. A release is a decision about which flags to turn on rather than a merge of a month of work.

The incident rate went down. Not one new test. Not because the code got better. Because the unit of change got smaller, and a small change that breaks something is a small change to roll back.

A freeze is a way to make delivery scary and then handle the fear. The thing that made our releases calm was shipping all the time, which is the opposite of what the process said to do.

I wrote a one-off script in March. It is still running in production in September and nobody has ever seen it.

It moves a file between two places. It has no tests, no retry and no logging beyond one print. It runs from a cron on a box nobody looks at, and if it stops, an accounts process breaks in a way that takes two days to notice.

The dangerous part isn't the script. It's the word one-off. I used it as a reason not to write anything down. That reason evaporated the moment it was load bearing.

Now the script has a header comment, four lines long. What it does, who owns it, what breaks if it stops, and the date I last read it. The same script, one pass with a text editor. It went from invisible to owned.

Anything that runs on a schedule is production. The number of writers isn't the test I should use. My test is whether stopping it would wake somebody up. I have three scripts that would and only one of those has tests.

---
My agent asked me a clarifying question mid-run. It changed how I write every brief.

The brief said make the export faster. It read the code, came back with a question. Faster for a single large export or faster for many small ones. Those are two different fixes and it couldn't tell which one I wanted.

I had been writing briefs that assumed the machine would guess. It does guess. The guess is reasonable, and reasonable isn't the same as right. One question from it saved me an afternoon of a fix that solved the wrong half.

I added one line to my template and it has paid for itself since. If the goal can be met two ways, stop and ask. It asks about once every five runs and about four of those have been worth the interruption, which is a ratio I would take from a person and pay for gladly. The fifth was a clarification I had already answered in the brief, which is on me and my writing.

A tool that stops when it is unsure is worth more than a tool that's fast. I had been optimizing for the thing that runs to the end. The end isn't the goal.

I patched an old service for three more months instead of rewriting it and the patch turned out to be the better engineering.

Everyone wanted the rewrite. I wanted it too, because the code is ugly and the names are wrong and every change takes a day longer than my instinct says. What I did instead was write down what was wrong, in order, and then fix the first one.

A missing index. A query in a loop. Four lines. The service got fast enough that nobody mentioned the rewrite for a month. Then a config value that was read in eleven places. An hour of work and a whole class of bug disappeared.

Three months of patching removed about forty percent of the pain from a service we had been calling unrecoverable. That made the rewrite a smaller job, one page of description and a list of what it must not break, and it sits on the roadmap where it belongs.

I'd still say a rewrite is sometimes right. It's a promise about a future nobody has written down yet. My three months of patching taught me what the service does, which is the knowledge the rewrite always needed and no estimate ever had.

The most useful thing I learned from physics wasn't a fact. It was a habit of asking what would change my mind.

Good experimental design has that built in. You state the result in advance and you state what would make it false. Otherwise the data gets interpreted to fit whatever you already believed.

I started doing the same thing with my own opinions about technology. What evidence would make me stop believing this. The answer is uncomfortable because I have to admit that nothing I have seen could do it.

That's the tell. If no possible observation would change your position, you aren't holding a belief, you're holding an identity. I have found this in my own work constantly. I was certain one tool was better until I wrote down what would prove me wrong, and then I noticed I had never tested the thing I was certain about.

Stating the test in advance is cheap. Skipping it costs years.

#AI #stem #reproducibility #programming #tech #Science #CriticalThinking #ScientificMethod #Thinking #Evidence #Learning #Bias #cleancode #DecisionMaking #DeepTech #Physics #Research #Knowledge #Reasoning #Habits
---
I have one rule for buying tools and it has saved me more than any discount ever did.

If I can't start using it in fifteen minutes on a real task, I close the tab. Not because a learning curve is bad, and not because I'm impatient, but because I have a drawer full of things I intended to learn later. Later doesn't come. There's always a different afternoon.

The tools that survived on my machine all had the same quality. That's the test. I did something useful in the first quarter of an hour, however small. That tiny win is what makes you come back tomorrow.

Anything that asks for a day of setup before the first win is asking me to trust it on faith. I have no faith left to allocate. I have given it away too many times.

This applies to free things too. Free isn't the same as cheap if it costs a weekend. The cost of a tool isn't the price. It's the price plus the days before it does anything.

A good day here looks like shipping one small boring thing and deleting 2 features that turned out to be guesses.

The work is slower than I expected and better for it. On my own projects I'd pick the interesting problem and let the edges rot. Here, the edges are somebody's portfolio, so the interesting problem is usually the one nobody wants. Rate limits. Missing data. A provider that returns yesterday's date.

I've also had more arguments about wording than I ever had about architecture, and I think that's correct for this product. A sentence that implies a guarantee is a bug, even if every number in it is right.

My favourite part is how fast a question from a user turns into a change. Small team. Short distance from somebody being confused to the thing being fixed.

I've worked places where the product was a rumour. Watching it move because someone asked is a different feeling.

The hardest part of my week was picking a name for a function.

I spent forty minutes on it. The function is eleven lines long.

That ratio sounds stupid until you write the wrong name, come back in March, and have to read the whole thing to work out what it does. A bad name means you re-read the code every time you touch it. A good name means you skip it. Small thing. Enormous cost.

I keep a list of names I regret. Most of them are honest attempts at being clever. processData. handleStuff. doTheThing. Every one of those tells the next reader nothing, and the next reader is me. I have opened a file I wrote and had no idea what a function did. That's the worst feeling in this job.

So now I name things for what comes out, not what goes in. If the name needs a comment, the name is wrong. I would rather write fetchUnpaidInvoices than getData a hundred times over. It sounds trivial, and it saves me more time than any tool I installed this year.

The three words under my button did more for conversion than anything I changed in the layout.

My sign up button said Get Started. It's the most common phrase on the internet and it tells you nothing about what happens next. People hovered and left. I changed it to Create a free account. Same position, same color, same size. The click rate moved enough that I noticed it in a day.

Then I changed the field labels from vague to literal. Email instead of Contact. Password instead of Security. I watched the number of people who filled in the wrong box drop to nothing.

That's the whole discipline. Say what happens. Don't be clever. Don't make the reader translate.

Small text has an outsized effect because it sits exactly where a person is deciding. A heading can be vague and nobody minds. A button can't, because that's the moment of commitment. I now spend more time on twelve words of interface copy than on any graphic on the page. It's the cheapest design work that exists and it's the part everyone treats as an afterthought.

---
I deleted every comment in one of my files and the code got easier to read.

None of them were wrong. They were all true when I wrote them. The problem is that the code moved six times and the comments didn't move once. So a comment said this handles the empty case and the code above it handled the full case. I trusted it for a week.

I think comments that explain what the code does are a tax. They go stale, and a stale comment is worse than no comment because it lies with authority. Nobody re-reads them. Nobody updates them. They just sit there getting less true.

What I kept is the handful that explain why. Why this order. Why this ugly workaround exists. Why we aren't using the obvious approach.

Those don't rot, because the reason doesn't change when the code does. They're short and they're ugly and every one of them has a date. That's the split I use now. What is visible in the code. Why isn't.
