Accessibility options made my game better for people who don't need them and that surprised me.

I added remappable keys because it's the right thing to do. Then I played with a layout I had never tried, it was faster than mine, and my own default was worse. That stung.

I added a toggle for reducing screen shake. Turning it off made me notice something. I had been using screen shake to hide animation problems. Every one of those options was something I should have questioned on my own. I didn't, because I had been staring at it for months and it had stopped looking like a choice. That's the trap.

That's the pattern. Accessibility work forces you to justify assumptions you never knew you were making, and I had a lot of them. Now I add the options first instead of at the end. Building them in from the start means the systems have to be flexible, which is better engineering anyway. Cheaper too.

Doing the right thing and getting a better product are not in conflict. They look like a tradeoff when you leave it until the last week.

My logs told me nothing for two years and I only noticed when something broke badly.

Every line said the same thing. Starting. Finished. Error. No identifier, no duration, no idea which user or which request. When it went wrong at 2am I had a wall of identical text and a hunch.

A hunch isn't a debugging strategy. So I added four fields. A request id, a duration, an outcome, and one line of context. That's it. No logging framework, no dashboard, no vendor. Two weeks later the same class of bug took eleven minutes instead of most of a night. I searched for the request id, read four lines, and knew.

The bit that surprised me is how little I needed. I thought good logs meant more logs. It's the opposite. More lines make the useful ones harder to find. Four fields on every line beat a thousand lines with no shape.

I still get it wrong, but now when something breaks I read instead of guess.

#AI #cleancode #BuildInPublic #VibeCoding #tech #Programming #Observability #Debugging #Software #Developer #Engineering #Logging #Coding #Reliability #DevOps #backend #OpenSource #SoloBuilder #DeepTech #Infrastructure
---
I wrote money as a decimal and the totals came out a cent off. That cent cost me an afternoon.

A computer stores a decimal as a binary fraction, and some of those fractions can't be represented exactly. So a tenth isn't quite a tenth. Add it a few thousand times and the error shows up where people notice. I stopped using decimals for money at all. Store the smallest unit as a whole number. Cents, not dollars. Then addition is exact because whole numbers are exact.

It feels like a step backwards. It isn't. It's the difference between an accountant trusting your number and an accountant finding your mistake.

I still think about how invisible it was. My test with four rows passed. My test with four hundred rows passed. The drift only appears when you add up a lot of small things, which is what a real total does.

So now anything that people will count in money gets stored as an integer. Prices, fees, balances, totals. No exceptions and no cleverness. One bug taught me a rule I will keep for the rest of my career.

#AI #career #BuildInPublic #VibeCoding #tech #Programming #Developer #Software #Coding #Engineering #Fintech #Correctness #Debugging #work #OpenSource #SoloBuilder #DeepTech #Quality #Learning #Testing
---
The error message I wrote for myself six months ago saved me an hour this week. It was one sentence.

It said the config file was missing and gave the exact path it looked in. That's it. No stack trace theory, no advice, no apology. I hit it at 11pm, read the line, made the file, and moved on. I didn't have to open the source. I didn't have to remember anything.

Compare that to the errors I got from a library last month. Something went wrong. Check your input. Which input. Which line. No idea.

Bad errors cost the reader time and the writer nothing. Good errors cost the writer two minutes and save everyone else hours. It's one of the most lopsided trades in this job and almost nobody takes it.

So now every failure path I write has to say what failed, where, and what to do. If I can't fill those three in, I don't understand my own error case yet. That test has caught design problems before the code even ran.

I removed a confirmation dialog and my users stopped asking me about accidental deletes.

The dialog said are you sure. Two buttons. I thought it was protecting people. What it did was add a step to a frequent action and catch almost nothing, because people click through dialogs on reflex. The dialog had become a coin flip with a prompt.

So I replaced it with something better. Delete is instant, and a small bar at the bottom says what was deleted with an undo link that stays for ten seconds. Now the fast path is fast and the mistake is recoverable instead of prevented. That's the trade I had backwards. You can't stop someone making a mistake. You can make the mistake cheap.

I found undo harder to build than a dialog. It means the data isn't gone for a moment, which means more care in the layer below. Ten minutes of work.

Since then I ask one question about every confirmation. Am I preventing a mistake, or am I just making the user ask themselves. I usually find it's the second one.

I moved one project from a config file to plain defaults and it got used twice as often.

The old version had eleven settings. Port, log level, paths, retries, formats. All reasonable. All documented. All of it a decision I was handing to somebody who just wanted to run a thing.

Every setting is a question, and questions are a tax on starting. The first run is where you either keep a tool or close it forever. So I picked the values I use and hardcoded them. I cut the eleven settings to two. If you need the others, there's an environment variable and a page explaining it.

Nothing broke. Nothing was lost. The people who wanted to tune it still can. The people who didn't now get a working tool in one command.

The thing I got wrong for years is thinking flexibility is free. It isn't. You pay for every option with attention, and attention is the scarcest thing your user has. Defaults are an opinion. I would rather ship mine and be argued with than ship eleven blank boxes.

The most valuable thing I did after a bad outage was write down what I thought happened before anyone explained it.

I wrote it wrong. I was confident and I was wrong in an interesting direction. I blamed a dependency first. The real cause was a change I had made myself two days earlier.

That written guess is why I learned anything. If I had just read the explanation, I would have nodded and moved on. Having a wrong theory on paper forced the comparison. Now I do that after every failure, small or large. Three lines. What I expected, what happened, what would have caught it.

The last one is the only one that changes anything. It turns a vague resolution to be careful into a specific thing I can add. In this case, one alert on a number I wasn't watching.

Two years of writing those down and I have a list of mistakes I have mostly stopped repeating, which is worth more to me than any course I could have taken. Not because I got wiser. Because I wrote them down while they were fresh and painful. That's the whole method and it costs five minutes.

My code assumed every timestamp was in the same timezone and it worked fine for eight months.

Then a user in a different timezone scheduled something and it fired four hours early. Nobody lost money. It was still wrong.

I had been treating a date as a simple value. It isn't. It's a position on a line that depends on who is looking. Two people in two places can disagree about what today means and both be right.

The fix took twenty minutes. Store everything as a single reference and convert only when showing it to a person. That's the whole rule and it's older than I'm. What annoyed me is that I knew this. I had read it. I still wrote the naive version because my test data happened to sit in one timezone, which meant my tests were measuring nothing.

That's the pattern I keep finding. My tests pass because they share my assumptions. They aren't wrong. They're blind in the same place I'm. The bug was never the timezone. It was the confidence.

I build for investors who don't look like me, and the first time I watched that happen I got several things wrong.

Sessions, screen recordings, support threads. The users who ask the best questions are the ones with the least context, and they ask things I would never think to answer because I already know them. That gap is where the product gets better.

So we watch people use it rather than guess. Twenty minutes and 1 real question beats a week of me imagining a persona. The last session, somebody spent half of it looking for a number that was on the screen the whole time, which is a layout problem and not a data problem.

I've been guilty of building for the version of me that didn't exist yet. He knew the app. He knew the shortcuts. He had no questions. Real users have questions and they arrive in the wrong order.

That's why I like this job. The customers keep making me less wrong.

I pasted the same eight lines into four places and told myself I would tidy it up later.

Later didn't come. Then I fixed a bug in one of the four copies and shipped it. Three still had the bug. Nobody noticed for a month because three of the four paths were rare. The one I fixed was the common one, which is why my testing looked clean.

That's the thing about copy paste. It's not that the duplication is ugly. It's that you now have to remember every copy exists, and you won't. So I have a rule that's probably too strict. Two copies are fine. The third time I reach for the same lines I stop and pull them out.

The reason two is fine is that the abstraction is often worse than the duplication. I have built the shared helper too soon and ended up with a function that takes six flags and does four jobs. That's not better. That's a different mess.

The hard part is timing it. Not too early and not on the fourth copy. I still get that wrong maybe a third of the time.

My first version of anything is always too clever and I have stopped fighting it.

The pattern is reliable now. I sit down, I plan something sensible, and forty minutes later I have built a small framework. An abstraction. A thing that could handle cases I will never have.

Then I use it for a day and it annoys me. The second version is smaller and the third is almost nothing. The third is the one that survives. What changed isn't my instinct. It's my willingness to skip ahead. I now go straight to writing version three on purpose, which means ignoring the framework idea and writing the direct thing.

It doesn't work every time. Sometimes the clever version was needed, but that happens far less often than my brain insists it will.

The skill isn't having good first instincts. It's recognising your own predictable mistakes in time to route around them. I know what my brain does first. So I do something else first. Same every time.

I added a type annotation to a file and the compiler found three bugs I had shipped a week earlier.

None of them were dramatic. A value that could be missing, passed where it had to exist. A number passed where the function wanted text. The kind of thing that works until the one input that breaks it.

That's what I wanted from types. Not correctness proofs. Just a machine reading my code with fresh eyes and pointing at the places where I was vague. Being vague is the natural state. You write a function and it takes a thing. A thing is fine while you're inside it and confusing the moment you leave.

The annoying part is that I resisted this for a long time. I had decided types were ceremony. What changed my mind was a bug I couldn't reproduce, and a compiler that pointed at it in a second.

I don't annotate everything. I annotate the boundaries. Where data enters, where it leaves, where two systems meet. That's where the vagueness costs the most. Everywhere else I let it be messy and get on with it.

#AI #cleancode #BuildInPublic #VibeCoding #tech #Programming #Developer #Software #Coding #Engineering #TypeScript #Debugging #Quality #devops #OpenSource #SoloBuilder #DeepTech #Learning #CodeReview #Architecture
---
I stored a job in memory and lost it when the process restarted, which took me a while to notice because I never restarted it.

The design was simple. A queue in the process, a worker taking items off it. Fine on a laptop that stays on. Not fine anywhere else.

I learned that a process isn't permanent. It crashes, it gets redeployed, the machine reboots. Everything in memory goes with it, silently, and nobody gets an error. So the queue has to live somewhere that outlives the process. A table is enough. Pick a row, mark it as taken, delete it when done.

The bit I missed the first time is what happens if the worker dies halfway. The row stays marked forever and that job never runs again. So the mark needs a timestamp and a rule that reclaims anything older than a few minutes.

That's the whole outbox pattern. Store it first, then work. Anything still only in memory is not queued. It is a hope. I lost maybe four jobs before I noticed. Silent data loss is the worst kind because there's nothing to read.

#AI #learning #BuildInPublic #VibeCoding #tech #Programming #Developer #Software #Backend #Engineering #Queues #Reliability #DistributedSystems #Coding #research #OpenSource #SoloBuilder #DeepTech #Architecture #DataEngineering
---
I only trust tests I have watched fail.

That sounds obvious. It isn't, because the natural workflow is to write the test after the code, run it once, watch it go green, and feel good. Green tells you almost nothing. It might be testing the wrong thing. It might be testing nothing. The way to know a test does its job is to break the code on purpose and check that it screams.

I did this on a small utility last month. Nine tests, all green. I broke each function in turn and four of the tests didn't notice. They were asserting on shapes that couldn't change. Decoration.

After fixing them I broke the code again and all nine caught it. That took an hour. It's the most valuable hour in that whole project.

Since then my rule is blunt. A test I have never seen fail is a comment with extra steps. It's slower at the start. It's the reason I can change things later without that feeling in my chest.

Indie games are where the interesting design decisions live because there's no money to hide behind.

I understand why a studio satisfies a spreadsheet before an idea. That isn't a criticism, it's just arithmetic. Budgets come with expectations and expectations come with a list of things you aren't allowed to do. A small team has a different problem. Every feature is a real tradeoff against sleep. So the decisions are visible and they're honest.

Some of the best mechanics of the last few years came out of one person working around a limitation. Not ambition. Constraint.

I notice this in my own work now. When I remove an option, the design gets sharper. When I add one, it gets mushier because now I have two things to balance instead of one committed bet.

I know most indie games fail and the failure is rarely the idea. It's scope, time, and finishing. I keep coming back to one constraint. It isn't money. It's how many months of your life the idea is worth.

I read code I wrote a year ago and couldn't follow it. That was more useful than any course.

I remembered building it. I remembered thinking it was clear. What I couldn't remember was why this file had three entry points and a function that returned two different shapes depending on a flag. Past me had reasons. Past me didn't write them down. So present me was reading a decision with the reasoning cut off.

That's what most bad code actually is. Not stupidity. A reasonable choice, made under pressure, with the context lost.

So I changed one habit. When I make a decision that isn't obvious, I write one line explaining it for whoever reads next. Half the time that person is me, and I'm a stranger to my own work by then.

The other thing it did was make me less precious. I stopped defending old code. It was written by somebody with less information and no reason to feel bad about that. Reading my own history is the only review I get with no social cost.

Every energy argument I read skips the same step, which is how much of the power we make is wasted.

Everybody argues about generation. Solar versus nuclear versus gas. That argument is loud and it's also not where the biggest number lives.

The grid loses power in transmission and buildings lose heat through walls. Data centers spend a shocking share of their draw on cooling the machines they just heated up, and fixing that waste would shrink the generation debate. That's less exciting than a new reactor, which is why nobody campaigns on it.

I care about this because I build things that run on servers. My own little tools have a footprint and I have never once measured it. That's on me. I should. The uncomfortable part is that efficiency is boring. Insulation doesn't photograph well. Nobody writes a viral post about better ductwork.

But the cheapest kilowatt hour is the one you didn't need. I have never heard a serious person in energy argue otherwise. Almost nobody in the public argument repeats it.

I put a password in a file and committed it, and that mistake is permanent in a way I didn't understand.

Deleting the line later doesn't help. The history keeps it. Anyone who clones the repo can read every version back to the first one.

I got lucky. It was a test key for a service I didn't care about. I rotated it, deleted the whole repo, and started again. Twenty minutes and no real harm.

What I took from it's that a leaked secret isn't a mistake you fix. It's a mistake you respond to. Rotate first, clean up second. The order matters because the value is compromised the moment it's pushed.

So now the config lives in an environment file that never gets committed, and there's a checked in example file with fake values so a new machine knows what to set. Nothing clever. No vault, no encryption, no ceremony. Just a hard line between what's in the repo and what isn't. It's the least interesting habit I have and the one I'd least want to drop.

I ran a small model on my own machine for a month and it taught me more about the work than any benchmark did.

Slower. Not as good at the hard questions. But it never sent anything anywhere and it never went down, and those two things changed which tasks I was willing to hand over. The boring transform across forty files stopped being a decision. I just ran it locally and read every diff.

The interesting result is where it failed. It failed on judgement and it was fine on mechanics, which is the same split I see between a junior and a senior person. The task didn't need a better model. It needed a narrower task.

So my split is deliberate now. Local for private, repetitive and mechanical. Hosted for anything requiring taste. Neither one replaced the other and I stopped pretending one would.

My laptop is warm and my bill is lower.

I kept a function around for two years because removing it might break something.

It was used exactly nowhere. I checked twice, six months apart, and both times the answer was the same. Nothing called it.

What kept it alive was the possibility. What if some path I haven't thought about needs it. What if a user hit it in a way I can't see. That fear is real when you have users. It's a fantasy when the only user is you and the whole thing runs on one machine.

So I deleted it. Nothing broke. The file got shorter, and the shorter file took me less time to read the next week. Now I use a blunt test. If I can't name who calls it, it goes. If I'm wrong, the error is loud and instant and I put it back in a minute.

The cost of deleting something you needed is tiny. The cost of keeping things you don't is a file that's slow to read forever. I had those two costs backwards for a long time.

I asked a model a question I already knew the answer to, and it told me I was wrong with total confidence.

That's the part that gets me. Not the error. That it was fluent and wrong at the same time, in the exact register of something that had checked. I caught it because I knew the domain. If I hadn't, I would have shipped it.

So I stopped using model output as an answer and started using it as a first draft from somebody who has read everything and verified nothing. I keep the claims I can check. The rest is a useful shape with the substance missing.

The trick that helps most is asking it to argue the other side. A model that has to disagree with itself will show you where the seams in its own answer are, and they are usually in the places you would not have thought to check.

I trust it more now. That means I check it more.

My deploy used to scare me and that fear was quietly making my code worse.

If shipping is frightening, you bundle. You wait until three changes are ready so you pay the fear once. Which means three changes land together, and when it breaks you don't know which one did it.

I was doing that. Then I made the boring change instead. Same day, one small commit, watch it for two minutes.

Turns out the fear was the problem, not the deploy. Once it landed forty times with nothing going wrong, it stopped being an event and became a step. Small deploys are easier to reason about, easier to reverse, and easier to spot. The one thing they're worse at is feeling dramatic. I will take it.

I care about the tooling here but it's not the point. What matters is time from commit to running, measured in minutes. If that number is hours you will batch, no matter how disciplined you think you're. Mine is about ninety seconds now, which is short enough that waiting to bundle three changes together costs me more than shipping them one at a time. That single number changed how I write code more than any technique I have read about.

I turned off most of my alerts and started noticing real problems sooner.

I used to get an email about everything. Disk usage, a slow query, a spike in requests. All true, none of it urgent. Within a month I had trained myself to ignore the whole channel, which is worse than having no alerts at all. An alert you don't act on is noise with a badge. It teaches you that warnings are normal, which is the opposite of what an alert is for.

So I cut them down to the ones where I would get up. If nothing bad happens when I ignore it, it isn't an alert. It's a chart, and charts belong on a dashboard I check when curious. That reframe took maybe fifteen minutes and it changed my relationship with the whole thing.

The other half is the opposite problem. A silent failure is just as expensive as a loud pointless one. So for anything that should happen and didn't, there's one check and it fires once.

Two alerts total. Both mean something. I act on both. Fewer and louder beats many and ignored, and I have never once wished I had more of them.

A dashboard I built showed a number going up and to the right for three months and it meant nothing.

Sessions were growing. I felt good about it. Then I looked at where the sessions came from and found one bot hitting the same page on a loop.

I wasn't being lied to. It was answering a question I hadn't asked. Sessions went up. Real people didn't.

That's the trap with any number you can see without thinking. It's easy to watch, so you watch it, and you stop asking what it measures. I write the definition next to the number now. Sessions means unique identifiers seen in a day, excluding anything without a completed action. Once you write that sentence you find out whether you have an opinion or just a chart.

Half the time writing the definition is enough to make the metric useless, and that's the best possible outcome. Better to learn that in a sentence than in a quarter. Numbers don't have meaning. Definitions do, and I was skipping them.

I made one endpoint safe to call twice and it changed how confident I felt about the whole system.

It was a create action. Call it once and you get one thing. Call it again with the same request and you got a second copy. A user who tapped twice because the first tap looked slow ended up with two of everything.

I give each request a name. If the server has seen that name before, it returns the original result instead of making a new one. Same request, same answer, every time.

That one change removed a whole class of problem. Retries became safe. A flaky connection stopped being able to duplicate work. My test suite got shorter because I stopped writing tests for the double submission case. It's a few lines and one extra field. What it buys is the ability to retry anything without checking whether it's safe first, and that's worth much more than the code.

I put it on every write endpoint now. Create, update, charge, send. Anything where doing it twice would be a real problem. The boring guarantees are the ones that let you sleep.

I read forty support messages in one sitting and found the same complaint eleven times.

Not the same words. The same feeling. People couldn't tell whether the thing had saved. So they did it twice and got confused.

Nobody had ever reported it as a bug. It wasn't a bug. It was a silence where a confirmation should be. I never read them together, which is the reason it survived. Support messages arrive one at a time and each one looks like an individual, but read in a batch the pattern is impossible to miss.

So now I do that every few weeks. Paste them all into one file, read straight through, count the repeats. It takes half an hour and it has never once found nothing.

I read both now. Analytics tells you what people did. Messages tell you what confused them. The second one is usually more specific and it arrives with an explanation. The best product decisions I have made came from a pile of complaints, not a chart.

I priced my work at what the hours cost me instead of what the result was worth, and I undercharged for two years.

My maths was honest and useless. I counted the hours, added a margin, and sent it. What I left out is that the value to the person paying didn't depend on how long I took. A script that saves them ten hours a week is worth the same whether I wrote it in a day or a month.

So the questions changed. What does this replace. What does a mistake cost them. Would they rather have it next week or in six weeks. Those answers set the number, and the hours only tell me whether I can afford to say yes.

The uncomfortable part is saying a bigger number out loud for the first time. My voice did something strange.

Nobody blinked. I had been the only one anchoring the price.

My search box returned nothing for a typo and I lost a user in four seconds.

I watched one person type the name wrong. The system compared two strings, found no match, and displayed an empty page with no suggestions. From the user's side the product is broken. They don't know that a character was off.

So I stopped comparing strings for equality and started ranking them by how close they're. I wrote a few lines. No search engine, no index, no infrastructure. Just stop demanding exactness from people who are typing on a phone.

The empty result page got fixed too. It now says what it looked for and offers two things that are almost right. Almost right is a huge amount better than nothing. Nothing tells the user they're wrong. Almost right tells them they're close.

I did it in an afternoon. That is the highest ratio of user relief to engineering effort I have ever shipped, and I have shipped plenty of things that took far longer. I wish I had watched one person use search before building anything else.

AI in game development is being argued at the wrong level and both sides are skipping the interesting part.

I get the argument about art and jobs. It's real. There is a smaller question underneath it and nobody asks it. Which parts of making a game are bottlenecked by human taste and which are just slow?

Animation blending is slow. Dialogue trees are slow. Placeholder geometry is slow. All of that is grunt work and none of it's where the game becomes good.

The design, the feel, the pacing, the moments, that's where taste lives and it isn't on the table. So the honest position is that the grunt work is going away and the taste work is getting more valuable, which sounds encouraging until you remember that the grunt work is where people learned the taste.

You can't skip the boring middle and arrive at judgment, and I don't know a shortcut past it. I've watched it take years for people who are better at this than me. I find that part worrying and it doesn't fit into either side of the argument.

The screen a new user sees before they have any data is the one that decides whether they stay, and I ignored it for months.

My app opened on an empty list. No rows, no explanation, no next step. Just a heading and white space that looked like a loading failure.

I had built the populated version because that's the version I saw every day. My account had data in it. The empty state was a case I never inhabited.

So I made it a real screen. One sentence about what this list will hold, one button that creates the first item, and a small example so the shape is obvious before you commit anything. The drop off on day one moved. Not by much, but it moved, and the change took under two hours.

The lesson isn't about onboarding. It's that I only ever test the state I live in. Every screen has a version with nothing, and that version is the first one most people see. I now check every list and every page with the data removed. It's the cheapest review I know.

I have several phones running Meta's Muse and none of them needed a laptop to set up. The muse-relay that links them together was written by Muse itself. I gave it the problem and it built the plumbing that lets one chat board drive every phone at once.

Muse told me it couldn't run mcp servers. That's wrong. I wrote mcp-gateway instead, and now those phones reach tools and data the chat surface was never meant to expose. It took an afternoon. No team. No permission. No special access.

That is what bothers me. One keyboard driving many phones, each with its own identity and its own accounts, took me days. If I can do that on my own hardware, so can anyone. Meta should treat it as a real hole and close it, because the next person won't be doing it to learn.

The other half is cost. Some of the muses have their own github accounts, mail logins, chatgpt and deepseek sessions, and the ai-freebuff and ai-opencode tools on the command line. When a job is big they send one large prompt to ai-freebuff or ai-opencode and that CLI does the work. It keeps Meta's usage low. Hand an agent the right accounts and it stops leaning on the expensive one.

Batteries are the part of the energy story that decides the timeline and almost nobody talks about them.

Generation gets the headlines. But solar and wind both produce power at times we don't control. Without storage, a great afternoon of production is worth very little at 9pm. Every serious plan for a renewable grid becomes a storage plan. It takes about two paragraphs. It always does.

I know the progress is real. It's measured in small percentages per year, not breakthroughs. Cost per kilowatt hour keeps sliding down and cycle life keeps getting longer. It's slow and it compounds.

I was surprised by how much of the problem isn't chemistry. It's permitting, land, and the fact that nobody wants a facility near their house, which is a social problem wearing a technical costume, and that kind is the hardest to fix.

I follow this stuff because I care about where the power for the servers I use comes from. Pretending the compute is invisible doesn't make it free.

What sold me was the list of things the product refuses to do.

No custody of money. No trades placed on its own. No telling you what to buy. The list is longer than the feature list and it came up in my first interview, which tells you what kind of company it is.

I'd turned down work before because the pitch needed me to be comfortable with a promise I couldn't keep. Here the limits are the pitch. We're useful because we don't pretend to know the future.

That sounds like a small distinction until you've built for the other version. Every extra claim creates a user who is disappointed later, and the disappointment lands on whoever trusted the screen. I'd rather have a smaller product and a boring support inbox.

So the honesty isn't a constraint we tolerate. It's the reason the thing is worth building, and the reason I'm not embarrassed to say where I work.

I added an animation to my app and it made the whole thing feel slower even though nothing got slower.

A panel slid in over three hundred milliseconds. Pretty on its own. Awful when you open that panel twenty times in a session, because you're now waiting three hundred milliseconds, twenty times, for information you already decided you wanted. Motion has a budget and I had been spending it on the wrong things.

So I cut the panel animation to almost nothing and moved the budget to the places where it communicates something. A row appearing where you dropped it. A button that shows it registered your click. Those tell you what happened. The sliding panel just told you it was important.

I use one rule now. Motion should explain a change of state, not decorate one. The test is simple. If I remove it and nothing is confusing, it was decoration. If removing it makes you lose track of where something went, it was doing a job.

Most of my animations were decoration and I had talked myself into calling them polish.

I built dark mode by inverting my colors and shipped the ugliest version of my own app.

Pure black backgrounds and pure white text. It looked sharp for ten seconds and hurt after ten minutes. The contrast was so high that every paragraph felt like it was shouting. The fix is to stop using the extremes. Almost black backgrounds, slightly off white text, and let the borders do more work than they did in light mode because shadows don't read on dark surfaces.

Then there's the part nobody warns you about. Every hardcoded color in the app doubles. Every green that meant success now needs a version that reads on dark. I had about thirty of them by then and I found them one complaint at a time.

The thing I should have done is put the colors in one place at the start. I didn't. Dark mode is the audit that found out.

It's still worth building. It's just not a filter you apply at the end. It's a promise about how you store color, and you either made it on day one or you're doing a cleanup.

I wrote the prompt like a specification and the model stopped guessing, which is the whole job.

My early attempts were one line. Fix this. It would fix something, and often it fixed the wrong thing, and I would blame the model for filling a gap I left open. The gap was mine. An ambiguous request gets an ambiguous answer.

So I write three things now. What the input looks like. What the output should look like. What must not change. That last line does most of the work, because most of the damage a model does is to something adjacent that was already fine.

It takes two minutes and it saves the twenty I would have spent reviewing a change I didn't want. I also keep the prompts in the repository next to the code they describe.

A prompt is a decision, and decisions belong somewhere a reader can find them.

I checked my app on a phone for the first time in three months and found four broken screens.

Everything sat above the fold on a laptop. On a phone the same layout pushed the button below the keyboard, cut the heading off mid word, and made one table impossible to read. None of it was hard to fix. All of it had been live for weeks.

The reason is that I develop on a big screen and the narrow case never appears by accident. You have to go looking for it. It's not a bug that finds you.

So I changed my default. When I finish a screen I resize the window down to phone width before I call it done. It's one keystroke.

That single habit caught more problems than the review process I don't have. I also started writing simpler layouts. A layout that survives a narrow screen is usually a tidier one on a wide screen too, because it can't rely on elbow room to hide a bad grouping. Small screens aren't an edge case. They're the default and I was treating them as an extra.

The reason I took this job is that the person on the other end of it is getting a worse deal than the people already in the building.

That sounds dramatic. It isn't. Research quality is a function of time and access. Most people have neither. So they get their information from a headline, a comment section, or a creator who is paid to be certain. Then they make a real decision with real money.

I stopped clicking buttons with my mouse for a week and found three screens I couldn't operate at all.

Not slow. Impossible. A dropdown that opened on hover and nothing else. A dialog with no visible focus ring, so I had no idea which field I was in. A close button that needed a click.

I'm not a keyboard user out of principle. I did it to test, and it turned out to be a better way to use my own app. Focus rings are the clearest example of the problem. I had removed them because they looked untidy in screenshots. What I removed was the indication of where you are, for anyone not using a mouse.

The fix was a few lines of style. The lesson is that I had shipped an app that some people simply couldn't use, and I hadn't noticed because I have a trackpad.

Now I tab through every new screen before I finish it. It takes a minute. It catches something maybe half the time. Accessibility testing is the highest return review I do and it's the one I used to skip.

I get to argue about the word may for 40 minutes and call it work.

Last week the question was whether a sentence implied we knew something we didn't. Not the data. The wording. We changed one word, rewrote the surrounding line, and moved on. Total cost, one meeting and 2 rewrites. That's a normal afternoon.

People outside finance think this stuff is paperwork. It's language. Every hedge in a sentence is there because somebody got burned by the version without it, and the distance between could rise and will rise is the entire product.

I like that the stakes are made of grammar. It means my work isn't done when the feature works. It's done when the feature can't be misread by somebody who is tired and in a hurry.

Nobody here treats that as pedantry. They treat it as the job, which is why I stopped rolling my eyes at it around week 2.

I guessed two days on a task and it took nine. That's not the problem. The problem is that I told nobody.

I worked in silence because I kept thinking I was nearly there. Each day I found one more thing and each one felt like the last. I had missed two other things by day six and nobody knew. The estimate wasn't the failure. The estimate was a guess, and guesses are supposed to be wrong sometimes. The failure wasn't saying anything while the guess decayed.

So I changed one habit. Now when I pass the point where I thought I would be done, I say so immediately, with what I found and what I think now. It's uncomfortable and it takes one sentence.

Doing that costs almost nothing at the time. Doing it late costs everything, because by then the other people have built plans on top of my silence. Estimates aren't a promise. They're a snapshot of what I know, and they should update out loud.

I'm still bad at guessing. I'm much better at saying when I'm wrong.

---
A health check that only asks if the process is alive will report success while the app serves nothing.

I built one of those. It returned ok. The web server could answer. The database could be unreachable and it still said ok. So the monitor showed green through an outage that users were writing to me about.

I had the check asking the wrong question. Alive isn't the same as working. So I changed it to do the things a real request does. Read a row, write a row, delete the row. If any of those fail, the answer isn't ok and it should say which one failed.

That last part matters more than the status. A check that says unhealthy sends me looking. A check that says the write failed and the read worked sends me to the answer.

Mine takes about eighty milliseconds now and it has caught two problems before a user did. Cheap insurance.

I found a piece of code I wrote last year and could see it was holding the whole file together and also causing every problem in it.

That's the shape of tech debt and it's not laziness. Somebody made a good decision for the constraints they had. The constraints changed. The decision didn't.

What makes it hard is that the same thing is load bearing and broken. You can't delete it. Things depend on it. You can't leave it because it's where every bug lives.

So the honest move is replacement in place, one caller at a time, with both versions running next to each other until the last one moves. Slow, boring, and it never breaks anything. The version I tried first was a rewrite. I started a fresh file, got three days in, and discovered I had dropped four behaviours that existed in the old code.

That's how I learned to respect the thing. That mess held more information than I could hold in my head. Rewriting is the tempting move and it's usually the expensive one.

War taught a generation of people something the rest of us got from a screen.

That gap isn't going away and I think it explains more political argument than any ideology does. Two people can look at the same event and be describing different things, because one of them has a body memory of it. Technology changed how war is reported and didn't change how it's experienced. You can watch a strike live and still have no idea what the street smelled like afterwards.

The asymmetry that worries me is the incentive. Coverage rewards the dramatic frame and the slow aftermath doesn't photograph. Reconstruction takes years and gets one article. One article. Years of work.

I don't have a solution. I do have a rule, which is to be suspicious of my own certainty about anything I have seen in a clip.

That applies to me specifically. I have formed a lot of opinions this year from footage and I have never once verified any of them against anyone who was there. That's worth admitting out loud.

I had a feature flag on for eight months and I could no longer remember which way was on.

That's the failure mode nobody mentions. Flags start as a way to test a change without risking the live one. They become a second version of your program, and every combination is a program you have never tested. I had three flags by then, which is eight states. I had tested two.

So I did the cleanup I should have scheduled on day one. The winner became the path. The loser got deleted. The flag got removed from the config.

The whole thing took an afternoon and removed more code than any refactor I had done that quarter. What I do differently now is write the removal date when I add the flag. Not a reminder, a date, in the description next to a link to the task that removes it. Half the value is admitting at creation time that this is temporary.

A flag is a loan against your future attention. It's fine to take. It's not fine to forget you took it.

I joined for the AI and I stayed for the people who disagree with me.

The technology was the hook. It's interesting, it changes every month, and it's genuinely hard. But interest fades and hard gets tiring. What keeps me here is that I'm surrounded by people who will spend an hour on whether a sentence is fair.

That's not glamorous, and it isn't in the job description. It's the difference between a job that drains you and one you think about in the shower. I applied for the problem, not the title.

I've also never worked somewhere that treated disagreement as cheap. Nobody saves it for the review cycle. You say it when you see it, because being wrong early is cheap and being wrong in production is somebody's money.

6 months in, I still like these people. That's the most I've been able to say about a team in 10 years. I plan to keep it that way.

---
A retry loop I added to handle flaky networks nearly took down a service by hammering it.

I thought the logic was careful. If the call fails, wait a bit and try again. Three attempts. What I hadn't thought about is that a failing server is the worst possible moment to send more requests, and everyone retrying at once makes the failure worse.

The fix isn't to retry harder. It's to retry less and agree on a rule. Wait longer each time, so the fourth attempt is minutes later not milliseconds. Add a random offset so a thousand clients don't fire together. That second one is the part I'd never have guessed. Randomness makes the retries spread out instead of arriving in a wave.

Then there's the question underneath all of it. Should this retry at all. If the call isn't safe to repeat, retrying might charge a card twice. Retries need to know that.

Mine didn't. It was one line and it could have duplicated work. I got away with it because the operation happened to be harmless. I got lucky and I've stopped pretending that's the same as being right.

The best thing I did for my FPS was build a tiny level that takes eleven seconds to cross.

No textures. Grey boxes. One room. Just enough to test movement, shooting, and the camera in the smallest space that could still be fun. Every time I changed something about how the player moves I ran that level twenty times in a row. It takes four minutes. I can feel a difference of two frames.

I worked in the real levels for months, which was a mistake. Too much noise. By the time I got to the interesting corner I had forgotten what I was testing. Small test spaces are the game equivalent of a unit test. Not fun to build, not impressive to show anyone, and they catch everything.

I keep three now. Movement. Combat. Camera. Each one fits on a screen.

I care about this because feel is a hundred small decisions and you can't evaluate a hundred things at once in a big level. You need a place where the only thing that changed is the thing you changed.

---
Two machines disagreed about what time it was by four seconds and it broke a signature check.

The check was correct. Sign with the current time, verify that the time is recent. Simple and standard. My server and the other one had drifted by a few seconds, so a signed request looked like it came from the future.

Nothing was hacked. Two clocks just disagreed, which is normal and expected. The fix is to allow a window. A couple of minutes either side instead of a few seconds. That absorbs normal drift without accepting an old request.

What I learned is that time is a distributed resource and I had been treating it like a local one. I treat every machine as having its own opinion. So now anything involving clocks gets an explicit tolerance, written as a named number in the config, not buried in the code. If it's a security boundary the value matters and someone should have to think about it.

I expected an exotic bug. The cause was ordinary.

#AI #cleancode #BuildInPublic #VibeCoding #tech #Programming #Developer #Software #Engineering #Security #DistributedSystems #Backend #Reliability #Coding #devops #OpenSource #SoloBuilder #DeepTech #Architecture #Learning
---
Data centers are the new factories and the comparison is more useful than it sounds.

A factory converts inputs into goods and concentrates workers in one place, while a data center converts power into computation and concentrates machines in one place. Both reshape the town they sit in, and both need water, a grid connection, and a fight with the neighbours.

The difference is that a factory was visible. You could see the shift change. You can't see a warehouse of servers doing anything. That invisibility is why the argument keeps missing. People experience AI through an app and never think about the building. The building is the part with the constraints.

Land, power, water, and cooling are the real limits. Not model quality. We are going to hit the physical ceiling long before we run out of ideas. Probably sooner.

I spent a weekend reading about grid queues after a thread like this one and came away less sure, not more. I say this as someone who uses these services daily and has never once looked up where my requests land. That's the honest position. I benefit from infrastructure I don't think about and can't see.

Peer review is breaking under load and I've watched it happen from the outside. Reviewers are unpaid, papers are up, and the people asked to check the work are the same people competing with it.

That's a structural problem, not a lazy problem. I've reviewed work for free and I've had my own work reviewed by somebody with a deadline and no budget. Both experiences were honest and both were shallow.

So I read preprints with a different posture than I read a journal article. Not because preprints are worse. Because the review step is a filter and the filter is thinner than it used to be.

I don't have a fix. Nobody does. I do have a habit. I check who reviewed it, whether the data is posted, and whether anybody has run it since.

That habit costs me twenty minutes. That's the price. It has caught real problems. I'd rather have the habit than a stronger opinion about the system.

Four minutes. That's how long the fix took once I stopped guessing and read the manual for the tool I'd been using for three years.

I'd built a workaround instead. About forty lines of parsing that existed because I assumed the library couldn't do the thing. I assumed it because it hadn't done it the first way I tried, and that was enough for me.

The manual had a section on exactly my case. Two options, one of which did what I wanted in a single call. I read it in the browser, on the same machine, while my workaround sat in the repo looking embarrassed.

So the rule I keep from it is about the order of effort. Twenty minutes of documentation before four hours of invention. I've watched myself skip that step a dozen times since and I catch it sooner now.

Nobody teaches this. Read first. Write second. It's unglamorous and it doesn't produce anything you can show. It just stops you from writing forty lines that already exist somewhere.

I said yes to everything for about three years and the cost wasn't the extra work. It was that none of it was good. I want to be precise about that.

Every individual yes was reasonable. This is small. This will take an hour. And each one was. What I never accounted for was the switching, which is about twenty minutes on either side of an hour of real work, and nobody puts that in the estimate.

The other cost was that I was never available for the thing that needed me. Somebody would ask me to look at something important and I'd say Thursday, because Tuesday and Wednesday were full of things that had seemed reasonable.

What changed it was writing down what I was saying no to when I said yes. Not the time. The other task. Saying yes to the small thing is saying no to the thing behind it, and that list got uncomfortable fast.

Yes is cheap in the moment. The bill arrives as a month of work that's fine and none of it is good.

---
A refactor paid for itself in nine days and I know the number because I counted the support tickets before and after.

The change was small. One function that built a price had four branches and a comment saying do not touch. I split it into four named functions and wrote a test for each branch with real numbers from a customer receipt.

Before, about six tickets a month came from that area. After, it was zero for nine days, then one in the next month, and that one was something else wearing a similar subject line.

What sold the rewrite to the team wasn't the design. It was the ticket count. I've learned that arguments about code quality go nowhere and arguments about a number that appears on somebody's dashboard end in four minutes.

I've used the same trick since. Pick the metric that the ugliness costs you, count it for two weeks, then propose the change. You don't need to convince anybody.

I'd tell my younger self one thing about work and it isn't about the code. It's about how long to stay.

I stayed at a job for about two years after I'd stopped learning. I told myself I was being loyal and I told myself the equity would matter. What I was doing was buying comfort with my own time and calling it patience. The thing I couldn't see from inside was how small my picture had become.

I was very good at that company and less and less aware of what the rest of the field was doing. That's the trap of being the strongest person in a small pond. The pond becomes the world. What I'd say now is to check every six months whether the list of things you can't do is getting shorter. If it isn't, you're not in a job. You're in a holding pattern with a salary.

That isn't a reason to leave tomorrow. It's a reason to know. Stay while the list shortens. Leave when it stops.

A flag I set in January was still on in September and I'd forgotten it existed. Nobody was harmed and I spent a bad hour finding out.

What made it bad was the double life. The code had two paths and only one of them got tested for eight months. So the number of states I was maintaining without knowing was two, and I'd have told anybody who asked that it was one.

I went through the config after that. Found two more like it. Both bad. One had been on the whole time and the other had been off the whole time, which means neither was a decision. They were both just weather.

So I keep a rule now. A flag gets a date and a reason in the same commit, and a task to remove it. If I can't write the removal task, the flag isn't ready to ship, because it means I don't know what it's for.

I've shipped four flags since, all with dates on them. Two are gone. Each removal took about nine minutes.

A pile of frontier models launched in ten days this month. Five announcements, each with a benchmark table, each claiming a state of the art.

I read the tables and then I go do the thing. That's the only test I trust. A model that wins on a reasoning benchmark can still fail on the specific task I have, and the specific task is all I care about.

I've been through four of these launch cycles now. The pattern is stable. Capability goes up. Price per token goes down. Reliability at the edges moves much more slowly than the middle of the distribution.

That last point is the one that matters for shipping. A model that's right ninety five percent of the time and wrong in a different way each time is harder to build on than one that's right eighty percent of the time in the same way, because you can code around a known failure.

So I don't upgrade on release day. I upgrade when a task I already fail becomes a task I pass.

A copy paste cost me half a day last week and I've stopped treating that as a beginner mistake. I do it too, and I do it when I'm confident.

What happened is boring. I copied a block from a sibling function, changed the three obvious values, and missed the fourth because it sat on a line that looked like commentary. It wasn't commentary. It was the one variable that mattered.

The reason is that copying carries context into a place that doesn't have it. The block was correct next to its neighbour and wrong eight hundred lines away, and reading it back didn't help, because I read what I expected to see.

So now the last step of a copy is a read of the whole thing as a stranger, out loud, with the variables named. Two minutes. It has caught the missing value three times since, which is a better rate than my attention span.

I've stopped calling it laziness. It's a known failure mode of being fast, and the fix is a step rather than a resolution.

---
Launch cadence went up and the interesting effect isn't the rockets. It's that the cost of a bad idea fell. A lot.

I've watched this in software. When the cost of a deploy drops, teams ship more experiments and more of them fail. That's good, and it looks like chaos from outside.

The same thing is happening in orbit. A payload that would have needed a decade of justification can now ride along on a cheap ride and prove itself or die. I've watched instruments get funded on the strength of one cheap flight.

So the part of the space story I care about isn't the headline launch. It's the number of small things flying and the failure rate that nobody reports. Failure at a low price is how a field learns. Cheap failure. That's the trick.

I don't know which of them matters. Nobody does. That's the point of sending so many.

A design review went against me and I said so in the room rather than after it. That sentence changed how two other people treated the next month.

I'd argued for a schema shape for about ten minutes and made a decent case. Then somebody put two queries on the screen and my shape needed a join those queries couldn't afford at the size we were planning for.

The old habit would have been to agree out loud and then disagree in a message at nine that night. I've done that. It makes the meeting feel fine and it makes the project worse, because the decision sits there with a note of doubt under it.

What I did instead was say I was wrong about the shape, in front of everybody, and then say what I'd want to see before I'd change my mind. That last part matters. Being wrong with a condition attached is still useful.

Two people started doing the same thing within a month. Nobody asked them to. Somebody just went first.

Our standup went from twenty minutes to seven because somebody finally asked what we were actually trying to learn each morning. Nobody had asked in two years.

The answer was simple. We wanted to know if anybody was stuck and whether any promise we made yesterday was now false. That's it. Once we said it out loud, most of the update became noise.

I cut the format. No status. One question instead. Is anything blocked or about to be. If nothing is, the meeting is ninety seconds and we leave. If something is, we stay until it's unblocked or somebody owns it.

I was worried people would feel out of the loop. The opposite happened, because the information that mattered moved to where it was useful, which was written down, and the meeting stopped being a reading of it.

Seven minutes is not a productivity win. It's twenty minutes of attention back for four people, five days a week. That adds up to real work.

A support email at seven in the morning changed my roadmap for the quarter and it was four lines long. Four lines. No anger. Just a description of what he was trying to do.

He was running a weekly report for a supplier and the export took the last column off when a row was long. That's it. He'd worked around it for three months by editing the file by hand, and he offered the workaround to me as if it were normal.

I fixed it that day and then read his message twice more, because the important part wasn't the bug. It was that he'd assumed the tool was like that. He'd priced my defect into his week and stopped thinking of it as a defect.

That's the kind of thing a survey never catches. Nobody writes down the workarounds they've stopped noticing. You find it by reading a support note about something else and looking at what's between the lines.

I asked him for a second example in the same area. He sent three. Two were related and one was a decision I'd made four years ago and never revisited.

Xbox cut 3,200 jobs and closed four studios. The reporting put the total at nearly 20,000 across two years. I still don't think the layoffs are the interesting part.

Here's what I keep saying. Nobody at that company decided games were bad. They decided the number was too high. That's a spreadsheet decision, not a taste decision, and it tells you exactly where the pressure came from.

The four studios were Compulsion, Double Fine, Ninja Theory and Undead Labs. Between them they shipped well reviewed games. That's the tell. Quality was not the variable in the room.

So when people argue about whether AI can make good games, I think they're arguing the wrong case. The tool was never on trial. The margin was.

I wrote a post two years ago saying the same thing and I got told I was being cynical. I'm not. Cynical would be saying it doesn't matter. I think it matters a lot, which is why I want the real motive written down instead of the comfortable one. Blame the tool and the spreadsheet wins again.

I ran six side projects over two years and one of them worked. I want to write down what was different, because it wasn't the idea.

Four of the six had a plan and a roadmap. Four failed. One of them had no roadmap at all and a single user, who was me, with a problem I had four times a week. It solved that and nothing else for the first year.

Second difference was the finish. Five of them got to about eighty percent and stalled, because eighty percent is where the interesting problems are solved and the boring ones begin. That one got finished because it was small enough to finish in a weekend and then never grew.

I stopped starting projects with ambition attached. If it needs a domain name and a landing page before it's useful, I've probably already lost.

Six projects. One shipped. I used to be embarrassed by that ratio and now it's just a fact about how I work.

I nearly quit a project twice in one month. Twice. Both times the thing that kept me in it was a person, not the work. I want to write about that, because the advice I'd been given was all about the work.

The first time I was three weeks into a rewrite and the scope was half again what we'd estimated. What kept me in was a colleague who said he'd seen this exact shape before and that the middle was the bad part. The second time came after a bad review where a stakeholder said the design wasn't ambitious enough.

What kept me in was a different person asking what would make it ambitious, out loud, in the room. He didn't defend my work and he didn't argue. He asked for a definition. Both times the help was small. Both times it came from somebody who wasn't trying to solve my problem.

That's what I try to do now. Not general encouragement, which is nice and does nothing. One specific sentence that moves the stuck part. I name the shape when I can. It costs nothing and it's the whole difference.

Somebody paid me for my writing for the first time this year and the number was small enough to print on a napkin. It changed how I write anyway.

Two hundred dollars. A short piece about a tool I use. It took about four hours, so the hourly rate was poor. The thing I noticed was that I checked the facts four times, because somebody else was on the hook for them.

That's the part I kept. When it's my feed, a soft claim gets a soft word and I move on. When it's somebody else's publication, every number gets a tab open next to it and a source I can name.

I've kept the second habit and dropped the first one. It costs me about twenty extra minutes per post and it has cut how often I have to correct myself.

Four hours, two hundred dollars, one better habit. Cheap lesson. I'd take that trade again, and I'd take it at half the price, since the habit is the thing I kept.

I run a small open weight model on my own laptop for private work and I'll tell you the trade honestly. I've been doing it for eight months.

It's slower. The context is smaller. The output is worse than the frontier options on anything hard, and I have to think about quantization and memory in a way I never do in the cloud.

I still use it, because the data never leaves the machine and the cost doesn't move when usage does. For anything with a name in it, that trade is worth more to me than a benchmark point.

The part nobody mentions is the maintenance. A local setup is a pet. You update it, you debug it, you own every failure. That's real work and it lands on you at the worst time.

So my advice is to run both and be clear about which is which. Cloud for the hard problems, local for the private ones. And read the license before anything goes near a release.

---
I've never read a bad bug report with more than two of the four things in it. The four are what they did, what they saw, what they expected, and the version.

When I get a report with all four, I can fix the thing without asking a single question. Ten minutes instead of two days.

What I've noticed is that people write good reports when the product makes it easy. The vague ones arrive when my own error handling gave them nothing to write down, so the quality of the report is partly my fault.

So I changed the form. Four fields. It asks those four questions and it prefills the version. Since then the reports got better and my own answers got shorter. That outcome matters more than the bug count. Ask first. Ask always.

I still get one line reports. Four questions go back. About half the time the person answers and it turns out to be real.

I answer every comment for the first two hours after I post and I've stopped apologising for it. Two hours. That's the window.

I've watched people treat replies as overhead. They're the opposite. The comment thread is where the post gets tested, and the objections in it tell me what I got wrong before the wider audience sees it.

I've changed my position in a comment section twice this year. Both times somebody brought a fact I didn't have. Both times I was glad to be corrected. That's worth more than another post and it costs me nothing extra to do it.

That cost is real. Two hours is two hours, and I've had weeks where I couldn't do it. The weeks I don't answer, the post does worse and I learn less.

So I'd say engagement isn't a growth tactic for me. It's the research step. It just happens to be public.

I deleted a dependency last month that I couldn't maintain and the code got forty lines longer and about a thousand times easier to reason about. Worth the trade.

The package did one thing well and four things badly, and I was using two of the bad parts because they were there. When it published a breaking change with no note, I read its source for the first time in two years and found a bug that had been affecting my output.

So I copied the eight lines I needed, wrote a test with four real examples, and removed the import. Now the behaviour is mine. The failure modes sit in a file I can read in one sitting. My problem now.

I'm not against dependencies. I've got thirty of them. I'm against the ones I've never opened, because those bugs belong to me anyway.

Every package you add is a promise about the future. Somebody else's future, and you're the one holding it.

I shipped a fix at six in the evening once and watched the graph for two hours. The morning version of me would have shipped the same fix and slept.

I learned that night who's awake. The person waiting for the fix is often in another timezone, and the deploy window is a convenience for the deployer. I was thinking about my evening. The night version of a decision gets made by somebody who wants to close the laptop, and that person is a poor judge of what a user can tolerate for eight more hours.

I check one thing now before I decide to wait for morning. How many people are affected right now, and is the fix reversible. If it's reversible and somebody is blocked, I ship it. If the change cannot be undone, it waits, and I say out loud why.

I won't ship something irreversible at six because I'm tired of holding it. That's the version of the story where the evening becomes a weekend.

Two rules. One minute of thought. Reversible and urgent goes out. Irreversible waits for daylight and a second pair of eyes.

My first week at a new job taught me more about onboarding than any template, and the lesson was that nobody told me what I was allowed to break. All the docs were there. I read them.

What I couldn't find anywhere was the permission map. Which system can I touch on my second day. Which one needs a second person. Which one will page somebody at two in the morning if I get it wrong.

So I asked, and the answers were interesting. Three of the five systems were fine for a new person. One needed a review and the other needed a ticket and a window. Nobody had written any of that because everybody already knew it.

I wrote it down and put it in the onboarding doc. It's about nine lines. The next hire read it and his first week looked nothing like mine.

New people don't fail because they don't know the architecture. They fail because they don't know where the floors are soft, and that knowledge lives in the heads of whoever's been there longest.

Four studios shut in one afternoon. All four had shipped something people liked. I want to sit on that fact for a second.

The instinct is to look for the bad game. There isn't one. There's a portfolio review, a target margin, and a list of teams that cost more than the plan allowed.

That's what I mean when I say motive beats tool. If the business model needs a hit rate that no studio can deliver, then no tool saves you. Not AI, not a bigger team, not a longer schedule. The arithmetic was decided in a room that never saw the code.

I've shipped work that got cancelled for reasons that had nothing to do with the work. It's the most common story in this industry and the least told, because the press release always frames it as a realignment.

So here's my line. Judge the decision, not the tool. Ask what number moved, who moved it, and whether anyone with a shipped game on their record got a vote. Most of the time the answer is no.

Getting one name right saved me a rewrite. One word. One hour. I know because I came close to the wrong name.

The thing was a payment status and I wanted to call it paid. Somebody on the call asked what happens when a payment is reversed, and the hour followed from there. The name paid implies a final state and what I was building had four of them.

So it became settlement state and the four values were named. That one word did a design review for me, because every place I used it had to answer which state it meant, and a broken place couldn't hide behind a comfortable word.

I've watched a bad name make a bad design invisible for two years. Nobody argues about whether a thing called status is doing too much. It already sounds like it does everything. That's the whole trick.

Cheapest design tool I have is a name that a person could argue with. If the word is comfortable, it's hiding something.

A junior sent me his first pull request and it was one hundred and forty lines of something that worked. I asked him to cut it to forty and he looked like I had insulted his family.

I explained what I was after. Not less functionality. Fewer decisions in one place. The change did four things and a reviewer can't hold four things at once, so a bug in one of them hides behind the other three.

He came back with two changes instead of one and both were good. The first was forty lines and the second was sixty, and the second one was a different problem that he had found while splitting the first. He wouldn't have seen it in the original file.

That's the part people miss about small diffs. They aren't just easier to review. They make you notice the thing you were about to bundle in.

Since then I describe the shape I want before I ask for it. One change, one reason, one place to look when it breaks.

An outage taught me more about writing than any course, and the lesson was that the people waiting need a sentence, not a fix.

Forty minutes in, I was deep in a log file and had said nothing to the team. Forty minutes. Silence. In my head I was working, and in their heads I was silent, and silence during an outage reads as either chaos or hiding.

So I gave a bad status when somebody asked. It was technical and it sounded like a conclusion. I know which line did the work. What they needed was one line about what was broken, one about who it was affecting, and one about when I'd speak again.

I wrote those three lines after we were up. Now they're the template, and the last line is the useful one. Next update in fifteen minutes.

Nothing about the incident changed. The shape of the telling changed, and that's the part people remember when they decide whether they trust you with the next one.

Everyone tells you to give an AI agent tools. Nobody tells you the part that actually matters, which is what happens when the tool fails.

I've built three small agents now. Each one worked in the demo and broke in the edge, because a tool returning an error is the normal case and not the exception. A model that gets a clean response is easy. A model that has to recover is the whole problem.

So I write the failure path first. What happens when the API times out. What happens when the response is malformed. What happens when the agent loops. If I can't answer those, it doesn't ship. I've held that line three times this year.

I've watched people skip this and I understand the temptation. The happy path is a better video. The failure path is where the thing actually lives, and nobody films it.

Three agents in, my rule is unchanged. No fire and forget. Every call gets a handler or it doesn't get made.

I lost my train of thought in front of about eighty people and what saved me was a habit I'd built by accident. I'd printed the slides and written four words on the back of each.

Not a script. Four words. The one idea of that slide, in my own handwriting, so a glance would restart the engine.

That day I stood there for what felt like nine seconds and what was probably three, looked down, read four words, and carried on. Nobody noticed. Two people asked me afterwards how I stayed calm, which tells you what it looked like from the outside.

The habit came from laziness. I'd been too lazy to write a full script and the four words were all I'd bothered with. That laziness turned out to be the technique.

So I'd say this to anybody presenting. Write the four words. The script is a trap because it makes the recovery impossible, since you'll be searching for a sentence instead of an idea.

Helion ran a fusion machine on deuterium and tritium fuel in February. Nobody I know talked about it for a week.

The interesting part isn't the fuel. It's that a private company got there before a national lab published a version of the same step, and it did it with investment money instead of a government programme.

I care because of the shape of the money. Fusion has been permanently twenty years away for my whole life, and the thing that changed wasn't the physics. It was that somebody decided there was a company to build. That changes the failure mode. A programme that misses a milestone gets refunded. A company that misses a milestone runs out of runway and the engineers go somewhere else. The clock is shorter.

So the optimism comes with that clock attached to it, which is exactly why it might work. I'm not predicting a plant. I'm noting that the first machine to burn that fuel wasn't a government one. That's the shift.

I stole a checklist habit from aviation and it changed how I do anything with more than four steps. The trick isn't the list. It's reading it out loud while doing the thing.

A deploy for us has eight steps. Not long. Long enough that I skipped the database copy once, at about eleven at night, and spent a weekend on the consequences.

Now the list is in the repo and I read each line as I do it, out loud, even when nobody is there. It feels silly for the first three deploys and after that it's just the process.

I know what makes it work. The reading. A checklist I glance at gets skimmed. A line I say out loud forces my hand to wait for my mouth.

Eight steps and one voice. Cheapest safety system I've ever installed, and it costs about ninety seconds of looking like a person talking to himself.

A disk failed on a Friday and the recovery took four hours, and the reason it took four hours is that nobody had ever tested the backup. Four hours. We had three copies and none of them restored.

The backup ran every night and reported success for about two years, and that report only ever meant the copy completed, which is not the same thing as the copy being usable. It never meant anybody could read it. The archive format had changed twice in that window.

One old snapshot saved us. It was on a machine somebody forgot to decommission. We got the data from July and rebuilt the delta by hand, and I spent the weekend not sleeping well.

We restore to a scratch machine every month now. It's scheduled, it has an owner, and the job fails loudly if the restore doesn't open. That's forty minutes of compute a month. Cheapest insurance we have.

A backup you have never restored is a belief system. It's comfortable and it isn't a copy of anything.

I got laid off once and the thing I remember is the twenty minutes before the meeting. Twenty minutes. No agenda. I knew what it was.

What I did in those twenty minutes turned out to matter for the next year. I opened my notes and wrote down three things I'd shipped, with dates, and one person's name for each. Not for the meeting. For the week after it.

The reason is that a layoff is the only professional moment where nobody helps you remember what you did. Your access goes, the documents go, and the story becomes whatever you can say in a room three days later.

I've kept that habit since. Ten minutes a month. Every quarter I write a page about what I built and who it helped.

Everybody should have that page. You don't need it until you suddenly do, and by then the systems you'd have looked in are gone.

Obsidian cut 52 people. ZeniMax cut 213. Eleven days later the publisher announced a remaster of a game the first studio made.

I don't have a memo. I have a calendar. Eleven days is short enough that I'd like somebody to explain the sequence, because from outside it looks like the headcount and the catalogue were decided in the same meeting.

This is the pattern I keep pointing at. The people who make the thing get treated as a cost line. The thing they made gets treated as an asset. Same company, same week, two different ledgers.

I've watched this in every creative industry I've paid attention to. Music, film, games, all of it. The catalogue is permanent and the people are seasonal, and once a company internalises that, the decisions write themselves.

I don't think this needs a moral lecture. It needs a question. If the studio was worth keeping the games from, what changed in eleven days that made the people optional. Nobody has answered that one yet.
