The first hour of a new project is the only hour I've never regretted slowing down, and I've watched myself try to skip it about nine times.

I write three sentences in that hour. Three sentences. What this is for, who it's for, and what would make me stop. Then I read them back and change a line, because a line is always wrong on the first pass.

I care most about the third sentence. I've watched two projects run for months with nobody able to say what would end them, which is how a project becomes a permanent cost.

After that hour I go fast. No plan, no diagram, no estimate. Those three sentences do the work. Everything after them is execution.

Nine projects in. Every project that started with that hour finished. The rest are the projects I still explain to people, and the explanation is longer than the hour ever was.

#AI #BuildInPublic #VibeCoding #tech #SoftwareEngineering #Developer #Engineering #Product #BuildInPublic #TechNews #Planning #Startup #Craft #Strategy #Projects #Advice
---
The interview question I always ask is what the worst bug was that they shipped. Not the best system. The worst bug. I've asked it in about forty interviews. It tells me more than any architecture discussion.

Some people say they have never shipped a bad bug. That isn't a good sign. Either they haven't shipped much or they aren't being honest, and both of those tell me something I need to know.

People I remember described the bug and then described what they changed about their process. One person said he now writes a test before fixing anything that cost him more than an hour, which is a rule I stole.

I also listen for blame. If the story ends with the person who wrote the code before them, I ask a second question about what they had control over. Almost everybody has one thing they could have done differently.

I'm not looking for somebody who's careful. I'm looking for somebody who has been wrong and has a method now.

---
I did a code review last month that took four minutes and changed what we shipped. Four minutes. I almost skipped it.

The change was nine lines in a file I didn't own. I opened it because the author asked, and she was three weeks into the job. Her diff put a retry around a call that was already retried higher up the stack, so a failure would now happen nine times instead of three.

Most reviews stop at style. Naming, spacing, a missing test. Those are the things you can see in four minutes and they cost you nothing. The expensive thing is usually a fact about the rest of the system, and that fact lives in a file you have to open to find.

So I spent three of those four minutes reading the retry code above hers. That's the whole trick. She said nobody had ever compared her change against the caller. Read the neighbours. Then read the change.

#AI #BuildInPublic #VibeCoding #tech #CodeReview #SoftwareEngineering #Developer #Engineering #Craft #BuildInPublic #TechNews #Mentorship #Teams #Hiring #Coding #Advice
---
I evaluate a model by the same test every time, and the test is a task I've already failed with the previous one. That's it.

Benchmarks are useful for a first filter and useless for a decision. What I want to know is whether the specific thing that broke last month breaks again this month, and the only way to know is to run it.

I keep a small set of tasks for this. Ten tasks. All drawn from real work. All with a known answer. I've run the same ten against five models this year. The scores moved in a way the leaderboards didn't predict.

I don't upgrade on launch day. That gap is why. A model that wins on a reasoning suite can still fail my task, and my task is the only one that pays.

So I'll keep the ten tasks and I'll add one every time something breaks. Over time that list becomes the most valuable thing I own.

#AI #BuildInPublic #VibeCoding #tech #AI #Evaluation #LLM #MachineLearning #Developer #Benchmarks #Engineering #BuildInPublic #TechNews #Testing #Quality #SoftwareEngineering

I built a dashboard that four people asked for and one person opened. It ran for a year and I never deleted it.

Nobody said anything because it looked fine. A chart is never wrong. It just sits there being a chart, and nobody has to admit they don't look at it. I should've asked first what decision it changes.

If the answer is nothing, it's a poster. If the answer is a decision somebody makes weekly, then you need to know the exact moment they make it, because a dashboard that needs a person to remember to open it gets opened twice.

I replaced it. One number, sent to one person, on Monday morning. The number was late reports and it came with one line about who owned each one. That version got read. Not because it looked better. Because it arrived on its own and nobody had to remember to open it.

Fusion has pulled in something like 15 billion dollars of investment, and the international energy agency now features it in its energy innovation report. Fifteen billion is real money and it is also small money.

Put it next to the other number. Data centers alone are adding demand that utilities describe in gigawatts, and a gigawatt of anything costs more than a decade of fusion research.

Fusion isn't the thing that solves the next five years of demand. It isn't. Anybody selling it that way is selling a story. What it can solve is the part after that. A grid running on batteries covers the evening. It doesn't cover a bad winter or a week of still air, and that's the hole a baseload source fills.

I'd rather argue about the schedule than the magic. Thirty companies with different designs is a portfolio, and a portfolio is the only honest way to bet on something this uncertain. I've no idea which one lands. So would anybody else.

A bug appeared every Tuesday afternoon for four months and the cause was a cron job nobody owned. I want to write down how we found it, because the method is the point.

We couldn't reproduce it. We tried for two weeks and failed, which is the normal outcome when the trigger is time. So we stopped trying to reproduce and started trying to record. Every time it happened we wrote three lines. What the user did, what time it was, what the system said.

After five occurrences the Tuesday pattern was obvious and nobody had seen it, because the reports were spread across a chat and a ticket and two emails. Then it was a thirty minute fix. A cleanup task ran on Tuesdays and deleted rows a report was still reading.

The lesson isn't about cron. It's that the four month cost came from the recording, not the fixing. We had five pieces of evidence and nowhere to put them. If you can't reproduce a bug, stop trying. Start a file. Make the next occurrence write itself down.

A one page document changed a decision I'd been arguing about for three weeks and it took somebody forty minutes to write. One page. Three weeks. I was the one arguing, and I was losing.

I'd been making the case in meetings. Each time I said it slightly differently, and each time somebody raised a concern I answered, and the concern came back the next week because nobody remembered the answer.

She wrote the page instead. It had the problem in two sentences, three options with a cost each, and a recommendation with a reason. Then she put it in a channel where the people who mattered would read it.

The argument ended in a day because it stopped being an argument and became a document people could point at. I'd been treating the meeting as the evidence, and the evidence was never in the room.

If you're arguing the same point for the third time, stop talking and write it down. The third repetition is the tell. That's the signal.

I read the source of a library for the first time in three years and found out I'd been using it wrong. Not badly. Just wrong, in a way the docs allowed.

The function had a default that made it look like it waited. It didn't wait. It returned immediately and the thing I thought was a callback was a timeout handler, so half my calls were racing and I'd been blaming the network. The docs weren't wrong. They were thin.

That's the trade with dependencies. The docs describe intent and the source describes behaviour. When those two differ, the source is the truth and the source is always available. I try to open the source of any package I put on a hot path. Not read all of it. Read the function I call and the one it calls.

Three minutes of reading has saved me two outages this year. The docs were fine both times and I was still wrong.

One tracker put 2026 at 10,863 layoffs and 135 closed studios in games and tech. I want to be careful with that number, because it's an aggregator and not a filing.

That said, the direction is not in dispute. Every quarter this year has been worse than the one before it, and the announcements have stopped being events. That's the part that should worry people. When a studio closure stops being news, the norm has moved.

I've been on the wrong side of that curve. You read the trades like weather, and after a while you stop feeling each one, which is how a slow disaster works.

So I'd rather cite a soft number I can defend than a hard number I cannot. The tracker is directional. The individual announcements are checkable. Go read three of them in the original if you want the texture, because the texture is where it stops being statistics.

The trend is real. I just won't dress it up as precision I don't have.

A take home assignment cost me a Saturday and the company hired somebody else, and I'd still do it again for the information it gave me.

The task was small. Parse a file, join two sources, output a summary. I spent six hours because I wrote tests and a short note about the two places where the specification was ambiguous. The note is the part that mattered to me.

Two people on the call read it and one of them disagreed with a decision I'd made, and we spent fifteen minutes on a real disagreement about a real document. That's more signal about a workplace than three rounds of behavioural questions. I didn't get the role. What I got was a picture of how they argue, which is the thing I wanted to know.

What I wouldn't do is a take home that takes a week and mirrors the job. That's unpaid work with an audience of one. Six hours is my line and I say it out loud before I start. Ask for the time estimate in writing. Ask what the evaluation looks for.
